\chapter*{Abstract}
This thesis studies deep mean-variance hedging in time-continuous mathematical finance. We present theoretical techniques developed in \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging} used to approximate hedging strategies with neural networks and apply them to mean-variance hedging. We discuss the continuous time generalization proposed in \parencite[Chapter 5]{Arandjelovic2025} and, in particular, the deep mean-variance hedging result \parencite[Thm.~5.35]{Arandjelovic2025}, formulated for square integrable martingale price processes, as well as general mean-variance hedging theory in the martingale and semimartingale cases. We use these ideas to propose an extension of the deep mean-variance result \parencite[Thm.~5.35]{Arandjelovic2025} to a more general setting, allowing price processes to have a finite variation part. The relevant theoretical tools, such as stochastic integration and the neural network density results, are introduced and discussed, aiming for a self-contained thesis while respecting the limited scope of this thesis.

\chapter{Introduction}
\label{chap:introduction}

\section{Motivation and context}
\label{sec:introduction-motivation-context}
One of the central problems addressed in mathematical finance is the pricing and hedging of contingent claims. This is best addressed in the framework of probability theory, formalizing the flow of information available in the market and the random evolutions of underlying prices. In probability theoretic jargon, the adapted notions are filtered probability spaces and random variables. The flow of information is then modeled by a filtration \(\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_t)_{0\leq t\leq T}\) with a fixed terminal time \(T\in(0,\infty]\), where the information available to the market participants at time \(t\) is given by the sets contained in the collection \(\mathcal{F}_t\). The market consists of a price process \(X\) representing the prices of some randomly evolving risky assets, some non-risky assets ( for instance, bank accounts) growing at a fixed interest rate, and agents trading in the market by holding or selling dynamic portfolios \((\eta_t,\vartheta_t)_{0\leq t\leq T}\), where \(\eta_t\) denotes the number of units of shares of the risk-less asset held at time \(t\) and \(\vartheta_t\) the number of units of shares of risky assets held at time \(t\). 
Such an ordered collection of random variables is called a \textit{stochastic process}. A stochastic process can be viewed in three related ways: as an indexed family of random variables, as a map on the product space \(\Omega\times[0,T]\), or as a path-valued random variable. Although these viewpoints describe the same underlying object, they emphasize different measurability properties. 
It is a fundamental requirement in mathematical finance that the value of \(\vartheta_t\) is known prior to time \(t\) in order to prevent insider trading and arbitrage. We call a strategy \(\vartheta\) that fulfills this requirement predictable.


Hedging, as understood in this thesis, is the problem of achieving a random payoff \(H\) with a dynamic portfolio  \((\eta_t,\vartheta_t)_{0\leq t\leq T}\) at time \(T\), without injecting or extracting money along the way. Such \textit{self-financing} portfolios are completely determined by \((\vartheta_t)_{0\leq t\leq T}\) and the initial wealth \(V_0\in\mathbb{R}\). Denoting by \(G_t(\vartheta)\) the cumulative gains and losses of holding the strategy \((\eta_t,\vartheta_t)_{0\leq t\leq T}\)  up to time \(t\), we can write down the value of a self-financing strategy at time \(t\) as follows:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:value process}
    V_t(\vartheta)=V_0+G_t(\vartheta),
\end{equation}
where we consider the initial capital \(V_0\) as a parameter.
In order to formalize hedging, we introduce a hedging criterion \(m\) corresponding to the choice of how one measures and weights the error committed by the hedging.
The problem of hedging can then be stated in general form.
\begin{equation}\label{eq:first notion of heding}
    \inf_{(V_0,\vartheta_t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\Theta}m(V_0+G_{T}(\vartheta)-H),
\end{equation}
where \(\Theta\) denotes the space of strategies that we allow for hedging. The
 set of portfolios over which the minimization should be considered, whether the infimum is achieved or not, and if so, whether it is unique and in what sense, constitutes central questions that one hopes to address in a mathematically precise way. In particular, the answers will depend on our choice of \(m\). This thesis predominantly treats \textit{mean-variance} hedging, which chooses a quadratic criterion for \(m\). The corresponding value to be minimized is then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:introduction-mean-variance-problem}
    \inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R},\vartheta\in\Theta}\mathbb{E}\Big[
        (V_0 + G_T(\vartheta)-H)^2
    \Big].
\end{equation}
In this case, we shall see that the main approach to solving \eqref{eq:first notion of heding} is to reduce \eqref{eq:introduction-mean-variance-problem} to a projection problem in the Hilbert space \(L^2\). The space \(L^2\), being an inner product space, admits an inherent notion of orthogonality. Finding the best approximation of \(H\) by a self-financing strategy  then reduces to orthogonally decomposing \(H\) into a part belonging to the subspace spanned by \(V_T(\vartheta)\), ranging over the strategies \(\vartheta\in\Theta\), and a part orthogonal to it. Note that by a subspace of a vector space, we always mean a linear subspace. A subset need not be closed under addition and scalar multiplication. 

In the case where the subspace \(\mathcal{A}(\Theta)\) spanned by \(V_0+G_T(\Theta)\) for \(\vartheta\in \Theta\) and \(V_0\in\mathbb{R}\) is a subspace of \(L^2\), the question regarding the existence of a solution to \eqref{eq:introduction-mean-variance-problem} reduces to the question of whether \(\mathcal{A}(\Theta)\) is a closed subspace of \(L^2\). 



By definition of the infimum over a real set, we can always find a sequence of portfolios \((V_0,\vartheta)\) such that the corresponding hedging errors converge to the infimum of  \eqref{eq:introduction-mean-variance-problem}. If the infimum is achieved in \(\mathcal{A}(\Theta)\), the question is thus whether we can find and explicitly characterize such an approximation sequence. If we were to approximate deterministic functions and not random variables, we might consider a set of functions that is sufficiently rich (dense) to approximate the target functions. A particularly useful choice with great practical importance is the set of neural networks. As previously stated, one can consider a stochastic process as a path-valued random variable. Hence, for a stochastic process, we might try to approximate its random paths. Indeed, this is a fruitful approach. However, the way in which these random paths are approximated using deterministic functions is subtle. This thesis discusses this approach, both in a finite setup as presented in \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging} and its continuous extension proposed by \parencite{Arandjelovic2025}. Heuristically, the key insight is to use the Doob-Dynkin representation lemma to represent the random variable representing the dynamic strategy \(\vartheta\) at every point in time as a deterministic function of the underlying randomness of the system, modeled by a market information process \(Y\). This allows us to write 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:heuristic Doob}
    \vartheta_t = f(Y_s,s\leq t)
\end{equation}
for some deterministic function \(f\). This can be done for any random variable whose value at time \(t\) can be determined using knowledge of \(Y\) up to time \(t\). The key insight is to approximate the deterministic function \(f\) and, thus, by \eqref{eq:heuristic Doob}, the random variable \(\vartheta_t\). We then get an approximation for the stochastic process \((\vartheta_t)_{0\leq t\leq T}\) by collecting approximated random variables. This indeed yields a candidate for the approximated stochastic process, but notice this procedure does not necessarily conserve any joint measurability of \(\vartheta\). In particular, the new stochastic process need not be predictable and hence need not be an allowed strategy.. In a discrete time setup, however, we do not encounter these measurability issues, and the approximating procedure works perfectly fine and has been proposed and worked out in \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging}. The continuous time situation needs careful thought on how to approximate \(\vartheta\). A satisfying approach is given in \parencite[Ch.5]{Arandjelovic2025} and discussed in detail in this thesis.


Understanding how to approximate stochastic processes in discrete and continuous time can be used to find explicit approximation sequences of the optimal minimizing element of \eqref{eq:first notion of heding}, if it exists in \(\mathcal{A}(\Theta)\), for different choices of \(m\). This leads to the deep mean–variance results of \parencite[Thm.4.9]{Buehler2018DeepHedging} and \parencite[Thm.~5.35]{Arandjelovic2025}.


In the time continuous paradigm, a well adapted choice is to take the price process \(X\) to be a square-integrable martingale, and the respective set of admissible strategies in \(L^2(M)\). The respective notion of stochastic integration preserves enough structure, allowing for a clean treatment of the solution theory of \eqref{eq:introduction-mean-variance-problem} and, moreover, the approximation of the optimal portfolio in the sense discussed in \parencite[Ch.5]{Arandjelovic2025}. Considering the more general case where \(X\) is a special
semimartingale with decomposition \(X = X_0 + M + A\)
, we are led to generalize the set of allowed strategies from \(L^2(M)\) to 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:extended admissible strategies}
    \Theta = L^2(M) \cap L^2(A).
\end{equation}
In this more general case, we consider similar questions: Given the choice \eqref{eq:extended admissible strategies}, does a solution to \eqref{eq:introduction-mean-variance-problem} exist, and if so, can it be approximated in the same manner as in the case where \(X\) is a square-integrable martingale and \(\Theta=L^2(M)\)? In order to study approximating sequences of the optimal portfolio in \(L^2(M,A)\), if it exists, we will examine the necessary and sufficient conditions under which it is enough to approximate the strategies in \(L^2(M)\). Generally speaking, the aim of this thesis is, first of all, to precisely formulate these questions and their respective contexts to answer them satisfactorily. 

\section{Scope and contribution}
\label{sec:introduction-scope-contribution}

The purpose of this thesis is purely theoretical. We do not study numerical implementations,
training algorithms, simulation procedures, or empirical performance. Instead, we investigate
the mathematical approximation problem underlying a continuous-time version of deep
mean–variance hedging. We do so by studying the existence of the optimal mean-variance hedging solution for different levels of generality of the price process \(X\), and by understanding the stochastic methods used to approximate the optimal hedging strategies. 

The role of neural networks in this thesis is therefore not primarily
computational, but functional-analytic: they provide approximation classes for the
deterministic functions from which predictable trading strategies are generated. 

Several parts of the thesis are expository. We recall the necessary background on hedging in mathematical finance,
convex risk measures, mean-variance hedging, neural network approximation, and stochastic
integration. This material is included to make the continuous time treatment of deep mean-variance hedging in Chapter \ref{ch:deep mean-square hedging in continuous time} as self-contained as possible,
 and to clarify the relation between deep-hedging and classical
mean-variance hedging, as well as the core insights involved in generalizing the discrete time setup of \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging} to the continuous time analog presented in \parencite{Arandjelovic2025}.

The main contributions of the thesis are the following. First, we make explicit how the deep
hedging framework of \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging} and, in particular, Proposition \parencite[Prop.4.9]{Buehler2018DeepHedging}, can be specialized to the quadratic loss and hence to a
finite discrete-time mean-variance hedging problem.

Secondly, we present the relevant results \parencite[Ch.~5]{Arandjelovic2025} and show how one can improve the integrability of the price process under \parencite[Thm.~5.35]{Arandjelovic2025} conditions by a change of variable approach. 
We argue why the approach taken in \parencite{Arandjelovic2025} is not only particularly elegant, but also why an arguably more direct generalization of the discrete time methods of \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging} is not well adapted. 

Finally, we show how the continuous-time mean variance hedging result  \parencite[Prop.5.35]{Arandjelovic2025} can be generalized to a larger class of price processes, namely semimartingales in \(\mathcal{S}^2\). In order to do so, we are naturally led to the study of the space of strategies \eqref{eq:extended admissible strategies} 
and the conditions for which \(\Theta=L^2(M)\cap L^2(A)=L^2(M)\). This has been studied exhaustively by \parencite{DelbaenSchachermayer1998ExistenceAbsolutelyContinuousLMM}. 

\section{Outline of the thesis}
\label{sec:introduction-outline}

Chapter \ref{ch:hedging} introduces the basic mathematical setup used throughout the thesis. We introduce and motivate the stochastic basis, discounted price processes,
predictable trading strategies, self-financing portfolios, trading gains, and the space of
terminal values generated by admissible strategies. We then discuss two hedging criteria:
convex risk measures and the quadratic criterion, leading to mean-square hedging.

Chapter \ref{ch:Deep Hedging in Discrete Time} reviews the finite discrete-time deep-hedging
framework of \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging}. We explain how predictability and the Doob-Dynkin lemma
reduce the approximation of trading strategies to the approximation of deterministic
functions of market information. We then specialize the framework to the quadratic loss
and formulate the corresponding finite discrete-time deep mean-variance hedging result.
We use \parencite[Prop.3.2;Cor.3.5.;Prop.4.9]{Buehler2018DeepHedging}.

Chapter \ref{ch:Neural Networks and Universal Approximation} recalls the neural network approximation results used
later. We focus on shallow feedforward neural networks and their density properties in
spaces of continuous functions and \(L^p\)-spaces. These results provide the deterministic
approximation tools needed to construct algorithmically generated trading strategies. Furthermore, we provide corresponding density results for generalized neural networks on more general domain spaces. In particular, we present \parencite[Prop.5.15;Thm.5.16]{Arandjelovic2025}.

Chapter \ref{ch:continuous time mean variance hedging} develops the continuous-time
mean-variance hedging framework. We first treat stochastic integration with respect to
square-integrable martingales and discuss the corresponding Hilbert space structure. We then
pass to special semimartingales. This chapter also recalls the closedness problem for
\(G_T(\Theta)\), which is central to mean-variance hedging in the semimartingale case. The construction of the stochastic integral and the respective martingale spaces follows mostly from \parencite[Sec.10.1;Sec.12]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications} and \parencite[Ch.II, Ch.IV]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}. The discussion on mean-variance hedging mostly follows \parencite[Sec.4]{Schweizer2001GuidedTourQuadraticHedging}.

Chapter \ref{ch:Approximation by Algorithmic Strategies in Continuous Time} studies the approximation of
predictable strategies by algorithmically generated strategies in continuous time. We explain
why a naive extension of the finite discrete-time argument is insufficient and discuss the relevant notion of convergence. We then construct suitable
classes of algorithmically generated predictable processes, prove corresponding density statements, and infer the resulting approximation theoretic results. 
In particular, we use the following results: \parencite[Prop.5.17;Prop.5.21;Prop.5.28;Thm.5.29,Thm.5.32,Thm.5.35]{Arandjelovic2025}


Chapter \ref{ch:deep mean-square hedging in continuous time} combines the approximation
results with the mean-variance hedging problem. We first formulate the resulting deep
mean-variance hedging statement in the martingale case. We then extend the argument to
suitable semimartingale price processes by using the \(L^2(M)\cap L^2(A)\)-framework and
the closedness properties of spaces of stochastic integrals.
This chapter uses results and ideas from \parencite{DelbaenMonatSchachermayerSchweizerStricker1997WeightedNormInequalities}. In particular: 
\parencite[Lemma.3.5;Prop.3.6.;Thm.3.7;Thm.4.1]{DelbaenMonatSchachermayerSchweizerStricker1997WeightedNormInequalities}.





\chapter{Mathematical setup of hedging}\label{ch:hedging}
In order to explain the concept of hedging, we first convey a conceptual understanding. This motivates the probabilistic set-up that leads to a well defined mathematical problem, whose solution may be studied analytically.


We explain hedging from the perspective of a seller of a European call option. The latter gives the buyer the option to buy the underlying asset at the time of maturity \(T\) at the strike price \(K\). The strike price and strike time are known at the time the option is acquired. Crucially, the owner of the option can only exercise the contract at maturity, not before. At maturity, the owner of the option will exercise his right to buy if the price of the underlying asset \(S\) at maturity is higher than the strike price \(K\). If the price of the underlying asset at maturity is below the strike price, the owner of the option does not exercise his right to buy. Thus, the payoff of the option for the owner is given by 
\begin{equation*}
    H = \max(S_T-K,0).
\end{equation*}
Note that the price \(S\) at the maturity of the underlying asset is random, whereas the strike price is deterministic. Hence, for a given state of the world \(\omega\), the random payoff of the European call option is \(H(\omega)=\max(S_T(\omega)-K,0)\). More generally, we will refer to \(T\) as the \textit{final} time.


From the perspective of the seller of the European call option, the gain of the buyer of the European call option is the amount of money he is obliged to pay. We call this a \textit{liability}. In order for the seller to be able to pay out the potential payoff of the call option, he might consider investing in the stock market himself and hoping to make at least a profit that enables him to compensate for the liability. This is what we call \textit{hedging}. The questions arising are: Is there a trading strategy that will give him, almost surely, a gain at least equal to the payoff of the liability? If not, how close can he get? Close in what sense? What is the right price I should ask for the option? These are questions that mathematical finance tries to answer precisely. In order to do so, one needs to set up a mathematical basis that allows for a precise formulation and analytical treatment of these questions.

\section{Self-financing strategies and trading gains}
The results presented in this thesis will be formulated on finite and infinite time horizons, corresponding to the choices \(T<\infty\) and \(T=\infty\) for the final time \(T\). Chapter \ref{ch:Deep Hedging in Discrete Time} is formulated for \(T<\infty\), while the rest of this thesis allows for \(T=\infty\) and a corresponding unbounded time horizon \(\mathbb{R}_+=[0,\infty)\). In this introductory section, we allow for both cases. To do so, we introduce the following notation.


Let \([0,T]\subseteq[0,\infty]\). For discrete time models, we take \([0,T]\subseteq\mathbb{N}\cup\{+\infty\}\), where we choose the convention that \(0\in\mathbb{N}\). For \(\mathbb{R}_+=[0,\infty)\), we adopt the following notation: 
\begin{align*}
    \mathbb{T}_+=\mathbb{R}_+\cap [0,T],
\end{align*}
and we call \(\mathbb{T}_+\) the \textit{time grid}.
In the case where \(T<\infty\) we have \(\mathbb{T}_+=[0,T]\). In the case where \(T=\infty\) we have \(\mathbb{T}_+=\mathbb{R}_+\). The discrete case is denoted in the same way and is understood from context whether \(\mathbb{T}_+\) is uncountable or countable.



We assume an underlying probability space 
\begin{equation*}
    (\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}).
\end{equation*}
Furthermore, we shall assume that the probability space is complete, that is, the \(\sigma\)-algebra \(\mathcal{F}\) contains 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:nullsets}
    \mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\coloneqq\Big\{A\in2^{\Omega}:\exists B\in\mathcal{F}, A\subseteq B, \mathbb{P}[B]=0\Big\}.
\end{equation} 
To model the flow of information available to agents in the market, we introduce a filtration  \(\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_t)_{t\in \mathbb{T}_+}\), where
\(\mathcal{F}_t\subseteq\mathcal{F}\) is non-decreasing in \(t\) as a sequence of sub-\(\sigma\) algebras.
Moreover, we shall be predominantly interested in the case where the available information is generated by  \(\mathbb{R}^{r}\)-valued random process \(Y=(Y_t)_{t\in \mathbb{T}_+}\), where \(r\) is a natural number. This means that we set 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{F}^0_t\coloneqq\sigma(Y_s:0\leq s\leq t).
\end{equation*}
The latter clearly defines a filtration on \((\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\), and is referred to as the natural filtration for the process \(Y\). The superscript \((\cdot)^0\) clarifies that we consider the raw filtration, which may be incomplete. 
We refer to \(Y\) as the \textit{market information process} and denote the corresponding filtration by \(\mathbb{F}^0\), referring to it as the \textit{natural} filtration of \(Y\). Furthermore, we define 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{F}_T^0\coloneqq\bigvee_{t\in\mathbb{T}_+}\mathcal{F}_t^0,
\end{equation*}
We call a filtration complete if \(\mathcal{F}_0\) contains \(\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\). Considering natural filtrations generated by some process, this is often not the case.
Thus, we might consider its completion defined by 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:completed filtration}
    \mathcal{F}_t\coloneqq\sigma(Y_s:0\leq s\leq t)\vee \mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}},\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{T}_+.
\end{equation}
We denote the corresponding filtration by \(\mathbb{F}\) and define
\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{F}_{T}\coloneqq \bigvee_{t\in\mathbb{T}_+}\mathcal{F}_t
\end{equation*}
The notation is consistent, as one can show that 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{F}_T=\mathcal{F}^0_T\vee\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}. 
\end{equation*}

This corresponds to the statement
\begin{equation}\label{eq:consistency of completion}
    \sigma(\sigma(\bigcup_{t\geq 0}\mathcal{F}_t)\cup\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}})=\sigma(\bigcup_{t\geq 0}\sigma(\mathcal{F}_t\cup\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}})),
\end{equation}
which follows by double inclusion (see Appendix \ref{Appendix:Consistency of completed sigma-algebra}).
We provide a useful characterization of the completed \(\sigma\)-algebra as stated in \parencite[Thm.~1.9]{Folland1999RealAnalysis}
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:characterisation of completed sigma-algebra}
Let \(\mathcal{A}^0\) be a \(\sigma\)-algebra and 
    consider \(\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}^0\vee\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\). Then we have that 
    \(\mathcal{C}=\left\{A\cup E:A\in\mathcal{A}^0,E\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\right\}\) is a \(\sigma\)-algebra and is equal to \(\mathcal{A}\).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    The fact that \(\mathcal{C}\) is a \(\sigma\)-algebra can be found in \parencite[Thm.~1.9]{Folland1999RealAnalysis}. The equality follows from \(\mathcal{A}=\sigma(\mathcal{C})\) by definition and the fact that \(\mathcal{C}\) is a \(\sigma\)-algebra.
\end{proof}
The example of a filtration generated by a process shows that we might not want to assume a completed filtration from the start.  Thus, the stochastic basis we assume through this thesis is either the completed probability space \((\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\) either with the raw filtration, yielding the filtered probability space 
\begin{equation*}
    (\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F}^0,\mathbb{P}),
\end{equation*}
or with the completed filtration
\begin{equation*}
    (\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
\end{equation*}
respectively. 
It might be the case that the raw filtration \(\mathbb{F}^0\) is already complete. In this case, we denote it as the complete filtration by \(\mathbb{F}\).


The price of \(d+1\) tradable assets will be modeled by a \(\mathbb{R}_+^{d+1}\)-valued random process \(S = (S_t)_{t\in\mathbb{T}_+}\), with \(S_t(\omega) = (S_t^0(\omega),\dots,S_t^d(\omega))\in \mathbb{R}_+^{d+1}\) for every \(t\in \mathbb{T}_+\). We call \(S\) the price process. Naturally, the stochastic process \(S\) is assumed to be \(\mathbb{F}\)-adapted, that is, the random variable \(S_t\) is \(\mathcal{F}_t\)-measurable. In other words, the price of the assets modeled by \(S\) at time \(t\) is supposed to be known at time \(t\) (and not in advance). We interpret \(S^0\) as the bank account or any other risk free asset. To fix the units of the asset prices once and for all, we define the discounted price of the \(i^{\rm{th}}\)-asset at time \(t\) by \(X_t^{i}=S_t^{i}/S_t^0\) for \(i=1,\dots,d\) and call the \(\mathbb{R}^d_+\)-valued process \(X\) the \textit{discounted} price process. The discounted price process of the bank account is handled separately and set to the constant process \(1\). We will refer to the discounted price process \(X\) simply as the price process, as we shall always refer to the discounted version. Thus, there is no risk of confusion.


We may want to trade in these \(d+1\) assets by holding a certain number \(\vartheta_t\) of risky assets \(X_t^i,\;i=1,\dots,d\) and a number \(\eta_t\) of the risk-free asset/bank account. We refer to \(\vartheta\) as the \textit{trading strategy} and call the process \((\vartheta,\eta)\) a \textit{portfolio}. It can be adjusted dynamically and generally evolves stochastically over time. We don't want to allow insider trading; that is, knowing the price at time \(t\) of a risky asset before time \(t\), hence giving the possibility to adjust the positions in a way that yields a profit without risk. In order to enforce this requirement, we require strategies to \textit{be predictable}; that is, we require that the strategy \(\vartheta_t\) is known just before time \(t\) (the trader cannot look at what happens at time \(t\) in the market and adjust accordingly). In discrete time, this exactly means that \(\vartheta_t\) is \(\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\)-measurable. In continuous time, we ask for a little bit more and require the process \(\vartheta\) to be measurable as a map on \((\Omega\times\mathbb{T}_+,\mathcal{P})\), where \(\mathcal{P}\) is the predictable \(\sigma\)-algebra generated by adapted, left-continuous processes. Any such process is adapted to the left-continuous filtration \(\mathbb{F}^-=(\mathcal{F}^{-}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{T}_+}\), where 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{F}_t^{-}\coloneqq\bigvee_{s<t}\mathcal{F}_s.
\end{equation*}
The converse is false in general.
The (discounted) \textit{wealth process} is then defined as 
\begin{equation*}
    V_t = \vartheta_t^{tr}\cdot X_t+\eta_t1.
\end{equation*}
We denote the accumulated gains and losses up to time \(t\) by trading the assets \(X\) by \(G_t(\vartheta) = (\vartheta \bullet X)_t\). For countable  \(\mathbb{T}_+\) we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:discrete accumulated gains}
    (\vartheta \bullet X)_t=\sum_{k=1}^{t}\vartheta_k(X_{k}-X_{k-1})
\end{equation}
and for \(\mathbb{T}_+\) uncountable, we set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:continuous accumulated gains}
    (\vartheta \bullet X)_t=\int_0^t\vartheta_s\diff X_s,
\end{equation} 
where the right hand side denotes the stochastic integral of \(\vartheta\) with respect to \(X\). While \eqref{eq:discrete accumulated gains} needs no further justification, we refer to \parencite[Ch.II,Ch.IV]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration} for an extensive discussion of the construction and properties of the stochastic integral \eqref{eq:continuous accumulated gains} in full generality. For a more pragmatic introduction, we refer to Section \ref{sec:Stochastic Integration for square-integrable Martingales} and \ref{sec:stochastic integration with respect to semimartingales}. We refer to both objects as stochastic integrals. 


We denote by \(L(X)\) the set of \(\vartheta\) for which \(\vartheta\bullet X\) is well-defined. This depends on the properties of the integrator \(X\).


Up to this point, we have allowed for external cash flows or costs. The cumulative amount of cash injected or taken out is given by the cumulative cost process 
\begin{equation*}
    C_t = V_t - (\vartheta \bullet X)_t,\quad t\in\mathbb{T}_+. 
\end{equation*}
Requiring that there is no external cash flow for \(t\in \mathbb{T}_+\) makes the portfolio \textit{self-financing}. Thus, for a self-financing portfolio, the cumulative cost process is constant, equal to the initial value of the  portfolio \(C_t=V_0\quad\forall t\in \mathbb{T}_+\). Hence, for a self-financing strategy, the value process \(V\) takes the form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:self financing strategy}
    V_t(\vartheta) = V_0 + (\vartheta\bullet X)_t=V_0+G_t(\vartheta),\quad t\in\mathbb{T}_+. 
\end{equation}
Observe that \eqref{eq:self financing strategy} is fully determined by the initial capital \(V_0\) and the strategy \(\vartheta\). Indeed, \(\vartheta\) and \(V_0\) determine \(V_t\) by  \eqref{eq:self financing strategy}. Finally, \(\eta\) is then determined by \(V_t = \vartheta_t^{tr}X_t+\eta_t\). Hence, we denote a self-financing strategy by \((V_0,\vartheta)\).


Let \(\Theta\) be a subset of predictable, \(\mathbb{R}^d\)-valued processes in \(L(X)\). 
The exact definition of \(\Theta\) depends on the context in which it is used and will be made explicit in any case.
We call a strategy \(\vartheta\) \textit{admissible} if \(\vartheta\in\Theta\). 
We define the following set:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:terminal value processes}
    \mathcal{A}(\Theta)\coloneqq \mathbb{R}+G_T(\Theta)=\left\{V_0+(\vartheta \bullet X)_T:(V_0,\vartheta)\in\mathbb{R}\times \Theta\right\},
\end{equation}
where \(G_T(\Theta)=\left\{(\vartheta \bullet X)_T:\vartheta\in \Theta\right\}\subseteq L^0(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\). We do not display the dependency of \(\mathcal{A}(\Theta)\) on the price process \(X\) as the latter is implicitly present in the dependency of \(\Theta\) on \(X\).
Hence, \(\mathcal{A}(\Theta)\) defines the space of terminal values of portfolios \((V_0,\vartheta)\in\mathbb{R}\times \Theta\). 

\section{General idea of hedging}\label{sec:general idea of hedging}
As explained in the introduction to the current chapter, we want to hedge a liability \(H\), which we model as a real-valued random variable whose value is known at the final time \(T\in (0,\infty]\), that is, \(H\in L^0(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_T,\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R})\). For a concrete example, one might think of \(H\) as a European call option and \(T\) as the strike time of that option.
We call a contingent claim \textit{attainable} if there exists a self-financing strategy \((\vartheta,\eta)\) such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:atteinable strategy}
    V_T = H,\quad \mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}.
\end{equation}
A market is \textit{complete} if all contingent claims are attainable. In this thesis, we focus on hedging in incomplete markets. Hence, for a given contingent claim \(H\), there doesn't necessarily exist a self-financing strategy that replicates \(H\). Also notice that if we allow for strategies that are not self-financing, condition \eqref{eq:atteinable strategy} can always be attained by defining the final value of the cost process as \(C_T=H-V_0-(\vartheta\bullet X)_T\) for any \(\vartheta\in\Theta\). We can approach hedging in incomplete markets in three ways.
\begin{itemize}
    \item Either we insist on the condition \(V_T = H\quad\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}\) and let go of the self-financing assumption for the replicating strategy \((V_0,\vartheta)\). This would entail a non-constant cost process, and the criterion we want to minimize is exactly the cumulative costs of the trading strategy.
    \item Alternatively, we could insist on the self-financing condition and let go of the perfect replication condition \eqref{eq:atteinable strategy}.
    \item Lastly, we could let go of both assumptions.
\end{itemize}
We will concentrate on the second option; that is, we will insist on the self-financing property but let go of the perfect replication property \eqref{eq:atteinable strategy}. 


In particular, the corresponding hedging problem is to find \textit{the optimal} self-financing strategy \((V_0,\vartheta)\) that best approximates the contingent claim at time \(T\). The nature of the problem now heavily relies on what we mean by optimal. In general terms, we consider hedging as the minimization of the hedging error 
\begin{equation*}
    V_T-H
\end{equation*}
under a generalized \textit{hedging criterion}.
\begin{equation*}
    m: L^0(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{F}_T;\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}.
\end{equation*}
Then, we define the optimal hedging portfolio as the solution to the following minimization problem
\begin{equation}\label{eq:general hedging}
    \inf_{(V_0,\vartheta)\in\mathbb{R}\times\Theta}m(V_T(\vartheta)-H).
\end{equation}
We also consider the related optimization for fixed initial capital \(V_0\)
\begin{equation}\label{eq:general hedging for fixed initial capital}
    \inf_{\vartheta\in \Theta}m(G_T(\vartheta)+V_0-H).
\end{equation}
At this point, no finiteness or attainability claims are made or deduced from \eqref{eq:general hedging} and \eqref{eq:general hedging for fixed initial capital}. If, however, \eqref{eq:general hedging} admits a solution \((V_0^*,\vartheta^*)\in\mathbb{R}\times \Theta\), in the sense that the infimum of \eqref{eq:general hedging} is attained by an element of \(\mathcal{A}(\Theta)=\mathbb{R}+G_T(\Theta)\), we call it an \(\Theta\)-\textit{optimal hedging portfolio}. The meaning of \textit{optimal} thus depends on the choice of the hedging criterion \(m\) and the class of admissible strategies \(\Theta\). If it is clear from context, we omit the \(\Theta\) in the naming of a \(\Theta\)-optimal trading portfolio. Respectively, if \eqref{eq:general hedging for fixed initial capital} admits a solution \(\vartheta^*\in\Theta\) for fixed \(V_0\), we call it an \((\Theta,V_0)\)-\textit{optimal hedging strategy}. Again we omit the \((\Theta,V_0)\)-labelling if it is clear from context.

\begin{remark}
Notice that we fixed a particular order in \eqref{eq:general hedging}. As the hedging criterion need not be symmetric, we shall adhere to this convention throughout the thesis. It can be interpreted as the random balance of money the seller of the liability \(H\) has at time \(T\). Hence, if \(V_T(\omega)-H(\omega)<0\) the seller has lost money by selling the option \(H\) and hedging against it via a hedging portfolio with a value \(V_T\) at the strike time, assuming the payoff of the option is instantaneous.
\end{remark}
In what follows, we will consider two different choices for \(m\): convex risk-measure and \(L^2\)-distance.


\subsection{Convex risk measures}
As our first choice, we consider the hedging measure \(m\) to be a \textit{convex risk measure}. As the name suggests, it is intended to measure the risk of holding an asset position. We require that it satisfies the following:
\begin{definition}\label{def:convex risk measure buehler convention}
    We call a map \(\rho: L^0(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_T,\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}\)
\end{definition} a \textit{convex risk measure} if it is
\begin{itemize}
    \item Monotone decreasing: If \(Z_1\geq Z_2\) then \(\rho(Z_1)\leq\rho(Z_2)\).
    \item Convexity: For \(\alpha\in[0,1]\), we have \(\rho(\alpha Z_1+(1-\alpha)Z_2)\leq \alpha\rho(Z_1)+(1-\alpha)\rho(Z_2)\) for any \(Z_1,Z_2\in L^0(\mathcal{F}_T;\mathbb{R})\). 
    \item Cash-Invariant: \(\rho(Z+c)=\rho(Z)-c \quad \text{for all } c\in\mathbb{R}\).
\end{itemize}
Note that we are not enforcing continuity. Nevertheless, if \(\Omega=\{\omega_1,\dots,\omega_N\}\) is finite, then trivially \(X\in L^0(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_T;\mathbb{R})\) can be identified with a subspace of \( \mathbb{R}^N\) since any \(X\in L^0(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_T;\mathbb{R})\) is determined by the tuple \((X(\omega_1),\dots,X(\omega_N))\) (if \(\mathcal{F}_T=\mathcal{F}\), we have \( L^0(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_T;\mathbb{R})\cong \mathbb{R}^N\)). Hence, in the finite case, we can view \(\rho\) as a convex function over a subspace of \(\mathbb{R}^N\), which implies that it is continuous \parencite[Thm.~10.1]{Rockafellar1970ConvexAnalysis}.


The above conditions can be translated into practically relevant requirements. The monotonicity reflects the fact that a position with a larger payoff has a smaller risk associated with it. Convexity formalizes the idea that diversification lowers risk. Cash invariance then models the simple idea that injecting capital lowers the risk of the position by that same amount. Note that the seller perspective we enforced in the order convention taken in \eqref{eq:general hedging} is aligned with the interpretation of \(\rho(X)\) as the risk of holding a position \(X\) in the market. Indeed, by the monotonicity property of the convex-risk measure, if \(V_T^{(1)}-H^{(1)}\) is pointwise smaller than \(V_T^{(2)}-H^{(2)}\), the respective risk is larger; that is, \(\rho(V_T^{(2)}-H^{(2)})\leq\rho(V_T^{(1)}-H^{(1)})\).


Furthermore, we call a position \(X\) \(\rho\)-acceptable if \(\rho(X)\leq 0\), and we call \(X\) a \(\rho\)-indifferent portfolio if \(\rho(X) = \rho(0)\); that is, holding the portfolio \(X\) is equivalent, from the point of view of the convex risk measure \(\rho\), to having no position in the market at all. We call \(\rho\) normalized if \(\rho(0)=0\).
\begin{remark}\label{rem:minimal amound of injected cash for acceptable position}
    Notice that by cash invariance \(\rho(X+\rho(X))=0\). In other words, \(\rho(X)\) is the smallest amount of cash that needs to be added to the portfolio \(X\) in order to make it \(\rho\)-acceptable.
\end{remark} 

We can now define the convex risk measure minimization problem. Let \(\rho\) be a convex risk measure and \(H\in  L^0(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_T,\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R})\) a liability. Then consider
\begin{equation}\label{eq:risk measure hedging}
    \pi_{\mathbb{R}}(H)\coloneqq\inf_{(V_0,\vartheta)\in\mathbb{R}\times \Theta}\rho\left(V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H\right).
\end{equation}

We interpret \(\pi_{\mathbb{R}}(H)\) as the hedging error under optimal hedging with risk-measure \(\rho\), or equivalently, recalling the discussion regarding the interpretation of the identity \(\rho(X+\rho(X))=0\), \(\pi_{\mathbb{R}}(H)\) might be regarded as the minimal amount of cash that makes the position \(V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H\) \(\rho\)-acceptable. 
\begin{remark}\label{rem:ill posed problem}
Notice an important subtlety about \eqref{eq:risk measure hedging}. Due to the cash-invariance property of the convex risk measure \(\rho\), we have 
\begin{align*}
    \pi_{\mathbb{R}}(H) &= \inf_{(V_0,\vartheta)\in\mathbb{R}\times \Theta}\rho\left(V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H\right)\\
    &=\inf_{\vartheta\in \Theta}\inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R}}\rho\left(V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H\right)\\
    &=\inf_{\vartheta\in \Theta}\inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R}}\rho\left((\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H\right)-V_0=-\infty,
\end{align*}
if \(\rho\left(H-(\vartheta\bullet X)_T\right)\) is finite for any \(\vartheta\in\Theta\),
 we can take \(-V_0\) arbitrarily negative. In this sense, the problem is ill defined. 
\end{remark}
Regarding Remark \ref{rem:ill posed problem}, 
we can take a different point of view and consider a fixed, finite, and deterministic \(V_0\in\mathbb{R}\) and analyze the minimization problem 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:risk measure heding with fixed initial capital}
    \pi_{V_0}(H)=\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\rho\left(V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H\right).
\end{equation}
In the context of convex risk-measure hedging, we denote \(\pi=\pi_{V_0}\) as there is no risk of confusion because we established that \(\mathbb{\pi}_{\mathbb{R}}\) is not well defined. 
The minimization problem \eqref{eq:risk measure heding with fixed initial capital} can be motivated and related to the realistic scenario where \(V_0\) is determined as a result of trading derivatives in the market at competitive prices. However, the infimum of \eqref{eq:risk measure heding with fixed initial capital} might still not be finite.  In the case where \(\Omega\) is finite, that is \(|\Omega|=N\in\mathbb{N}\), it is enough to require \(\pi(0)>-\infty\), as shown in \parencite[Cor.~3.5.]{Buehler2018DeepHedging}. This is based on the observation that the map 
\begin{equation*}
    H\mapsto \pi(-H)
\end{equation*}
is a convex risk-measure as in Definition \ref{def:convex risk measure buehler convention}. Indeed, cash-invariance and monotonicity follow directly from the corresponding properties of \(\rho\). For convexity, notice that 
\begin{align*}
    &\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\rho\big(V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X)_T+\alpha H_1+(1-\alpha) H_2\big)
    \\&=\inf_{\vartheta_1\in\Theta}\inf_{\vartheta_2\in\Theta}\rho\Big(\alpha\big((V_0+(\vartheta_1\bullet X)_T+H_1\big)+(1-\alpha) \left(V_0+(\vartheta_2\bullet X)_T+H_2\right)\Big)
    \\&\leq \alpha\inf_{\vartheta_1\in\Theta}\rho\big( (V_0+(\vartheta_1\bullet X)_T+H_1\big)+(1-\alpha)\inf_{\vartheta_2\in\Theta}\rho\big( (V_0+(\vartheta_2\bullet X)_T+H_2\big),
\end{align*}
 where we used the convexity of \(\Theta\) in the second step and the convexity of \(\rho\) in the third step. This shows that \(H\mapsto\pi(-H)\) is a convex risk-measure. The fixed initial wealth problem of \eqref{eq:risk measure heding with fixed initial capital} does not suffer from the issue observed in Remark \ref{rem:ill posed problem} if we assume the additional condition \(\pi(0)>-\infty\), as shown by the following lemma.


\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:Finiteness of inf for finite sample space}
    Assume \(\Omega = \left\{\omega_1,\dots,\omega_N\right\}\) for \(N\in\mathbb{N}\), \(\Theta\) a convex subset of predictable, real-valued processes and \(\pi(0)>-\infty\). Then \(\pi(H)>-\infty\) for all \(H\in L^0(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_T,\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R})\).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    As \(\Omega\) is finite, the set \(\inf_{\omega\in\Omega} H(\omega)\eqcolon\inf H>-\infty\). Hence, using the monotonicity and cash-invariance of the convex risk measures \(\hat{\pi}(H)=\pi(-H)\), in particular, \(H\mapsto \pi(H)\) is increasing, we get
    \begin{equation*}
        \pi(H)\geq\pi(\inf H)=\pi(0)+\inf H>-\infty.
    \end{equation*}
\end{proof}



\subsection{Mean-square hedging}\label{sec:high level discussion of mean-square hedging}
For the second choice of the hedging measure \(m\), we take the squared \(L^2\)-norm that is 
\begin{equation*}
    m(X)=\mathbb{E}\big[X^2\big].
\end{equation*}
In order to make \eqref{eq:general hedging} well defined for this choice of \(m\), we assume \(H\in L^2(\mathbb{P})\) and \(\Theta\subseteq\Theta^{(2)}\), where
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Theta2}
    \Theta^{(2)}=\big\{X\text{-integrable, predictable stochastic process}:\;(\vartheta\bullet X)_T\in L^2(\mathcal{F}_T,\mathbb{P})\big\} .
\end{equation}
The corresponding hedging problem then takes the form 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mean squared hedging}
\inf_{(V_0,\vartheta)\in\mathbb{R}\times \Theta}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\big(V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X)_T\big)-H\Big)^2\bigg].
\end{equation}
In this context, if a solution of the form \(V_0^*+(\vartheta^*\bullet X)_T\in\mathbb{R}+ G_T(\Theta)\) exists, we call \(V_0^*\) the \(\Theta\)-mean-variance price and \(\vartheta^*\) the \(\Theta\)-mean-variance strategy. We might not always make the dependency on \(\Theta\) explicit if it is clear from the context.
\begin{remark}\label{rem:if G_T closed then mean variance hedging is projection onto closed hilbert subspace}
    Observe that \eqref{eq:mean squared hedging} doesn't suffer from the same ill posedness issue as \eqref{eq:risk measure hedging} as \(V_0\) enters quadratically.
Furthermore, notice that if \(G_T(\Theta)\) is a closed subspace of \(L^2\), then so is \(\mathcal{A}(\Theta)\). This follows from the following general fact.
\end{remark}
Furthermore, we choose this moment to disclaim some standard abuses of notation that will be employed throughout this thesis. Technically 
\begin{equation*}
L^p(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mu)=\mathcal{L}^p(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mu)/\sim,
\end{equation*} 
where \(f\sim g\iff \int_{\Omega}|f-g|^p\diff \mu=0\) for \(f,g\in \mathcal{L}^p\).
Hence \(L^p\) contains equivalence classes of functions. When writing \(f\in L^p\), we shall abuse notation in implicitly choosing a representative \(f\) of the equivalence class \([f]\). Similarly, we say two stochastic processes \(X,Y\) are indistinguishable if 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{P}[\{X_t(\omega)=Y_t(\omega)\;\forall t\in \mathbb{R}_+\}]=1.
\end{equation*}
When writing \(X=Y\) for two stochastic processes, we shall always mean equality up to indistinguishability. 

\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:A closed if G closed}
    Let \(\mathcal{X}\) be a normed vector space and \(\mathcal{N}\) and \(\mathcal{U}\) be subspaces of \(\mathcal{X}\). Further assume that \(\mathcal{N}\) is closed in \(\mathcal{X}\) and \(\mathcal{U}\) is finite-dimensional. Then \(\mathcal{U}+\mathcal{N}\) is closed in \(\mathcal{X}\).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Recall that for \(\mathcal{N}\subseteq \mathcal{X}\) closed, the quotient space 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{X}/\mathcal{N}=\left\{[x]=x+\mathcal{N}:x\in \mathcal{X}\right\},
    \end{equation*}
   equipped with the norm
    \begin{equation*}
        \big\lVert [x]\big\rVert = \inf \left\{\lVert x-z\rVert_\mathcal{X}:z\in \mathcal{N}\right\}
    \end{equation*}
    is a normed vector space. Consider the quotient map
    \begin{equation*}
        \pi:x\to[x]=x+\mathcal{N}.
    \end{equation*}
 This defines a continuous, linear mapping between the normed spaces \(\mathcal{X}\) and \(\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{N}\).
    Then, as the image of a finite-dimensional space under a linear map, \(\pi(\mathcal{U})\) is a finite-dimensional subspace of \(\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{N}\); hence, in particular, closed. Then 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{U}+\mathcal{N}=\pi^{-1}\big(\pi(\mathcal{U})\big)
    \end{equation*}
    is closed in \(\mathcal{X}\) as the preimage of a closed set under a continuous map.  
\end{proof}
Applying the previous lemma to \(\mathcal{U}=\mathbb{R}\) and \(\mathcal{N}=G_T(\Theta)\) yields that \(\mathcal{A}(\Theta)\) is closed in \(L^2\) if \(G_T(\Theta)\) is closed. In this case, \eqref{eq:mean squared hedging} reduces to a \(L^2\) projection problem of the \(L^2\)-random variable \(H\) onto the closed subspace \(\mathcal{A}(\Theta)\). 
Indeed, then \eqref{eq:mean squared hedging} asks for the element in the closed subspace \(\mathcal{A}(\Theta)\) that minimizes the \(L^2\)-distance to \(H\). Recalling that \((L^2(\mathbb{R)},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^2})\) is a Hilbert space, the existence and uniqueness of the minimizer of \eqref{eq:mean squared hedging} follow from the Hilbert projection theorem (see \parencite[Thm.~12.3]{Rudin1991FunctionalAnalysis}). 

Due to the continuity of the following map
\begin{equation*}
\begin{cases}
    (\mathcal{A}(\Theta),\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^2})\to(\mathbb{R},|\cdot|)\\
    V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X)_T\mapsto\lVert V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H\rVert_{L^2},
\end{cases}
\end{equation*}
we have 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mean squared hedging over closure}
\inf_{V_T\in\mathcal{A}(\Theta)}\mathbb{E}[\left(V_T-H\right)^2]=\inf_{V_T\in \overline{\mathcal{A}(\Theta)}}\mathbb{E}[\left(V_T-H\right)^2],
\end{equation}
where the closure \(\overline{\mathcal{A}(\Theta)}=\overline{G_T(\Theta)+\mathbb{R}}\) is taken in \(L^2\). 
This follows from the following lemma.
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:minimization of closure}
     Let \(\mathcal{X}\) be a normed topological vector space, \(\mathcal{Y}\subseteq \mathcal{X}\) a subset, and \(d:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}\) continuous with respect to the topology induced by the norm on \(\mathcal{X}\). Then 
     \begin{equation*}
         \inf_{y\in \mathcal{Y}} d(y)=\inf_{y\in\overline{\mathcal{Y}}} d(y).
     \end{equation*}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
 Because \(\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{Y}}\), we have \(\inf_{y\in \mathcal{Y}} d(y)\geq\inf_{y\in\overline{\mathcal{Y}}} d(y)\). For the other direction, we need continuity of \(d:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}\). Let \(y\in\overline{\mathcal{Y}}\). There exists \((y_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq \mathcal{Y}\) such that \(\lim_{n\to\infty}y_n = y\) in \(\mathcal{X}\). By the continuity of \(d\), we thus have \(\lim_{n\to\infty}d(y_n) = d(y)\). Furthermore, by definition,
    \begin{equation*}
        d(y)=\lim_{n\to\infty}d(y_n)\geq\inf_{y\in \mathcal{Y}} d(y).
    \end{equation*}
    Taking the infimum over \(y\in\overline{\mathcal{Y}}\) on both sides of the above, we get,
    \begin{equation*}
        \inf_{y\in\overline{\mathcal{Y}}} d(y) \geq \inf_{y\in \mathcal{Y}} d(y).
    \end{equation*}
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
   Notice that the above proof also works if \(d\) is only upper-semi-continuous. Then we have \(d(y)\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}{d(y_n)}\geq\inf_{y\in \mathcal{Y}}d(y)\) and the conclusion is the same. Furthermore, we can generalize the above to a general topological space \(\mathcal{Y}\) by replacing sequences with nets. Indeed, all we have used is that for \(y\in\overline{\mathcal{Y}}\) there exists \((y_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq \mathcal{Y}\) such that \(\lim_{n\to\infty}y_n = y\) in \(\mathcal{X}\). For a topological space, such a net always exists.
\end{remark}

In particular, \(\overline{\mathcal{A}(\Theta)}\) is closed in \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\) by the definition of the topological closure. Thus, by the Hilbert projection theorem, \eqref{eq:mean squared hedging over closure} admits a unique solution as the \(L^2\)-projection on the closed subspace \(\overline{\mathcal{A}(\Theta)}\). 
Nevertheless, this only yields asymptotically optimal portfolios. Indeed, even though there exists a unique minimizer to the optimization problem over \(\overline{\mathcal{A}(\Theta)}\), we can only express it as an \(L^2\)-limit of portfolios \((V_0^n,\vartheta^n)\) with \(V_T^n\in\mathcal{A}(\Theta)\). The following lemma shows that, at least for pricing purposes, this approach is useful.
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:cl(R+G)=R+cl(G)}
   \(\overline{\mathbb{R}+G_T(\Theta)}=\mathbb{R}+\overline{G_T(\Theta)}\), where the closures are taken in \(L^2(\mathcal{F}_T,\mathbb{P})\).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    For the inclusion '\(\subseteq\)' notice that by Lemma \ref{lemma:A closed if G closed} we have that \(\mathbb{R}+\overline{G_T(\Theta)}\) is a closed set containing \(\mathbb{R}+G_T(\Theta)\). But \(\overline{\mathbb{R}+G_T(\Theta)}\) is, by definition, the smallest such set. For the converse inclusion, let \(a+g\in\mathbb{R}+\overline{G_T(\Theta)}\). There exists \((g_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq G_T(\Theta)\) such that \(\lim_{n\to\infty}g_n=g\) in \(L^2\). But then
    \begin{equation*}
        \lVert a+g-(a+g_n)\rVert_{L^2}=\lVert g-g_n\rVert_{L^2}\to 0.
    \end{equation*}
    As \(a+g_n\in\mathbb{R}+G_T(\Theta)\) this shows that \(a+g\in\overline{\mathbb{R}+G_T(\Theta)}\).
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
Clearly, we never used a particular property of \(G_T(\Theta)\) other than it being a subspace of \(L^2(\mathcal{F}_T,\mathbb{P})\). Hence, the statement in Lemma \ref{lemma:cl(R+G)=R+cl(G)} works for any subspace \(\mathcal{G}\) of \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\).
\end{remark}


If, however, we want to learn an explicit expression of the mean-variance strategy, we will need to study the conditions on \(\Theta\) and \(X\) for which \(G_T(\Theta)\) is closed in \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\). Indeed, if this is the case, by the same Hilbert projection argument, we get the existence of a unique \(V_T^*\in \mathbb{R}+G_T(\Theta)\), of the form \(V_T^*=V_0^*+\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta^*_t\diff X_t\). We touch upon this question in \cref{ch:deep mean-square hedging in continuous time} and Section \ref{sec:extension of deep mean squared hedging to semimartingales}. In this case, one might study explicit expressions of the mean-variance portfolio, as is done in \parencite[Thm.~4.6]{Schweizer2001GuidedTourQuadraticHedging} and \parencite[Thm.~3]{Schweizer1994ApproximatingRandomVariablesStochasticIntegrals}.

Hence, studying the existence of a mean-variance solution reduces to the search for conditions on \(\Theta\) and/or \(X\) such that \(G_T(\Theta)\) is closed in \(L^2\). Note that this is a particular property of the mean-variance hedging problem, as it is essentially a projection problem on a subspace of a Hilbert space. In particular, if \(\Omega\) and the time horizon \(\mathbb{T}_+\) are both finite, the following proposition shows that a solution always exists because the set \(\mathcal{A}(\Theta)\) is always closed in \(L^2\).
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:existence of finite deep mean variance solution}
    Let \(\Omega=\left\{\omega_1,\dots,\omega_N\right\}\) for \(N\in\mathbb{N}\) and \(\mathbb{T}_+=\left\{t_0,\dots,t_n\right\}\) with \(t_0=0\) and \(t_n=T<\infty\). Then, \(\mathbb{R}+G_T(\Theta)\) is a closed subspace of \(L^2(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R})\) for any subspace \(\Theta\) of predictable processes on \((\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})\) with filtration \(\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_k)_{k=0}^{n}\).
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Clearly, 
    \begin{equation*}
        \Theta\subseteq\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n} L^0(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{k-1}).
    \end{equation*}
    But \( L^0(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{k-1})\) is a subspace of the set of real-valued functions on \(\Omega\). Every such function \(f\) is trivially determined by the tuple \(\left\{f(\omega_1),\dots,f(\omega_N)\right\}\). Thus, \( L^0(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{k-1})\) can be identified with a subspace of \(\mathbb{R}^N\). Hence, \(\dim(\Theta)\leq N\cdot n\). The map 
    \begin{align*}
        I_T:&\Theta\to  L^0(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}),
        \\&\vartheta\mapsto\sum_{k=1}^n\vartheta_k(X_{k}-X_{k-1}).
    \end{align*}
    is clearly linear. Hence, as \(\Theta\) has finite dimension, \(Im(I_T)=G_T(\Theta)\) has finite dimension. Trivially \(G_T(\Theta)\subseteq L^2(\Omega,\mathcal{F})\), as 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:L2 norm in finite setup is double sum}
        \lVert (\vartheta\bullet X)_T\rVert_{L^2}^2=\sum_{i=1}^N\mathbb{P}[\{\omega_i\}]\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^n\vartheta_k(\omega_i)\big(X_k(\omega_i)-X_{k-1}(\omega_i)\big)\bigg)^2<\infty.
    \end{equation}
    Hence, \(G_T(\Theta)\) is a finite-dimensional subspace of \(L^2\) and hence closed (see \parencite[Cor.~1.22]{Buehler2018DeepHedging}).
\end{proof}
This shows the existence of the mean-variance hedging solution in the finite setup discussed in section \ref{sec:The deep-hedging framework of Bühler et al.}.

\chapter{Deep hedging in discrete time}\label{ch:Deep Hedging in Discrete Time}
After a general discussion of the hedging problem and, in particular, the mean-variance hedging problem, we discuss a particular approach for approximating the mean-variance solution. This approach, called \(\textit{deep-hedging}\) was introduced in \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging}. The latter treats the hedging problem in a finite sample space and finite time grid.



The section will be structured as follows. First, we heuristically discuss its main ideas before presenting the main theoretical results of \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging}. Although the latter particularly treats convex risk-measure hedging in detail, their approach is more general and extends to mean-variance hedging. In the last part of this section, we treat the mean-variance hedging problem with this approach, laying the foundations for the time-continuous generalization of \parencite{Arandjelovic2025} presented in Subsection \ref{sec:algorithmically generated random variables} and Chapter \ref{ch:deep mean-square hedging in continuous time}. 

\section{The deep-hedging framework}\label{sec:The deep-hedging framework of Bühler et al.}
The main idea in the deep-hedging approach is to approximate optimal hedging strategies by a suitable set of approximants. In our context, suitable refers to the property that the approximation should be universal and practical. By universal, we mean that the approximation system is rich enough to approximate any admissible strategy arbitrarily well, independent of the efficiency of the approximation. By practical, we refer to the efficiency of the approximation, given a finite amount of computational resources. Neural networks will form a well suited choice to construct the approximation system and are, in particular, practical, see \parencite{Bolcskei2018OptimalApproximationSparseDNN}. Nevertheless, we shall remain general in this section and refer to the universal properties required for the asymptotic result \parencite[Prop.~4.9.]{Buehler2018DeepHedging}, instead of introducing neural networks at this stage. The key idea in \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging} lies in a general approach to obtaining an approximation system for adapted stochastic processes on a finite time grid from well-known approximation systems of deterministic functions. 


The basic constraints in \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging} lie in their implementation friendly, finite sample space and finite time grid setup. Although these are theoretically very restrictive assumptions, this allows the authors of \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging} to formulate their approach for a wide class of trading strategies, allowing for 
non-constant trading costs.
In light of our discussion of the continuous time generalization and in accordance with Section \ref{sec:general idea of hedging}, we consider a frictionless market, that is, without trading costs. We show how the theoretical results from \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging} apply to mean-variance hedging in a finite sample space and finite time grid setup. This, in particular, adds to \parencite[Remark.~3.4]{Buehler2018DeepHedging} and constitutes the conceptual bridge to our discussion of deep mean-variance hedging in Chapter \ref{ch:deep mean-square hedging in continuous time}.
\subsection{Market model and trading strategies}
The current setup follows the general setup described in Section \ref{sec:general idea of hedging} under the following simplifying assumptions. We assume finitely many trading times \(0=t_0<t_1<\dots<t_n=T<\infty\), and a finite sample space \(\Omega=\{\omega_1,\dots,\omega_N\}\) such that \(\mathbb{P}[\{\omega_i\}]>0\) for all \(i=1,\dots,N\). We consider the filtration \(\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_k)_{k=0}^n\), with \(\mathcal{F}_T=\mathcal{F}_n\), generated by the \(\mathbb{R}^r\)-valued market process \(Y=(Y_k)_{k=0}^n\), which is 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{F}_k=\sigma(Y_0,\dots,Y_k).
\end{equation*} 
Notice in particular that the assumptions we set on the measure space imply that \(\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}=\left\{\emptyset\right\}\); hence, the measure space is complete, and so is any filtration on it. 
The Doob-Dynkin representation lemma (see \parencite[Lemma.~1.14]{Kallenberg2002FoundationsModernProbability}) states that any observable whose value can be determined with the information available up to time \(t_k\) can be represented as a deterministic function of the knowledge of \(Y\) up to time \(t_k\). In other words, \(\{Y_0,\dots,Y_k\}\) is the richest possible feature set.  


Recall our discussion in Remark \ref{rem:ill posed problem} showcasing that the optimization problem \eqref{eq:risk measure hedging} is ill defined in the sense that \(\pi(H)=-\infty\) for all \(H\in L^0(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{F}_T;\mathbb{R})\) if we don't impose additional assumptions on \(\mathcal{A}(\Theta)\) and \(\rho\). Thus, in order to best showcase the approach of \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging}, we consider the closely related hedging problem \eqref{eq:general hedging for fixed initial capital}, which doesn't suffer from this degenerate behavior:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:risk measure hedging from buehler}
    \pi(H)=\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\rho\big((\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H\big),
\end{equation}
for \(H\in L^0(\mathcal{F}_T;\mathbb{R})\). Furthermore, Lemma \ref{lemma:Finiteness of inf for finite sample space} still applies to \eqref{eq:risk measure hedging from buehler} and ensures finiteness if \(\pi(0)>-\infty\). Equation \eqref{eq:risk measure hedging from buehler} is then the hedging problem that is explicitly addressed in \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging}.


\section{Approximation of trading strategies}\label{sec:Approximation of trading strategies}
Adopting the point of view presented in \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging}, we are not interested in sufficient conditions for the infimum in \eqref{eq:risk measure hedging from buehler} to be attained, but rather in the consistent approximation of \(\pi(H)\) for any \(H\in L^0(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{F}_T;\mathbb{R})\) by approximating strategies in \(\Theta\). The underlying idea is to leverage the \(\mathbb{F}\)-predictability of admissible strategies \(\vartheta=(\vartheta_k)_{k=1}^n\) by approximating the deterministic function of the Doob-Dynkin representation of its components with a suitable set of functions. In mathematical terms, consider \(\vartheta_k\), that is the strategy \(\vartheta\) at time \(t_k\). By assumption, it is predictable and thus, in particular, adapted; hence \(\mathcal{F}_{k-1}\)-measurable. We can write down its Doob-Dynkin representation
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Doob-Dynkin representation}
   \vartheta_k=f_k(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1}),
\end{equation}
where \(f_k:\mathbb{R}^{r\times k}\to\mathbb{R}^d\) is Borel-measurable. We refer to \eqref{eq:Doob-Dynkin representation} as the Doob-Dynkin representation of \(\vartheta_k\) and we call \(f_k\) the Doob-Dynkin function of \(\vartheta_k\). For simplicity, we assume \(\vartheta_k\) takes values in \(\mathbb{R}\). The multidimensional case follows analogously. The idea is to find a set of functions \(\Psi\) that can approximate all such \(f_k\) arbitrarily well.



In the current setup, \(\Omega\) is assumed to be finite. Hence, we have that for any \(p\geq 1\)
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\big[|f_k(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1}|^p\big]=\sum_{i=1}^N \mathbb{P}[\{\omega_i\}]\big|f_k\big(Y_0(\omega_i),\dots,Y_{k-1}(\omega_i)\big)\big|^p<\infty,
\end{equation*}

is finite, as a finite sum of finite terms.
In particular, due to
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[f_k(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{L}(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})}[f_k],
\end{equation*}
where \(\mathcal{L}(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\) denotes the law of \((Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\) on \((\mathbb{R}^{r\times k},\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{r\times k}))\), that is, the push-forward measure \(\mathbb{P}_*(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\coloneqq\mathbb{P}\circ(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})^{-1}\). We have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:f is in Lp for finite setting}
    f_k\in L^p\big(\mathbb{R}^{r\times k},\mathcal{L}(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\big),\quad p\geq 1.
\end{equation}
For the purpose of the current chapter, it is sufficient to approximate elements of \(L^1(\mathbb{R}^{l},\mu)\) for any \(l\in\mathbb{N}\) and finite measures \(\mu\).  
In more precise terms, we consider a subspace of \(L^1(\mathbb{R}^l,\mu)\), denoted as \(\Psi(\mathbb{R}^l,\mu)\)  such that the following topological statement holds. 
\begin{equation*}
\overline{\Psi(\mathbb{R}^l,\mu)}^{L^1}=L^1(\mathbb{R}^{l},\mu)
\end{equation*}
for any \(l\in\mathbb{N}\) and finite measure \(\mu\). We assume the existence of such a \(\Psi(\mathbb{R}^l,\mu)\) for any \(l\in\mathbb{N}\) and any finite measure \(\mu\) on \(\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^l)\). Such a \(\Psi(\mathbb{R}^l,\mu)\) is assumed to exist for every pair \(l\) and every \(\mu\). We simply denote it by \(\Psi\), and the respective parameters \(l\) and \(\mu\) are deduced from context.  We give a concrete example for \(\Psi\) in Chapter \ref{ch:Neural Networks and Universal Approximation}.
As \(L^1(\mathbb{R}^l,\mu)\) equipped with the norm \(\lVert f\lVert_{L^1}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^l}|f(x)| \mu(\diff x)\) is a complete normed space, we can equivalently reformulate the density statement as follows. 
\begin{align*}
\forall f\in L^1(\mu)\;\forall \epsilon>0\;\exists \psi\in\Psi:\;\lVert f-\psi\rVert_{L^1(\mu)}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{l}}|f(x)-\psi(x)| \mu(\diff x)<\epsilon.
\end{align*}
We will only motivate and introduce a concrete incarnation of such a \(\Psi\) at the end of this section and discuss it in \cref{ch:Neural Networks and Universal Approximation}. For now, and for the rest of this section, we shall remain general.



We now return to the original task of approximating \(\pi(H)\) of \eqref{eq:risk measure hedging from buehler} by approximating the strategies in \(\Theta\) using \(\Psi\). Following \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging}  we introduce a stratification on \(\Psi\) as follows. Denote by \((\Psi_M)_{M\in\mathbb{N}}\) a sequence of subsets of \(\Psi\) such that 
\begin{itemize}
    \item \(\Psi_M\subseteq\Psi_{M+1}\quad\forall M\in\mathbb{N}\),
    \item \(\bigcup_{M\in\mathbb{N}}\Psi_M=\Psi\),
    \item for all \(M\in\mathbb{N}\) there exists a parameter space \(\Sigma_M\subseteq\mathbb{R}^q\) for some \(q\in\mathbb{N}\) depending on \(M\).
\end{itemize}
The last property leads us to interpret \(\Psi_M\) as a subfamily of \(\Psi\) parameterized by \(\Sigma_M\). For instance, we might think of \(\Psi\) as polynomials of arbitrary degree and \(\Psi_M\) as polynomials of degree less than or equal to \(M\in\mathbb{N}\) or as the number of non-zero weights in a deep neural network (see \parencite[Def.~4.1.]{Buehler2018DeepHedging} and \parencite[rem.4.3.]{Buehler2018DeepHedging}).


As we want to approximate strategies using \(\Psi\), it is natural to define the following subset of \(\Theta\):
\begin{equation}\label{eq:restricted strategies}
    \Theta_M\coloneqq\Big\{\vartheta=(\vartheta_k)_{k=1}^n\in\Theta:\vartheta_k=F_k(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1},\vartheta_{k-1}),\quad F_k\in\Psi_M\Big\}.
\end{equation}
Because \(\Psi_M\subseteq\Psi_{M+1}\) for all \(M\geq1\) we have \(\Theta_M\subseteq\Theta_{M+1}\).
Due to the Doob-Dynkin representation theorem, \(\vartheta_{k-1}\) is itself already some measurable function of \((Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\); thus, we can omit the \(\vartheta_{k-1}\) dependency in the definition \eqref{eq:restricted strategies}. Thus, \(\Theta_M\) is the set of strategies whose components (in time) have a Doob-Dynkin function in \(\Psi_M\). 
We further denote
\begin{equation*}
    \Theta_{\infty}\coloneqq \bigcup_{M\in\mathbb{N}}\Theta_M.
\end{equation*}
We can state the restricted optimization problem. 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:restricted risk measure hedging from buehler}
\pi^M(H)=\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta_M}\rho\big((\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H\big)
\end{equation}
We call \(\pi^M(H)\) the restricted hedging error. 
The theorem we will state and prove shows that the restricted hedging errors from \eqref{eq:restricted risk measure hedging from buehler} converge to the  hedging error \(\pi(H)\) of \eqref{eq:risk measure hedging from buehler} for \(M\to\infty\). 

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:approximation by restricted hedging errors}
Assume \(\pi(0)>-\infty\).
 Let \(\Theta_M\subseteq \Theta\) be as in \eqref{eq:restricted strategies} and \(\pi^M(H)\) as in \eqref{eq:restricted risk measure hedging from buehler}. Then, for any \(H\in L^0(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_T,\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R})\),
 \begin{equation*}
     \lim_{M\to\infty}\pi^M(H)=\pi(H).
 \end{equation*}
\end{theorem}
Before giving the proof, we argue how we expect the proof to proceed. Recall that the strategy \(\vartheta=(\vartheta_k)_{k=1}^n\) is a predictable process. Hence, \(\vartheta_k\) is \(\mathcal{F}_{k-1}\)-measurable with \newline \(\mathcal{F}_{k-1}=\sigma(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\). By Doob-Dynkin, for every \(k=1,\dots n\), there exists a \newline\(f_k:\mathbb{R}^{r\times k}\to\mathbb{R}\)-measurable function such that
\begin{equation*}
    \vartheta_k=f_k(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1}).
\end{equation*}
As we have argued previously in \eqref{eq:f is in Lp for finite setting} we have \(f_k\in L^1(\mathbb{R}^{r\times k},\mathcal{L}(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1}))\). We can thus approximate \(f_k\) by elements in \(\Psi\) arbitrarily well. Because \(\bigcup_{M\in\mathbb{N}}\Psi_M=\Psi\), we can do so with elements of \(\Psi_M\) as well for \(M\in\mathbb{N}\) large enough. The hope is that we can also approximate the hedging error arbitrarily well in the sense that we can get \(\epsilon\)-close to \(\pi(H)\) by \(\pi^M(H)\) for \(M\) large enough. To show that this works requires care and will be done in the following proof.
\begin{proof}
 First, notice that due to the monotonicity \(\Psi_M\subseteq\Psi_{M+1}\subseteq\Psi\) we have \(\Theta_M\subseteq\Theta_{M+1}\subseteq\Theta\) hence 
 \begin{equation*}
     \pi^M(H)\geq\pi^{M+1}(H)\geq\pi(H),\;\forall M\in\mathbb{N}.
 \end{equation*}
 Hence, it is enough to show that \(\pi^M(H)\) converges to \(\pi(H)\) from above. This means that it is enough to show that for any \(\epsilon>0\), there exists \(M\in\mathbb{N}\) such that 
 \begin{equation*}
     \pi^M(H)\leq \pi(H)+\epsilon.
 \end{equation*}
 This makes sense as we have \(\pi(H)>-\infty\) by Lemma \ref{lemma:Finiteness of inf for finite sample space} because we assumed \(\pi(0)>-\infty\).
 Furthermore, by the definition of the infimum over a real set, for every \(\frac{\epsilon}{2}>0\), there exists \(\vartheta\in\Theta\) such that
 \begin{equation*}
     \rho((\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H)\leq\pi(H)+\frac{\epsilon}{2}.
 \end{equation*}
 Consider such a process \(\vartheta=(\vartheta_k)_{k=1}^n\). By assumption, \(\vartheta_k\) is \(\mathcal{F}_{k-1}\)-measurable. So, by the Doob-Dynkin lemma, there exists a measurable \(f_k:\mathbb{R}^{r\times k}\to\mathbb{R}^d\) such that 
 \begin{equation*}
     \vartheta_k=f_k(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1}),
 \end{equation*}
 for all \(k=0,\dots,n\). As argued in \eqref{eq:f is in Lp for finite setting} we have \(f_k\in L^1(\mathbb{R}^{r\times k},\mathcal{L}(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1}))\). Let's denote \(\mu\coloneqq\mathcal{L}(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\). By the density of \(\Psi\) in \(L^1\) for any \(l\in\mathbb{N}\) and finite measure \(\mu\), there exists a sequence of functions \((F_k^m)_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq \Psi\) converging to \(f_k\) in \(L^1(\mathbb{R}^{r\times k},\mu)\). In particular, we have for 
 \begin{equation*}
     \vartheta_{k}^m\coloneqq F_{k}^m(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})
 \end{equation*}
 that \(\vartheta_{k}^m\to f_k(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\) in \(L^1(\Omega,\mathbb{P})\) as \(m\to\infty\). Passing to a subsequence and by a diagonal argument, we get \(\mathbb{P}\)-almost sure convergence for all \(k\) simultaneously. Morally speaking, we want to pass the limit \(m\to\infty\) inside \(\rho\). Even if \(\rho\) has sufficient regularity, \(\mathbb{P}\)-a.s. convergence is not enough to conclude. However, recall that we assumed in the current setting that \(\mathbb{P}[\{\omega\}]>0\) for all \(\omega\in\Omega\); hence, \(\mathbb{P}\)-almost sure convergence is equivalent to convergence everywhere. Thus
 \begin{equation*}
     \lim_{m\to\infty}\vartheta_k^m(\omega)=\vartheta_k(\omega)\quad\forall\omega\in\Omega,k=0,\dots,n.
 \end{equation*}
 In particular, 
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:everywhere convergence of stochastic integral}
(\vartheta^m\bullet X)_T(\omega)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\vartheta^m_k(\omega)\big(X_{k}(\omega)-X_{k-1}(\omega)\big)\to(\vartheta\bullet X)_T(\omega)\text{ as }m\to\infty.
\end{equation}
 for all \(\omega\in\Omega\).
Note that the continuity of the stochastic integral is trivial in this finite setting. When trying to naively generalize this approach to a time-continuous one with general sample space \(\Omega\), this step is highly non-trivial.


 Furthermore, notice that \( L^0(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_T,\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R})\simeq\mathbb{R}^{N}\). Indeed, any random variable \(Z\) on \(\Omega=\left\{\omega_i\right\}_{i=1}^N\) can be identified bijectively with the tuple \((Z(\omega_1),\dots,Z(\omega_N))\in\mathbb{R}^N\). Hence, we can identify the convex risk-measure \(\rho\) with a convex map \(\tilde{\rho}:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}\) such that \(\rho(Z)=\widetilde{\rho}\big(Z(\omega_1),\dots,Z(\omega_N)\big)\). As a convex map on \(\mathbb{R}^N\), the map \(\widetilde{\rho}\) is continuous (see \parencite[Thm.~10.1]{Rockafellar1970ConvexAnalysis}). Now \eqref{eq:everywhere convergence of stochastic integral} translates under the previous identification to 
 \begin{equation*}
     \lim_{m\to\infty}
\big((\vartheta^m\bullet X)_T(\omega_1),\dots, (\vartheta^m\bullet X)_T(\omega_N)\big)=\big((\vartheta\bullet X)_T(\omega_1),\dots, (\vartheta\bullet X)_T(\omega_N)\big)
\end{equation*}
in \(\mathbb{R}^N\).
 Thus, we have
  \begin{align*}
     &\lim_{m\to\infty}\widetilde{\rho}\Big(\big((\vartheta^m\bullet X)_T(\omega_1),\dots, (\vartheta^m\bullet X)_T(\omega_N)\big)-(H(\omega_1),\dots,H(\omega_N)\big)\Big)
     \\&=\widetilde{\rho}\Big(\big((\vartheta\bullet X)_T(\omega_1),\dots, (\vartheta\bullet X)_T(\omega_N)\big)-(H(\omega_1),\dots,H(\omega_N)\big)\Big)
\end{align*}
 or expressed in terms of the original map \(\rho\)
 \begin{equation*}
     \lim_{m\to\infty}\rho\big((\vartheta^m\bullet X)_T-H\big)=\rho\big((\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H\big).
\end{equation*}
In particular, there exists \(n\in\mathbb{N}\) large enough such that 
\begin{equation*}
    \rho\big((\vartheta^n\bullet X)_T-H\big)\leq\rho\big((\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H\big)+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\leq\pi(H)+\epsilon.
\end{equation*}
Since for every \(\vartheta^n\) there exists \(M\in\mathbb{N}\) large enough for which \(\vartheta^n\in\Theta_M\), there exists \(M\in\mathbb{N}\) such that 
\begin{equation*}
    \pi^M(H)\leq\pi(H)+\epsilon,
\end{equation*}
which ends the proof.
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
 Observe that we showed in particular that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \pi^{\infty}(H)=\pi(H),
    \end{equation*}
    where 
    \begin{equation*}
    \pi^{\infty}(H)=\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta_{\infty}}\rho\big((\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H\big).
    \end{equation*}
 Indeed, we found a sequence \((\vartheta_k^m)_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\Theta_{\infty}\) such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \lim_{m\to\infty}\rho\big((\vartheta^m\bullet X)_T-H\big)=\pi(H),
    \end{equation*}
    which implies 
    \begin{equation*}
        \inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta_{\infty}}\rho\big((\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H\big)=\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\rho\big((\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H\big).
    \end{equation*}
    Observe that this does not directly follow from Lemma \ref{lemma:minimization of closure} and the continuity of the map \(\rho:\mathbb{R}^N\to\mathbb{R}\), because \(\Psi\) is dense in \(L^1\) with respect to the \(L^1\)-topology, which is not the topology for which \(\rho\) is continuous. The argument used in the proof to establish convergence everywhere is crucial. 
\end{remark}
\section{From convex risk measures to quadratic loss}
In this subsection, we show that the ideas developed in the proof of Theorem \ref{thm:approximation by restricted hedging errors} can also be applied to the problem \eqref{eq:mean squared hedging} in the setup of \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging}. Note that this is not a mere consequence of Theorem \ref{thm:approximation by restricted hedging errors}, as the square of the \(L^2\)-norm is not a convex risk measure; for instance, it doesn't satisfy cash-invariance and monotonicity property. The observation that the ideas are applicable to the mean-variance hedging problem is made in \parencite[Rem~.3.4.]{Buehler2018DeepHedging} for the case where \(V_0\) is fixed exogenously. We show it here explicitly and extend the remark by demonstrating how to treat the joint minimization over \((V_0,\vartheta)\in\mathbb{R}\times \Theta\). 
For fixed \(V_0\) we show a statement analogous  to  Theorem \ref{thm:approximation by restricted hedging errors}. 
We argue that this is a stronger statement than the mean-variance hedging problem stated in Proposition \ref{prop:finite deep mean variance hedging}. 




We consider the same setup as in all of this chapter; that is a finite sample space \(\Omega\) and finite trading times \((t_i)_{i=0}^n\), and \(t_n=T<\infty\). We also take \(\vartheta_k\) real-valued for all \(k=0,\dots,n\) and  neglect trading costs: \(C_t=0,\; \forall t\geq 0\). Consider the following mean-variance hedging problem for fixed \(V_0\) and \(H\in L^0(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_T,\mathbb{P})\)
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hedging error}
    \Delta(H) = \inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X\big)_T-H)^2\Big]
\end{equation}
and in analogy to \parencite[Thm.~4.9.]{Buehler2018DeepHedging}, we introduce
\begin{equation}\label{eq:restricted mean variance hedging error}
    \Delta^M(H) = \inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta_M}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H\big)^2\Big]
\end{equation}
for \(\Theta_M\) as in \eqref{eq:restricted strategies} and \(V_0\in\mathbb{R}\) fixed as in  \eqref{eq:risk measure heding with fixed initial capital}. We want to show the following deep mean hedging theorem.
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:finite deep mean variance hedging}
    \begin{equation*}
        \inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H\big)^2\Big]=\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta_{\infty}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H\big)^2\Big].
    \end{equation*}
\end{proposition}
More precisely, we show the stronger statement
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:strong discrete deep mean variance hedging}
Let \(\Theta_M\) be as in \eqref{eq:restricted strategies}, \(\Delta^M(H)\) as in \eqref{eq:restricted mean variance hedging error}, and \(\Delta(H)\) as in \eqref{eq:hedging error} respectively. Then, for any \(H\in L^0(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_T,\mathbb{P})\)
    \begin{equation*}
        \lim_{M\to\infty}\Delta^M(H)=\Delta(H).
    \end{equation*}
\end{theorem}
First, the following lemma shows that Theorem \ref{thm:strong discrete deep mean variance hedging} implies Proposition \ref{prop:finite deep mean variance hedging}.
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:strong form implies weak form}
    Assume \(\lim_{M\to\infty}\Delta^M(H)=\Delta(H)\).  Then in particular, \(\Delta^{\infty}(H)=\Delta(H)\).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let
\[
J(\vartheta):=\mathbb{E}\Big[(V_0+(\vartheta\cdot X)_T-H)^2\Big].
\]
for fixed \(H\).
Since \(\Theta_M\subseteq \Theta_\infty\subseteq \Theta\), taking infima yields
\[
\Delta(H)=\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta}J(\vartheta)
\leq
\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta_\infty}J(\vartheta)
=
\Delta^\infty(H)
\leq
\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta_M}J(\vartheta)
=
\Delta_M(H)
\]
for every \(M\in\mathbb N\). Letting \(M\to\infty\) and using the assumption
\[
\lim_{M\to\infty}\Delta^M(H)=\Delta(H)
\]
we obtain
\[
\Delta(H)\leq \Delta^\infty(H)\leq \Delta(H).
\]
\end{proof}
For the proof of Theorem \ref{thm:strong discrete deep mean variance hedging} we proceed very similarly to the strategy used in the proof of Theorem \ref{thm:approximation by restricted hedging errors}.
\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem \ref{thm:strong discrete deep mean variance hedging}]
Replacing \(\pi\) by \(\Delta\) and \(\rho(\cdot)\) by \(\mathbb{E}[(\cdot)^2]\), the proof is the same as for Theorem \ref{thm:approximation by restricted hedging errors} provided that for an everywhere converging sequences \(\vartheta_k^m\to\vartheta_k\) we have
