\begin{equation}\label{eq:continuity of L2 norm in finite setup}
    \lim_{m\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(V_0+(\vartheta^m\bullet X)_T-H\big)^2\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H\big)^2\Big].
\end{equation}
As previously observed in \eqref{eq:L2 norm in finite setup is double sum} we can rewrite 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{E}\Big[\big(V_0+(\vartheta^m\bullet X)_T-H\big)^2\Big]=\sum_{i=1}^N\mathbb{P}[\{\omega_i\}]\Big( V_0+\sum_{k=1}^n\vartheta_k^m(\omega_i)\big(X_k(\omega_i)-X_{k-1}(\omega_i)\big)-H(\omega_i)\Big)^2.
\end{equation*}
Hence \eqref{eq:continuity of L2 norm in finite setup} is obvious by the continuity of addition and the square function.
\end{proof}
This shows that, as alluded to in \parencite[Remark~3.4]{Buehler2018DeepHedging}, the methods developed for the convex risk measure hedging problem can be used very similarly for mean-variance hedging.

One can provide a related, functional analytic proof of Proposition \ref{prop:finite deep mean variance hedging}. It uses the same core approach in approximating the Doob-Dynkin functions but exploits the observation that \(\Theta\) can be understood as the closure of \(\Theta_{\infty}\) with respect to the appropriate topology. Then Proposition \ref{prop:finite deep mean variance hedging} follows directly from Lemma \ref{lemma:minimization of closure} and the fact that the map \(J:\vartheta\mapsto\lVert V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H\rVert_{L^2}\) is continuous with respect to the topology in which \(\overline{\Theta_{\infty}}=\Theta\). For details, see Appendix \ref{Appendix:Direct proof of Finite Deep mean variance Hedging}.



We finish the treatment of the discussion of the finite sample space and finite time grid mean-variance hedging problem by extending Theorem \ref{thm:strong discrete deep mean variance hedging} to the joint minimization \eqref{eq:mean squared hedging}, minimizing over the initial capital as well. For this, we introduce the following notation.
\begin{align*}  
&J(V_0,\vartheta)= \mathbb{E}\Big[\big(V_0+(\vartheta\cdot X)_T-H\big)^2\Big]\\
&\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}(H)=\inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R}}\inf_{\vartheta\in \Theta}J(V_0,\vartheta)
\\
&\Delta^M_{\mathbb{R}}(H)=\inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R}}\inf_{\vartheta\in \Theta_M}J(V_0,\vartheta)
\end{align*}
Furthermore, we write for fixed \(V_0\in\mathbb{R}\)
\begin{align*}
    &\Delta_{V_0}(H)=\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta}J(V_0,\vartheta)\\
    &\Delta^M_{V_0}(H)=\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta_M}J(V_0,\vartheta)
\end{align*}
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:finite deep mean variance hedging with variable V0}
    Let \(\Theta,\Theta_{\infty}\) and \(\Theta_M\) be as in Theorem \ref{thm:strong discrete deep mean variance hedging}. Then
    \begin{equation*}
        \lim_{M\to\infty}\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}^M(H)=\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}(H).
    \end{equation*}
 In particular, we have 
    \begin{equation*}
    \inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R}}\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(H-V_0-(\vartheta\bullet X)_T\big)^2\Big]=\inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R}}\inf_{\vartheta\in\Theta_{\infty}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(H-V_0-(\vartheta\bullet X)_T\big)^2\Big].
    \end{equation*}
    \begin{proof}
        The proof uses Theorem \ref{thm:strong discrete deep mean variance hedging}. First, observe that due to 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathbb{R}\times\Theta_M\subseteq \mathbb{R}\times\Theta_{M+1},\;\forall M\in\mathbb{N}
        \end{equation*}
        we have
        \begin{equation*}
            \Delta_{\mathbb{R}}^M\geq \Delta_{\mathbb{R}}^{M+1},\;\forall M\in\mathbb{N}.
        \end{equation*}
        Hence, it suffices to show that for every \(\epsilon>0\) there exists \(M\in\mathbb{N}\) such that 
        \begin{equation*}
           \Delta_{\mathbb{R}}^M(H)< \Delta_{\mathbb{R}}(H)+\epsilon.
        \end{equation*}
        By definition of the infimum over a real subset, for every \(\epsilon>0\) there exists \(V_0^{\epsilon/2}\) such that 
        \begin{equation*}
        \Delta_{V_0^{\epsilon/2}}(H)<\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}(H)+\frac{\epsilon}{2}.
        \end{equation*}
        Fix this \(V_0^{\epsilon/2}\). By Theorem \ref{thm:strong discrete deep mean variance hedging} we have
        \begin{equation*}
            \lim_{M\to\infty}\Delta_{V_0^{\epsilon/2}}^M(H)=\Delta_{V_0^{\epsilon/2}}(H).
        \end{equation*}
        As we observed in Theorem \ref{thm:strong discrete deep mean variance hedging}, the convergence is from above, so equivalently we have that for every \(\epsilon>0\) there exists \(M_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\in\mathbb{N}\) big enough such that
        \begin{equation*}
           \Delta^{M_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}}_{V_0^{\epsilon/2}}(H)< \Delta_{V_0^{\epsilon/2}}(H)+\frac{\epsilon}{2}.
        \end{equation*}
Thus, we have
        \begin{align*}
            \Delta^{M_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}}_{\mathbb{R}}\leq\Delta^{M_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}}_{V_0^{\epsilon/2}}(H)<\Delta_{V_0^{\epsilon/2}}(H)+\frac{\epsilon}{2}<\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}(H)+\epsilon,
        \end{align*}
which shows the first claim. The second part follows exactly as in Lemma \ref{lemma:strong form implies weak form} from Proposition \ref{prop:finite deep mean variance hedging with variable V0} and the trivial observation
        \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{R}\times\Theta_M\subseteq \mathbb{R}\times\Theta_{\infty},\;\forall M\geq 1.
        \end{equation*}
        \end{proof}
\end{proposition}
\begin{remark}
 Regarding the existence of the mean-variance solution, observe that in the finite sample space and finite time grid setting of this chapter, the mean-variance setting of Proposition \ref{prop:finite deep mean variance hedging} and Proposition \ref{prop:finite deep mean variance hedging with variable V0} attains the infimum in \(\Theta\) and, respectively, in \(\mathbb{R}\times\Theta\) for any subspace of predictable strategies \(\Theta\). This follows from the observation made in Proposition \ref{prop:existence of finite deep mean variance solution}.
\end{remark}

\chapter{Neural networks and universal approximation}\label{ch:Neural Networks and Universal Approximation}
\section{Feedforward neural networks}
Regarding our discussion in Section \ref{sec:The deep-hedging framework of Bühler et al.} all we required was an approximation system \(\Psi\), which is rich enough to approximate integrable functions, that is
\begin{equation*}
    \overline{\Psi}^{L^1}=L^1(\mathbb{P}).
\end{equation*}
The reader familiar with the work of \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging} or by simple guesswork regarding the title of this thesis will have inferred that we shall take \(\Psi\) to be the set of neural networks. Indeed, the theorems we will discuss in this section show that shallow, feed-forward neural networks fulfill the assumptions for \(\Psi\). It still remains to argue why one would choose neural networks in particular, as it is by far not the only candidate for \(\Psi\) (see \parencite[Thm.~2.4]{SteinShakarchi2005RealAnalysis}). An empirical argument can be made by simply referring to the overwhelming success of neural network based architecture in a multitude of applications. 
For a more theoretically grounded argument we refer to the arguments provided by \parencite{Bolcskei2018OptimalApproximationSparseDNN}.


The main result  \parencite[Thm.~5.13]{Bolcskei2018OptimalApproximationSparseDNN} implies, in particular, that any function class \(\mathcal{C}\) that can be optimally represented by an affine system generated by a function that, in turn, can be approximated by a neural network of finite depth and finite width is also optimally representable by neural networks. In \parencite{Bolcskei2018OptimalApproximationSparseDNN} neural networks are used in a more general sense, allowing for more layers than the single layer we will use. Nevertheless, the shallow feedforward neural networks we will consider are a subclass of the latter. The optimal representability by neural networks roughly states that the largest possible decay rate with respect to the number of non-zero weights (connectivity) of the worst case approximation error of functions in \(\mathcal{C}\) by neural networks with polynomially bounded weights is equal to the optimal decay rate of the error that the minimal length encoder/decoder pair makes when approximating functions in \(\mathcal{C}\). This is quite a mouthful and very compact. 
In short, they show that neural networks are the most efficient approximation system within a large class of possible approximation systems, namely the affine systems.
For details and explanations of the involved notions, we refer to \parencite{Bolcskei2018OptimalApproximationSparseDNN}. 


This result shows an astonishing universality of neural networks among all possible affine approximation systems  as the optimal approximation system regarding encoding complexity and justifies their central role in this chapter and hereinafter. 

\section{Universal approximation}
In this section, we formally introduce shallow, feedforward neural networks and discuss their approximation theoretical properties topologically. In other words, we show that the set of shallow feed-forward neural networks, for an appropriate activation function, forms a dense subset of continuous functions on a compact domain and of \(L^p\) spaces for any \(p\geq 1\) under a finite measure. We present the proofs given in \parencite{Hornik1991} in detail, as their arguments generalize well to more abstract spaces, as we will see at the end of this section.


\subsection{Density of shallow feed--forward neural networks}
The main interest of this section is to approximate functions of \(k\) real variables by finite linear combinations of the form 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proto NN}
    \sum_{j=1}^Nc_j\psi(A_j(\cdot)),
\end{equation}
where \(A_j(x)=w_j^{tr}x+b_j\) is an affine linear map from \(\mathbb{R}^k\) to \(\mathbb{R}\) with \(\psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\) \textit{sigmoidal} that is
\begin{align*}
\psi(t)\to
    \begin{cases}
    1\quad\text{ as } t\to +\infty\\
    0\quad\text{ as } t\to -\infty.
    \end{cases}
\end{align*}
Notice that \(\psi\) is necessarily non-linear. 
The class of functions of the form \eqref{eq:proto NN} is of central importance to us in this section and the remainder of this thesis.
\begin{definition}\label{def:shallow feedforward neural networks}
    Let \(\psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\) be a sigmoidal map and \(k\in\mathbb{N}\). Then we define
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)\coloneqq\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\Big\{\psi(A(\cdot)):A:\mathbb{R}^k\to\mathbb{R}\text{ affine linear}\Big\}.
    \end{equation*}
    We call elements of \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)\) shallow feedforward neural networks with activation function \(\psi\).
\end{definition}
In Definition \ref{def:shallow feedforward neural networks}, the word "shallow" refers to the fact that \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)\) involves neural networks with one hidden layer. 
For a more general notion of neural networks, including multiple layers, see \parencite[Def.~4.1]{Buehler2018DeepHedging}.
Moreover, every affine linear map \(A:\mathbb{R}^k\to\mathbb{R}\) can be written in the form 
\begin{equation*}
    A(x)=w^{tr}x+b, w\in\mathbb{R}^k,b\in\mathbb{R}.
\end{equation*}
Thus, for \(G\in \mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)\), we can write
\begin{equation*}
    G(x)=\sum_{j=1}^Nc_j\psi(w_j^{tr}x+b_j).
\end{equation*}
We call \(\left\{w_{ij}\right\}_{(i,j)\in\{1,\dots,k\}\times\{1,\dots,N\}}\) the weights of \(G\) and \(\left\{b_{j}\right\}_{j\in \{1,\dots,N\}}\) the biases. Together, they form the parameters of the neural network \(G\)



This is the simplest class of neural networks one can define. As it is the only class of neural networks of interest in this thesis, we refer to them plainly as \textit{neural networks}. 
\begin{remark}\label{rem:NN don't form algebra}
    Notice that \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)\) is a vector space by definition, but it is not closed under pointwise multiplication. In particular, \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)\) doesn't form an algebra of functions.
\end{remark}
The first question we address is whether continuous functions on compact domains \(K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^k\) can be approximated by elements of 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(K)\coloneqq \Big\{G|_K:G\in\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)\Big\},
\end{equation*}
with respect to the \(\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}=\sup_{x\in K}|f(x)|\). We need to check whether
\begin{equation*}
    \forall{f}\in C(K)\quad\forall\epsilon>0\quad\exists G\in\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(K):\lVert f-G\rVert_{\infty}<\epsilon,
\end{equation*}
or in topological notation
\begin{equation}\label{eq:density of NN in C(K)}
    \overline{\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(K)}^{\lVert\cdot\rVert_\infty}=C(K;\mathbb{R}).
\end{equation}
Note that this says nothing about the efficiency of the approximation for a given amount of computational resources. Concretely, it says nothing about the number of weights needed to approximate a given \(f\in C(K)\) to a given precision \(\epsilon\) in the supremum norm. 



We will understand in the following that the main insight leading to the stated density result is that \eqref{eq:density of NN in C(K)} holds if \(\psi\) is continuous and is able to distinguish signed Borel-measures on \(K\). More precisely, we want \(\psi\) to be such that the linear map
\begin{equation*}
    \Phi:\mu\mapsto\left( A\mapsto\int_{K}\psi(A(x))\mu(\diff x)\right)
\end{equation*}
is injective, 
where \(\mu\) is a signed Borel-measure and \(A\mapsto\int_{K}\psi(A(x))\mu(\diff x)\) defines a non-linear function on the space of affine linear maps. In other words, we want \(\psi\) to be such that 
\begin{equation*}
    \Phi(\mu)=0\implies\mu =0. 
\end{equation*} 
\begin{definition}\label{def:discriminatory maps over reals}
    We call a map \(\psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\) discriminatory if it is Borel-measurable, bounded, and if
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:discriminatory maps over reals}
        \int_{\mathbb{R}^k}\psi(w^{tr}x+b)\mu(\diff x)=0
    \end{equation}
    for all \(w\in\mathbb{R}^k,b\in\mathbb{R}\) and \(\mu\) finite, signed measures on \((\mathbb{R}^k,\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^k))\), it implies that \(\mu\) is the zero measure on \(\mathbb{R}^k\).
\end{definition}
Notice that it is important that the discriminatory property refers to signed-measures. Indeed, if we only required the discriminatory property to hold for positive measures, the constant functions would be trivially discriminatory. The notion of a discriminatory function is constructed to yield density results, for instance in the space of continuous functions on compact sets or the \(L^p\) spaces for \(p\in[1,\infty)\), as we shall see shortly. But for \(\psi=C\in\mathbb{R}\), the corresponding neural networks are precisely the constant functions, which are clearly not dense in the space of continuous function over a non-trivial domain or \(L^p\), showing that we really need the notion of discriminatory functions to hold for signed measures.
The motivation for Definition \ref{def:discriminatory maps over reals} arises naturally in the proof of the following theorem. 
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:density of NN in C(K)}
    Let \(\psi\) be a continuous discriminatory map, and \(K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^k\) a compact subset. Then
    \begin{equation*}
         \overline{\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(K)}^{\lVert\cdot\rVert_\infty}=C(K;\mathbb{R}).
    \end{equation*}
\end{theorem} 
This is a classic result and essentially follows from the Hahn-Banach theorem (see \parencite[Thm.~5.2]{SteinShakarchi2011FunctionalAnalysis})
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:Hahn Banach corollary}
    Let \(Y\) be a subspace of a normed linear space \(\mathcal{X}\). If \(\mathcal{Y}\) is not dense in \(\mathcal{X}\), then there exists a bounded, linear function \(F\in \mathcal{X}^*\), \(F\neq 0\) such that \(F(y)=0\) for all \(y\in \mathcal{Y}\).
\end{lemma}
For a proof, see \parencite[Corollary.~4.8.7]{Friedman1982FoundationsModernAnalysis}.
\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem \ref{thm:density of NN in C(K)}]
    The proof goes by contradiction. First, notice that \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(K)\) is a subspace of \(C(K;\mathbb{R})\) because \(\psi\) is continuous and bounded by assumption. For the sake of contradiction, assume \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(K)\) is not dense in \(C(K;\mathbb{R})\) for the uniform topology. By Lemma \ref{lemma:Hahn Banach corollary}, there must exist a nonzero continuous functional \(\Lambda\in C(K;\mathbb
    R)^*\) such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \Lambda(f)=0,\quad\forall f\in\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(K).
    \end{equation*}
     By Riesz–Markov–Kakutani (see \parencite[Thm.~7.10.4.]{Bogachev2007MeasureTheory}), we know that \(C^*(K;\mathbb{R})\simeq\mathcal{M}_r(K)\), where \(\mathcal{M}_r(K)\) denotes the set of finite signed Radon measures on \(K\), that is finite signed, inner regular Borel measure. Hence, for every \(\Lambda\in C(K;\mathbb
    R)^*\) there exists a unique \(\mu\in\mathcal{M}_r(K)\) such that 
 \begin{equation*}
     \Lambda(f)=\int_K f\diff\mu,\quad\forall f\in C(K;\mathbb{R}).
 \end{equation*}
   Because \(\psi(A)\in\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(K)\subseteq C(K;\mathbb
    R)\) for all affine linear maps \(A\) on \(K\), we have
    \begin{equation*}
        \int_K \psi(w^{tr}x+b)\mu(\diff x)=0,\quad\forall w\in\mathbb{R}^k,b\in\mathbb{R}.
    \end{equation*}
    Consider the measure 
    \begin{equation*}
        \overline{\mu}(B)\coloneqq \mu(B\cap K),\;\forall B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^k).
    \end{equation*}
    Then, \(\overline{\mu}\) defines a finite, signed measure on \(\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^k)\) (see Lemma \ref{lemma:radon measures on compact subspaces} for an in depth treatment of this construction). Now it follows by standard measure theoretic induction that 
    \begin{equation*}
         \int_K \psi(w^{tr}x+b)\mu(\diff x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^k}\psi(w^{tr}x+b)\overline{\mu}(\diff x).
    \end{equation*}
    Hence, we can rewrite \eqref{eq:discriminatory property in proof} as 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:discriminatory property in proof}
    \int_{\mathbb{R}^k}\psi(w^{tr}x+b)\overline{\mu}(\diff x)=0,\quad\forall w\in\mathbb{R}^k,b\in\mathbb{R}.
    \end{equation}
    
Recall that we assumed that \(\psi\) is discriminatory, which precisely states that \eqref{eq:discriminatory property in proof} implies that \(\mu=0\). Thus, \(\Lambda=0\), whence the contradiction.
\end{proof}

In summary, the theorem, broadly speaking, works by contradiction through Lemma \ref{lemma:Hahn Banach corollary}. The arguments are quite general and mainly require an explicit characterization of the topological dual of the ambient space in which we want to show density. The notion of  discriminatory activation functions is chosen to be well suited to conclude density.
For \(C(K;\mathbb{R})\) as above, the topological dual is explicitly characterized by the Riesz-Markov-Kakutani theorem. The discriminatory property of \(\psi\) turns out to be the natural notion in this context. We know from the Riesz theorem for \(L^p\)-spaces (see \parencite[Thm.~4.4.1.]{Bogachev2007MeasureTheory}) that a similar characterization of bounded linear functionals on \(L^p\)-spaces through an integral mapping exists. The non-trivial part is to show that the same notion of discriminatory activation functions is well adapted to the \(L^p\)-case.
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:density of NN in L^p}
    Let \(\psi\) be a discriminatory function and \(\mu\) finite positive measure on \(\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^k)\). Then
    \begin{equation*}
         \overline{\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)}^{\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^p}}=L^p(\mathbb{R}^k,\mu)
    \end{equation*}
    for \(p\in[1,\infty)\).
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
    The proof is analogous to the proof of Theorem \ref{thm:density of NN in C(K)}, simply using that, by the Riesz Theorem, every continuous linear functional  \(F\in L^p(\mathbb{R}^k,\mu)^*\) is of the form
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:dual of L^p}
        F(f)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^k}fg\diff \mu,\quad\forall f\in L^p(\mathbb{R}^k,\mu)
    \end{equation} for some \(g\in L^q(\mathbb{R}^k,\mu)\), where \(\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1\). 

    
   First, notice that due to the finiteness of \(\mu\), \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)\) is a subspace of  \(L^p(\mathbb{R}^k,\mu)\). Then, by the same strategy as in Theorem \ref{thm:density of NN in C(K)}, assuming \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)\) is not dense in \(L^p(\mathbb{R}^k,\mu)\), there must exist \(F\in L^p(\mathbb{R}^k,\mu)^*\) that annihilates \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)\). By \eqref{eq:dual of L^p}, this implies
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:Lp Riesz}
        \int_{\mathbb{R}^k}fg\diff \mu=0,\quad\forall f\in \mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k).
    \end{equation}
The key observation is that by setting \(\sigma(B)\coloneqq\int_B g\diff \mu\), \(\sigma\) defines a finite signed measure on \(\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^k)\), and 
\eqref{eq:Lp Riesz} reads as 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Lp discriminatory}
        \int_{\mathbb{R}^k}f\diff \sigma=0,\quad\forall f\in \mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k).
\end{equation}
We find ourselves in the same situation as in the proof of Theorem \ref{thm:density of NN in C(K)}.
    From \eqref{eq:Lp discriminatory} and the assumption that \(\psi\) is discriminatory, we get that 
   \begin{equation*}
        \int_{\mathbb{R}^k}\psi(w^{\rm{tr}}x+b) \sigma(\diff x)=0,\quad\forall w\in\mathbb{R}^k,\forall b\in\mathbb{R}.
\end{equation*}
This implies that
    \begin{equation*}
        \sigma = 0.
    \end{equation*}
    But this is contradictory, as we assumed \(F\neq 0\). It remains to argue that \(\sigma\) indeed defines a finite measure. 
    This follows from Hölder's inequality (for the same Hölder conjugate pair \(p,q\) as used in the representation \eqref{eq:dual of L^p}) and the fact that \(\mu\) is finite by assumption:
    \begin{align*}
        |\sigma(B)|=\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^k}1_B g\diff \mu\bigg|\leq \lVert 1_{B}\rVert_{L^p(\mu)}\lVert g \rVert_{L^q(\mu)}=(\mu(\mathbb{R}^k))^{1/p}\lVert g\rVert_{L^q(\mu)}<\infty,\quad\forall B\in \mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^k).
    \end{align*}
    This concludes the proof.
    \end{proof}
\begin{remark}
 When proving the density of a set of functions \(\Psi\) in \(C(K;\mathbb{R)}\), one usually tries to show that \(\Psi\) is an algebra of functions, containing the constant functions and distinguishing points. Then one concludes by the Stone-Weierstrass theorem (see \parencite[Thm.~4.45]{Folland1999RealAnalysis}). Notice that this strategy won't work for \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)\). Indeed, as noted in \cref{rem:NN don't form algebra}, neural networks do not form an algebra, as they are not closed under point-wise multiplication. Surprisingly, as shown in \parencite{HornikStinchcombeWhite1989UniversalApproximators}, there is still a way to prove Theorem \ref{thm:density of NN in C(K)} via a Stone-Weierstrass argument. We give the broad idea here and refer to \parencite{HornikStinchcombeWhite1989UniversalApproximators} for the details. Observe that the family of functions of the form
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:multiplicatively closed NN}
     \sum_{i=1}^Nc_i\prod_{j=1}^M\psi(A_{ij(\cdot)})
 \end{equation} 
 for \(A_{ij}\) affine linear maps, is most certainly closed under multiplication. Thus, showing the density of the set of maps with representation \eqref{eq:multiplicatively closed NN}, for any finite \(N,M\in\mathbb{N}\) in \(C(K;\mathbb{R})\), can be done via the Stone-Weierstrass theorem. The remarkable part is that one can argue that this even implies that \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)\) is dense in \(C(K;\mathbb{R})\) as well. The logical chain of reasoning goes as follows:
    \begin{align*}
    &\rightarrow\text{approximate any continuous squashing function by shallow neural networks}\\&\rightarrow\text{approximate the cosine function by shallow neural networks}\\&\rightarrow\text{approximate linear combinations of compositions of cosine functions with affine linear maps}
    \\&\rightarrow\text{notice that the latter forms a dense set in }C(K;\mathbb{R})\text{ via Stone-Weierstrass. Conclude}.
    \end{align*}
    We used the terminology of \parencite{HornikStinchcombeWhite1989UniversalApproximators}, where \textit{squashing function} is a non-decreasing, sigmoidal map
    \end{remark}
    We finish this section with a lemma showing that there are, in fact, very large classes of functions that are discriminatory.
    \begin{proposition}\label{prop:sigmoidal implies discriminatory}
        Let \(\psi\) be bounded, measurable, and sigmoidal. Then \(\psi\) is discriminatory.
    \end{proposition}
      In particular, continuous sigmoidal maps are discriminatory as well as non-decreasing sigmoidal maps. For the latter, recall that a non-decreasing map from \(\mathbb{R}\) to \(\mathbb{R}\) has at most countably many discontinuities and is thus measurable as a function from \(\mathbb{R}\) to \([0,1]\). 
 For the proof of Proposition \ref{prop:sigmoidal implies discriminatory}, we refer to \parencite[Lemma.~1]{Cybenko1989}.
 A more involved result from \parencite{Hornik1991} shows that for the notion of neural networks introduced in Definition \ref{def:shallow feedforward neural networks}, the conditions can be further relaxed.
 \begin{proposition}\label{prop:non-constant implies discriminatory}
     Let \(\psi\) be bounded, measurable, and non-constant. Then \(\psi\) is discriminatory.
 \end{proposition}
 For the proof, see \parencite[Thm.~5]{Hornik1991}.
\subsection{Neural networks on topological vector spaces}
We will now turn to the task of defining neural networks on more general spaces than \(\mathbb{R}^k\) and show that we can still obtain analogous density results. First, this is of independent mathematical interest. Secondly, this might be of practical interest, as one might want to consider neural networks on path-spaces, which are generally not finite-dimensional. In particular, we will use this viewpoint in our \textit{naive} approach explained in Section \ref{sec:The naïv approach and what goes wrong} and in Proposition \ref{prop:reduction to countably generated sigma algebra is not fundamental, product topology} and \ref{prop:reduction to countably generated sigma algebra is not fundamental in the case of E = C}. 


Note that if we want to generalize neural networks to be defined on more abstract spaces such as general locally convex spaces, it is immediately clear that we will need to generalize the notion of shallow feedforward neural networks from Definition \ref{def:shallow feedforward neural networks}, as they rely on the parametrization of affine maps on \(\mathbb{R}^k\). As previously noted, recall that for any such affine map \(A\), there exist \(w\in\mathbb{R}^k\) and \(b \in \mathbb{R}\) such that 
\begin{equation*}
    A(x)=w^{tr}x+b.
\end{equation*}
We can identify any \(w^{tr}\) with the continuous linear form \(\langle w,\cdot\rangle\in(\mathbb{R}^k)^*\) and vice versa (this follows from Riesz-Fischer \parencite[Cor.~4.3.2.]{Bogachev2007MeasureTheory} on the Hilbert space \((\mathbb{R}^k,\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle)\)). This motivates the following generalization of Definition \ref{def:shallow feedforward neural networks} to topological vector spaces \(E\) with a topological dual \(E^*\).
\begin{definition}\label{def:NN on locally convex X}
    Let \(E\) be a topological vector space and \(\mathcal{A}\) a \(\sigma\)-algebra on \(E\). Furthermore, let \(\Gamma\) be a set of real-valued, \(\mathcal{A}\)-measurable maps on \(E\) and \(\psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\) be measurable and sigmoidal. Then we define 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(E,\Gamma)\coloneqq\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\left\{\psi(F(\cdot)+b):F\in \Gamma,b\in\mathbb{R}\right\}.
    \end{equation*}
    We call elements of \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(E,\Gamma)\) shallow feedforward neural networks with the activation function \(\psi\).
\end{definition}
In particular, elements in \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(E,\Gamma)\) are \(\mathcal{A}\)-measurable functions.
\\\\
\textbf{Example:} We are mostly interested in the case where \(\Gamma=E^*\). If we take \(E=C(K;\mathbb{R})\) for \(K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^k\) compact, by Riesz-Markov-Kakutani, elements in Definition \ref{def:NN on locally convex X} take the explicit form:
\begin{equation*}
     \mathcal{NN}_{\psi}\big(C(K;\mathbb{R}),C(K;\mathbb{R})^*\big)=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\bigg\{f\mapsto\psi\bigg(\int_K f\diff \mu+b\bigg):\mu\in\mathcal{M}(K),b\in\mathbb{R}\bigg\}
\end{equation*}
We obtain a similar characterization for \(E=L^p(\mu)\) by Riesz
\begin{equation*}
     \mathcal{NN}_{\psi}\big(L^p(\mu),(L^p(\mu))^*\big)=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\bigg\{f\mapsto\psi\bigg(\int_E fg\diff \mu+b\bigg):g\in L^q(\mu),b\in\mathbb{R}\bigg\},
\end{equation*}
where \(\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1\).



We shall now give the analog density result to Theorem \ref{thm:density of NN in L^p} for topological vector spaces \(E\) instead of the common Euclidean spaces \(\mathbb{R}^k\). For what is to follow, we shall assume:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \(E\) is a topological vector space,
    \item\(\Gamma = E^*\),
    \item\(\mathcal{A}=\sigma(E^*)\).
\end{itemize}
Under this assumption, we will omit \(\Gamma =E^*\) in the notation of Definition \ref{def:NN on locally convex X}.
Note that the proof given for Theorem \ref{thm:density of NN in L^p} doesn't require any property specific to \(\mathbb{R}^k\). Indeed the Riesz theorem characterizing the dual of \(L^p(E,\mathcal{E},\mu)\) applies to every underlying measure space \((E,\mathcal{E},\mu)\). Only the discriminatory property of \(\psi\), which is, in turn, adapted to \(\mathbb{R}^k\) needs to be modified accordingly.

\begin{definition}\label{def:discriminatory property of X}
 We call a map \(\psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\) discriminatory if it is Borel-measurable, bounded, and if
    \begin{equation*}
        \int_{E}\psi(F(x)+b)\mu(\diff x)=0
    \end{equation*}
    for all \(F\in E^*,b\in\mathbb{R}\) and \(\mu\), a finite signed measure on \((E,\sigma(E^*))\) implies that \(\mu=0\).    
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:density of NN in L^p on topological vector space}
    Let \(\psi\) be a discriminatory map in the sense of Definition \ref{def:discriminatory property of X}, \(\mu\) a finite, positive measure, and \(p\in[1,\infty)\). Then
    \begin{equation*}
         \overline{\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(E)}^{\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^p}}=L^p(E,\mathcal{A},\mu).
    \end{equation*}
\end{theorem} 
\begin{proof}
    Notice that \(\psi\), being discriminatory, is in particular bounded. Hence \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(
    E)\subseteq L^p(E,\mu)\) as \(\mu\) is finite measure. The rest of the proof is completely analogous to Theorem \ref{thm:density of NN in L^p} as the notion of a discriminatory activation function \(\psi\) is defined appropriately in Definition \ref{def:discriminatory property of X}. Indeed, notice that the identification of the dual of \(L^p(E,\mathcal{A},\mu)\) with \(L^q(E,\mathcal{A},\mu)\) for a Hölder conjugate pair \(p,q\) through the Riesz Theorem works for any measure space \((E,\mathcal{A},\mu)\). For a topological vector space \(E\) we might apply it to the measure space \((E,\sigma(E^*),\mu)\) for a finite measure \(\mu\) on \(\sigma(E^*)\). The Hahn-Banach based proof by contradiction of Theorem \ref{thm:density of NN in L^p}  is now analogous to the current case. Indeed, the Hahn-Banach Corollary \ref{lemma:Hahn Banach corollary} applies to the Banach space \(L^p(E,\mathcal{A},\mu)\) for any finite measure space \((E,\mathcal{A},\mu)\). Hence, we again have the existence of a continuous linear functional \(\Lambda\in L^p(E,\mathcal{A},\mu)^*\) such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \Lambda(f)=\int_{E}fg\diff \mu=0,\quad\forall f\in \mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(E).
    \end{equation*}
    We again define \(\sigma(B)\coloneqq\int_B g\diff \mu\) for all \(B\in\sigma(E^*)\), and by Hölder, this defines a finite measure. Hence, we get that in particular 
     \begin{equation*}
        \int_{E}\psi(F(x)+b) \sigma(\diff x)=0,\quad\forall F\in E^*,\forall b\in\mathbb{R}.
\end{equation*}
    By the discriminator property \ref{def:discriminatory property of X} we conclude that \(\sigma=0\), yielding the contradiction and hence the claim. 
\end{proof}
At this point, it is of great importance to generalize Proposition \ref{prop:sigmoidal implies discriminatory} to the notion of the discriminatory function introduced in Definition \ref{def:discriminatory property of X}. Indeed, without such a result, the density of neural networks might be a vacuous statement, since it is not straightforward whether there exists any \(\psi\) that satisfies the requirements of Definition \ref{def:discriminatory property of X}. The following proposition shows that not only does such a \(\psi\) exist, but functions with this property are quite common.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:necessary conditions for generalized discriminatory property}
    Let \(\psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\) be measurable, bounded, and sigmoidal. Then \(\psi\) is discriminatory in the sense of Definition \ref{def:discriminatory property of X}.
\end{proposition}
Before reproducing the proof of \parencite[Prop.~5.15]{Arandjelovic2025}, we state a useful Lemma from \parencite[Lemma.~7.13.5.]{Bogachev2007MeasureTheory}. For the statement, we require the definition of the characteristic functional of a measure \(\mu\) on \(Cyl(E,\Gamma)\) for \(\Gamma\subseteq E^*\) and \(Cyl(E,\Gamma)\) defined as follows:
\begin{equation*}
    Cyl(E,\Gamma)=\left\{ C(f_1,\dots,f_n,B):(f_i)_{i=1}^n\subseteq\Gamma, n\in\mathbb{N},B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^n)\right\},
\end{equation*}
where \(C(f_1,\dots,f_n,B)=\left\{x\in E:(f_1(x),\dots,f_n(x))\in B)\right\}\) is called a \(\Gamma\)-cylinder set. Clearly, we have
\begin{equation*}
    \sigma\big(Cyl(E,\Gamma)\big)=\sigma(\Gamma). 
\end{equation*}
We define the Fourier transform of a measure \(\mu\) on \(Cyl(E,\Gamma)\) as 
\begin{equation*}
    \tilde{\mu}(f)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{it}\mu\circ f^{-1}(dt)
\end{equation*}
for \(f\in \Gamma\). Observe that
\begin{equation*}
    \int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{it}\mu\circ f^{-1}(dt)=\int_E e^{if(x)}\mu(\diff x).
\end{equation*}
Note that this definition is consistent with the definition of the Fourier transform of measures on \(\mathbb{R}^n\) \begin{equation*}
    \tilde{\mu}(y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^n}e^{i\langle y,k\rangle}\mu(\diff k).
\end{equation*}
Indeed, by Riesz-Fischer for the Hilbert-space \((\mathbb{R}^n,\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle)\), for any \(f\in (\mathbb{R}^n)^*\) there exists a unique \(y\in\mathbb{R}^n\) such that \(f(\cdot)=\langle y,\cdot\rangle\).
From here on, we shall take \(\Gamma=E^*\).
The next lemma shows that the Fourier transform of measures on \((E,Cyl(E,E^*))\) characterizes the measures completely. This result is based on the respective statement for the measures on \(\mathbb{R}^n\), see \parencite[Prop.~3.8.6]{Bogachev2007MeasureTheory}.
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:Fourier equality iif measure equality for general X}
    Let \(E\) be a topological vector space and \(E^*\) its topological dual. Let \(\mu,\nu\) be measures on \(\sigma(E^*)\) and \(\tilde{\mu}=\tilde{\nu}\), then one has \(\mu=\nu\). 
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    For all \(f_1,\dots,f_n\in E^*\) define the following measures on \((\mathbb{R}^n,\sigma\big ((\mathbb{R}^n)^*\big))\)
    \begin{align*}
        &\sigma\coloneqq \mu\circ(f_1,\dots,f_n)^{-1}
        \\& \lambda\coloneqq \nu\circ(f_1,\dots,f_n)^{-1}
    \end{align*}
    We show that \(\widetilde{\sigma}=\widetilde{\lambda}\).
    
    \begin{align*}
        \widetilde{\sigma}(y)&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^n}e^{i\langle y,k\rangle} \mu\circ(f_1,\dots,f_n)^{-1}(\diff k)\\&=\int_E e^{i\sum_{j=1}^n y_jf_j(x)}\mu(\diff x)\\&
        =\widetilde{\mu}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^n y_jf_j(x)\bigg)=\widetilde{\nu}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^n y_jf_j(x)\bigg)=\widetilde{\lambda}(y).
    \end{align*}
    for all \(y\in\mathbb{R}^n\).
 Now, from \parencite[Prop.~3.8.6]{Bogachev2007MeasureTheory}, this implies 
    \begin{equation*}
        \sigma=\mu\circ(f_1,\dots,f_n)^{-1}=\nu\circ(f_1,\dots,f_n)^{-1}=\lambda.
    \end{equation*}
    But by definition, \(\sigma(E^*)\) is generated by the intersection stable generating system of cylinder sets \(C(f_1,\dots,f_n,B)\), hence \(\mu=\nu\) on all of \(\sigma(E^*)\). 
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}\label{rem:radon measures uniquely determined by values on sigma(X*)}
For locally convex Hausdorff spaces \(E\), Lemma \ref{lemma:Fourier equality iif measure equality for general X} also holds true for Radon measures; that is, inner regular Borel-measures. This is not trivial, as for topological vector spaces we have in general that \(\sigma(E^*)\subseteq\mathcal{B}(E)\) (any continuous map \(f\in E^*\) is in particular Borel-measurable). It follows from \parencite[Prop.~7.12.1.]{Bogachev2007MeasureTheory}  that Radon measures on locally convex spaces are uniquely determined by their values on \(Cyl(E,E^*)=\sigma(E^*)\). In particular, if a Radon measure is zero on \(\sigma(E^*)\) it so on all of \(\mathcal{B}(E)\) and hence, if two Radon measures on \(\mathcal{B}(E)\) agree on \(\sigma(E^*)\), they agree on all of \(\mathcal{B}(E)\).
\end{remark}
Before giving the proof of Proposition \ref{prop:necessary conditions for generalized discriminatory property} we recall a functional version of the Monotone class theorem as it will be used repeatedly in this thesis. We take the version from \parencite[Thm.~7.4.1.]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications} and refer to the latter for a proof.
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:Monotone class}
    Suppose \( \mathcal{H} \) is a real vector space of real-valued, bounded functions, such that
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item \( \mathcal{H} \) contains the constant functions,
  \item \( \mathcal{H} \) is closed under uniform convergence, and
  \item for every uniformly bounded increasing sequence \( \{f_n\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{H} \) of positive
  functions the limit \( f=\lim_{n} f_n \) is in \( \mathcal{H} \).
\end{enumerate}
Suppose \( \mathcal{C} \) is a subset of \( \mathcal{H} \) which is closed under multiplication and, as usual, write \( \sigma(\mathcal{C}) \) for
the \( \sigma \)-algebra generated by \( \mathcal{C} \) (that is the smallest \( \sigma \)-algebra such that \( f \) is measurable
for every \( f\in \mathcal{C} \)). Then \( \mathcal{H} \) contains all bounded \( \sigma(\mathcal{C}) \)-measurable functions.
\end{theorem}
We can finally turn to the proof of Proposition \ref{prop:necessary conditions for generalized discriminatory property}, which is a reproduction of the proof appearing in \parencite[Prop.~5.15.]{Arandjelovic2025} in the special case of \(L^p\) spaces.
\begin{proof}[Proof of \ref{prop:necessary conditions for generalized discriminatory property}]
    The proof goes by contradiction. For the sake of contradiction, assume that \(\psi\) is a measurable, bounded but not discriminatory in the sense of Definition \ref{def:discriminatory property of X}. Hence, let \(\nu\) be a non-zero, finite measure, such that 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:contradictory assumption}
        \int_E \psi(f(x)+b)\nu(\diff x)=0,\;\forall f\in E^*,\forall b\in\mathbb{R}.
    \end{equation}
    Furthermore, we write \(\nu=\nu^+-\nu^-\), where \(\nu^+\) and \(\nu^-\) denote the positive and negative parts of the signed measure \(\nu\). We show that the conditions in the proposition imply that \(\nu^+=\nu^-\) and thus \(\nu=0\). We do this by showing the equivalent claim 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:proof strategy of necessary conditions for generalized discriminatory property}
        \widetilde{\nu}^+(f)=\widetilde{\nu}^-(f),\quad\forall f\in E^*.
    \end{equation} Indeed, if \eqref{eq:proof strategy of necessary conditions for generalized discriminatory property} holds  then \(\nu^+=\nu^-\) by Lemma \ref{lemma:Fourier equality iif measure equality for general X}. We show \eqref{eq:proof strategy of necessary conditions for generalized discriminatory property} by a monotone class argument.
 More precisely, notice that
    \begin{equation*}
        \widetilde{\nu}^+(f)-\widetilde{\nu}^-(f)=\widetilde{\nu}(f)=\int_E e^{if(x)}\nu(\diff x)=\int_E \cos\big(f(x)\big)\nu(\diff x)+i\int_E\sin\big(f(x)\big)\nu(\diff x).
    \end{equation*}
    Hence, to show that this expression is equal to \(0\), we need to show that the complex number on the right is equal to \(0\), that is, that both its real and imaginary parts vanish. We set up the monotone class argument:
    Let 
    \begin{align*}
        \mathcal{H}=\bigg\{ \text{bounded, measurable } h: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}: \int_E h\big(f(x)\big) \nu(\diff x) = 0, \; \forall f \in E^* \bigg\}.
    \end{align*}
    The goal is to show that \(\mathcal{H}\) contains the indicators of half-open real intervals \(I=(a,b]\) for \(a\leq b\), taking the convention \((a,b]=\emptyset\) for \(a=b\). It is well known that these form a generating, intersection stable system for \(\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})\). If we show the latter, then by the monotone class theorem ( see \parencite[Thm.~7.4.1.]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications}), \(\mathcal{H}\) contains all bounded Borel-measurable maps; hence, both integrals for \(h\in\left\{\cos,\sin\right\}\) vanish, proving the claim.
 

    
    \(\mathcal{H}\) is a real-vector space, containing constant functions and closed under  bounded monotone limits. Indeed, the first claim is clear by definition. It contains \(1_{\mathbb{R}}\) since for every \(f\in E^*\) we have by \eqref{eq:contradictory assumption}
    \begin{equation*}
        \int_E1_{\mathbb{R}}\big(f(x)\big)\nu(\diff x)=\nu(E)=\frac{1}{\psi(b)}\int_E\psi(0+b)\nu(\diff x)=0,
    \end{equation*}
    for any \(b\in\mathbb{R}\) such that \(\psi(b)\neq 0\), which exists since \(\psi\) is sigmoidal and with \(0\in E^*\).
    For the bounded monotone limits, we conclude by dominated convergence for the positive and negative parts of \(\nu\). Indeed, let \((h_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq \mathcal{H}\) be uniformly bounded by \(C\in\mathbb{R}_+\), converging pointwise to \(f\). Then \(f\) is bounded and measurable as well, and 
    \begin{equation*}
        \lim_{n\to\infty}\left(\int_Eh_n(f(x))\nu^+(\diff x)-\int_Eh_n(f(x))\nu^-(\diff x)\right)=0+0,\quad\forall f\in E^*
    \end{equation*}
    by dominated convergence. It remains to show that the maps \(1_{(a,b]}\) for \(a\leq b\) are in \(\mathcal{H}\). It is enough to show that \(1_{(\theta,\infty)}\in\mathcal{H}\) for any \(\theta\in\mathbb{R}\). Then, because \(\mathcal{H}\) is a vector space of real-valued functions, the claim follows. Indeed, consider a real sequence \((s_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) increasing to \(\infty\). Then:
    \begin{align*}
\int_E 1_{(\theta,\infty)}\big(f(x)\big)\,\nu(\diff x)=\nu(f(x)>\theta)
&=\lim_{\gamma\to-\infty}\Big(\nu(f(x)>\theta)+\psi(\gamma)\nu(f(x)=\theta)\Big)\notag\\
&=\lim_{\gamma\to-\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_E\psi\big(s_n(f(x)-\theta)+\gamma\big)\,\nu(\diff x).
\end{align*}
    Indeed, the first equality holds by the sigmoidality of \(\psi\), and the second equality uses the fact that \(\psi\) is bounded by assumption and \(\nu^{+/-}\) are finite measures; thus, \(\psi\) is \(\nu\) integrable and by dominated convergence 
    \begin{equation*}
        \lim_{n\to\infty}\int_E\psi\big(s_n(f(x)-\theta)\big)\nu(\diff x)=\int_E\lim_{n\to\infty}\psi\big(s_n(f(x)-\theta)\big)\nu(\diff x),
        \end{equation*}
        with \(\psi_n=\psi(s_n(f(x)-\theta))\leq\lVert \psi\rVert_{\infty}<\infty\),  for the positive and negative parts of \(\nu\). Indeed, for \(\gamma\in\mathbb{R}\), if \(f(x)>\theta\), then \(s_n(f(x)-\theta)\to\infty\) as \(n\to\infty\), and by the sigmoidality of \(\psi\) we have that \(\lim_{n\to\infty}\psi(s_n(f(x)-\theta)+\gamma)=1\). If \(f(x)<\theta\), by the same argument: \(\lim_{n\to\infty}\psi(s_n(f(x)-\theta)+\gamma)=0\), and for \(f(x)=\theta\), \(\lim_{n\to\infty}\psi(s_n(f(x)-\theta)+\gamma)=\psi(\gamma)\). But
        \begin{equation*}
            \int_E\psi\big(s_n(f(x)-\theta)+\gamma\big)\,\nu(\diff x)=0
        \end{equation*}
        by \eqref{eq:contradictory assumption}, hence the claim.
\end{proof}


We finish this section with the proof of the generalized version of Theorem \ref{thm:density of NN in C(K)}. Notice that it requires more structure on \(E\); in particular, we take it to be a locally convex Hausdorff space. 
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:density of NN in C(K) generalized}
    Let \(E\) be a locally convex Hausdorff space, \(\psi\) a continuous, discriminatory map in the sense of \ref{def:discriminatory property of X}, and \(K\subseteq E\) a compact subset. Then
    \begin{equation*}
       \overline{\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(K)}^{\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty}}=C(K;\mathbb{R}),
    \end{equation*}
    where \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(K)=\{G|_K:G\in\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(E)\}\)
\end{theorem}
In the proof of Theorem \ref{thm:density of NN in C(K)} we showed that if \(\psi\) is discriminatory, the discriminatory property \eqref{eq:discriminatory maps over reals} over a compact \(K\) subset still implies that any measure on \(\mathcal{B}(K)\) is necessarily the zero measure. More precisely, if for a compact subset \(K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^k\), the condition
\(\int_K\psi(w^{\rm{tr}}x+b)\mu(\diff x)=0\) for all \(w\in\mathbb{R}^k\) and \(b\in \mathbb{R}\) implies that the measure \(\mu\) on \(\mathcal{B}(K)\) is the zero measure. We make this observation explicit at this point as it will be used again in the proof of \ref{thm:density of NN in C(K) generalized} in a slightly more technical setting. In particular, we need the claim for Radon-measures. 

\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:radon measures on compact subspaces}
 Let \(E\) be a Hausdorff topological vector space and \(\mathcal{B}(E)\) the respective Borel-\(\sigma\)-algebra, which is the \(\sigma\)-algebra generated by the open sets in \(E\). Furthermore, let \(K\) be a compact subset of \(E\) and \(\mathcal{B}(K)\) the respective Borel-\(\sigma\)-algebra generated by the open sets in \(K\), where we equip \(K\) with the subspace topology. Let \(\mu\) be a Radon-measure on \(\mathcal{B}(K)\). Then the set-function on \(\mathcal{B}(E)\)
 defined as
 \begin{equation*}
     \overline{\mu}(B)\coloneqq \mu(B\cap K),\;B\in\mathcal{B}(E),
 \end{equation*}
 defines a Radon-measure on \(\mathcal{B(E)}\). In particular, we have 
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:inclusion mapping}
     \overline{\mu}=i_*\mu,
 \end{equation}
 where \(i:K\to E\) is the embedding function.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    It is a standard result that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{B}(K)=\{A\cap K:A\in\mathcal{B}(E)\}.
    \end{equation*}
    (see \parencite[Lemma.~6.2.4.]{Bogachev2007MeasureTheory}). Hence, \(\overline{\mu}\) is well defined. \(\overline{\mu}\) clearly defines a measure as for a countable collection of disjoint sets \((B_i)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(E)\) we have
    \begin{equation*}
        \overline{\mu}\bigg(\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}B_i\bigg)=\mu\bigg(\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}(B_i\cap K)\bigg)=\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu(B_i\cap K)=\sum_{i\geq 1}\overline{\mu}(B_i).
    \end{equation*}
    Furthermore, one can check from the definition of the total variation measure that \(|\overline{\mu}|(B)=|\mu|(B\cap K)\) for all \(B\in\mathcal{B}(E)\). 
    
    
           For the Radon property of \(\overline{\mu}\), notice that as \(\mu\) is Radon by assumption, there exists a compact set \(K_{\epsilon}\subseteq B\cap K\) such that for any set \(B\cap K\in\mathcal{B}(K)\) and any \(\epsilon>0\), \(|\mu|(B\cap K / K_{\epsilon})<\epsilon\). In particular, \(K_{\epsilon}\subseteq B\) and 
       \begin{equation*}
           |\overline{\mu}|(B/K_{\epsilon})=|\mu|(B/ K_{\epsilon}\cap K)=|\mu|(B\cap K_{\epsilon}^c\cap K)=|\mu|(B\cap K/ K_{\epsilon})<\epsilon.
       \end{equation*}
       As we equipped \(K\) with the subspace topology the inclusion mapping \(i:K\mapsto E\) is continuous, and hence \(K_{\epsilon}\) is compact in \(E\) as well. Note that the expression \(B/K\) for a compact set \(K\) and a measurable set \(B\) appearing in the definition of a Radon-measure is a measurable set, as compact sets are closed in Hausdorff spaces.

    
       For property \eqref{eq:inclusion mapping}, simply notice that \(i:K\to E\) is continuous by the definition of the subspace topology and hence \(\mathcal{B}(K)/\mathcal{B}(E)\)-measurable. Then by definition, for \(A\subseteq\mathcal{B}(E)\)
    \begin{equation*}
        i_*\mu(A) = \mu\big(i^{-1}(A)\big)=\mu(A\cap K)=\overline{\mu}(A).
    \end{equation*}
\end{proof}

The following is a direct consequence of the lemma \ref{lemma:radon measures on compact subspaces}
\begin{corollary}\label{cor:discriminatory property extends also true for subspace}
 Let \(\mu\) and \(\overline{\mu}\) be as in Lemma \ref{lemma:radon measures on compact subspaces}, then 
 \begin{equation*}
     \int_K f|_K\diff \mu=\int_E f\diff \overline{\mu}.
 \end{equation*}
 for all \(\mathcal{B}(E)\)-measurable and \(\overline{\mu}\) integrable maps \(f\).
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem \ref{thm:density of NN in C(K) generalized}]
Notice that since every \(G\in\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(K)\) is of the form 
    \begin{equation*}
        G(x) =\sum_{i=1}^Nc_i\psi\big(F_i|_K(x)+b_i\big),
    \end{equation*}
    where \(\psi\) is continuous by assumption, and \(F_i\in E^*\) are, in particular, continuous. Hence, \(G:K\to\mathbb{R}\) is continuous as a composition of continuous maps. The proof follows the proof of Theorem \ref{thm:density of NN in C(K)} closely. Assume by contradiction that \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(K)\) is not dense in \(C(K;\mathbb{R})\). Then, by Lemma \ref{lemma:Hahn Banach corollary}, there exists a non zero \(\Lambda\in C(K;\mathbb{R})^*\) such that \(\Lambda|_{\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(K)}=0\). By the Riesz-Markov-Kakutani theorem, there exists a finite, signed Radon measure \(\mu\) on \(\mathcal{B}(K)\) such that 
    \begin{equation*}
    \Lambda(f)=\int_Kf\diff \mu,\;\forall f\in C(K;\mathbb{R}).
    \end{equation*}
As \(\psi(F|_K(\cdot)+b)\in\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(K)\) for all \(F\in E^*\) we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:equation 2}
    \int_K\psi(F(x)+b)\mu(\diff x)=0,\;\forall F\in E^*,\forall b\in\mathbb{R}.
\end{equation}
It remains to argue that \(\mu\) is the zero measure. By Corollary \ref{cor:discriminatory property extends also true for subspace} we can rewrite \eqref{eq:equation 2} as 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:equation 3}
    \int_{E}\psi(F(x)+b)\overline{\mu}(\diff x)=0,\;\forall F\in E^*,\forall b\in\mathbb{R},
\end{equation}
where \(\overline{\mu}\) is as in Lemma \ref{lemma:radon measures on compact subspaces}.
By assumption, \(\psi\) is discriminatory in the sense of Definition \ref{def:discriminatory property of X}, and \(\overline{\mu}\) is a finite signed Radon measure on \(\mathcal{B}(E)\). In particular, it is a finite signed measure on \(\sigma(E^*)\). Because \(\psi\) is discriminatory in the sense \ref{def:discriminatory property of X}, the condition \eqref{eq:equation 3}
    implies that \(\overline{\mu}\) restricted to \(\sigma(E^*)\) is the zero-measure. Since finite signed Radon measures on a locally convex Hausdorff space are uniquely determined by their restrictions to \(\sigma(E^*)\), it is the zero measure on all of \(\mathcal{B}(E)\). In particular, this implies that \(\mu\) is the zero measure on \(\mathcal{B}(K)\), which concludes the proof.
 \end{proof}

\begin{definition}\label{def:standing assumptions}
    We refer to the following set of requirements as \textit{standing assumptions}:
    \begin{itemize}
    \item \(E\) is a topological vector space with topology \(\tau_E\).
    \item \(\Gamma=E^*\).
    \item \(\mathcal{A}=\sigma(E^*)\), where \(E^*\) is the topological dual of \(E\).
    \item \(\mu\) is a finite signed-measure on \(\mathcal{A}\).
    \item \(\psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\) is bounded, measurable, and sigmoidal. 
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}


\chapter{Continuous-time mean--variance hedging}\label{ch:continuous time mean variance hedging}
In this chapter, we explore some theoretical aspects of the treatment of mean–variance hedging on a continuous time grid. In particular, we study the continuous-time mean-variance hedging problem according to the properties of the price-process \(X\) and the corresponding strategies. Together with the approximation properties of shallow-feed forward neural networks, this will lay the foundations to develop deep-mean-variance hedging results in continuous time.

As alluded to in Chapter \ref{ch:hedging}, a general treatment of the mean-variance hedging problem requires a sophisticated notion of stochastic integration. As we aim for self-containment as much as reasonably possible in the scope of this thesis, we discuss the construction of the stochastic integral in the \textit{square integrable} case, which forms the cornerstone of the theory of stochastic integration. We give a few remarks on the general case, while mainly referring to standard works such as \parencite[Ch.~ II,IV]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration} and \parencite[Sec.~12]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications} for the details and missing proofs. 

 
We need to follow the classic concept of local properties in the stochastic sense.
\begin{definition}\label{def:local martingale}
    Let \(Z\) be a stochastic process on the stochastic basis \((\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})\) with \(\mathbb{T}_+=\mathbb{R}_+\). Let \(\mathcal{C}\) be some class of stochastic  processes. We say that \(Z\) is locally in the class \(\mathcal{C}\) if there exists a sequence of stopping times \((\tau_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) such that for almost all \(\omega\), \((\tau_n(\omega))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) is an increasing sequence such that \(\lim_{n\to\infty}\tau_n(\omega)=\infty\), and such that \(Z^{\tau_n}\) is in \(\mathcal{C}\). We call such a sequence of stopping times a localizing sequence for the process Z and denote the class of processes that are locally in \(\mathcal{C}\) by \(\mathcal{C}_{\rm{loc}}\). 
\end{definition}
A class that is of particular interest to us in this chapter is the class of local martingales; that is, the class of processes that are locally a martingale. 


Furthermore, it is a standard result that any martingale \(M\) with respect to a right-continuous filtration admits a càdlàg modification; that is, a process \(\widetilde{M}\) such that \(\widetilde{M}\) is again a martingale on the same filtered probability space, has càdlàg paths, and satisfies
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{P}\big[M_t=\widetilde{M}_t,\;\forall t\in \mathbb{R}_+\big].
\end{equation*}
We shall assume the underlying filtered probability space \((\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})\) to be complete and that the filtration satisfies the usual hypotheses.
For any martingale in this and the following chapters, we shall always take the càdlàg version, even if not explicitly mentioned.
\section{Self financing strategies in continuous times}\label{sec:Self financing strategies in continuous times}
In the continuous time setting, the gains process of a self-financing strategy \eqref{eq:self financing strategy} is given by
\begin{equation*}
    V_t = V_0 + \int_0^t \vartheta_t\diff X_t,\;t\in\mathbb{R}_+,
\end{equation*}
where \(\int_0^t \vartheta_t\diff X_t\) denotes the stochastic integral of a strategy \(\vartheta\) with respect to the real-valued price process \(X\). Thus, we need to understand stochastic integration with respect to stochastic processes in order to understand self-financing strategies in continuous time mathematical finance and, in particular, mean-variance hedging. The definition of the stochastic integral with respect to \(X\) depends on the properties of \(X\). In particular, the class of processes \(\vartheta\) for which \(\int\vartheta\diff X\) is well-defined depends on the properties of \(X\). We call this process \(X\)-integrable.

As the class of finite variation processes is a recurring object in this thesis, we define them formally as the class of adapted, càdlàg processes with paths of finite variation.
In the case where \(X\) is a process of finite variation, the integration theory is classical pathwise Lebesgue-Stieltjes integration with respect to the induced signed measure on \(\mathbb{R}\):
\begin{equation*}
    \diff X(\omega)=\diff X_{+}(\omega)-\diff X_{-}(\omega)
\end{equation*}
where \(X=X_+-X_-\) is the decomposition of \(X\) into two non-decreasing processes \(X_+,X_-\). For non-decreasing processes, we can associate a positive measure, namely the Lebesgue-Stieltjes measures. For every \(\omega\) the integration theory is then the well-known Lebesgue integration theory (see \parencite[Ch.6,sec.3.3]{SteinShakarchi2005RealAnalysis}). 
If \(X\) is of infinite variation, this no longer works. For Hölder continuous integration, we might still be able to define a sensible integral in a similar way (Young integral: see \parencite{Young1936}). For processes with more pathological behavior, such as Brownian motion, we can't classically define an integration theory. In particular, we can't define a pathwise integral with respect to Brownian motion (see \parencite[Thm.~56]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}). 



We shall distinguish between the statistical, or real-world, probability measure \(\mathbb{P}\)
and the equivalent probability measures under which the discounted price process becomes a
local martingale. We call a probability measure \(\mathbb{Q}\) an equivalent local martingale
measure (ELMM) for \(X\) if \(\mathbb{Q} \approx \mathbb{P}\) and \(X\) is a local \(\mathbb{Q}\)-martingale. We denote the set of all such measures by
\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal M_e(X)
    :=
    \{\text{probability measure }\mathbb{Q}: \mathbb{Q}\approx \mathbb{P},\; X \text{ is a local } \mathbb{Q}\text{-martingale}\}.
\end{equation*}
Elements of \(\mathcal M_e(X)\) are also called risk-neutral measures. We shall assume that \(\mathcal{M}_e(X)\neq\emptyset\), which according to \parencite[Cor~.1.2.]{DelbaenSchachermayer1994FTAP} is equivalent to assuming that there is \textit{no free lunch with vanishing risk} (NFLVR). In a complete market,
the equivalent local martingale measure is unique, while in incomplete markets, one
typically has several such measures. 



From the perspective of stochastic integration theory, we might allow all \(X\)-integrable strategies \(\vartheta\). For our treatment of mean-variance hedging, we already argued that it is a good idea to restrict to a class of admissible integrands \(\Theta\) to processes for which the random variable \(\int_0^T\vartheta\diff X\) is itself a square-integrable random variable. For the purpose of this thesis, this shall be the relevant restriction on \(\vartheta\). We mention at this point that, from a mathematical finance perspective, one might consider restricting the set of admissible strategies further. A common financial admissibility condition, such as that found in \parencite[Def.~3.4.]{RheinlaenderSexton2011HedgingDerivatives}, is to require that admissible strategies are such that the associated value process, and thus, up to its initial value, the stochastic integral with respect to the price process, is a \(\mathbb{Q}\)-super-martingale for every \(\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{M}_e\). In particular, a strategy \(\vartheta\) that is locally bounded, predictable, and bounded from below is admissible. Indeed, for locally bounded and predictable strategies, the value process is a local martingale (see \parencite[Ch.~IV,Thm.~29]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}). 
This notion of admissible strategies prohibits pathological examples such as the suicide strategy (see  \parencite[Ex.~3.3.]{RheinlaenderSexton2011HedgingDerivatives}) that yield a sure profit with unlimited risk. In this thesis, we are concerned with mean-variance hedging as a mathematical problem independent of its financial context. Hence, if not stated otherwise, admissible strategies are \(X\)-integrable strategies. In different contexts, we might put different assumptions on the strategies we call admissible. 

\section{Square integrable martingale case}
In this section, we solve the mean-variance problem \eqref{eq:mean squared hedging} in the square-integrable martingale setting, which assumes the price process \(M\) to be a square-integrable martingale (see the definition below). To do so, we first introduce the concept of a square integrable martingale we use in this thesis. Furthermore, we construct the stochastic integral for square integrable martingale integrators. This allows us to introduce the notions and concepts needed in subsequent sections. 

\subsection{\(\mathcal{H}^p\) martingale spaces}
In order to discuss stochastic integration with respect to square integrable martingales, we introduce and study the space of square integrable martingales and its geometry. We start with a discussion of the general \(\mathcal{H}^p\)-spaces.
 
\begin{definition}\label{def:H^p}
 Let \(p\in[1,\infty)\) and \(M\) be a stochastic process. Let
    \begin{equation*}
        \lVert M\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^p}\coloneqq \lVert M_\infty^*\rVert_{p}=\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}|M_t|^p\Big]^{1/p},
    \end{equation*}
    where we use the notation \(M^*_\infty\coloneqq\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}|M_t|\) and \(\lVert\cdot\rVert_p\) to denote the \(L^p\)-norm. We denote by \(\mathcal{H}^p\) the space of martingales such that 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:finite Hp norm}
         \lVert M\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^p}<\infty.
    \end{equation}
    We identify indistinguishable martingales in \(\mathcal{H}^p\).
\end{definition}
We call processes in \(\mathcal{H}^p\) p-integrable martingales. More generally, we call processes that satisfy \eqref{eq:finite Hp norm} p-integrable processes. Furthermore, we denote by \(\mathcal{H}^p_0\) processes \(M\) in \(\mathcal{H}^p\) with \(M_0=0\). Finally, \(\mathcal{H}^p_{\rm{loc}},\mathcal{H}^p_{0,\rm{loc}}\) denotes the respective localized spaces.
\begin{remark}\label{rem:equivalence of H^p convetions}
 There are different conventions for defining \(\mathcal{H}^p\) in the literature. Another common convention is to define \(\mathcal{H}^p\) as the space of martingales such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\mathbb{E}\big[|M_t|^p\big]<\infty.
    \end{equation*}
    Nevertheless, notice that due to Jensen's inequality, \(|M|^p\) is a submartingale, and due to Doob's \(L^p\)-inequality for submartingales and \(p>1\), we have
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}|M_t|^p\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq q\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\mathbb{E}\big[|M_t|^p\big]^{\frac{1}{p}}<\infty,
    \end{equation*}
    where \(\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1\). The converse inequality is always true; hence, for \(p>1\), both conventions for the definition of \(\mathcal{H}^p\) yield the same space of martingales. The upside of the convention taken in Definition \ref{def:H^p} is that a local martingale in \(\mathcal{H}^p\) is automatically a true martingale by Lemma \ref{lemma:local martingale is a true martingale}.
\end{remark}
The following lemma will be crucial for the study of the geometry of \(\mathcal{H}^p\).
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:equivalence of H^p norm}
 Let \(\mathcal{H}^p\) be as in Definition \ref{def:H^p}. Then \(\mathcal{H}^p\) has the following property:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item For \(q\leq p\), we have \(\lVert M\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^q}\leq\lVert M\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^p}\) implying \(\mathcal{H}^p\subseteq\mathcal{H}^q\). In particular, \(\mathcal{H}^p\subseteq\mathcal{H}^1\) for all \(p\in[1,\infty)\).
        \item If \(p\in(1,\infty)\) and \(M\) is a uniformly integrable martingale, then 
        \begin{equation}
            \lVert M_\infty\rVert_{L^p}\leq\lVert M_\infty^*\rVert_{L^p}\leq q\lVert M_\infty\rVert_{L^p}
        \end{equation}
        for \(p,q\) Hölder-conjugates, that is \(\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1\).
    \end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Part (1.) is the usual inclusion of \(L^p(\mu)\) spaces for finite measure \(\mu\), which we recall follows from Jensen's inequality \parencite[Thm.~4.3]{LeGall2022MeasureTheoryProbabilityStochasticProcesses}. For (2.), first notice that by the martingale convergence theorem, \(M_\infty\coloneqq\lim_{t\to \infty}M_t\) is well defined as an almost sure limit, since \(M\) is uniformly integrable by assumption. The first inequality is due to the monotonicity of the Lebesgue-integral, and the second follows from Doob's \(L^p\) inequality (see \parencite[Prop.~2.17.]{RheinlaenderSexton2011HedgingDerivatives}). 
\end{proof}
In particular, by \parencite[Thm.~12.27]{LeGall2022MeasureTheoryProbabilityStochasticProcesses}, this implies that for any \(M\in\mathcal{H}^p\) with \(p>1\) the limit process
\begin{equation*}
    M_{\infty}\coloneqq \lim_{t\to\infty}M_t
\end{equation*}
exists almost surely.


For the following lemma, we need an easy criterion for which a local martingale is actually a true martingale. Furthermore, it gives sufficient conditions for a local martingale to be a uniformly integrable martingale
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:local martingale is a true martingale}
    Let \(M\) be a local martingale such that \(\mathbb{E}[M_t^*]<\infty\) for all \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\). Then \(M\) is a martingale. If \(E[M_\infty^*]<\infty\), then \(M\) is a uniformly integrable martingale. 
\end{lemma}
For a proof, see \parencite[Thm.~5.1, II]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}.
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:martingales in H^p are UI}
    For all \(p\in[1,\infty)\), we have that \(M\in\mathcal{H}^p\) is uniformly integrable and \(|M_t|^p\to|M_\infty|^p\) in \(L^1\).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    For the first part, it suffices to notice that \(M\in\mathcal{H}^p\) for \(p\in[1,\infty)\) implies  \(E[M_\infty^*]<\infty\), by point (1.) of Lemma \ref{lemma:equivalence of H^p norm}. Thus, \(M\) is uniformly integrable by Lemma \ref{lemma:local martingale is a true martingale}, and in particular \(M_\infty\) exists as an almost sure limit \(\lim_{t\to \infty}M_t=M_\infty\). Now, because \(|M_t|^p\leq (M_\infty^*)^p\) and \((M_\infty^*)^p\) is integrable, the family \((|M_t|^p)_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\) is uniformly integrable by \parencite[Example.~2.5.3.]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications} for \(\phi(\cdot)=|\cdot|^p\). Hence, the almost sure convergence of \(|M_t|^p\) to \(|M_\infty|^p\) implies the respective \(L^1\) convergence of the latter.
\end{proof}

We can use our general insight from Lemma \ref{lemma:equivalence of H^p norm} to identify \(\mathcal{H}^p\) with the Banach space \(L^p(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{\infty},\mathbb{P})\). Indeed, the identification is explicitly given by the mapping that associates a martingale with its terminal value.
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:H^p identification}
    Let \(\mathcal{H}^p\) be as in Definition \ref{def:H^p} for \(p\in(1,\infty)\). The following map 
    \begin{align}
    &\mathcal{H}^p\to L^p(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_\infty,\mathbb{P}),\notag
    \\
    &M\mapsto M_\infty
\end{align}
is bijective.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
   First of all, because the processes in \(\mathcal{H}^p\) are uniformly integrable by Lemma \ref{lemma:martingales in H^p are UI},  \(M_{\infty}\) is well-defined. Injectivity follows from the fact that \(M\in\mathcal{H}^p\) is uniformly integrable, and hence by \parencite[Thm.12.27]{LeGall2022MeasureTheoryProbabilityStochasticProcesses} the limit \(M_\infty=\lim_{n\to\infty} M_t\) exists almost surely and 
   \begin{equation}\label{eq:closed martingale}
       M_t = \mathbb{E}[M_\infty|\mathcal{F}_t],\quad t\in\mathbb{R}_+.
   \end{equation}
   Hence, clearly if \(M_{\infty}=N_{\infty}\) we have by \eqref{eq:closed martingale} that \(M_t=N_t\) for all \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\). For surjectivity, let \(Z\in L^p(\mathcal{F}_{\infty})\).  Set \(M_t = \mathbb{E}[Z|\mathcal{F}_t]\). Then we have 
   that \(M\) is a uniformly integrable martingale and 
   \begin{align*}
       \mathbb{E}\big[\big(\mathbb{E}[Z|\mathcal{F}_t])^*_{\infty}\big)^p\big]&\leq \Big(\frac{p}{p-1}\Big)^p\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|\mathbb{E}[Z|\mathcal{F}_\infty]\big|^p\Big]
       \\&\leq \Big(\frac{p}{p-1}\Big)^p\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{E}[|Z|^p|\mathcal{F}_\infty]\Big]=\Big(\frac{p}{p-1}\Big)^p\mathbb{E}[|Z|^p]<\infty,
   \end{align*} 
   where the first inequality is due to Doob's \(L^p\) martingale inequality, the second inequality is due to conditional Jensen's, the third due to the tower property of conditional expectation, and the last is by the assumption that \(Z\in L^p(\mathcal{F}_{\infty})\).
   We then have that the uniformly integrable martingale \(M\) is mapped under the map in Lemma \ref{lemma:H^p identification} to \(M_{\infty}=\mathbb{E}[Z|\mathcal{F}_{\infty}]=Z\).
\end{proof}



Moreover, (2.) of Lemma \ref{lemma:equivalence of H^p norm}  states that the norm on \(\mathcal{H}^p\) for \(p>1\) is equivalent to the norm on \(L^p\) under the stated identification. In particular, this implies that the dual of \(\mathcal{H}^p\) can be identified with \(\mathcal{H}^q\), where \(p,q>1\) are taken to be Hölder conjugates. More explicitly, any continuous linear form \(\phi\in(\mathcal{H}^p)^*\) can be identified with some \(N\in\mathcal{H}^q\) and written as \(\phi(M)=\mathbb{E}[M_\infty N_\infty]\). 


The identification of \(\mathcal{H}^p\) with \(L^p\) suggests that the case \(p=2\) has a particularly rich Hilbert-space structure. According to the identification in Lemma \ref{lemma:H^p identification}, \(\mathcal{H}^2\) can be identified with the Hilbert space \(L^2(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{\infty},\mathbb{P})\). Explicitly, we can define an inner product on \(\mathcal{H}^2\) that is given by 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:scalar product on H^2}
    (M,N)_{\mathcal{H}^2}=\mathbb{E}[M_{\infty}N_{\infty}].
\end{equation}
This inner product induces the same topology as the \(\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^2}\)-norm in Definition \ref{def:H^p} by the first part of Lemma \ref{lemma:equivalence of H^p norm}. Furthermore, the inner product structure yields a natural notion of orthogonality. 
\begin{definition}\label{def:weak orthogonality}
    We call \(X,Y\in\mathcal{H}^2\) \textit{weakly orthogonal} if \((X,Y)_{\mathcal{H}^2}=0\).
\end{definition}
The terminology \textit{weak} suggests that there might be a related \textit{strong} orthogonality. Indeed, this is the case and is studied in Section \ref{sec:Kunita–Watanabe Decomposition}.
In the following section, we construct the stochastic integral for square integrable martingales \(M\) by first defining integration with respect to \(M\) for \textit{simple processes}. We will find that the integration map \(\vartheta\mapsto\int\vartheta\diff M\) defines an isomorphism of Hilbert-spaces for a well chosen class of stochastic processes. This observation, originally due to Kunita and Watanabe (see \parencite{KunitaWatanabe1967SquareIntegrableMartingales}), is used to extend the integral by continuity to a more general class of integrands.

\subsection{Integration of simple integrands}
As in classic integration theory, we start by defining the integral for the class of piecewise constant processes, where, for stochastic processes, the pieces are intervals on \(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+\). More precisely, we shall take as intervals predictable sets of the form \((\tau,\sigma]\coloneqq \{(\omega,t)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+:\tau(\omega)< t\leq\sigma(\omega)\}\), where \(\tau\leq\sigma\) are random times. We take the convention \((\tau,\tau]=\emptyset\) for any random time \(\tau\). For the purposes of this section, it is enough to consider deterministic times. The stochastic intervals will only appear as indicator sets of indicator functions. Hence, there is no risk of confusion between stochastic intervals and ordinary real intervals when using deterministic times as \(1_{\{(\omega,r):s<r\leq t\}}=1_{\Omega}1_{(s,t]}=1_{(s,t]}\). In what follows, we consider all the stochastic processes to take values in \(\mathbb{R}\).

\begin{definition}\label{def:elementary predictable process}
    We call \(\vartheta\) an \textit{elementary predictable process} if there exists a finite, increasing sequence of deterministic times, \(0=t_0\leq\dots\leq t_{n+1}<\infty\) and a sequence of bounded random variables \((\varphi_i)_{i=0}^n\subseteq L^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}_{t_i},\mathbb{P})\) such that 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:representation of elementary predictable process}
        \vartheta = \varphi_01_{\left\{0\right\}}+\sum_{i=0}^n\varphi_i1_{(t_i,t_{i+1}]}.
    \end{equation}
In particular, we have that \(\varphi_i\) is \(\mathcal{F}_{t_i}\)-measurable. Moreover, as \(\varphi_i\) is essentially bounded for every \(i=0,\dots,n\), we have that \(\vartheta\) is an essentially bounded stochastic process; that is, there exist \(\mathcal{N}\in\mathcal{F}\) such that \(\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{N}]=0\) and \(C>0\) such that \(|\vartheta_t(\omega)|\leq C\) for all \(\omega\in \mathcal{N}^c\).  We denote the set of elementary predictable processes by \(\mathcal{E}\) and we say \(\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}\) is associated with a sequence \((t_k)_{k=0}^{n+1}\).
\end{definition}
The association of \(\vartheta\) with \((t_k)_{k=0}^{n+1}\) should be understood in the sense that it fixes a representation of \(\vartheta\), which is not unique. Indeed, we might define a refined grid by adding a point \(t_i<s_i<\dots<t_{i+1}\) for some \(i=1,\dots,n\). This changes the representation of \(\vartheta\) in the sense that we associate it with the refined sequence \(t_0<\dots<t_i<s_i<t_{i+1}<t_{n+1}\), but this clearly doesn't change the values \(\vartheta\) takes, as the latter is constant on \((t_i,t_{i+1}]\). Hence, saying that we associate \(\vartheta\) with the grid \((t_i)_{k=0}^{n+1}\) should be understood as the identification of a representation \eqref{eq:representation of elementary predictable process} with \((t_i)_{k=0}^{n+1}\).


Furthermore, notice that elementary processes are adapted, left-continuous processes; thus, they are predictable. In particular, observe that \(\mathcal{E}\) is clearly closed under summation; moreover, it is also closed under pointwise multiplication. To see this, take two elements of \(\vartheta,\eta\in\mathcal{E}\) and their associated sequences \((t^\vartheta_k)_{n=0}^{n+1}\), \((t^\eta_k)_{k=0}^{l+1}\) for some \(n,l\in\mathbb{N}\). They both form a partition of \([0,\infty]\) if we add the points \(t^{\vartheta
}_{n+2}=t^{\eta}_{l+2}=\infty\) to both sequences. Take their common refinement and notice that the pointwise product of two indicator functions of disjoint sets vanishes.
\\\\
For later use, we denote by \(\mathcal{E}^{\infty}\) the set of processes as in Definition \ref{def:elementary predictable process}, but for infinite sets \((t_k)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\) where \(0=t_0\leq t_1\leq\dots<\infty\) and \(\lim_{n\to\infty}t_n=\infty\). Hence, processes of the form 
\begin{equation}
      \vartheta = \varphi_01_{\left\{0\right\}}+\sum_{i= 0}^\infty \varphi_i1_{(t_i,t_{i+1}]}.
\end{equation}
We identify a process \(\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}\) associated with the sequence \((t_k)_{k=0}^n\), to the process in \(\widetilde{\vartheta}\in\mathcal{E}^{\infty}\) associated with the sequence \(\tilde{t}_t=t_k\; k=0,\dots,n\), and \(t_k = t_n+k\) for \(k>n\) with \(\widetilde{\varphi}^i=\varphi_i\) for \(i\leq n\) and \(\varphi_i=0\) for \(i>n\). Hence, we identify \(\mathcal{E}\) as a subset of \(\mathcal{E}^{\infty}\). Observe that a process in \(\mathcal{E}^{\infty}\) is not necessarily essentially bounded. 


We define a stochastic integral of \(\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}\) with respect to integrators \(X\), which we assume to be a classical semimartingale, that is, an adapted, càdlàg process \(X\), that can be written as 
\begin{equation}\label{def:semimartingale}
    X = X_0+M+A\;
\end{equation}
with \(M\) a local martingale and \(A\) a process of finite variation. We call \(X\) a special semimartingale if we can choose \(A\) in Definition \ref{def:semimartingale} to be predictable. In that case, the decomposition \(X=X_0+M+A\) is unique and is referred to as the canonical decomposition of \(X\).
We define a stochastic integral of \(\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}\) with respect to a classical semimartingale \(X\) as follows:
\begin{definition}\label{def:integral of simple processes}
Let \(\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}\). Then we set 
\(I_X(\vartheta)=(I_X(\vartheta)_t)_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\)
 with
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:stochastic integral for elementary process}
        I_X(\vartheta)_t\coloneqq \sum_{i=0}^n \varphi_i(X_{t_{i+1}\wedge t}-X_{t_{i}\wedge t}),\quad \forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+.
    \end{equation}
    We call \(I_X(\vartheta)\) the stochastic integral process of \(\vartheta\) with respect to \(X\) and use the following notations interchangeably
    \begin{equation*}
        I_X(\vartheta)=\vartheta\bullet X=\int \vartheta\diff X.
    \end{equation*}
    We shall omit \(X\) in the left-most notation if it is clear from the context. Notice that by construction \(I_X(\vartheta)_0=0\). 
\end{definition}
It is clear that the pathwise definition of the integral \eqref{eq:stochastic integral for elementary process} is independent of the representation of \(\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}\).
\subsection{Stochastic integral for square-integrable local martingales}\label{sec:Stochastic Integration for square-integrable Martingales}

 We start this subsection by showing that the construction of the stochastic integral with respect to square integrable martingales is not just a special case in which the construction works nicely but constitutes the essential stepping stone towards a general theory of stochastic integration for the class of semimartingales.


Consider the decomposition of a classical semimartingale
\begin{equation*}
    X = X_0+M+A.
\end{equation*}
According to the fundamental theorem of local martingales (see \parencite[Thm.~29,Ch.III]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}), every local martingale can be decomposed as a sum of a local martingale with bounded jumps and a local martingale of finite variation. The finite variation part of the local martingale can be absorbed in the finite variation part of Definition \ref{def:semimartingale}. Hence, we may choose the local martingale \(M\) to have bounded jumps and thus to be a locally square-integrable local martingale. Assume that integration with respect to locally square-integrable processes \(M\) is understood for a respective class of integrable processes. Up to the non-uniqueness of the decomposition in Definition \ref{def:semimartingale} of a classical semimartingale, we naturally define the integral of some \(X\)-integrable process \(\vartheta\) with respect to \(X\) as 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:heuristic integral with respect to semimartingale}
    \int \vartheta\diff X\coloneqq\int \vartheta\diff M+\int \vartheta\diff A.
\end{equation}
In particular, integration with respect to the finite variation measures is well understood via the Lebesgue-Stieltjes integral. Hence, the main conceptual gap in \eqref{eq:heuristic integral with respect to semimartingale} is, up to localization, the construction of \(\int \vartheta\diff M\) for a square-integrable martingale \(M\).


We use without proof the result that for \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\) one can define its predictable quadratic variation process (see \(\langle M\rangle\) \parencite[Def.11.1.1.]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications}).
One can even show that the process \(\langle M\rangle\) exists if and only if \(M\in\mathcal{H}_{0,\rm{loc}}^2\), where \(\mathcal{H}_{0,\rm{loc}}^2\) is the space of processes that are locally in \(\mathcal{H}_{0}^2\) (see \parencite[Rem.~11.3.3.]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications}).
