Furthermore, recall that for general semimartingales, one can always define their optional quadratic variation \([M]\) (see \parencite[Def.11.1.4.]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications}). The key observation of this section is the following.
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:Ito isometry for simple processes}
    For \(\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}\) and \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\), we have \(\int \vartheta\diff M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\) and the following equation holds
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:Ito isometry for simple processes}
        \mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(\int_0^{\infty} \vartheta_t\diff M_t\right)^2\bigg]=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta_t^2\diff\langle M\rangle_t\bigg],
    \end{equation}
    where \(\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta_t^2\diff\langle M\rangle_t\) denotes the pathwise Lebesgue-Stieltjes integral for the finite variation process \(\langle M\rangle\).
\end{proposition}
The statement involves two sub-statements. First, it postulates the equality \eqref{eq:Ito isometry for simple processes}; secondly, it states that \(\int \vartheta\diff M\) is a square-integrable martingale starting at \(0\); hence, the terms in \eqref{eq:Ito isometry for simple processes} are finite. 
The proof of the martingale property, as well as the equality in \eqref{eq:Ito isometry for simple processes}, is proven by direct calculation (see \parencite[Lemma.~12.1.4]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications}).
The crucial idea is to elevate Proposition \ref{prop:Ito isometry for simple processes} to an isometry of normed spaces, where we understand the latter as a linear mapping between normed spaces under which the norms are preserved. An isometry is, in particular, injective. From our identification \eqref{eq:scalar product on H^2}, we have
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(\int_0^{\infty} \vartheta_t\diff M_t\right)^2\bigg]=\left(\int \vartheta_t\diff M_t,\int \vartheta_t\diff M_t\right)_{\mathcal{H}^2}.
\end{equation*}
Recall that by Lemma \ref{lemma:equivalence of H^p norm} the inner-product \((\cdot,\cdot)_{\mathcal{H}^2}\) induces a norm on \(\mathcal{H}^2\), which is equivalent to the one introduced in Definition \ref{def:H^p}. 
Thus, we want to understand Proposition \ref{prop:Ito isometry for simple processes} as the following statement. Let 
\begin{align}\label{eq:integral map as isometry}
     I:&L\to\mathcal{H}_0^2,\notag\\
     &\vartheta\mapsto \int \vartheta\diff M.
\end{align}
The map \(I\) defines a linear isometry between \(L\) and \((\mathcal{H}_0^2,(\cdot,\cdot)_{\mathcal{H}^2})\) for some appropriate normed vector space \(L\) of predictable processes. From \eqref{eq:Ito isometry for simple processes} the natural candidate is the following space for \(p=2\).
\begin{definition}\label{def:L^p(M)}
    Let \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^p\). We denote \(\mathcal{L}^p(M)\) as the space of predictable processes \(\vartheta\) such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^p(M)}=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta_t^2\diff[M]_t\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}\bigg]^{\frac{1}{p}}<\infty.
    \end{equation*}
    for \(p\in[1,\infty)\)
\end{definition}
As usual, we then define
\begin{equation*}
    L^p(M) = \mathcal{L}^p(M)/\sim,
\end{equation*}
where the equivalence relation is defined as  \(\vartheta\sim\eta\) if and only if \(\lVert\vartheta-\eta\rVert_{L^p(M)}=0\).
\begin{remark}\label{rem:L^2(M)}
In the case \(p=2\), the predictable quadratic variation process exists, and we have
\begin{equation*}
     \lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta_t^2\diff[M]_t\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta_t^2\diff\langle M\rangle_t\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}.
\end{equation*}
This is a consequence of
\([M]-\langle M\rangle\) being a local martingale (see \parencite[Lemma.~11.3.4.]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications}). 
\end{remark}

Hence, Proposition \ref{prop:Ito isometry for simple processes} now reads as the statement that the map \(I:L^2(M)\cap\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{H}^2_0\) given by \(\vartheta\mapsto \int \vartheta\diff M\) as defined in \eqref{eq:integral of simple processes} is an isometry of normed vector spaces. This important structural insight will enable us to extend the stochastic integral to all of \(L^2(M)\). Indeed, we will show that \(\mathcal{E}\) is a dense subset of \(L^2(M)\). The stochastic integral for \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\) and  \(\vartheta\in L^2(M)\) is then defined abstractly as the unique continuous extension of the linear, bounded map \eqref{eq:integral map as isometry} to \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}=L^2(M)\).

First, we make an interesting observation justifying the suggestive notation \(L^p(M)\) as some sort of \(L^p\)-space, at least in the case \(p=2\). For \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\) we define the following measure on the product measure space \(\left(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+,\mathcal{P}\right)\):
\begin{equation}\label{def:P_M measure}
    \mathbb{P}_M(A)=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\infty}1_A\diff\langle M\rangle_t\bigg],\quad\forall A\in\mathcal{P}.
\end{equation}
The following lemma assures that for \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\) the measure \(\mathbb{P}_M\) is finite. The proof uses the well-known Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality (BDG) for local martingales (see \parencite[Thm.~11.5.5.]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications}). As it is used repeatedly in this thesis, we recall it as the theorem.
\begin{theorem}
    For any \(1\leq p<\infty\), there exist constants \(c_p,C_p>0\) such that, for any local martingale \(M\) with \(M_0=0\),
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:BDG}
        c_p\mathbb{E}\big[[M]_{\infty}^{p/2}\big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\mathbb{E}\big[(M_{\infty}^*)^p\big]^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq C_p\mathbb{E}\big[[M]_{\infty}^{p/2}\big]^{\frac{1}{p}}.
    \end{equation}
\end{theorem}
For a proof, see \parencite[Thm.~11.5.5.]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications}.
\begin{lemma}\label{eq:P_M is finite measure}
    Suppose \(M\) is a local martingale starting at \(0\). Then \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\) if and only if \(\mathbb{E}[[M]_{\infty}]<\infty\)
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
 The statement is a simple observation of the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality for \(p=2\). There exist constants \(c_2,C_2>0\) such that 
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:BDG p=2}
     c_2\mathbb{E}\big[[M]_{\infty}\big]^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\mathbb{E}\big[(M^*_\infty)^2\big]^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq  C_2\mathbb{E}\big[[M]_{\infty}\big]^{\frac{1}{2}}.
 \end{equation}
 Observe that the middle term corresponds exactly to the \(\mathcal{H}^2\)-norm as we introduced it in Definition \ref{def:H^p}. The lemma is now straightforward. If \(M\) is a local martingale with \(M_0=0\) and \(\mathbb{E}[[M]_\infty]<\infty\), then by \eqref{eq:BDG p=2} 
 \begin{equation*}
     \lVert M\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^2}\leq C_2\mathbb{E}\big[[M]_{\infty}\big]^{\frac{1}{2}}<\infty.
 \end{equation*}
 By Lemma \ref{lemma:local martingale is a true martingale} is a true martingale with finite \(\mathcal{H}^2\)-norm and \(M_0=0\) by assumption. Hence in \(\mathcal{H}^2_0\). Conversely, assume \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\). Again by \eqref{eq:BDG p=2}.
 \begin{equation*}
     \mathbb{E}\big[[M]_{\infty}\big]^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\frac{1}{c_2}\mathbb{E}\big[(M^*_\infty)^2\big]^{\frac{1}{2}}=\frac{1}{c_2}\lVert M\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^2}<\infty.
 \end{equation*}
 Hence \(\mathbb{E}[[M]_{\infty}]<\infty\), which completes the proof.
\end{proof}
Hence, by Definition \ref{def:L^p(M)}, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:L^2(M) as Hilbert space}
    L^2(M) = L^2(\Omega\times \mathbb{R}_+,\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M),
\end{equation}
where \(\mathcal{P}\) is predictable, \(\sigma\)-algebra, that is the sub-\(\sigma\)-algebra of \(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}_+)\) generated by adapted, left-continuous processes. Remark \ref{rem:L^2(M)} now reads as the statement that 
the measures
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{P}_{[M]}(B):=\mathbb E\left[\int_0^{\infty} 1_B\,\diff [M]\right],
\qquad
\mathbb{P}_{\langle M\rangle}(B):=\mathbb E\left[\int_0^{\infty} 1_B\,\diff \langle M\rangle\right]
\end{equation*}
coincide on the predictable \(\sigma\)-algebra. In turn, this justifies the notation \(\mathbb{P}_M\) indifferent to whether \(\langle M\rangle\) or \([M]\) is used.


Equation \eqref{eq:L^2(M) as Hilbert space}  implies \(L^2(M)\) is a Hilbert space with inner product \((X,Y)_{L^2(M)}=\mathbb{E}[\int_0^{\infty}X Y\diff\langle M\rangle_t]\).
 
We turn to the proof that the processes in \(L^2(M)\) are approximated by elementary predictable processes. The proof will be based on an application of the functional version of the monotone class (see theorem \parencite[Thm.~7.4.1.]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications}), which crucially uses the following fact
\begin{equation*}
    \sigma(\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{P}. 
\end{equation*}
First, we show a simple but useful lemma.
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:sigma algebra of bounded functions}
    Let \(\mathcal{M}\) be a class of real-valued functions on \(\Omega\) that is stable under truncation, that is for every \(f\in\mathcal{M}\) and any \(n\in\mathbb{N}\) the truncated function \(-n\vee f\wedge n\in\mathcal{M}\). We denote by \(b\mathcal{M}\) the class of essentially bounded functions in \(\mathcal{M}\). Then
    \begin{equation*}
        \sigma(b\mathcal{M})=\sigma(\mathcal{M}).
    \end{equation*}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    The inclusion \(\sigma(b\mathcal{M})\subseteq\sigma(\mathcal{M})\) is clear. For the converse, let us consider \(X\in\mathcal{M}\) and define
    \begin{equation*}
        X^n = -n\vee X\wedge n\in b \mathcal{M}.
    \end{equation*}
    It is clear that \(X^n\to X\) pointwise as \(n\to \infty\), that is \(X^n(\omega,t)\to X(\omega,t)\) for all \((\omega,t)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+\). Because pointwise limits preserve measurability, \(X\) is \(\sigma(b\mathcal{M})\)-measurable as the pointwise limit of processes in \(b\mathcal{M}\). Because \(\sigma(\mathcal{M})\) is the smallest \(\sigma\)-algebra such that all \(X\in\mathcal{M}\) are measurable, the claim follows.
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
    We sometimes abuse language in saying that a process is bounded when we mean that it is essentially bounded. In our cases, we can always restrict ourselves to a full measure set on which the essentially bounded function/process is bounded. 
\end{remark}
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:elementary processes generated predictable sigma algebra}
    For \(\mathcal{E}\) as in Definition \ref{def:elementary predictable process} and \(\mathcal{P}\) denoting the predictable \(\sigma\)-algebra, we have that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \sigma(\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{P}.
    \end{equation*}
    \begin{proof}
        By definition, \(\mathcal{P}\) is generated by the family of adapted, left-continuous processes. Every element of \(\mathcal{E}\) is clearly adapted and left-continuous; hence, \(\sigma(\mathcal{E})\subseteq\mathcal{P}\) is clear. It remains to show that \(\mathcal{P}\subseteq\sigma(\mathcal{E})\). For this, we first show that every bounded, adapted left-continuous process is the limit of elements of \(\mathcal{E}^{\infty}\). By Lemma \ref{lemma:sigma algebra of bounded functions} the boundedness assumption is without loss of generality.
        
        
           Let \(X\) be a bounded, adapted, left-continuous process. We define
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:approximating adapted left-continuous process}
            X^n= X_01_{\left\{0\right\}}+\sum_{k=0}^\infty X_{k/n}1_{(k/n,(k+1)/n]}
        \end{equation}
        As \(X\) is adapted and bounded, \(X_{k/n}\) is \(\mathcal{F}_{k/n}\)-measurable and bounded and thus, \(X^n\in\mathcal{E}^{\infty}\). Then, for every fixed \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\), there exists a unique \(k(n)\in\mathbb{N}\) such that \(\frac{k(n)}{n}<t\leq \frac{k(n)+1}{n}\). Hence, \(X_t^n=X_{k(n)/n}\), thus \(X_t^n\) is \(\mathcal{F}_{k(n)/n}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_t\)-measurable and thus adapted. Furthermore, notice that \(\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{k(n)}{n}\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}|\frac{k(n)}{n}-t|+t\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}+t=t\) and \(\frac{k(n)}{n}<t\). Hence, \(k(n)\to t\) from below. Thus, for every \((\omega,t)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+,\;X^n_t(\omega)\to X_t(\omega)\) as \(n\to\infty\) by the left-continuity of \(X\). This implies 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{P}\subseteq\sigma(\mathcal{E}^{\infty}).
        \end{equation*}
        Indeed, every bounded, adapted, left-continuous process \(X\) is \(\sigma(\mathcal{E}^{\infty})\) as the point-wise limit of elements in \(\mathcal{E}^{\infty}\). But \(\mathcal{P}\) is the smallest such \(\sigma\)-algebra.



 Furthermore, every 
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:approximating adapted left-continuous processes 2}
     \vartheta=\varphi_01_{\left\{0\right\}}+\sum_{i= 0}^\infty \varphi_i1_{(t_i,t_{i+1}]}\in\mathcal{E}^{\infty}
 \end{equation}
 is clearly the pointwise limit of the processes
        \begin{equation*}\label{eq:approximating approximating-sequence of adapted left-continuous processes}
            \vartheta^{m}=\varphi_01_{\left\{0\right\}}+\sum_{k=0}^m\varphi^{i}1_{(t_k,t_{k+1}]}\in\mathcal{E},
        \end{equation*}
        for \(m\to\infty\).
 Again, as pointwise limits of measurable processes are measurable, we have
        \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{P}\subseteq\sigma(\mathcal{E}^{\infty})\subseteq\sigma(\mathcal{E}).
        \end{equation*}
        \end{proof}
\end{lemma}
Taking a closer look, we see that the previous proof can be used to show the following useful corollary.
\begin{corollary}
    Let \(\mathcal{A}=\{A\times(s,t], A\in\mathcal{F}_s, s<t\in\mathbb{R}_+\}\cup \{A\times \{0\}, A\in\mathcal{F}_0\}\). Then \(\sigma(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{P}\).
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    Every indicator function \(1_B=1_A1_{(s,t]}\) with \(B\in\mathcal{A}\) is adapted and left-continuous; hence, it is predictable. The converse inclusion follows from the very same argument as in Lemma \ref{lemma:elementary processes generated predictable sigma algebra}. Indeed, every bounded, adapted left-continuous process \(X\) is the limit of processes \(X^n\) of the form \eqref{eq:approximating adapted left-continuous process}. Every \(X^n\) is the limit of processes \(X^n_m\) of the form \eqref{eq:approximating adapted left-continuous processes 2}. The latter is clearly seen to be \(\sigma(\mathcal{A})\)-measurable as \(X_{k/n}\) is \(\mathcal{F}_{k/n}\)-measurable. Indeed, for \(B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})\)
    \begin{align*}
    (X_{\frac{k}{n}}1_{(\frac{k}{n},\frac{k}{n}+\frac{1}{n}]})^{-1}(B)=
    \begin{cases}
        X_{\frac{k}{n}}^{-1}(B)\times (\frac{k}{n},\frac{k}{n}+\frac{1}{n}] \text{ if }0\notin B\\
        X_{\frac{k}{n}}^{-1}(B)\times (\frac{k}{n},\frac{k}{n}+\frac{1}{n}]\cup \Omega\times \mathbb{R}/(\frac{k}{n},\frac{k}{n}+\frac{1}{n}]\text{ if }0\in B
    \end{cases}
    \end{align*}
    but \( \Omega\times \mathbb{R}/(\frac{k}{n},\frac{k}{n}+\frac{1}{n}]=\big(\Omega\times(\frac{k}{n},\frac{k}{n}+\frac{1}{n}]\big)^c\in\sigma(\mathcal{A})\). In every case
    \((X_{\frac{k}{n}}1_{(\frac{k}{n},\frac{k}{n}+\frac{1}{n}]})^{-1}(B)\in\sigma(\mathcal{A})\).
    Hence, \(X^n_m\) is \(\sigma(\mathcal{A})\)-measurable as a finite sum of measurable processes, and \(X^n\) is \(\sigma(\mathcal{A})\)-measurable as a pointwise limit of measurable processes; hence, so is \(X\).
\end{proof}
We can finally state and prove the density of elementary processes in \(L^2(M)\).
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:density of E in L2(M) via monotone class}
    Let \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\) and \(\mathcal{E}\) be defined in Definition \ref{def:elementary predictable process}. Then \(\mathcal{E}\) is dense in \(L^2(M)\).
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    The proof is based on the functional version of the monotone class theorem and dominated convergence. By Lemma \ref{lemma:sigma algebra of bounded functions} and Lemma \ref{lemma:elementary processes generated predictable sigma algebra} we have 
    \begin{equation*}
        \sigma(\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{P}.
    \end{equation*}
    Let 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{H}=\left\{ \vartheta\in b\mathcal{P}:\vartheta\in L^2(M),\forall \epsilon>0, \exists \vartheta_{\epsilon}\in \mathcal{E}\text{ with }\lVert \vartheta_{\epsilon}-\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}<\epsilon\right\},
    \end{equation*}
    where \(b\mathcal{P}\) denotes the set of processes that are essentially bounded and predictable.

    
      Suppose we were able to show that \(\mathcal{H}\) is a monotone class containing the algebra of functions \(\mathcal{E}\). We would conclude by the monotone class theorem  that \(\mathcal{H}\) contains all bounded \(\mathcal{P}\)-measurable processes, which would show that \(\mathcal{E}\) is dense in \(bL^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M)\). To get density in \(L^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M)\) we observe that for \(\vartheta\in L^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M)\) we might consider, without loss of generality, the bounded process
    \begin{equation*}
        \vartheta^n=-n\vee \vartheta\wedge n\in bL^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M).
    \end{equation*}
 Clearly, \(\vartheta^n\mapsto \vartheta\) pointwise and \(|\vartheta^n|\leq|\vartheta|\) hence \(|\vartheta^n-\vartheta|\leq 2|\vartheta|\) with \(\vartheta\in L^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M)\) by assumption. Thus, by dominated convergence
    \begin{equation*}
        \vartheta^n\overset{L^2(M)}{\to}\vartheta.
    \end{equation*}
    In particular, for every \(\epsilon>0\) there exists \(n\) large enough such that
    \begin{equation*}
        \lVert \vartheta^n-\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}<\frac{\epsilon}{2}.
    \end{equation*}
    Furthermore, if \(\mathcal{E}\) is dense in \(bL^2(M)\) there exists a sequence \((\xi^n_m)_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathcal{E}\) such that 
    \begin{equation*}
         \xi^n_m\overset{L^2(M)}{\to}\vartheta^n.
    \end{equation*}
    Hence for every \(\epsilon>0\) there is \(m\) big enough such that
    \begin{equation*}
        \lVert \xi^n_m-\vartheta^n\rVert_{L^2(M)}<\frac{\epsilon}{2}.
    \end{equation*}
    Hence, for these choices for \(n,m\)
    \begin{equation*}
        \lVert \xi^n_m-\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}\leq\lVert \xi^n_m-\vartheta^n\rVert_{L^2(M)}+\lVert \vartheta^n-\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}<\epsilon.
    \end{equation*}
    In other words, we just argued that \(bL^2(M)\) is dense in \(L^2(M)\); hence
    \begin{equation*}
        \overline{\mathcal{E}}^{L^2(M)}=\overline{bL^2(M)}^{L^2(M)}=L^2(M).
    \end{equation*}
 It remains to show that the application of the monotone class theorem is justified. 

 
      First, we need to show that \(\mathcal{H}\) contains \(\mathcal{E}\). Clearly, processes in \(\mathcal{E}\) are predictable. Furthermore, as \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\), we have by \eqref{eq:P_M is finite measure} that \([M]_\infty\) is integrable. Hence, as elements in \(\mathcal{E}\) are bounded, uniformly in \((\omega, t)\) on \(\mathcal{N}^c\times\mathbb{R}_+\), where \(\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{N}]=0\) we have for \(\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}\)
    \begin{equation*}
        \lVert\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}^2=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta_t^2\diff [M]_t\bigg]\lesssim\mathbb{E}\big[[M_\infty]\big]<\infty.
    \end{equation*}
    Hence, \(\mathcal{H}\) contains \(\mathcal{E}\). Notice, that this crucially uses the integrability of \([M]_\infty\) which, according to \eqref{eq:P_M is finite measure} is the case if and only if \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\). In particular, it is not enough to require \(M\in\mathcal{H}_{0,\rm{loc}}^2\).  
    
    What is more, \(\mathcal{H}\) contains the constant processes. Indeed, let \(c\in\mathbb{R}\) and  \(X_t(\omega)=c\) for all \((\omega,t)\in\Omega\times \mathbb{R}_+\) and consider \(X^n=c1_{\left\{0\right\}}+c1_{(0,n]}\). Then \(X^n\in \mathcal{E}\) and \(X^n\to X\) pointwise. Furthermore, as the measure \(\mathbb{P}_M\) is finite for \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\) we have that the constant process equal to \(c^2\) is in \(L^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M)\). It is then a direct application of dominated convergence that yields 
    \begin{equation*}
        X^n\overset{L^2(M)}{\to}X.
    \end{equation*}
In particular, for every \(\epsilon>0\) there exists \(n\) such that 
\begin{equation*}
    \lVert X^n-c\rVert_{L^2(M)}<\epsilon.
\end{equation*}
Clearly, \(\mathcal{H}\) forms a vector space of functions (on \(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+\)). Indeed, if \(\vartheta_1,\vartheta_2\in\mathcal{H}\), for every \(\epsilon>0\) there exist \(\vartheta^1_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}},\vartheta^2_{\frac{\epsilon}{2|\alpha|}}\in\mathcal{E}\) such that, for all \(\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\):
    \begin{equation*}
        \big\lVert \vartheta^1+\alpha \vartheta^2- \vartheta^1_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}-\alpha \vartheta^2_{\frac{\epsilon}{2|\alpha|}} \big\rVert_{L^2(M)}\leq \epsilon,
    \end{equation*}
    due to the triangle inequality.
 It remains to show that \(\mathcal{H}\) is closed under bounded monotone limits. Hence, let \(0<\vartheta_0<\vartheta_1<\dots\) with \(\vartheta_j\in\mathcal{H}\) such that \(\lim_{n\to\infty}\vartheta_n = \vartheta\) pointwise, with \(\vartheta\) bounded. Fix \(\epsilon>0\). Then, due to dominated convergence 
    \begin{equation*}
        \lim_{n\to\infty}\lVert \vartheta-\vartheta_n\rVert_{L^2(M)}=0.
    \end{equation*}
    Hence, there exists \(n\in\mathbb{N}\) large enough such that \(\lVert \vartheta-\vartheta_n\rVert_{L^2(M)}<\epsilon/2\). Now \(\vartheta_n\in\mathcal{H}\); hence, there exists \(\vartheta^n_{\epsilon/2}\in \mathcal{E}\) such that \(\lVert \vartheta_n-\vartheta^n_{\epsilon/2}\rVert_{L^2(M)}<\epsilon/2\). By the triangle inequality, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \lVert \vartheta-\vartheta^n_{\epsilon/2}\rVert_{L^2(M)}\leq\lVert \vartheta-\vartheta_n\rVert_{L^2(M)}+\lVert \vartheta_n-\vartheta^n_{\epsilon/2}\rVert_{L^2(M)}<\epsilon.
    \end{equation}
 Hence, we conclude by the monotone class theorem. 
\end{proof}
One might wonder whether the latter can be generalized to general \(p\in[1,\infty)\). The proof given does not hold for the general case without modification. Indeed, the main reason for giving the proof in the case \(p=2\) is that the proof remains tractable, as the repeated application of dominated convergence can be done on the joint space \((\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+)\), as \(L^2(M)\simeq L^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P_M})\) forms a Hilbert space. For \(p\neq 2\), however, there is no such \(L^p\)-identification. We need to show how to adapt the dominated convergence arguments in the previous proof. The rest is analogous and does not depend on the choice of \(p\). Let's take a look at the place we used dominated convergence (DCT) in the proof of Proposition \ref{prop:density of E in L2(M) via monotone class}. In the following we refer to the case where we allow for and \(p\in[1,\infty)\) as the general case. For the following assume \(M\in\mathcal{H}^p_0\).


The first time we used DCT was to argue that it is enough to show the claim for bounded functions in \(L^2(M)\). In the general case, we argue by successive application of DCT. 
Again, assume the claim to be shown for bounded processes in \(L^p(M)\). Let \(\vartheta\in L^p(M)\) and consider \(\vartheta^n=-n\vee \vartheta\wedge n\in bL^p(M)\). Again, it is clear that \(\vartheta^n\to\vartheta\) pointwise with \(|\vartheta^n-\vartheta|\leq 2|\vartheta|\). First notice, that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:integral is integrable}
    \mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\int_0^{\infty}|\vartheta|_s^2\diff [M]_s\Big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\Big]<\infty\implies\int_0^{\infty}|\vartheta|_s^2\diff [M]_s<\infty,\;\;\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}
\end{equation}
 Furthermore, observe that \([M]\) is a non-negative, non-decreasing process, hence for every \(\omega\), \(\diff [M](\omega)\) defines a Lebesgue–Stieltjes measure. As \(M\in\mathcal{H}^p\), the BDG inequalities imply that \(\diff [M]\) is, almost surely, a finite measure. 
 Indeed
 \begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{E}\big[[M]^{\frac{p}{2}}\big]\leq \frac{1}{c_p}\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}|M|^p\Big]=\lVert M\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^p}<\infty,
\end{equation*}
which implies that \([M]<\infty\) almost surely. 
Hence outside of a \(\mathbb{P}\)-nullset  \(\mathcal{N}\) we can apply DCT for every \(\omega\), that is pathwise, with the domination of \(\vartheta^2\in L^1(\diff [M](\omega)) \) to obtain
\begin{equation*}
    \lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}|\vartheta_s^n(\omega)-\vartheta_s(\omega)|^2\diff [M]_s(\omega)=0.
\end{equation*}
Next, as \([M]\) is non-decreasing and \(|\vartheta_s^n(\omega)-\vartheta(\omega)|_s^2>0\) for all \(\omega\in\Omega\),
\(\big(\int_0^{\infty}|\vartheta_s^n-\vartheta|_s^2\diff [M]_s\big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\) is dominated by \(\big(\int_0^{\infty}|\vartheta|_s^2\diff [M]_s\big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\) which is in \(L^1(\mathbb{P})\) by the observation made in \eqref{eq:integral is integrable}. The same bounds are clearly true for the \(\frac{p}{2}\)-power of the respective terms. Hence, again by DCT we conclude.
\begin{equation*}
    \lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\int_0^{\infty}|\vartheta^n_s-\vartheta_s|^2\diff[M]_s\Big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\Big]=0
\end{equation*}
Together, we get
\begin{equation*}
    \vartheta^n\overset{L^p(M)}{\to}\vartheta.
\end{equation*}
For the assertion that the constant functions are in
\begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{H}^{(p)}=\left\{ \vartheta\in b\mathcal{P}:\vartheta\in L^p(M),\forall \epsilon>0, \exists \vartheta_{\epsilon}\in \mathcal{E}\text{ with }\lVert \vartheta_{\epsilon}-\vartheta\rVert_{L^p(M)}<\epsilon\right\}
\end{equation*}
we observe that the constant process \(X\equiv c\in L^p(M)\). Indeed 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(\int_0^{\infty}c^2\diff [M]\bigg)^{\frac{p}{2}}\bigg]=|c|^{p}\mathbb{E}\Big[[M]_\infty^{\frac{p}{2}}\Big]<\infty,
\end{equation*}
where the finiteness of the last term is due to the BDG inequalities. Hence, setting  \(X^n=c1_{\left\{0\right\}}+c1_{(0,n]}\), with the obvious bound
\begin{equation*}
    |X^n|\leq |c|
\end{equation*}
we argue by the same successive DCT argument as before that 
\begin{equation*}
    X^n\overset{L^p(M)}{\to}c.
\end{equation*}
In particular this shows that for \(M\in\mathcal{H}^p_0\) we have \(\mathcal{E}\subseteq L^p(M)\) because elements in \(\mathcal{E}\) are uniformly bounded in \((\omega,t)\in \mathcal{N}^c\times\mathbb{R}_+\) where \(\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{N}]=0\) (recall that strictly speaking we only required \(\varphi_i\) in the Definition \ref{def:elementary predictable process} to be essentially bounded.


 The stability under bounded monotone limits follows analogously. Hence, we get the following proposition:
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:density of E in Lp(M) via monotone class}
    Let \(M\in\mathcal{H}^p_0\) for \(p\in[1,\infty)\) and \(\mathcal{E}\) as defined in Definition \ref{def:elementary predictable process}. Then \(\mathcal{E}\) is dense in \(L^p(M)\).
\end{proposition}

Finally, having proven that the elementary processes are dense in \(L^2(M)\), we can use the isometry property for the map \eqref{eq:integral map as isometry} to extend the integral to a bounded linear operator in \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{L^2(M)}=L^2(M)\) by the standard extension theorem (see \parencite[Prop.~5.4,Ch.I]{SteinShakarchi2005RealAnalysis}) (we actually only need the ambient space of the codomain, \(\mathcal{H}^2_0\) in our case, to be complete). 

This uses, in particular, that \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\) and \(L^2(M)\) are normed vector spaces and \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\) is complete. Furthermore we use that the linear isometry in \eqref{eq:Ito isometry for simple processes} is, by definition, bounded and hence continuous as a linear operator on \(L^2(M)\). We can thus define the stochastic integral for \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\) and \(\vartheta\in L^2(M)\).
\begin{definition}\label{def:square-integrable stochastic integral}
    Let \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{0}\). We define the stochastic integral
    \begin{align*}
        I_X:&L^2(M)\to\mathcal{H}^2_{0}\\
        &\vartheta\mapsto\int \vartheta\diff M
    \end{align*}
    as the unique continuous extension of the map in Proposition \ref{prop:Ito isometry for simple processes} to \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{L^2(M)}=L^2(M)\).  Furthermore we define
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:square integrable stochastic integral at time t}
            \int_0^t\vartheta_s\diff M_s\coloneqq \int_{(0,t]}\vartheta_s\diff M_s\;\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+=\bigg( \int_0^\cdot\vartheta_s\diff M_s\bigg)_{t}
    \end{equation}
    and \(\int_0^\infty\vartheta_s\diff M_s=\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{(0,t]}\vartheta_s\diff M_s\) if it exists.
\end{definition}
Observe, that as the stochastic integral in Definition \ref{def:square-integrable stochastic integral} is in \(\mathcal{H}^2_0\) by construction, it is in particular a uniformly integrable martingale, hence its almost sure limit 
\begin{equation*}
    \int_0^\infty\vartheta_s\diff M_s=\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{(0,t]}\vartheta_s\diff M_s
\end{equation*}
exists. The definition \eqref{eq:square integrable stochastic integral at time t} imposes \(( \int_0^\cdot\vartheta_s\diff M_s)_{0}=0\). This is in accordance with \eqref{eq:stochastic integral for elementary process} In the current case \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\) one could also take a more general approach, and at the initial value \(\vartheta^0M_0=( \int_0^\cdot\vartheta_s\diff M_s)_{0}=(\int_{[0,\cdot]}\vartheta_s\diff M_s)_0\) without harm. Nevertheless, as we want to model the cumulative gains process \(G_{t}(\vartheta)\) of \eqref{eq:value process} we want the stochastic integrals to start at \(0\) by definition. Regarding the mean-variance hedging problem \eqref{eq:mean squared hedging} one could also take a different convention, allowing for the stochastic integral to have non-zero initial value and absorb the initial value into \(V_0\)
\begin{equation*}
 V_0+\int_{[0,\infty)}\vartheta_s\diff M_s=V_0+\vartheta_0M_0+\int_{(0,\infty)}\vartheta_s\diff M_s=\widetilde{V_0}+\int_{(0,\infty)}\vartheta_s\diff M_s   
\end{equation*}
Eventually, this leads us to consider the same objects \(=(\int_{(0,t]}\vartheta_s\diff M_s)_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\). 



The isometry property of the integral in Theorem \ref{def:square-integrable stochastic integral} is generally known as Itô's isometry. 
\begin{theorem}[Itô Isometry]\label{thm:Ito Isometry}
    Let \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\); then for \(\vartheta\in L^2(M)\)
    \begin{equation*}
        \lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}^2=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta_t^2\diff \langle M\rangle_t\bigg]=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta_t\diff M_t\right)^2\bigg].
    \end{equation*}
\end{theorem}
According to \parencite[Thm.~2.5.]{Schweizer2025BrownianMotionStochasticCalculus}, it even holds when \(M\) is only locally in \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\). 
We will refer to the setting in Definition \ref{def:square-integrable stochastic integral} as the \textit{square-integrable martingale setting}. We provide a useful characterization of the integral in Definition \ref{def:square-integrable stochastic integral}
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:characterisation of stochastic integral}
    Let \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\) and \(\vartheta\in L^2(M)\). The stochastic integral \(\vartheta\bullet M\) is the unique process in \(\mathcal{H}^2_0\) such that, for every \(N\in\mathcal{H}^2\),
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:Schweizer integral characterization}
        \langle \vartheta\bullet M,N\rangle=\int \vartheta\diff\langle M,N\rangle,\quad N\in\mathcal{H}_0^2.
    \end{equation}
\end{proposition}
Proposition \ref{prop:characterisation of stochastic integral} might be used as an alternative definition of the stochastic integral of \(\vartheta\in L^2(M)\) and \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\). In particular, one can show Proposition \ref{prop:characterisation of stochastic integral} for \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{0,\rm{loc}}\). This shows that one can construct the stochastic integral \(\int \vartheta\diff M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\) for \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{0,\rm{loc}}\) and \(\vartheta\in L^2(M)\). This extension is not clear from our construction, as we crucially used \(\mathcal{E}\subset L^2(M)\), which required the integrability of \([M]_{\infty}\), which is the case if and only if \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\). 
For the proof of Proposition \ref{prop:characterisation of stochastic integral}, we refer to \parencite[Thm.~2.5.]{Schweizer2025BrownianMotionStochasticCalculus}. Moreover, \parencite[sec.~4.2.]{Schweizer2025BrownianMotionStochasticCalculus} shows that the Itô-isometry also holds for the integral \(\int \vartheta\diff M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\) for \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{0,\rm{loc}}\) and \(\vartheta\in L^2(M)\).


Returning to our construction of the integral for \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\) and \(\vartheta\in L^2(M)\) one can  extend the integral to integrators in \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{0,\rm{loc}}\) and integrands in \(\vartheta\in L^2_{\rm{loc}}(M)\), where the latter is defined to be the class of predictable processes \(\vartheta\) such that there exists a localizing sequence \((\tau_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) for which \(\vartheta1_{(0,\tau_n]}\) is in \(L^2(M)\). 

\begin{definition}\label{def:L^p(M)_loc}
 For \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{0,\rm{loc}}\), the space \(L_{\rm{loc}}^2(M)\) consists of predictable processes \(\vartheta\) for which there is  a sequence of stopping times \(\tau_n\nearrow\infty\) almost surely, such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\tau_n}\vartheta_t^2\diff \langle M\rangle_t\bigg]<\infty,\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.
    \end{equation*}
\end{definition}
In particular, we have that for \(M\in\mathcal{H}_{0,\rm{loc}}^2\), \(\vartheta\in L^2_{\rm{loc}}(M)\) is equivalent to \(\vartheta1_{(0,\tau_n]}\in L^2(M)\) for each \(n\in\mathbb{N}\), and is equivalent to \(\vartheta\in L^2(M^{\tau_n})\) for each \(n\in\mathbb{N}\).



The extension of Definition \ref{def:square-integrable stochastic integral} for the localized objects is then done by the usual "stitching together" procedure of localization. We define 
\begin{equation}\label{def:piece of local stochastic integral}
    I_M(\vartheta)^{(k)}\coloneqq \vartheta1_{(0,\tau_k]}\bullet M^{\tau_k},\quad \text{ on } (0,\tau_k] \text{ and } I_M(\vartheta)_0=0 ,
\end{equation}
where \((\tau_k)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\) is a common localizing sequence of \(\vartheta\) and \(M\). We need to check that \eqref{def:piece of local stochastic integral} is consistent and well defined. For the following consideration, we restrict ourselves to \(\mathcal{N}^c\), where \(\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{N}]=0\) on which \(\lim_{k\to\infty}\tau_k=\infty\). We might choose \(\tau_{k-1}\leq\tau_{k}\) for every \(k\in\mathbb{N}\) on \(\mathcal{N}^c\). Consistency means, in this context,
\begin{equation*}
    I_M(\vartheta)^{(k)}=I_M(\vartheta)^{(k-1)}\text{ on } (0,\tau_{k-1}].
\end{equation*}
where \((0,\tau_{k-1}]\subseteq(0,\tau_{k}]\)
for every \(k\in\mathbb{N}\) as \(\tau_{k-1}\leq \tau_{k}\). To see this, recall the well-known property of the stochastic integral of Definition \ref{def:square-integrable stochastic integral}.
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:stopping stochastic integrals}
    Let \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\), \(\tau\) a stopping time and \(\vartheta\in L^2(M)\). Then
\begin{equation*}
    (\vartheta\bullet M)^{\tau}=\vartheta\bullet(M)^{\tau}=\vartheta1_{(0,\tau]}\bullet M.
\end{equation*}
\end{lemma}
Hence
\begin{equation*}
\big(I_M(\vartheta)^{(k)}_t\big)^{\tau_{k-1}}=\big(\vartheta1_{(0,\tau_k]}\bullet M^{\tau_k}\big)^{\tau_{k-1}}=\vartheta1_{(0,\tau_k]\cap(0,\tau_{k-1}]}\bullet M^{\tau_k}=I_M(\vartheta)^{(k-1)}_t.
\end{equation*}
which shows consistency. 



The well-definedness refers to the fact that one needs to check that \(\vartheta1_{(0,\tau_k]}\bullet M^{\tau_k}\) is well-defined in the sense of the integrals we understand at this point, that is, the integral of Definition \ref{def:square-integrable stochastic integral}. Concretely, we need to show that 
\begin{equation*}
    \vartheta1_{(0,\tau_k]}\in L^2(M^{\tau_k}).
\end{equation*} 
But this is obvious by Definition \ref{def:L^p(M)_loc}.
Furthermore, as \(\tau_k\nearrow\infty\), \((0,\tau_k]\nearrow\Omega/\mathcal{N}\times\mathbb{R}_+\), we can thus define the stochastic integral for \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{0,\rm{loc}}\) and \(\vartheta\in L^2_{\rm{loc}}(M)\) by stitching together the local construction \ref{def:piece of local stochastic integral}.
\begin{align}\label{def:local sotchastic integral}
    I_M(\vartheta)_t(\omega)=
    \begin{cases}
        I_M(\vartheta)^{(1)}1_{(0,\tau_1]}+\sum_{k\geq 1}I_M(\vartheta)^{(k+1)}_t(\omega)1_{(\tau_k(\omega)<t\leq\tau_{k+1}(\omega)]}\text{ for } t\in\mathbb{R}_+/\left\{0\right\}\\
        0 \text{ for } t=0
    \end{cases}
\end{align}
for \(\omega\in\mathcal{N}^c\). We might define \(I_M(\vartheta)\) on all of \(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+\) by setting \(I_M(\vartheta)_t(\omega)=0\) for all \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\) and \(\omega\in\mathcal{N}\).
For a characterization of this integral analogous to Proposition \ref{prop:characterisation of stochastic integral} we refer to \parencite[Prop.~2.20]{Schweizer2025BrownianMotionStochasticCalculus}. 

\begin{remark}\label{rem:local integrator global integrand yields global integral}
The stochastic integral for the local objects, as defined in \eqref{def:local sotchastic integral}, is itself only a locally square-integrable local martingale. 
   For \(M\in\mathcal{H}_{0,\rm{loc}}^2\) and \(\vartheta\in L^2(M)\) the respective stochastic integral is still in \(\mathcal{H}^2_0\) \parencite[Thm.~2.5.]{Schweizer2025BrownianMotionStochasticCalculus}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Kunita–Watanabe Decomposition}\label{sec:Kunita–Watanabe Decomposition}
Before turning to stochastic integration with respect to classical semimartingales, we take advantage of the reader's current familiarity with the square-integrable setup to introduce and state the celebrated Kunita Watanabe decomposition, which shall play a central role in the solution theory for the mean-variance hedging problem for square-integrable martingale price processes. For this, we will need a stronger notion of orthogonality on \(\mathcal{H}^2_0\) than the one induced by the inner product structure. The fact that \(\mathcal{H}^2_0\) is a space of uniformly integrable martingales leads to a richer geometry than the mere Hilbert space structure would suggest.
\begin{definition}\label{def:stong orthogonality}
    Let \(M,N\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\). We say that \(M\) is strongly orthogonal to \(N\) and vice versa if their product \(MN\) is a martingale. In this case, we denote \(M\perp N\).
\end{definition}
In particular, for \(M\perp N\),
\begin{equation*}
    (M,N)_{\mathcal{H}^2}=\mathbb{E}[M_{\infty}N_{\infty}]=\mathbb{E}[M_0N_0]=0.
\end{equation*}
Hence, strong orthogonality implies weak orthogonality of \(M,N\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\). 
Furthermore, we have that \(M\perp N\iff \langle M,N\rangle=0\) ( see \parencite[Rem.~11.2.5.]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications}).



As explained in Remark \ref{rem:if G_T closed then mean variance hedging is projection onto closed hilbert subspace}, in the context of mean-variance hedging, given a fixed price process \(X\), we are especially interested in studying the strategies for which the gains process evaluated at the final time is again square-integrable; in our notation \(\Theta\subseteq\Theta^{(2)}\). In the square-integrable martingale setting, we provided a partial answer affirming that for \(X\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\), strategies in \(L^2(M)\) fulfill this requirement. More precisely, we are interested in such strategies as they allow us to consider the mean-variance hedging results \eqref{eq:mean squared hedging} as a projection problem of the liability \(H\) onto the \(L^2\)-subspace \(\mathbb{R}+G_\infty(\Theta)\). In the best case, \(G_\infty(\Theta)\) is closed in \(L^2\); hence, we know that there exists an optimal element in \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) such that no other element in \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) is closer to \(H\) with respect to the \(L^2\) distance. For \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\) it turns out that \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M))\) is closed in \(L^2\). In other words, every \(H\in L^2(\mathcal{F}_T)\) can be decomposed into \(H=H_{\parallel}+H_{\perp}\) where \(H_{\parallel}\in G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) and  \(H_{\perp}\) such that \((Z,H_{\perp})_{L^2}=0\) for all \(Z\in G_\infty(\Theta)\). The astonishing fact is that this decomposition can be done with respect to a stronger notion of orthogonality, namely the one introduced in Definition \ref{def:stong orthogonality}. This turns out to be a deep fact about the geometry of the martingale space \(\mathcal{H}^2_0\) and is deeply related to the study of \textit{stable subspaces}. The decomposition of \(H\) into \(H_{\parallel}\in G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) and some strongly orthogonal part yields the celebrated Kunita–Watanabe decomposition. We state the most important results leading up to these results.. In particular, the following objects play a key role.

\begin{definition}\label{def:stable subspace}
    A \textit{stable} subspace of \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\) is a closed subspace \(\mathcal{A}\) of \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\) which is stable under stopping: For every \(M\in\mathcal{A}\) and every stopping time \(\tau\), the stopped process \(M^{\tau}\) is in \(\mathcal{A}\).
\end{definition}
The fact that the following space of integrals constitutes an example of a stable subspace is then the key observation of this section.
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:stochastic integrals as stable subspace}
    For every \(M\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^2\) the space 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{I}^2(M)=\left\{\int \vartheta\diff M:\vartheta\in L^2(M)\right\}
    \end{equation*}
    is a stable subspace of \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\). 
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    The closedness of \( \mathcal{I}^2(M)\) in \(\mathcal{H}^2_0\) follows directly from Theorem \ref{thm:Ito Isometry} as \( \mathcal{I}^2(M)\) is the isometric image under the map \(\vartheta\to \vartheta\bullet M\) of the Hilbert space \(L^2(M)\). Stability under stopping follows from Lemma \ref{lemma:stopping stochastic integrals}. Indeed, let \(\tau\) be a stopping time, \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\), \(\vartheta\in L^2(M)\), and \(N=\vartheta\bullet M\). Then, Lemma \ref{lemma:stopping stochastic integrals} asserts that \(\vartheta1_{(0,\tau]}\in L^2(M)\) and \((\vartheta\bullet M)^{\tau}=\vartheta1_{(0,\tau]}\bullet M\).  Hence, \((\vartheta\bullet M)^{\tau}\in \mathcal{I}^2(M)\), which shows that \(\mathcal{I}^2(M)\) is stable under stopping. 
\end{proof}
In particular, this implies that  
\begin{equation}\label{eq:closedness of vartheta bullet M_T in L^2}
     G_{\infty}\big(L^2(M)\big)=\left\{\int_0^{\infty} \vartheta\diff M:\vartheta\in L^2(M)\right\}
\end{equation}
is a closed subspace of \(L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty})\). Indeed, notice that under the identification discussed in Lemma \ref{lemma:H^p identification},
Theorem \ref{thm:Ito Isometry} states an isometry of Hilbert-spaces: \(L^2(M)\to L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty})\). In particular, \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M))\) is closed as the isometric image of the complete space \(L^2(M)\) in \(L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty})\). The following Proposition shows the fundamental importance of a stable subspace in understanding the geometry of \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\). 
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:weak and strong stable subspaces}
    Suppose \(\mathcal{A}\) is a stable subspace of \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\) and denote by 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{A}^{\Delta}=\left\{N\in\mathcal{H}_0^2:(N,M)_{\mathcal{H}^2}=0,\quad\forall M\in\mathcal{A}\right\}
    \end{equation*}
    the weakly orthogonal complement of \(\mathcal{A}\) in \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\). Then \(\mathcal{A}^{\Delta}\) is also strongly orthogonal to \(\mathcal{A}\) and a stable subspace of \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\). 
\end{proposition}
For the proof, we refer to \parencite[Prop.~5.5.]{Schweizer2025BrownianMotionStochasticCalculus}
Note that the latter is conceptually astonishing. Weak orthogonality of two processes only assures \(L^2\)-orthogonality of their terminal values. It says nothing about the relation between the processes overall. Nevertheless, the weak orthogonal complement of stable subspaces is, in particular, strongly orthogonal.
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:KW}
    Fix \(M\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^2\). Then every \(N\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^2\) can be uniquely written as 
    \begin{equation*}
        N = \int \vartheta\diff M +L
    \end{equation*}
    for some unique \(\vartheta\in L^2(M)\) and some \(L\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^2\) with \(L\perp M\). Furthermore, \(\vartheta=\frac{\diff \langle M,N\rangle}{\diff \langle M\rangle}\) is a predictable density of \(\langle M,N\rangle\) with respect to \(\langle M\rangle\). 
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
    Assume that \(M,N\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\). Let \(\hat{N}\) be the weakly orthogonal projection in \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\) onto the closed subspace \(\mathcal{I}^2(M)\). By the Hilbert projection theorem, there exists \(\vartheta\in L^2(M)\) such that \(\hat{N}=\int \vartheta\diff M\). Consider
    \begin{equation*}
        L\coloneqq N-\hat{N}
    \end{equation*}
    such that \(L\) is weakly orthogonal to \(\mathcal{I}^2(M)\). By Lemma \ref{lemma:stochastic integrals as stable subspace} \(\mathcal{I}^2(M)\), it follows that \(\mathcal{I}^2(M)\) is a stable subspace. Hence, by Proposition \ref{prop:weak and strong stable subspaces}, \(L\) is also strongly orthogonal to \(\mathcal{I}^2(M)\) and, in particular, to \(M\) because the constant process \(1\) is in \(L^2(M)\) (this uses that \(\mathbb{P}_M\) is a finite measure for \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\)). In particular, \(\langle M,L\rangle=0\). Hence 
    \begin{equation*}
        \langle M,N\rangle =\langle M,\int \vartheta\diff M\rangle+\langle M,L\rangle =\int \vartheta\diff \langle M,M\rangle,
    \end{equation*}
    which shows that \(\vartheta=\frac{\diff \langle M,N\rangle}{\diff \langle M\rangle}\). Uniqueness follows from the uniqueness of the Hilbert projection, asserting that for a given \(N\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\) its projection \(\hat{N}\) onto \(\mathcal{I}^2(M)\) is unique. Hence, so is \(L=N-\hat{N}\). Furthermore, by the Itô-isometry, the map
    \begin{align*}
        I:&L^2(M)\to\mathcal{H}^2_0
        \\&\vartheta\mapsto \int \vartheta\diff M
    \end{align*}
    is an isometry, and thus in particular injective. Hence if \(\hat{N}\in \mathcal{I}^2(M)\) is unique, so is the corresponding element \(\hat{\vartheta}\in L^2(M)\).
\end{proof}
We have already used, at multiple instances, that one can identify a random variable \(Z\) with a martingale by setting \(M_t\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[Z|\mathcal{F}_t]\). This allows us to give a decomposition theorem for \(L^2\) random variables as a corollary of Theorem \ref{thm:KW}. 
\begin{corollary}\label{cor:random variable level Kunita Watanabe}
    Fix \(M\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^2\). Then every random variable \(F\in L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty},\mathbb{P})\) can be uniquely written as 
    \begin{equation*}
        F = \mathbb{E}[F|\mathcal{F}_0]+\int_0^{\infty}H_t\diff M_t+L_{\infty}
    \end{equation*}
    for some \(H\in L^2(M)\) and some \(L\in \mathcal{H}_{0}^2\) such that \(L\perp M\).
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    We apply Theorem \ref{thm:KW} to the process \((V_t)_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\) with \(V_t\coloneqq \mathbb
    E[F|\mathcal{F}_t]- E[F|\mathcal{F}_0]\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\). We get 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:KW of V^F}
        V_t=\int_0^t H\diff M+L_t,\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+.
    \end{equation}
    Noticing that \(F=\mathbb{E}[F|\mathcal{F}_{\infty}]\) we obtain the claim by considering \eqref{eq:KW of V^F} at \(t=\infty\). 
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
Notice that if \(\mathcal{F}_0\) is trivial and completed, that is \(\mathcal{F}_0=\left\{\emptyset,\Omega\right\}\vee\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\), then \(\mathbb{E}[F|\mathcal{F}_0]=\mathbb{E}[F]\), \(\mathbb{P}\)-almost surely (with exact equality if \(\mathcal{F}_0=\left\{\emptyset,\Omega\right\}\)). 
\end{remark}

\subsection{Mean--variance hedging in square-integrable martingale setting}\label{sec:Mean Variance Hedging in square-integrable martingale setting}
We now have all the tools to discuss mean-variance hedging in the case where the price process is a square-integrable martingale. We restate the mean variance hedging problem in the square-integrable martingale setting.
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:square-integrable mean variance via KW}
    Let \(H\in L^2(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{F}_{\infty})\), \(M\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^2\) and \(L^2(M)\) defined as in Definition \ref{def:L^p(M)}. Furthermore, assume \(\mathcal{F}_0\) to be trivial.
    Consider the following minimization problem
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:square-integrable mean square hedging}
        \inf_{(V_0,\vartheta)\in \mathbb{R}\times L^2(M)}\bigg\lVert V_0+\int_{0}^{\infty}\vartheta\diff M-H\bigg\rVert_{L^2}.
    \end{equation}
    Then the infimum is attained in \(\mathbb{R}+G_{\infty}(L^2(M))\) and the optimal strategy \((V_0^*,\vartheta^*)\) is given by \((\mathbb{E}[H],\vartheta^H)\), where the latter are provided by the Kunita Watanabe decomposition of \(H\):
    \begin{equation*}
        H = \mathbb{E}[H]+\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta^H\diff M+L_{\infty}.
    \end{equation*}
    \((V_0^*,\vartheta^*)\) is the unique element in \(\mathbb{R}\times L^2(M)\) minimizing \eqref{eq:square-integrable mean square hedging}.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Existence follows from  the observation made in \eqref{eq:closedness of vartheta bullet M_T in L^2}, asserting that \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M))\) is a closed subspace of \(L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty})\). In particular, we have that  
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{A}\big(L^2(M)\big)=\mathbb{R}+G_{\infty}\big(L^2(M)\big)\subseteq L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty}),
    \end{equation*}
    is closed by Lemma \ref{lemma:A closed if G closed}. Hence, existence follows from the Hilbert projection theorem.

    In order to show that the solution is given by \((\mathbb{E}[H],\vartheta^H)\) consider The Kunita Watanabe decomposition of \(H\) as given in Corollary \ref{cor:random variable level Kunita Watanabe}. 
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:KW of liability H}
    H=\mathbb{E}[H]+\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta^H\diff M+L_{\infty} ,
 \end{equation}
 where \(L\perp M\). 


 
          Observe from \(L\perp M\iff\langle L,M\rangle=0\), \(\langle L,\int H\diff M\rangle = \int H\diff\langle L,M\rangle\), and the fact that \(M\perp N\implies (M_{\infty},N_{\infty})_{L^2}=0\),, or directly from the construction of \(L\) in the proof of Theorem \ref{thm:KW},, that \(L\) is in the weakly orthogonal complement of  \(\mathcal{I}^2(M)\). In other words, \(L_{\infty}\) is weakly orthogonal to all terminal values of integral processes in \(\mathcal{I}^2(M)\), that is, to all elements of \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M))\). Moreover, \(L\) is in the weak orthogonal complement of \(\mathbb{R}+G_{\infty}(L^2(M))\). Indeed
\begin{align}\label{eq:L weakly orthonogal to A}
    \bigg(L_{\infty},V_0+\int_0^{\infty} \vartheta^H\diff M\bigg)_{L^2}\notag&= (L_{\infty},V_0)_{L^2}+ \bigg(L_{\infty},\int_0^{\infty} \vartheta^H\diff M\bigg)_{L^2}\\&=V_0\mathbb{E}[L_{\infty}]=0
\end{align}
for all \(V_0\in\mathbb{R}\) and for all \(\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta^H\diff M\in G_{\infty}(L^2(M))\). The first equality in \eqref{eq:L weakly orthonogal to A} follows because \(L_{\infty}\) is orthogonal to all elements in \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M))\), and the second equality follows because \(L\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\) implies that \(L_{\infty}\) exists and \(\mathbb{E}[L_{\infty}]=\mathbb{E}[L_0]=0\). Hence, \eqref{eq:KW of liability H} is the orthogonal decomposition of \(H\) onto \(L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty})=\mathcal{A}(L^2(M))\oplus \mathcal{A}(L^2(M))^{\perp}\), and the claim follows by Pythagoras 
\begin{align*}
   \lVert Z-H\rVert_{L^2}^2&=
   \bigg\lVert Z -\left(\mathbb{E}[H]+\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta^H\diff M\right)\bigg\rVert_{L^2}^2+\lVert L_{\infty}\rVert_{L^2}^2
   \\&\geq \lVert L_{\infty}\rVert_{L^2}^2=\bigg\lVert \mathbb{E}[H]+\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta^H\diff M-H\bigg\rVert_{L^2}^2,
\end{align*}
for any \(Z\in\mathcal{A}(L^2(M))\). Taking \(\inf\) over such \(Z\) yields the claim. This shows that \(\mathbb{E}[H]+\int \vartheta^H\diff M\) is a minimizer in \(\mathbb{R}+G_{\infty}(L^2(M))\) and by the fact that \(\mathcal{A}(L^2(M))\) is closed in \(L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty})\) it is the unique minimizer in \(\mathcal{A}(L^2(M))\). The claim in the theorem, however, is that \((\mathbb{E}[H],\vartheta^H)\) is unique as a pair in \((\mathbb{R},L^2(M))\); that is, we claim that the decomposition of the optimal element in \(\mathcal{A}(L^2(M))\) into \(\mathbb{E}[H]+\int_0^\infty \vartheta^H\diff M\) is unique. This follows from the fact that \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M))\) doesn't contain non-zero constant random variables. Indeed, assume \(c\in\mathbb{R}/\{0\}\) and \(c=\int \vartheta^{(c)}\diff M\in G_{\infty}(L^2(M)) \) for \(\vartheta^{(c)}\in L^2(M)\). Then 
\begin{equation*}
    c=\mathbb{E}[c]=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^\infty\vartheta^{(c)}\diff M\bigg]=0
\end{equation*}
as \(\int_0^\infty\vartheta^{(c)}\diff M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\). This yields a contradiction. Hence, assume there is \((V_0^*,\vartheta^*)\) such that
\begin{equation*}
    V_0^*+\int_0^\infty\vartheta^*\diff M = \mathbb{E}[H]+\int_0^\infty\vartheta^H\diff M.
\end{equation*}
Then 
\begin{equation*}
    V_0^*-\mathbb{E}[H]=\int_0^\infty\vartheta^H\diff M-\int_0^\infty\vartheta^*\diff M \in G_{\infty}\big(L^2(M)\big).
\end{equation*}
By our previous consideration, this implies that \(V_0^*-\mathbb{E}[H]=0\), hence \(\mathbb{E}[H]=V_0^*\) and \(\int_0^\infty\vartheta^H\diff M=\int_0^\infty\vartheta^*\diff M \). The fact that \(\vartheta^H=\vartheta^*\) in \(L^2(M)\) is then a direct consequence of Itô's isometry, stating that the map 
\begin{align*}
    I_{\infty}:L^2(M)\to G_{\infty}\big(L^2(M)\big)\subset L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty})
\end{align*}
is an isometry; hence, in particular, it is injective.
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
 For self-consistency and simplicity, we formulated the result for \(M\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^2\). Nevertheless, it should be noted that all the results in Section \ref{sec:Kunita–Watanabe Decomposition} hold true for \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{0,\rm{loc}}\) see \parencite[Sec.~4.5.]{Schweizer2025BrownianMotionStochasticCalculus}. In this context, one extends the definition of strong orthogonality to  \(M\in\mathcal{H}_{0,\rm{loc}}^2\) by saying that \(M,N\in\mathcal{H}_{0,\rm{loc}}^2\) are strongly orthogonal if \(MN\) is a local martingale. 
\end{remark}

\subsection{Stochastic integration with respect to semimartingales}\label{sec:stochastic integration with respect to semimartingales}
In light of the discussion of mean-variance hedging for  continuous semimartingale price processes, the discussion of Section \ref{sec:algorithmically generated random variables} and Section \ref{sec:extension of deep mean squared hedging to semimartingales} requires some elementary results from more general stochastic integration theory, which we present here. The approach is heavily based on \parencite{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}, and we refer to the latter for details, missing proofs, and the construction of the stochastic integral in full generality.
As mentioned in the introductory discussion around \eqref{eq:heuristic integral with respect to semimartingale}, the construction of the stochastic integral with respect to square-integrable martingales constitutes the conceptual cornerstone for the development of stochastic integration with respect to semimartingales. Nevertheless, we present a different approach, due to \parencite{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}, which is insightful on its own and yields some results needed in Section \ref{sec:algorithmically generated random variables}.


For the interested reader, it should be noted that the notion of semimartingales used in \parencite[Chapter.~2]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration} is seemingly different from the classical one used in Definition \ref{def:semimartingale} at first sight. 

In \parencite[Chapter.~4]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}, semimartingales are defined to be processes for which the integration of simple predictable processes is continuous under uniform convergence. The latter guaranties a notion of bounded convergence for the respective integral, which is given a central role in the construction of a \textit{sensible} notion of an integral. It is the celebrated Bichteler-Dellacherie Theorem (see \parencite[Thm.~47]{DellacherieMeyer1982ProbabilitiesPotentialB}) that establishes the connection between these two notions. 
Furthermore, as we consider the left and right-limit processes of adapted processes in this subsection, we assume that the underlying complete probability space \((\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\) is equipped with a filtration \(\mathbb{F}\) satisfying the usual hypotheses. The right continuity of \(\mathbb{F}\) entails that a right-limit process of an adapted process \(Z\), denoted by \(Z^+=(Z_t^+)_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}=(Z_{t+})_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\) with
\begin{equation*}
    Z_{t+}\coloneqq\lim_{s\to t,s>t}Z_s
\end{equation*}
whenever it exists,
is again adapted. The left-limit process is defined accordingly with
\begin{equation*}
    Z_{t-}\coloneqq\lim_{s\to t,s<t}Z_s
\end{equation*}
whenever it exists, is always adapted if \(Z\) is adapted.

A key conceptual difference in the following approach is that we need to enlarge the definition of elementary predictable processes from Definition \ref{def:elementary predictable process} to the notion of \textit{simple predictable processes}. We take the convention \((\tau,\tau]=\emptyset\) for any random time \(\tau\).

\begin{definition}\label{def:simple predictable process}
    We call \(\vartheta\) a \textit{simple predictable process} if there exists a finite, increasing sequence of stopping times, \(0=\tau_0\leq\dots\leq\tau_{n+1}<\infty\), and a sequence of bounded random variables \((\varphi_i)_{i=0}^n\subseteq L^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}_{\tau_i},\mathbb{P})\) such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \vartheta = \varphi_01_{\left\{0\right\}}+\sum_{i=0}^n\varphi_i1_{(\tau_i,\tau_{i+1}]}.
    \end{equation*}
In particular, we have that \(\varphi_i\) is \(\mathcal{F}_{\tau_i}\)-measurable. We denote the set of simple predictable processes by \(\mathcal{S}\) and we say \(\vartheta\in\mathcal{S}\) is associated with a sequence \((\tau_k)_{k=0}^{n+1}\).
\end{definition}
The same remarks as in Definition \ref{def:elementary predictable process} about the association of \(\vartheta\) with the sequence of stopping times apply here. 


Clearly \(\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{S}\). We denote by \(\mathbb{L}\) the class of adapted càglàd processes, and by \(\mathbb{D}\) the class of adapted càdlàg processes. As usual, we denote by \(b\mathbb{L}\) and \(b\mathbb{D}\) the respective subclasses of bounded processes. Notice that due to the right-continuity of the filtration, the left-limit process of a process in \(\mathbb{D}\) is in \(\mathbb{L}\) and vice versa. We will construct the stochastic integral \(\int \vartheta\diff X\) for \(\vartheta\in \mathbb{L}\) and \(X\), a classical semimartingale. The idea is similar to our previous construction in that we first define the stochastic integral for simple processes before extending it by density to a larger class of integrable processes. In particular, one needs to identify the right topology for which \(\vartheta\mapsto \int\vartheta\diff X\) is continuous for simple processes and such that the simple processes themselves form a dense subset in a larger class with respect to that topology. In the square-integrable martingale setting, the choices were inspired by the Itô isometry motivating the \(L^2(M)\)-topology for the elementary processes and the \(L^2\)-topology on \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\). For the current case, we don't have the Itô isometry at our disposal. The topology we choose is \(\rm{ucp}\)
-topology. 
\begin{definition}\label{def:UCP convergence}
    Let \((X^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) be real-valued stochastic processes. We say \(X^n\to X\) converges \textit{uniformly on compact sets} (\(\rm{ucp}\)) if 
    \begin{equation*}
        \lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}[(X^n-X)^*_t>\epsilon]=0\;\forall\epsilon>0,\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+,
    \end{equation*}
    where \((X^n-X)^*_t=\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}|X^n_s-X_s|\) and  \((X^n-X)^*_{\infty}=\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}|X^n_t-X_t|\). 
\end{definition}
Put differently, \(X^n\overset{\rm{ucp}}{\to}X\), if \((X^n-X)^*_t\) converges to \(0\) in probability for all \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\). 
Clearly, if \((X^n-X)_{\infty}^*\to 0\) converges in probability, then \(X^n\overset{\rm{ucp}}{\to}X\). Similar to the convergence in probability of random variables, the \(\rm{ucp}\)-topology is induced by the following metric
\begin{equation*}
    d_{\rm{ucp}}(X,Y)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^n}\mathbb{E}[1\wedge(X-Y)^*_n].
\end{equation*}
The space \((\mathbb{D},d_{\rm{ucp}})\), equipped with the proposed metric, is complete. For later reference, we also introduce \(p\)-\(\rm{ucp}\) convergence
\begin{definition}\label{def:p-UCP convergence}
    Let \((X^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) be real-valued stochastic processes and \(p\in[1,\infty)\). We say \(X^n\to X\) converges in \(p\)-\(\rm{ucp}\) if 
    \begin{equation*}
        \lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\big[|(X^n-X)^*_t|{^p}\big]=0,\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+.
    \end{equation*} 
\end{definition}
The idea is very similar to the \(\rm{ucp}\)-convergence, in the sense that we generalize the usual \(L^p\)-convergence of random variables to stochastic processes by requiring the \(L^p\)-convergence to be uniform in time. As \(L^p\)-convergence implies convergence in probability, it is immediate that \(p\)-\(\rm{ucp}\) convergence implies \(\rm{ucp}\) convergence. 



The stochastic integral for the process Definition \ref{def:simple predictable process} with respect to a classical semimartingale \(X\) is naturally defined as \(I_X(\vartheta)=(I_X(\vartheta)_t)_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\) with
\begin{equation}\label{def:simple integration}
     I_X(\vartheta)_t=\sum_{i=0}^n \varphi_i(X_{\tau_{i+1}\wedge t}-X_{\tau_i}\wedge t),\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+
\end{equation}
accordingly.
Observe that, by definition, \(I_X(\vartheta)_0=0\). This is the convention taken in \parencite[Eq.~4.35]{JacodShiryaev2003LimitTheoremsStochasticProcesses} and is adapted to our purposes, as we are trying to model gains processes by stochastic integrals; whence, they should naturally start at zero.
We show in Proposition \ref{prop:UCP density of simple process in cadlag processes} that \(\mathcal{S}\) is dense in \(\mathbb{L}\) for the \(\rm{ucp}\)-topology.
Furthermore, one can show that  
\begin{equation}\label{eq:UCP continuity of integral map}
    I_X:(\mathcal{S},d_{\rm{ucp}})\to(\mathbb{D},d_{\rm{ucp}})
\end{equation}
is continuous. 
Hence, there exists a unique continuous extension 
\begin{equation*}
    \widetilde{I_X}:\mathbb{L}\to\mathbb{D}.
\end{equation*}
 For the proof of the continuity of the map \eqref{eq:UCP continuity of integral map}, we refer to \parencite[Thm.~11]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}. For the density of \(\mathcal{S}\) in \(\mathbb{L}\), we reproduce the proof given in \parencite[Thm.~10,Ch.II]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration} as the proof technique differs fundamentally from the monotone class argument used in Proposition \ref{prop:density of E in L2(M) via monotone class}. 
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:UCP density of simple process in cadlag processes}
    The space \(\mathcal{S}\) is dense in \(\mathbb{L}\) for the \(\rm{ucp}\)-topology.
\end{proposition}
Before giving the details, we provide some conceptual ideas about the proof strategy. Given some \(Y\in\mathbb{L}\), we want to approximate it by elements of \(\mathcal{S}\) in the \(\rm{ucp}\)-sense. After arguing that we can take \(Y\in b\mathbb{L}\) without loss of generality, the idea is to approximate every path of \(Y\) by step functions that are \(\epsilon\)-close uniformly in \(\omega\in\Omega\) and \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\). We want to do this for every random path of \(Y\); thus, we have to construct these step functions by cutting up \(Y\) whenever \(Y\) leaves a \(\epsilon\)-tube. As this is a random event, we do this by stopping times. This will yield step functions that are \(\epsilon\) close uniformly in \((\omega,t)\).


Nota bene, this yields an approximating sequence in \(\mathcal{S}\) that is not in \(\mathcal{E}\) in general, as we shall need to stop the simple predictable processes at random times for them not to leave the \(\epsilon\)-tube. It then remains to check that the approximating sequence in \(\mathcal{S}\) indeed approximates \(Y\) in \(\rm{ucp}\). The main technical difficulties originate from the construction of a sequence of stopping times with the right properties; they should increase to \(\infty\) in order to form a partition of \(\mathbb{R}_+\) and confine the difference of the stopped process and the original process by some positive \(\epsilon\) uniformly in \((\omega,t)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+\). 
\begin{proof}
 First, notice that for \(Y\in\mathbb{L}\), we can stop \(Y\) with the stopping times 
    \begin{equation*}
        T_n=\inf\left\{t:|Y_t|>n\right\}
    \end{equation*}
    Because \(Y\) is left-continuous by assumption, \(Y^{T_n}1_{T_n>0}\) is bounded by \(n\); in particular, \(Y^n\coloneqq Y^{T_n}1_{T_n>0}\in b\mathbb{L}\). Notice that the multiplication by \(1_{T_n>0}\) is necessary as we might have \(Y_0(\omega)=n+1\), then \(T_n=0\) and \(Y^{T_n}=Y_0>n\). Now,
    \begin{equation*}
        Y_n\overset{\rm{ucp}}{\to}Y
    \end{equation*}
 Indeed,
s    \begin{equation*}
        \Big\{\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}|Y_t^n-Y_t|>\epsilon\Big\}\subseteq \left\{T_n< t\right\}
    \end{equation*}
    for every \(\epsilon>0\) as \(\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}|Y_t^n-Y_t|=0\) for \(t\leq T_n\). Furthermore, 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{P}[T_n< t]\leq\mathbb{P}[Y_t^*>n]
    \end{equation*}
    and the right-hand side tends to \(0\) for every \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\) by continuity of measures for the set \(\{Y_t^*>n+1\}\subseteq\{Y_t^*>n\}\) for every \(n\in\mathbb{N}\) and \(\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\{Y_t^*>n\}=\emptyset\) for fixed \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\). Hence, \(b\mathbb{L}\) is dense in \(\mathbb{L}\) for the \(\rm{ucp}\)-topology, so we might assume \(Y\in b\mathbb{L}\) without loss of generality. The idea is to approximate \(Y\) by a step-wise constant process in \(\mathcal{S}\) in a uniform \(\epsilon\)-tube. We do this as follows. Let 
    \(Z_t\coloneqq Y_t^{+}\). Define the following stopping times for fixed \(\epsilon>0\)
    \begin{align*}
        &T_0^{\epsilon}=0\\
        &T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}=\inf\left\{t:t>T_{n}^{\epsilon},\;|Z _{T_{n}^{\epsilon}}-Z_t|>\epsilon\right\}.
    \end{align*}
    Since \(Z\) is càdlàg, the \(T_n^{\epsilon}\) are stopping times strictly increasing to \(+\infty\). Notice that the right-continuity of \(Z\) is crucial. Indeed, take \(Z_t=0\) for \(t\leq t_0\) where \(t_0>0\) is some fixed time and \(Z_t=\epsilon+1\) for \(t>t_0\). Then \(T_1^{\epsilon}=t_0\) and \(Z_{t_0}=0\) hence \(T_2^{\epsilon}=T_1^{\epsilon}\) because \(\{t:t>T_1^{\epsilon},|Z _{T_{2}^{\epsilon}}-Z_t|>\epsilon|\}=\{t>T_1^{\epsilon}\}\). 
    
    
 Hence, we can build the uniform \(\epsilon\)-approximants
    \begin{equation*}
        Z^{\epsilon}=\sum_{n\geq 0} Z_{T_n^{\epsilon}}1_{[T_{n}^{\epsilon},T_{n+1}^{\epsilon})}.
    \end{equation*}
 By construction, \(Z^{\epsilon}\) tends uniformly to \(Z\) as \(\epsilon\) tends to \(0\). Indeed, for all \((\omega,t)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+\) we have 
    \begin{equation*}
        |Z_t(\omega)-Z_t^{\epsilon}(\omega)|\leq\sum_{n\geq 0}\big|Z_{t}(\omega)-Z_{T_n^{\epsilon}(\omega)}(\omega)\big|1_{\big[T_{n}^{\epsilon}(\omega),T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}(\omega)\big)}(t)\leq\epsilon,
    \end{equation*}
    because 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:bounding difference}
        |Z_{t}(\omega)-Z_{T_n^{\epsilon}(\omega)}(\omega)|\leq\epsilon,\quad t\in\big[T_{n}^{\epsilon}(\omega),T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}(\omega)\big).
    \end{equation}
 Indeed, for \(t<T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}\) this is true, because if not, and assuming, without loss of generality, that \(t>T_{n}^{\epsilon}\), we have \(t\) such that \(|Z_{t}(\omega)-Z_{T_n^{\epsilon}(\omega)}(\omega)|>\epsilon\) and \(T_{n}^{\epsilon}<t<T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}\) contradicting the definition of \(T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}\). On the other hand, we can't bound the difference in \eqref{eq:bounding difference} for \(t\in \big(T_{n}^{\epsilon}(\omega),T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}(\omega)\big]\) as we have no control over the difference in \eqref{eq:bounding difference} at the time \(T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}(\omega)\). However, we have the respective bound for the left-limit process
 \begin{equation*}
       |Z_{t}^-(\omega)-Z_{T_n^{\epsilon}(\omega)}(\omega)|\leq\epsilon,\quad t\in\big(T_{n}^{\epsilon}(\omega),T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}(\omega)\big].
 \end{equation*}
 This motivates the definition of 
 \begin{equation*}
        U^{\epsilon}=1_{\left\{0\right\}}Y_0+\sum_{n\geq 0} Z_{T_n^{\epsilon}}1_{(T_{n}^{\epsilon},T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}]} 
    \end{equation*}
for which \(|Z_t^{-}(\omega)-U^{\epsilon}_t(\omega)|\leq\epsilon\)
uniformly in \((\omega,t)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+\).
First, it is clear that for every \((\omega,t)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+\), the series consists of one term \(Z_{T_{n_0}^\epsilon}\) for \(n_0:t\in(T_{n_0}^\epsilon(\omega),T_{n_0+1}^\epsilon(\omega)]\).
From the previous considerations, it follows that
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{P}\Big[\sup_{s\leq t}|U^{\epsilon}_s-Z_{s-}-Y_01_{\{0\}}|>\epsilon\Big]=0
\end{equation*}
for all \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\) and \(\epsilon>0\). Hence, fix \(\epsilon>0\) and consider the sequence \((U^{\frac{1}{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\). We have that \(U^{\frac{1}{n}}\) converges to \(Y=Z^-+Y_01_{\{0\}}\) uniformly on compacts. Finally, let 
 \begin{equation*}
        Y^{n,\epsilon}=1_{\left\{0\right\}}Y_0+\sum_{k=0 }^nZ_{T_k^{\epsilon}\wedge n}1_{\big(T_{k}^{\epsilon}\wedge n,T_{k+1}^{\epsilon}\wedge n\big]}.
    \end{equation*}
Clearly, \(Y^{n,\epsilon}\) converges to \(U^{\epsilon}\) in \(\rm{ucp}\). Indeed, we have
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{P}\Big[\sup_{s\leq t}|U_s^{\epsilon}-Y_s^{n,\epsilon}|>0\Big]\leq \mathbb{P}\big[t>T_{n+1}^\epsilon\wedge n\big]\to 0 \text{ as }n\to\infty.
\end{equation*}
Hence, by a diagonal argument, there exists a null-sequence \(\epsilon(n)\) such that \(Y^{n,\epsilon(n)}\) converges to \(Y\) in \(\rm{ucp}\).
As \(Y^{n,\epsilon}\in\mathcal{S}\), the claim is shown.
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
Observe two things. A monotone class argument, as in Proposition \ref{prop:density of E in L2(M) via monotone class}, would not be well adapted, as it relies heavily on a function class that is closed under bounded, monotone, pointwise convergence, which is ill conditioned for making statements about convergence in \(\rm{ucp}\). More precisely, \(\rm{ucp}\) convergence is too strong in the sense that it is generally not implied by uniformly bounded monotone convergence; hence, a class \begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{H}_{\rm{ucp}}=\left\{ U \in \mathcal{A}:\forall \epsilon\;\exists H\in\mathcal{S}:\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}|H_t-U_t|<\epsilon,\; \mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}, \forall t\in\mathbb{R}+\right\}
\end{equation*} 
is not closed under bounded monotone limits, as this is a pointwise property and doesn't conserve a uniform bound (not in probability or almost surely). \\\\
Secondly, the proof idea above crucially relies on partitioning the positive real line into random intervals \((\tau,\sigma]\) or \([\tau,\sigma)\) for stopping times \(\tau\leq\sigma\), in a way that allows us to approximate \(Y,Y_+,Y_-\) uniformly on such a random interval. We have \(\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}} (T_n^{\epsilon},T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}]=\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+\). Partitioning \(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+\) in this way can only be done by stopping times; thus, the above argument cannot be reduced to a proof for \(\mathcal{E}\) instead of \(\mathcal{S}\).
\end{remark}
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:UCP continuity of general stochastic integral}
    Let \(X\) be a classical semimartingale. Then the mapping 
    \begin{equation*}
        I_X:(\mathcal{S},d_{\rm{ucp}})\to(\mathbb{D},d_{\rm{ucp}}) 
    \end{equation*}
    is continuous. 
\end{theorem}
We refer to \parencite[Thm.~11,Ch.II]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration} for a proof.
This allows us to define the integral on \(\mathbb{L}\) by continuous extension.
\begin{definition}\label{def:stochastic integral w.r.t. semimartingales}
    Let \(X\) be a classical semimartingale. We define the stochastic integral
    \begin{align*}
        I_X:\;&\mathbb{L}\to\mathbb{D}\\
        &\vartheta\mapsto\int \vartheta\diff X
    \end{align*}
    as the unique continuous extension of the map in Theorem \ref{thm:UCP continuity of general stochastic integral} to \(\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{\rm{ucp}}=\mathbb{L}\). Furthermore, we define
    \begin{equation*}
        \int_0^t\vartheta_s\diff X_s\coloneqq \int_{(0,t]}\vartheta_s\diff X_s,\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+
    \end{equation*}
    and \(\int_0^\infty\vartheta_s\diff X_s=\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{(0,t]}\vartheta_s\diff X_s\) if it exists.
\end{definition}
One can significantly enlarge the space of integrands to predictable processes (see \parencite[Ch.IV]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}). The construction is more involved. Loosely speaking, in order to allow for general predictable integrands, one first restricts the integrators to semimartingales in \(X\in \mathcal{S}^2\), where \(\mathcal{S}^2\) contains special semimartingales \(X\) with finite \(\mathcal{S}^2\)-norm, that is, special semimartingales such that 
\begin{equation*}
    \lVert X\rVert_{\mathcal{S}^2}=\Big\lVert [M]_{\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\rVert_{L^2}+\bigg\lVert \int_0^{\infty}\diff |A|_t\bigg\rVert_{L^2}<\infty,
\end{equation*}
where \(X=X_0+M+A\) denotes the canonical decomposition of \(X\) and \(|A|\) denotes the variation process of \(A\). We have \(X=X_0+M+A\in\mathcal{S}^2\) if and only if \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\) (see \eqref{eq:P_M is finite measure}) and \(\mathbb{E}[\sup_{0\leq t<\infty}|A|_t^2]<\infty\).
For \(X\in\mathcal{S}^2\) we define the class of \(X\)-integrable processes as the class of predictable processes \(\vartheta\) such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:X integrable processes for X in S^2}
\bigg\lVert\left(\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta_t^2\diff [M]_t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg\rVert_{L^2}+\bigg\lVert \int_0^{\infty}|\vartheta_t|\diff |A|_t\bigg\rVert_{L^2}<\infty.
\end{equation}
Notice that the first term in \eqref{eq:X integrable processes for X in S^2} corresponds exactly to \(\lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}\).
For later reference, this space will play a central role in Section \ref{sec:extension of deep mean squared hedging to semimartingales} and will be denoted by \(L^2(M,A)\). For \(X\in\mathcal{S}^2\) with canonical decomposition \(X=X_0+M+A\) and \(\vartheta\in L^2(M,A)\), we have that \(\int \vartheta\diff X\) exists, is in \(\mathcal{S}^2\) and 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:canonical decomposition of integral process}
    \int \vartheta\diff X = \int \vartheta\diff M+\int \vartheta\diff A,
\end{equation}
is the canonical decomposition of \(\int \vartheta\diff X\). The fact that \eqref{eq:canonical decomposition of integral process} is indeed the canonical decomposition of \(\int\vartheta\diff X\) follows from the useful fact that, for a predictable \(\vartheta\) and a finite variation process \(A\), the integral \(\int\vartheta\diff A\) can be chosen to be predictable as well (see \parencite[Prop.~3.5.,Ch.I]{JacodShiryaev2003LimitTheoremsStochasticProcesses}). 
Hence, \eqref{eq:X integrable processes for X in S^2} is precisely equal to \(\lVert\int \vartheta\diff X\rVert_{\mathcal{S}^2}\).
Furthermore, we can define
\begin{equation*}
   \int_0^\infty\vartheta_s\diff X_s \coloneqq \lim_{t\to\infty}\int_0^t\vartheta_s\diff X_s,
\end{equation*}
which exists for \(\vartheta\in L^2(M,A)\) and the decomposition \eqref{eq:canonical decomposition of integral process}.
We also define the class of semimartingales that are locally in \(\mathcal{S}^2\) by semimartingales \(X=X_0+M+A\) with \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{0,\rm{loc}}\) and \(|A|\) predictable, finite variation, and locally square-integrable, that is, 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sup_{0\leq t<\infty}|A|^{\tau_n}_t\Big)^2\bigg]<\infty
\end{equation*}
for a localizing sequence \((\tau_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\). We denote this set of semimartingales by  \(\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{loc}}\). By the definition of \(L^2(M,A)\), \(\int\vartheta\diff X\in\mathcal{S}^2\) for \(X\in\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{loc}}\) and \(\vartheta\in L^2(M,A)\).
 This is the most general notion of stochastic integration we shall use in this thesis and shall be considered as a standing assumption
\begin{align*}
    \text{All semimartingales in this thesis are taken to be in \(\mathcal{S}_{\rm{loc}}^2\)},
\end{align*}
In particular, this implies that \(\langle M\rangle\) exists. Hence, the relevant notion of integrability in this thesis is given by the following definition.
\begin{definition}\label{def:X integrable processes for X in S^2_loc}
    Let \(X\in\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{loc}}\) with canonical decomposition \(X=X_0+M+A\). Then we call a process \(\vartheta\), \(X\)-integrable if it is predictable and
    \begin{equation*}
        \bigg\lVert\left(\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta_t^2\diff \langle M\rangle_t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg\rVert_{L^2}+\bigg\lVert \int_0^{\infty}|\vartheta_t|\diff |A|_t\bigg\rVert_{L^2}<\infty.
    \end{equation*}
\end{definition}

We mention that one can define a more general notion of stochastic integration. 
The crucial observation is then to notice that every (classical) semimartingale is (pre-)locally in \(\mathcal{S}^2\). Hence, we can extend the integral by localization to all classical semimartingales \(X\). The set of processes for which the integral with respect to a classical semimartingale is defined is then the set of predictable processes for which the integral in the definition at the bottom of page 165 of \parencite{Protter2005StochasticIntegration} is well defined. In particular, locally bounded, predictable processes are integrable in this sense.



\section{Mean-variance hedging in the semimartingale case}\label{sec:Mean-variance Hedging with continuous price process}

We now have sufficient knowledge of stochastic integration to consider the mean-variance hedging problem when
the discounted price process \(X\) is not a martingale but may have a finite variation drift added to the square-integrable martingale part. This is
the natural situation to consider if one considers the mean-variance hedging problem under the statistical measure \(\mathbb{P}\). Recall that under the statistical measure, the discounted asset prices need not be 
martingales. Consequently, the Hilbert space argument in the square-integrable martingale setup of 
Section \ref{sec:Mean Variance Hedging in square-integrable martingale setting} no longer applies.
 For the following, we essentially follow \parencite{Schweizer2001GuidedTourQuadraticHedging}. We will only provide proofs selectively for the following results, as they are all nicely presented in \parencite[Chapter.~4]{Schweizer2001GuidedTourQuadraticHedging}. 



We recall the problem at hand. 
Let \(H \in L^2(\mathbb{P})\) be a contingent claim. For a suitable class \(\Theta\subseteq\Theta^{(2)}\) of 
square-integrable strategies, the mean-variance hedging problem is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:MV hedging restatement}
        \inf_{V_0 \in \mathbb R,\ \vartheta \in \Theta}
        \mathbb{E}\bigg[
        \bigg(
        V_0 + \int_0^{\infty} \vartheta_t \diff X_t -H
        \bigg)^2
        \bigg].
\end{equation}
Thus, one wants to approximate \(H\) in \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\) by terminal values of self-financing
strategies that are, in turn, represented, up to a deterministic offset, as terminal values of stochastic integrals with respect to the price process \(X\).
\begin{equation*}
