        V_0 + G_{\infty}(\vartheta)
        =
        V_0 + \int_0^{\infty} \vartheta_t\,dX_t .
\end{equation*}
We can equivalently formulate \eqref{eq:MV hedging restatement} as the problem of projecting the random variable \(H\) onto the affine space
\[
        \mathbb R + G_{\infty}(\Theta),
        \qquad
        G_{\infty}(\Theta)
        :=
        \bigg\{
        \int_0^{\infty} \vartheta_t\,dX_t : \vartheta \in \Theta
        \bigg\}.
\]
For \(X\in \mathcal{H}^2_0\) and \(\Theta = L^2(X)\), it is a consequence of the Itô-isometry that the space \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\subseteq L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty},\mathbb{P})\) is closed, assuring the existence of the mean-variance hedging problem. Furthermore, the Kunita–Watanabe decomposition, relying on the geometric properties of \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\), allowed us to explicitly characterize the solution to the mean-variance hedging problem. Fundamentally, the results in the square-integrable martingale case rely on the property of the stochastic integral of \(\vartheta\in L^2(M)\) with respect to \(X\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^2\) that we have.
\begin{equation*}
   \int \vartheta\diff X\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^2.
\end{equation*}
In the case where \(X\in\mathcal{H}^2_{0,\rm{loc}}\) and \(\vartheta\in L^2_{\rm{loc}}(M)\), we saw in Definition \ref{def:local sotchastic integral} that the stochastic integral need not be a true martingale. In the general case, that is for a classical semimartingale \(X\) and  \(X\)-integrable \(\vartheta\), the resulting stochastic integral might not even be a local martingale (see Emery's example after \parencite[Thm.~34]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}). Hence, the study of the mean-variance problem for more general price processes is more involved. The following discussion largely follows the overview \parencite{Schweizer2001GuidedTourQuadraticHedging}. Recall the standing assumption that \(X\in\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{loc}}\). We refer to this setting as the semimartingale case.



In the semimartingale case, we want to proceed similarly to the square-integrable martingale case by reducing the mean-variance hedging problem to a projection problem in \(L^2\). As observed in Remark \ref{rem:if G_T closed then mean variance hedging is projection onto closed hilbert subspace}, we can do so if \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) is a closed subspace of  \(L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty})\). Thus, we are interested in the study of \(\Theta\), for which \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\subseteq L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty})\) and sufficient conditions on \(X\) and \(\Theta\) respectively, for which \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\subseteq L^2(\mathbb{P})\) is closed. Following the notation introduced in \eqref{eq:Theta2}, we assume \(\Theta\subseteq\Theta^{(2)}\), for which \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\subseteq L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty})\) by definition. We will give a concrete example of such a \(\Theta\) at the end of this section.


A natural first step is to consider the mean-variance hedging minimization problem over the larger space 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{R}+\overline{G_{\infty}(\Theta)}^{L^2},
\end{equation*}
which is a \(L^2\)-closed subspace by Lemma \ref{lemma:A closed if G closed}. In particular, the mean-variance solution \((V_0^*,g^*)\) exists in \(\mathbb{R}+\overline{G_{\infty}(\Theta)}^{L^2}\) and is unique as an element of the latter by the Hilbert projection theorem. Notice two things. The uniqueness of the mean-variance solution refers to uniqueness as an element \(V_0^*+g^*\in\mathbb{R}+\overline{G_{\infty}(\Theta)}^{L^2}\) and not necessarily as a pair  \((V_0^*,g^*)\). In particular, the mean-variance price \(V_0^*\) might not be unique. This is easily seen from the following observation. If  \((V_0^*,g^*)\) is a mean-variance solution, so is \((\widetilde{V_0}^*,\widetilde{g}^*)\) with \(\widetilde{V_0}^*\coloneqq V_0^*+a\), \(\widetilde{g}^*\coloneqq g^*-a\) whenever \(a\in\overline{G_{\infty}(\Theta)}^{L^2}\) with \(a\in\mathbb{R}/\left\{0\right\}\). Indeed
\begin{equation*}
    \widetilde{V_0}^*+\widetilde{g}^*=V_0^*+a+g^*-a=V_0^*+g^*.
\end{equation*}
 We argue that this is indeed all that can go wrong regarding the degeneracy of the decomposition of the mean-variance solution. Indeed, assume \(\overline{G_{\infty}(\Theta)}^{L^2}\) does not contain any non-zero constant random variables. As \(\overline{G_{\infty}(\Theta)}^{L^2}\) is itself a subspace, this is equivalent to requiring that it does not contain the payoff equal to \(1\). Assume  \((V_0^*,g^*)\) and \((\widetilde{V_0}^*,\widetilde{g}^*)\), where both are mean-variance portfolios. Then
\begin{align*}
    V_0^*-\widetilde{V_0}^*=\widetilde{g}^*-g^*\in\overline{G_{\infty}(\Theta)}^{L^2}.
\end{align*}
But \(V_0^*-\widetilde{V_0}^*\) is a constant random variable, which by assumption is not an element of \(\overline{G_{\infty}(\Theta)}^{L^2}\) except if it is zero. Hence \(V_0^*=\widetilde{V_0}^*\) and 
\(g^*=\widetilde{g}^*\). The condition that \(\overline{G_{\infty}(\Theta)}^{L^2}\) does not contain any non-zero constant random variables even allows for an explicit formula for the mean-variance price, as we shall see in Lemma \ref{lemma:no approximate profits}. Observe that the uniqueness argument also shows that if the mean-variance solution exists in \((V_0^*,g^*)\in\mathbb{R}\times G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) and \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) does not contain non-zero constants, then \((V_0^*,g^*)\in\mathbb{R}\times G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) is unique. The reason why the no-approximate profit condition Definition \ref{def:no approximate profits} is formulated for the \(L^2\) closure of \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) and not \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) directly is due to the first equivalence in Lemma \ref{lemma:no approximate profits}, which only holds for the stronger condition where \(1\notin\overline{G_{\infty}(\Theta)}^{L^2}\).



In accordance with \parencite{Schweizer2001GuidedTourQuadraticHedging}, the following developments will be formulated for a general subspace \(\mathcal{G}\subseteq L^2(P)\) and \(\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{R}+\mathcal{G}\) accordingly. The reader might think of \(\mathcal{G}=G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) for some set of admissible strategies \(\Theta\subseteq\Theta^{(2)}\). In the following, all the closures are taken with respect to the \(L^2\)-topology.
\begin{definition}\label{def:no approximate profits}
    We say that \(\mathcal{G}\) admits \textit{no approximate profits} in \(L^2\) if \(\overline{\mathcal{G}}\) does \textbf{not} contain the constant \(1\). 
\end{definition}
In addition to the connection with the uniqueness of the mean-variance pair previously discussed, 
the no approximate profit condition is a no-arbitrage condition. Indeed, if \(\mathcal{G}\) admits no approximate profits, one cannot approximate the riskless payoff \(V_{\infty}=1\) by a self-financing strategy with initial wealth \(V_0=0\) in \(\mathcal{G}\). As \(\mathcal{G}\) is a subspace of \(L^2\), if \(1\notin\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{L^2}\), \(\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{L^2}\) does not contain any constant random variable. Furthermore, in alignment with the general insight of mathematical finance that the existence of a local martingale measure and no-arbitrage-like conditions are intimately related, the no approximate profit condition can be formulated as an existence statement of an absolutely continuous measure. In what follows, we only display the measure with which the expectation is taken if it is not  \(\mathbb{P}\).
\begin{definition}\label{def:signed martingale measure}
A \emph{signed $\mathcal{G}$-martingale measure} is a signed measure $\mathbb{Q}$ on
$(\Omega,\mathcal{F})$ with $\mathbb{Q}[\Omega]=1$, $\mathbb{Q}\ll \mathbb{P}$ with $\frac{d\mathbb{Q}}{d\mathbb{P}}\in L^2(\mathbb{P})$, and
\begin{equation*}
\mathbb{E}_\mathbb{Q}[g] =\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{d\mathbb{Q}}{d\mathbb{P}}g\bigg] = 0,\quad\forall g\in\mathcal{G}.
\end{equation*}
$\mathbb{P}^2_s(\mathcal{G})$ denotes the convex set of all signed
$\mathcal{G}$-martingale measures, and an element $\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{G}}$
of $\mathbb{P}^2_s(\mathcal{G})$ is called \emph{variance-optimal} if it minimizes
\begin{equation*}
\bigg\lVert\frac{\diff\mathbb{Q}}{\diff\mathbb{P}}\bigg\rVert_{L^2}
=
\sqrt{1+\operatorname{Var}\bigg[\frac{\diff\mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\bigg]}
\end{equation*}
 for all $\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}^2_s(\mathcal{G})$, where \(\operatorname{Var}[\frac{\diff\mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}]\) denotes the variance of the random variable \(\frac{\diff\mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\in L^2(\mathbb{P})\).
\end{definition}
Notice that we only require \(\mathbb{Q}\) to be a signed measure. Furthermore, observe that for \(\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}^2_s(\mathcal{G})\) we have 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{E}_\mathbb{Q}[g] =\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{d\mathbb{Q}}{d\mathbb{P}}g\bigg] = 0,\quad\forall g\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}.
\end{equation*}
This follows from the continuity of the map
\(L^2(\mathbb{P})\to \mathbb{R}\) defined by \(f\mapsto (g,f)_{L^2}\).
The following lemma justifies the former definition
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:no approximate profits}
Let $\mathcal{G}$ be a subspace of $L^2(\mathbb{P})$. Then:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\mathcal{G}$ admits no approximate profits in $L^2$ if and only if
$\mathbb{P}^2_s(\mathcal{G})\neq\emptyset$.
\item If $\mathcal{G}$ admits no approximate profits in $L^2(\mathbb{P})$, then the
variance-optimal signed $\mathcal{G}$-martingale measure $\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{G}}$
exists, is unique, and satisfies
\begin{equation*}
\frac{d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{G}}}{d\mathbb{P}}\in\bar{\mathcal{A}}.
\end{equation*}
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
For completeness, we reproduce the proof given in \parencite[Lemma.~4.1.]{Schweizer2001GuidedTourQuadraticHedging}.
For this, we need a couple of auxiliary lemmas.

\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:Hahn Banach Corollary}
    Let \(\mathcal{Y}\) be a subspace of a normed topological vector space \(\mathcal{X}\). Let \(x_0\in \mathcal{X}\) be such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \inf_{y\in Y}\lVert y-x_0\rVert=d>0.
    \end{equation*}
    Then, there exists \(F_0\in \mathcal{X}^*\) such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        F_0(x_0)=1,\;\sup_{x\in \mathcal{X},\lVert x\rVert\leq 1}|F_0(x)|=\frac{1}{d},\;F_0(y)=0,\quad\forall y\in \mathcal{Y}.
    \end{equation*}
\end{lemma}
This is a classic result and a direct corollary  of the Hahn-Banach theorem. For a proof, see \parencite[Thm.~4.8.3.]{Friedman1982FoundationsModernAnalysis}. 
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:Z is closed and convex}
The set 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{Z}=\left\{\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}:\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{\mathbb{P}}^2_s(\mathcal{G})\right\},
\end{equation*}
is a closed, convex subset of \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\). 
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By definition of \(\mathbb{\mathbb{P}}^2_t(\mathcal{G})\), \(\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\in L^2(\mathbb{P})\). Furthermore, 
    every measurable function \(Z\in \mathcal{G}^{\perp}\), where \(\mathcal{G}^{\perp}\) denotes the orthogonal complement of \(\mathcal{G}\) in \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\), such that \(\mathbb{E}[Z]=1\), induces a signed measure \(\mathbb{Q}(A)=\mathbb{E}[1_AZ]\), which is absolutely continuous to \(\mathbb{P}\) by construction; hence, we have 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{Z}=\mathcal{G}^{\perp}\cap \{Z\in L^2(\mathbb{P}):\mathbb{E}[Z]=1\}.
\end{equation*}
As the map \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\) to \(\mathbb{R}\) defined as \(Z\mapsto\mathbb{E}[Z]-1\) is continuous and the orthogonal complement of a linear space \(\mathcal{G}\) is always closed, \(\mathcal{Z}\subseteq L^2(\mathbb{P})\) is closed. Regarding convexity, it is clear that for \(\lambda\in [0,1]\)
\begin{equation*}
    \lambda\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}_1}{\diff \mathbb{P}}+(1-\lambda)\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}_2}{\diff \mathbb{P}}=\frac{\diff \widetilde{\mathbb{Q}}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}
\end{equation*}
with \(\widetilde{\mathbb{Q}}=\lambda\mathbb{Q}_1+(1-\lambda)\mathbb{Q}_2\in \mathbb{\mathbb{P}}^2_s(\mathcal{G})\).
\end{proof}

We now have all the technical results in order to prove Lemma \ref{lemma:no approximate profits}.
\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma \ref{lemma:no approximate profits}]
For the first equivalence, note that for any \(\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}_s^2(\mathcal{G})\) can be identified with the continuous linear form \(F\in L^2(\mathbb{P})^*\) given by 
\begin{equation*}
    F(Z)=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\diff\mathbb{Q}}{\diff\mathbb{P}}Z\bigg].
\end{equation*}
By definition of \(\mathbb{P}_s^2(\mathcal{G})\) we have that \(F(g)=0\) for all \(g\in\mathcal{G}\) and \(F(1)=1\). In particular,  assume that \(1\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}\). Thus, there exists \((f_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{G}\) such that \(\lim_{n\to\infty}F(f_n)=F(1)\). But \(F(f_n)=0,\;\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\), hence the contradiction. Conversely, assume \(1\notin\overline{\mathcal{G}}\). As \(\overline{\mathcal{G}}\) is by definition of the closure a closed subspace of \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\) the condition
\(1\notin\overline{\mathcal{G}}\) implies that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:distance from closed set}
    \inf_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\lVert g-1\rVert_{L^2}>0.
\end{equation}
Notice that \eqref{eq:distance from closed set} is not true in general if we merely required \(1\notin\mathcal{G}\).
By Lemma \ref{lemma:Hahn Banach Corollary} there exists a continuous linear form on \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\) such that \(F(g)=0,\;\forall g\in\mathcal{G}\) and  \(F(1)=1\). By the Riesz-Fischer theorem there exists an element \(Z_F\in L^2\) such that
\begin{equation*}
    F(X)=(Z_F,X)_{L^2}=\mathbb{E}[Z_FX],\;\forall X\in L^2.
\end{equation*}
Then 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{Q}(A)=\mathbb{E}[Z_F1_A],\quad\forall A\in\mathcal{F}
\end{equation*}
defines a signed measure on \((\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\) with \(Z=\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\). In particular, the properties of \(F\) yielded by Lemma \ref{lemma:Hahn Banach Corollary} assure that the signed measure constructed is in  \(\mathbb{P}_s^2(\mathcal{G})\). This shows the equivalence (1.).

For part (2.), first recall that there exists a statement closely related to the Hilbert projection theorem, asserting that for a closed, convex subset \(K\) of a Hilbert space, there exists a unique element in \(K\) with minimal norm (see \parencite[Thm.~1.44]{BuehlerSalamon2017FunctionalAnalysis}). Hence, the existence and uniqueness of the variance-optimal signed \(\mathbb{G}\)-martingale measure follows from Lemma \ref{lemma:Z is closed and convex} and the fact that, due to (1.), \(\mathcal{Z}\) is not empty.  We denote the variance-optimal signed \(\mathbb{G}\)-martingale measure by \(\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{G}}\). It remains to show that 
\begin{equation*}
\frac{d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{G}}}{d\mathbb{P}}\in\bar{\mathcal{A}}.
\end{equation*}
We do this as follows. We argue first that for any \(Z\in\mathcal{Z}\) the projection \(\widetilde{Z}\) of \(Z\) onto \(\overline{\mathcal{A}}\) is again in \(\mathcal{Z}\). If this is true, we have by Lemma \ref{lemma:cl(R+G)=R+cl(G)} that \(\widetilde{Z}=\widetilde{a}+\widetilde{g}\) for some \(\widetilde{a}\in\mathbb{R}\) and \(\widetilde{g}\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}\). Hence, 
\begin{equation*}
    (Z,\widetilde{Z})=(Z,\widetilde{a})+(Z,\widetilde{g})=\widetilde{a}=(\widetilde{Z},\widetilde{Z}).
\end{equation*}
which, in turn, implies that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dP_G/dP is in cl(A)}
    \lVert Z\rVert_{L^2}^2=\lVert Z-\widetilde{Z}\rVert_{L^2}^2+\lVert \widetilde{Z}\rVert_{L^2}^2\geq\lVert \widetilde{Z}\rVert_{L^2}^2,\quad\forall Z\in\mathcal{Z}.
\end{equation}
Hence, we must have \(\frac{\diff \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}\). Indeed, if not, \eqref{eq:dP_G/dP is in cl(A)} shows that there is an element in \(\mathcal{Z}\) with a smaller \(L^2\)-norm, contradicting the definition of \(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{G}}\). It remains to show the claim that \(\widetilde{Z}\), being the projection of \(Z\in\mathcal{Z}\) onto \(\overline{\mathcal{A}}\), is again in \(\mathcal{Z}\). This follows from the characterizing property of the orthogonal projection 
\begin{equation*}
    Z-\widetilde{Z}\in\big(\overline{\mathcal{A}}\big)^{\perp}.
\end{equation*}
with \(\overline{\mathcal{A}}=\mathbb{R}+\overline{\mathcal{G}}\). Hence, \(\mathbb{E}[(Z-\widetilde{Z})\cdot 1]=0\) implying
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{E}[\widetilde{Z}]=\mathbb{E}[Z]=1.
\end{equation*}
Furthermore, 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{E}\Big[\widetilde{Z}g\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[(\widetilde{Z}-Z)g\Big]=0,\quad\forall g\in\mathcal{G}
\end{equation*}
as \(Z-\widetilde{Z}\in(\overline{\mathcal{A}})^{\perp}\) and \(\mathcal{G}\subseteq
\overline{\mathcal{G}}\subsetneq\mathbb{R}+\overline{\mathcal{G}}\). This shows everything.
\end{proof}
The following observations further suggest that finding the variance-optimal \(\mathcal{G}\)-measure is, in some sense, dual to the problem of approximating risk-less profits in \(\mathcal{G}\):



For any $g\in\mathcal{G}$ and any $\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}^2_s(\mathcal{G})$, we have, by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,
\begin{equation*}
1 = \mathbb{E}_\mathbb{Q}[1-g]
= \mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{d\mathbb{Q}}{d\mathbb{P}}(1-g)\bigg]
\leq \left\lVert\frac{d\mathbb{Q}}{d\mathbb{P}}\right\rVert_{L^2}\lVert1-g\rVert_{L^2}.
\end{equation*}
In particular, the above implies, by taking \(g=0\), that \(\Big\lVert\frac{d\mathbb{Q}}{d\mathbb{P}}\Big\rVert_{L^2}\geq 1\) for all \(\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}^2_s(\mathcal{G})\) and thus \(\inf_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}^2_s(\mathcal{G})}\Big\lVert\frac{d\mathbb{Q}}{d\mathbb{P}}\Big\rVert_{L^2}\geq 1>0\). Therefore, we may write
\begin{equation}\label{eq:martingale measure heuristic}
\frac{1}{\inf\limits_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}^2_s(\mathcal{G})}\Big\lVert\frac{d\mathbb{Q}}{d\mathbb{P}}\Big\rVert_{L^2}}
=
\sup\limits_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}^2_s(\mathcal{G})}\frac{1}{\Big\lVert\frac{d\mathbb{Q}}{d\mathbb{P}}\Big\rVert_{L^2}}
\leq
\inf\limits_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\|1-g\|_{L^2}.
\end{equation}
As \(\mathcal{G}\) does not admit approximate profits, the right-hand side is strictly larger than \(0\). If this were not the case, there would exist a sequence in \(\mathcal{G}\) converging to \(1\) in \(L^2\). This implies that \(1\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{L^2}\) which contradicts the assumption. Nevertheless, one might ask how close one can get to the risk-less payoff equal to \(1\) by elements in \(\mathcal{G}\). Equation \eqref{eq:martingale measure heuristic} shows that the variance optimal measure yields a lower bound for this question. 



The following proposition ties the recently introduced concepts together and shows that, indeed, the mean-variance price can be characterized if \(\mathcal{G}\) does not admit approximate profits, and it is given by the variance optimal signed \(\mathcal{G}\)-measure in the following way.
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:mean variance price}
Suppose that $\mathcal{G}$ is a subspace of $L^2$ that admits no
approximate profits in $L^2$. If a contingent claim $H\in L^2$ admits a
$\mathcal{G}$-mean-variance optimal pair $(V_0^*,g^*)$, the
$\mathcal{G}$-approximation price of $H$ is given by
\[
V_0^*=\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathcal{G}}[H],
\]
where $\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathcal{G}}$ denotes expectation under the variance-optimal
signed $\mathcal{G}$-martingale measure $\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{G}}$.
\end{proposition}
We provide the proof, as it is rather short and ties together the recently introduced concepts.
\begin{proof}
    By Lemma \ref{lemma:no approximate profits} part (2.), if \(\mathcal{G}\) admits no approximate profits, we can project \(H\) orthogonally onto the \(L^2\)-closed subspace \(\overline{\mathcal{A}}=\mathbb{R}+\overline{\mathcal{G}}
    \) to get a \(\mathcal{G}\)-mean-variance solution of the form \(V_0^*+g^*\in \mathbb{R}+\overline{\mathcal{G}}\). In particular
    \begin{equation*}
        H-V_0^*-g^*\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}^{\perp}.
    \end{equation*}
   Because \(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{G}}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}\) by Lemma \ref{lemma:no approximate profits} (2.), we have 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{E}\bigg[(H-V_0^*-g^*)\frac{\diff \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\bigg]=0.
    \end{equation*}
    Hence, 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{E}\bigg[(H-g^*)\frac{\diff \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\bigg]=\mathbb{E}\bigg[V_0^*\frac{\diff \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\bigg]=V_0^*\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\diff \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\bigg]
    \end{equation*}
    because \(V_0^*\in\mathbb{R}\). But \(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{G}}\in\mathbb{P}^2_s(\mathcal{G})\), hence \(\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\diff\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\bigg]=1\) and \(\mathbb{E}\Big[g^*\frac{\diff\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\Big]=0\). We get
    \begin{equation*}
        V_0^*=\mathbb{E}\bigg[(H-g^*)\frac{\diff\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\bigg]=\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathcal{G}}[H].
    \end{equation*}
\end{proof}
Note that by Lemma \ref{lemma:no approximate profits} (2.) under the no-approximate profit assumption, the variance optimal signed \(\mathcal{G}\)-martingale measure is unique; hence, so is the mean-variance price \(V_0^*\) by Proposition \ref{prop:mean variance price}. Moreover, Proposition \ref{prop:mean variance price} yields a formula for its calculation. 
If we want a more explicit characterization of the mean variance strategy, we need to study conditions under which \(\mathcal{G}\) is closed in \(L^2\). For \(\mathcal{G}=G_{\infty}(\Theta)\), we investigate conditions on \(\Theta\subseteq\Theta^{(2)}\). The following objects play a key role in the study of the closedness of \(\mathcal{G}=G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) in \(L^2\). 




Let \((\tau_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) be a localizing sequence of \(X\in\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{loc}}\). We denote by \(\Theta_{\rm{simple}}\) the real span of predictable processes: \(\phi=\xi 1_{(\sigma,\tau]}\) for stopping times \(\sigma\leq\tau\) such that there exists \(n\) for which \(\tau\leq\tau_n\) and \(\xi\) are bounded and \(\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}\)-measurable. 


Consider the set of measures \(\mathbb{Q}\) such that \(\mathbb{Q}\approx\mathop{P}\) and \(\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}_s^2(G_{\infty}(\Theta_{\rm{simple}}))\).
We denote this set by \(\mathbb{P}^2_e(X)\). We can characterize \(\mathbb{P}^2_e(X)\) by the set of equivalent local martingale measures with square integrable density as follows.


\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:ELMM and P_e^2(X)}
    \(\mathbb{Q}\) is an ELMM for X with \(\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\in L^2(\mathbb{P})\) if and only if \(\mathbb{Q}\) is in \(\mathbb{P}_e^2(X)\).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    We show both directions. First, assume \(\mathbb{Q}\) is ELMM for \(X\) with \(\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\in L^2(\mathbb{P})\). 
    Let \(g\in G_{\infty}(\Theta_{\rm{simple}})\) and \(\delta_n\) be stopping times forming the localizing sequence of \(X\) such that \(X^{\delta_n}\) is a \(\mathbb{Q}\)-martingale. Without loss of generality, assume \(g=\xi(X_{\tau}-X_{\sigma})\) for two stopping times \(\sigma\leq\tau\) and \(\xi\) being an \(\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}\)-measurable random variable. The general claim for a linear combination of such processes follows from the linearity of expectation. 
    Furthermore, by definition of \(g\in G_{\infty}(\Theta_{\rm{simple}})\) there exists \(N\in\mathbb{N}\) large enough such that \(\sigma\leq\tau\leq \tau_N\), where \((\tau_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) is the localizing sequence of \(X\) such that \(X^{\tau_n}\) is square-integrable, that is 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\Big[\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}|X_t^{\tau_n}|^2\Big]<\infty.
    \end{equation*}
    Because a stopped martingale is again a martingale, \(X^{\tau_N}\) is also a local \(\mathbb{Q}\)-martingale for the same localizing sequence \((\delta_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\). Hence, 
    \(X^{\delta_n\wedge \tau_N}=(X^{\tau_N})^{\delta_n}=(X^{\delta_n})^{\tau_N}\) is square integrable with respect to \(\mathbb{P}\) and a martingale on \((\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{Q})\).
 Denoting \(\psi_n\coloneqq \tau_N\wedge \delta_n\), we have
    \begin{align}\label{eq:common localizing sequence}
        \mathbb{E}_\mathbb{P}\bigg[\Big(\frac{\diff\mathbb{Q}}{\diff\mathbb{P}}\Big)\xi(X_{\tau\wedge\psi_n}-X_{\sigma\wedge\psi_n})\bigg]&
        =\mathbb{E}_\mathbb{Q}\Big[\xi((X^{\psi_n})_{\tau}-(X^{\psi_n})_{\sigma})\Big]\notag\\&
        =\mathbb{E}_\mathbb{Q}\Big[\xi\mathbb{E}_\mathbb{Q}[(X^{\psi_n})_{\tau}-(X^{\psi_n})_{\sigma}|\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}]\Big]=0,
    \end{align}
    where the last equality follows from the optional stopping theorem and the fact that, by assumption, \(X^{\psi_n}\) is a \(\mathbb{Q}\)-martingale. More precisely, the \(\mathbb{Q}\)-local martingale \(X^{\psi_n}\) is a \(\mathbb{Q}\)-uniformly integrable martingale. Indeed 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\big[(X^{\psi_n})^{*}_{\infty}\big]\leq \bigg\lVert \frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\bigg\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})}\big\lVert (X^{\tau_N})^{*}_{\infty}\big\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})}<\infty,
    \end{equation*}
    implies by \parencite[Thm.~51,Ch.I]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration} that \(X^{\psi_n}\) is uniformly integrable with respect to \(\mathbb{Q}\). This also justifies the previous application of the optional stopping theorem. 
    Furthermore, we have
    \begin{equation*}
        |X_{\tau\wedge\psi_n}-X_{\sigma\wedge\psi_n}|\leq2\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}|X^{\tau\wedge\psi_n}_{t}|\leq2\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}|X^{\tau_N}_{t}|,
    \end{equation*}
    which is in \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\) by assumption. Furthermore, \(\xi\) is bounded and \(\frac{\diff\mathbb{Q}}{\diff\mathbb{P}}\in L^2(\mathbb{P})\). In particular, their product is in \(L^1(\mathbb{P})\) by Cauchy-Schwarz. Thus, we can pass to the limit \(n\to\infty\) in \eqref{eq:common localizing sequence} by dominated convergence to get 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{E}_\mathbb{P}\Big[\Big(\frac{\diff\mathbb{Q}}{\diff\mathbb{P}}\Big)\xi(X_{\tau}-X_{\sigma})\Big]=0
    \end{equation*}
    as \(\tau\wedge\psi_n=\tau\wedge \tau_N\wedge\delta_n=\tau\wedge\delta_n\).
 Conversely, assume \(\mathbb{Q}\) is in \(\mathbb{P}_e^2(X)\) that is  \(\mathbb{Q}\approx \mathbb{P}\) and \(\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}_s^2(G_{\infty}(\Theta_{\rm{simple}})\). We want to show that \(X\) is a local \(\mathbb{Q}\)-martingale. Choose as a candidate for the respective localizing sequence the sequence \((\tau_n)_{n\mathbb{N}}\). Due to Cauchy-Schwarz,
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{E}_\mathbb{Q}\big[|X_{t\wedge \tau_n}|\big]\leq\bigg\lVert\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\bigg\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})}\lVert X_{t\wedge \tau_n}\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})}<\infty.
    \end{equation*}
    For the martingale property, we need to show that
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{E}_\mathbb{Q}[X^{\tau_n}_t|\mathcal{F}_s]=X^{\tau_n}_s,\quad \mathbb{Q}\text{-a.s.},\forall n\in\mathbb{N}
    \end{equation*}
    for \(s\leq t\). 
 Equivalently, we want to show
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{E}_\mathbb{Q}[1_A(X^{\tau_n}_t-X^{\tau_n}_s)]=0,\quad \forall A\in\mathcal{F}_s.
    \end{equation*}
    for \(s\leq t\). Observe, that on the event \(\left\{\tau_n\leq s\right\}\), we have \(X_{\tau_n\wedge t}-X_{\tau_n\wedge s}=0\). Hence, it is enough to show that 
    \begin{equation*}
          \mathbb{E}_\mathbb{Q}[1_{A\cap \left\{s>\tau_n\right\}}(X^{\tau_n}_t-X^{\tau_n}_s)]=0\quad \forall A\in\mathcal{F}_s.
    \end{equation*}
    This follows directly from the observation that 
    for \(\sigma = \tau_n\wedge s\) and \(\tau = \tau_n\wedge t\) we find
    \begin{equation*}
        1_{A\cap\left\{\tau_n>s\right\}}(X^{\tau_n}_t-X^{\tau_n}_s)\in G_{\infty}(\Theta_{\rm{simple}}).
    \end{equation*}
    Indeed, notice that 
    \begin{equation*}
        A\cap\left\{\tau_n>s\right\}\in\mathcal{F}_{\tau_n\wedge s}.
    \end{equation*}
    We check this by the definition of the stopped \(\sigma\)-algebra (see \eqref{eq:stopped sigma-aglebra}). Let \(r\geq 0\) and consider \(A\cap\left\{\tau_n>s\right\}\cap\left\{\tau_n\wedge s\leq r\right\}=A\cap\left\{\tau_n>s\right\}\cap\left\{s\leq r\right\}\).
    If \(s\leq r\) the latter is equal to \(A\cap\left\{\tau_n>s\right\}\). In this case, \(A\) and \(\left\{\tau_n>s\right\}\) are in \(\mathcal{F}_s\subseteq\mathcal{F}_r\). If \(s>r\) the latter is equal to \(\emptyset\in\mathcal{F}_r\). This shows that \(A\cap\left\{\tau_n>s\right\}\in\mathcal{F}_{\tau_n\wedge s}\). Furthermore, we have \(\sigma\leq\tau\leq \tau_n\) for every \(n\in\mathbb{N}\). This shows everything.
     \end{proof}
     
    \begin{definition}
        The \textit{variance optimal signed martingale measure} \(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}\) for \(X\) is defined as the variance optimal signed \(G_{\infty}(\Theta_{\rm{simple}})\)-martingale measure.
    \end{definition}
    \(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}\) is generally a signed measure. The next lemma shows that for continuous \(X\), it is in \(\mathbb{P}^2_e(X)\); hence, it is particularly a probability measure. \begin{lemma}\label{lemma:variance optimal signed measure in P_e^2(X)}
        If \(X\in\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{loc}}\) is continuous and \(\mathbb{P}^2_e(X)\neq \emptyset\), then \(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}\in\mathbb{P}_e^2(X)\).
    \end{lemma}
    Recall that we have as a standing assumption that \(X\in\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{loc}}\). For this price-process, the natural class of admissible strategies has been introduced in 
    \eqref{eq:X integrable processes for X in S^2} as the space \(L^2(M,A)\) of \(X\)-integrable processes. Fixing this choice for the space of admissible strategies \(\Theta=L^2(M,A)\), we provide necessary and sufficient conditions for the closedness of \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) in \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\). 
Before its statement, however, we need one additional ingredient.
\begin{definition}\label{def:reverse Hölder}
If $M$ is a uniformly integrable martingale such that $M_0=1$ and $M_{\infty}>0$ $\mathbb{P}$-a.s., then we say that
$M$ \emph{satisfies the reverse Hölder inequality under $\mathbb{P}$}, denoted by $\mathcal{R}_p(\mathbb{P})$, where
$1<p\le +\infty$, if and only if there is a constant $C$ such that for every $t$, we have
\[
\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(\frac{M_{\infty}}{M_t}\bigg)^p \bigg|\, \mathcal{F}_t\bigg]\leq C.
\]
For \(p=+\infty\) we require that \(\frac{M_{\infty}}{M_t}\) is bounded by \(C\). 
\end{definition}
We say that an ELMM \(\mathbb{Q}\) satisfies \(\mathcal{R}_p(\mathbb{P})\) if the respective density process, defined as follows 
\begin{equation*}
    Z_t\coloneqq \mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\bigg|\mathcal{F}_t\bigg],
\end{equation*}
satisfies \(\mathcal{R}_p(\mathbb{P})\).
We can finally state the key theorem of this section.
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:closedness of G_T(Theta)}
Let \(X\in\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{loc}}\) be continuous and \(\Theta=L^2(M,A)\) as defined in \eqref{eq:X integrable processes for X in S^2}. Then the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \(\mathbb{P}_e^2(X)\neq\emptyset\) and
 $G_{\infty}(\Theta)$ is closed in $L^2(\mathbb{P})$.
\item There exists some \(\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}_e^2(X)\) satisfying the inequality
      $\mathcal{R}_2(\mathbb{P})$.
\item The variance-optimal martingale measure \(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}\) is in \(\mathbb{\mathbb{P}}_e^2(X)\) and satisfies the \(\mathcal{R}^2(\mathbb{P})\)-inequality.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
For a proof, discussion, and the complete statement, we refer to \parencite[Thm.~4.1]{DelbaenMonatSchachermayerSchweizerStricker1997WeightedNormInequalities}.
Theorem \ref{thm:closedness of G_T(Theta)} yields necessary and sufficient conditions for the existence of a mean-variance portfolio. It will be needed in that sense for the statement and proof of Proposition \ref{prop:continuous time deep mean variance hedging for semimartingales under compactness}. 

    
\chapter{Approximation by algorithmic strategies in continuous time}\label{ch:Approximation by Algorithmic Strategies in Continuous Time}
\section{The naive approach and what goes wrong}\label{sec:The naïv approach and what goes wrong}
In this chapter, we present in detail the approach laid out in 
\parencite[Chapter~5]{Arandjelovic2025}. The latter should be understood as an extension of the setup in \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging} to a continuous time grid setup. It leverages similar ideas developed in the latter, such as the Doob-Dynkin representation of measurable functions and the density of neural networks, but in the context of continuous time stochastic calculus. One must deal with measurability and regularity issues not present in the finite setup; nevertheless, the core ideas remain similar. In this section, we guide the reader through this extension with the goal of arriving at a neural approximation theory for wealth processes of self-financing strategies, and in particular, a continuous time version of the deep-hedging result stated in Proposition \ref{prop:finite deep mean variance hedging}. This will lead us to a continuous time deep-mean-variance theorem in the square-integrable martingale setup. In addition, we show how to improve the integrability condition of this deep-mean-variance result \parencite[Thm.~5.32.]{Arandjelovic2025}. 


Upon closer inspection of the proof of Proposition \ref{prop:finite deep mean variance hedging}, we can infer that one of the crucial properties used is the fact that approximating the self-financing strategy \(\vartheta\) point-wise, for every time, also approximates the respective wealth processes. In the finite case, this is trivial, as the wealth processes are finite sums over the strategy at different times. More generally, however, this is a statement about the continuity of the integral map
\begin{align}\label{eq:stochastic integral map}
I:  \vartheta\mapsto \vartheta\bullet X.
\end{align}
As we have seen in Section \ref{sec:Self financing strategies in continuous times}, such continuity statements exist for stochastic integrals in different settings and with respect to different topologies. Broadly speaking, the general strategy is to approximate strategies \(\vartheta\) by finding a subset \(\mathcal{D}\) such that \(\overline{\mathcal{D}}=\Theta\), where the closure is taken in the topology in which \eqref{eq:stochastic integral map} is continuous. If \(\Theta\) is first-countable, which is the case for all instances of \(\Theta\) in this thesis, we can find an approximating sequence \((\vartheta_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) such that \(\lim_{n\to\infty}\vartheta_n=\vartheta\) for any \(\vartheta\in\Theta\). By the continuity of the integral map \eqref{eq:stochastic integral map}, we then get that \(\vartheta_n\bullet X\) approximates \(\vartheta\bullet X\) as well. Now, this does not tell us how to construct the dense subset \(D\) in any way. 


The main insight of \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging} is that one can construct \(\mathcal{D}\) by finding a suitable approximation system of deterministic functions to approximate the Doob-Dynkin function of \(\vartheta_t\) for every \(t\in\mathbb{T}_+\). The most direct approach to generalizing \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging} to the uncountable setting where \((\vartheta_t)_{t\in\mathbb{
R}_+}\) would be to do the same but for the uncountable collection of random variables \((\vartheta_t)_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\). To gain a deeper intuition for the approach taken in \parencite{Arandjelovic2025} and presented in Section \ref{sec:algorithmically generated random variables}, we take this 'naive' approach as a heuristics and argue why it is not the appropriate strategy to generalize \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging}. 



In the following examples, we take \(T<\infty\) for simplicity. Let \(\vartheta\) be such that 
\begin{equation*}
      \vartheta_t\in L^2(\mathbb{P}),\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+.
\end{equation*}
Furthermore, we assume that the market information process, viewed as a path-valued random variable, \(Y:\Omega\to \mathbb{R}^{[0,T]}\) takes values in the space of continuous functions \(C[0,T]\) equipped with the topology of uniform convergence  and equipped with the Borel-\(\sigma\)-algebra. In order to keep the notation light, we set \(E=C[0,T]\). In this case, one can show that \(\mathcal{B}(E)=\sigma(E^*)\) and \(\sigma(Y_s:0\leq s \leq t)=\sigma(Y^t)\) (see the proof of Proposition \ref{prop:reduction to countably generated sigma algebra is not fundamental, product topology} and \ref{prop:reduction to countably generated sigma algebra is not fundamental in the case of E = C} for a more detailed justification). As usual, we consider the filtration \(\mathbb{F}^0=(\mathcal{F}^0_t)_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\), where \(\mathcal{F}^0_t=\sigma(Y_t:0\leq s\leq t )\) on a complete probability space \((\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\). We can then consider the following heuristics



Consider a \(\mathbb{F}\)-predictable strategy \(\vartheta\). In particular, \(\vartheta\) is \(\mathbb{F}\)-adapted; thus, for every \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\) we have, by Doob-Dynkin, that there exists some \(\mathcal{B}(E)/\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})\)-measurable \(f^t:E\to\mathbb{R}\) such that
\begin{equation*}
    \vartheta_t=f_t(Y^t),
\end{equation*}
where \(Y^t=(Y_{s\wedge t})_{s\in\mathbb{R}_+}\) denotes the stopped process, as usual. Notice, crucially, the dependence in \(t\) of the map \(f_t\). By assumption, 
\(
    \vartheta_t\in L^2(\mathbb{P})
\) for all \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\).
Thus, we have
\begin{equation*}
    f^t\in L^2(E,\mathcal{B}(E),\mathbb{P}\circ(Y^t)^{-1}),\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+,
\end{equation*}
where we used \(\mathcal{B}(E)=\sigma(E^*)\).
By Theorem \ref{thm:density of NN in L^p on topological vector space}, we can approximate \(f_t\) by a sequence of neural networks \((f_t^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{NN}_\psi(E)\) in \(L^2(E,\mathcal{B}(E),\mathbb{P}\circ(Y^t)^{-1})\). Setting \(\vartheta^n_t\coloneqq f^n_t(Y^t)\), we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:approximating sequence pointwise}
    \vartheta_t^n\to\vartheta_t,\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+
\end{equation}
in \(L^2(E,\mathcal{B}(E),\mathbb{P}\circ(Y^t)^{-1})\),
where \(\vartheta_t^n\) is \(\mathcal{F}_t\)-measurable.
As discussed in the introductory part of this chapter, the continuity of the integral map is crucial for deep-hedging results. In particular, for an approximating sequence, as in \eqref{eq:approximating sequence pointwise}, converging to \(\vartheta\), the integrals \(\vartheta^n\bullet X\) need to be, at the very least, well defined. As introduced in Section \ref{sec:Stochastic Integration for square-integrable Martingales} and Section \ref{sec:stochastic integration with respect to semimartingales}, the broadest class of integrable processes is the set of predictable processes. The Doob-Dynkin-based construction of \(\vartheta_t^n\) is fundamentally pointwise in \(t\). Predictability, however, is a measurability condition on the product space \(\Omega\times[0,T]\). Hence, one would need to justify why \(\vartheta^n=(\vartheta^n_t)_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\) can be chosen to be predictable. 


This motivates the approach taken in \parencite[Ch.5]{Arandjelovic2025} and presented in the following subsection.



\section{From measurable random variables to algorithmically generated strategies}\label{sec:main ideas of alexandar}
 In light of the issues found in Section \ref{sec:The naïv approach and what goes wrong} and the central importance of the continuity of the integral map 
 \begin{equation*}
     \vartheta\mapsto I_X(\vartheta),
 \end{equation*}
 we take the following approach inspired by the construction of the stochastic integral, building on the respective notions for simple processes, as discussed in Section \ref{sec:From simple processes to stochastic integral} and \ref{sec:stochastic integration with respect to semimartingales}. Instead of approximating the integrands directly or pointwise in time, we approximate the simple processes. Clearly, this allows for an approximation of the respective notions of the stochastic integral of these simple processes; see \ref{def:integral of simple processes} and \eqref{def:simple integration}. The crucial insight is that the stochastic integral for the respective classes of integrands \(L^2(M)\) and \(\mathbb{L}\) is obtained by \textbf{continuous} extension. Hence, if we are able to approximate simple and elementary predictable processes, we can approximate the respective stochastic integral in the respective topologies. We will see that we can do this in a way that provides a predictable approximation of \(\vartheta_t\), preventing us from running into similar problems as encountered in Approaches 1 and 2.

 
More precisely, recall the representation of simple predictable processes introduced in Definition \ref{def:simple predictable process}
 \begin{equation*}
     \vartheta_t = \varphi_01_{\left\{ 0\right\}}+\sum_{k=0}^n \varphi^k1_{(\tau_k,\tau_{k+1}]},
 \end{equation*}
 for a non-decreasing family of stopping times \(0<\tau_1,\dots<\tau_n<\infty\) and \(\varphi_i\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}_{\tau_k},\mathbb{P})\). The idea is to approximate the Doob-Dynkin function of the random variables \(\varphi_i\) rather than directly approximating the Doob-Dynkin function of \(\vartheta_t\). The resulting processes
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:neural simple predictable processes}
     \vartheta_t = \varphi_n^01_{\left\{ 0\right\}}+\sum_{k=0}^n \varphi_n^k1_{(\tau_k,\tau_{k+1}]},
 \end{equation}
 for "neural" approximations \(\varphi_n^k\) of \(\varphi^k\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}_{\tau_k},\mathbb{P})\) are, again, predictable by construction. It remains to show that the class of such neural approximations of \(\vartheta=(\vartheta_t)_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\) is rich enough to develop stochastic integration.
 Note that the terminology used for \eqref{eq:neural simple predictable processes} is only used in this introductory section and will be substituted for more appropriate and precise terminology throughout this section. This section closely follows and comments on the results of \parencite[Chapter.~5]{Arandjelovic2025}.


 \subsection{Algorithmically generated random variables}\label{sec:algorithmically generated random variables}
 In Section \ref{sec:The deep-hedging framework of Bühler et al.} we didn't need to bother with measurability issues, as all the notions were finite linear combinations of measurable objects on a finite sample space with a finite time grid. The continuous time grid generalization, however, requires careful consideration regarding measurability. 

 
 The first part of this section is devoted to understanding whether and how we can reduce measurability with respect to an uncountably generated \(\sigma\)-algebra to measurability with respect to a countably generated one. This will be handy as it will enable us to consider the usual Doob-Dynkin representation of countably many random variables. As we will see in Proposition \ref{prop:reduction to countably generated sigma algebra is not fundamental, product topology} and \ref{prop:reduction to countably generated sigma algebra is not fundamental in the case of E = C}, under sufficient assumptions, this reduction to measurability with respect to a countably generated \(\sigma\)-algebra can be circumvented. Nevertheless, it is of independent interest, and one may argue that it yields a more direct generalization of \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging}.

 
 In all that follows, we recall that the underlying probability space 
 \begin{equation*}
     (\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
 \end{equation*}
 is assumed to be complete.
 \begin{proposition}\label{prop:reduction to countable measurability}
     Let \((\Omega,\mathcal{F})\) and \((S,\mathcal{S})\) be measurable spaces. Let \(I\) be a non-empty set and \((F_i)_{i\in I}\) be an indexed family of sub-\(\sigma\)-algebras of \(\mathcal{F}\). For any subset \(J\subseteq I\), we define \(\mathcal{F}_J=\sigma(\bigcup_{j\in J}\mathcal{F}_j)\). Let \(X:\Omega\to S\) be a \(\mathcal{F}_I/\mathcal
     S\)-measurable map such that the restricted \(\sigma\)-algebra on \(X(\Omega)\); \(\mathcal{S}_X\coloneqq\left\{B\cap X(\Omega):B\in \mathcal{S}\right\}\) is \textbf{countably} generated. Then there exists a \textbf{countable} subset \(J\subseteq I\) such that \(X\) is \(F_{J}/\mathcal{S}\)-measurable. 
 \end{proposition}
 \begin{remark}\label{Remark:countably generated sigma algebra}
      Notice that the claim is not only that we can reduce measurability to a countably generated \(\sigma\)-algebra, but that it has the form \(\mathcal{F}_J=\sigma(\bigcup_{j\in J}\mathcal{F}_j)\) for \(J\subseteq I\) countable. The mere fact that for countably generated \(\mathcal{S}_X\) one can find a sub-\(\sigma\)-algebra \(\mathcal{F}_X\subseteq\mathcal{F}_I\) that is countably generated follows easily. Indeed, as \(\mathcal{S}_X\) is countably generated, there exists a sequence \((S_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) in \(\mathcal{S}\) such that \(\mathcal{S}_X=\sigma(S_n\cap X(\Omega):n\in\mathbb{N})\). Setting 
 \(\mathcal{F}_X=\sigma(X^{-1}(S_n):n\in\mathbb{N})\), we have that \(\mathcal{F}_X\subseteq\mathcal{F}_I\) and that \(X\) is \(\mathcal{F}_X\)-measurable. Indeed, this is part of the proof of Proposition \ref{prop:reduction to countable measurability}. 
 \end{remark}
The claim that one can find a countably generated \(\sigma\)-algebra of the form \(\mathcal{F}_J\) is  due to the following general property.
 \begin{lemma}\label{lemma:reduction to countable measurability}
     Let \(I\) be a non-empty set and \(\mathcal{F}_J=\sigma(\bigcup_{j\in J}\mathcal{F}_j)\) for any \(J\subseteq I\). Then 
     \begin{equation}\label{eq:reduction to countable measurability}
         \mathcal{F}_I=\bigcup_{\substack{J\subseteq I\\ J\text{ countable}}}\mathcal{F}_J.
     \end{equation}
 \end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
 Part of the claim is that the right-hand side is a \(\sigma\)-algebra. For notational convenience in this proof, we denote the right hand side of \eqref{eq:reduction to countable measurability} by \(\mathcal{G}\). We check that \(\mathcal{G}\) is a \(\sigma\)-algebra.
 Indeed, \(\Omega\in \mathcal{F}_J\) for every countable \(J\subseteq I\); in particular, \(\Omega\in \mathcal{G}\). If \(A\in\mathcal{G}\), then there exists a countable \(J\subseteq I\) such that \(A\in\mathcal{F}_J\). Hence, \(A^c\in\mathcal{F}_J\), thus, \(A^c\in\mathcal{G}\). Consider a sequence \((A_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) in \(\mathcal{G}\). 
 Then \(\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\), there is a \(J_n\subseteq I\) countable such that \(A_n\in \mathcal{F}_{J_n}\). Observe that by the definition of \(\mathcal{F}_J\), for a countable \(J_1\subseteq J_2\subseteq I\), we have \(\mathcal{F}_{J_1}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{J_2}\). Thus, setting \(J=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}J_n\), which is countable as a countable union of countable sets, we have \(\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}A_n\in\mathcal{G}\). Hence, \(\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}A_n\in\mathcal{F}_{J}\). \(\mathcal{G}\) is a \(\sigma\)-algebra. 



 To show equality, it suffices to show that 
 \begin{equation*}
     \mathcal{F}_I\subseteq \bigcup_{\substack{J\subseteq I\\ J\text{ countable}}}\mathcal{F}_J.
 \end{equation*}
Indeed, the converse is clear as \(A\in\mathcal{G}\) if and only if there exists a countable \(J\subset I\) such that \(A\in\mathcal{F}_J\), but \(\mathcal{F}_J\subseteq \mathcal{F}_I\).
By definition, \(\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}_i\) generates \(\mathcal{F}_I\); hence, it is enough to show that this generating set is in \(\mathcal{G}\). For \(B\in\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}_i\), there exists an \(i\in I\) such that \(B\in \mathcal{F}_i\subseteq\mathcal{G}\), as the singleton \(\left\{i\right\}\) is a countable subset of \(I\). This concludes the proof. 
\end{proof}
 
 Before proceeding with the proof of Proposition \ref{prop:reduction to countable measurability}, recall that the Borel \(\sigma\)-algebra of a separable metric space is countably generated; hence, the above can be applied to \((S,\mathcal{B}(S))\), for a separable metric space \(S\) (see Appendix \ref{Appendix:Borel sigma-algebra of separable metric space is countably generated} for a proof).
 Furthermore, we will be especially interested in the case where \(I=\mathbb{R}_+\) and \(\mathcal{F}^0_t=\sigma(Y_t:0\leq s\leq t)\) for some stochastic process \(Y\). This is a valid choice regarding the assumptions in Proposition \ref{prop:reduction to countable measurability} as for \(J=[0,t]\subseteq\mathbb{R}_+\) we have \(\mathcal{F}^0_t=\sigma(\bigcup_{s\in[0,t]}\mathcal{F}^0_t)=\sigma(Y_t:0\leq s\leq t)\). Taking \((S,\mathcal{S})\) to be \((\mathbb{R},\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}))\) and denoting \(\mathcal{F}^0_{\infty}=
\sigma(\bigcup_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\mathcal{F}_t)\),  Proposition \ref{prop:reduction to countable measurability} implies, in particular, that for the real-valued random variable X, which is \(\mathcal{F}_{\infty}/\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})\)-measurable, there exists a countable subset \(\left\{t_1,t_2,\dots\right\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_+\) such that \(X\) is \(\sigma(Y_{t_1},Y_{t_2},\dots)/\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})\)-measurable.
 We proceed with the proof of Proposition \ref{prop:reduction to countable measurability}
 \begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition \ref{prop:reduction to countable measurability}]
 The case where \(I\) is countable is clear, so we assume \(I\) to be uncountable.
 The first part follows as suggested in \cref{Remark:countably generated sigma algebra}. We restate it for completeness. By assumption, \(\mathcal{S}_X\) is countably generated. Hence, there exists a sequence \((S_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) in \(\mathcal{S}\) such that \(\mathcal{S}_X=\sigma(S_n\cap X(\Omega):n\in\mathbb{N})\). 
 Clearly, \(X\) is \(\mathcal{F}_X\)-measurable. Now we conclude by Lemma \ref{lemma:reduction to countable measurability}. We have \(\mathcal{F}_X\subseteq\mathcal{F}_I\), or equivalently
 \begin{equation*}
     \sigma(X^{-1}(S_n):S_n\in\mathcal{S})\subseteq\bigcup_{\substack{J\subseteq I\\ J\text{ countable}}}\mathcal{F}_J.
 \end{equation*}
 In particular, for every \(n\in\mathbb{N}\), there exists a countable set \(J_n\subseteq I\) such that
 \(X^{-1}(S_n)\in \mathcal{F}_{J_n}\). Hence, setting \(J=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}J_n\) we have 
 \begin{equation*}
     X^{-1}(S_n)\in\mathcal{F}_J\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{N},
 \end{equation*}
 where \(J\), a countable union of countable sets, is countable.
 But \(\mathcal{F}_X=\sigma(X^{-1}(S_n):S_n\in\mathcal{S})\) is the smallest \(\sigma\)-algebra for which \(X^{-1}(S_n)\), for all \(n\in\mathbb{N}\), are measurable, thus \(\mathcal{F}_X\subseteq \mathcal{F}_J\). We conclude by recalling again that \(X\) is \(\mathcal{F}_X\) and thus also \(\mathcal{F}_J\)-measurable.
   \end{proof}
As mentioned, we will be especially interested in the case where \(I=\mathbb{R}_+\) and where the collection of \(\sigma\)-algebras \((\mathcal{F}_i)_{i\in I }\) is given by the natural filtration of the market information process \(Y\), that is \(\mathcal{F}^0_t=\{Y_t:0\leq s\leq t\}\). Then \(\mathbb{F}=\{\mathcal{F}^0_t\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\) defines a filtration on \((\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\). It is standard and advantageous to work with a filtration satisfying the usual hypotheses: right-continuity and completeness. We recall from \eqref{eq:completed filtration} that the completion can be done canonically by defining \(\mathcal{F}_t=\sigma(\mathcal{F}_t^0\cup\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}})\), where \(\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\) is as in \eqref{eq:nullsets}.  We denote the completed filtration by \(\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_t)_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\). 
Furthermore, we recall at this point the notion of a stopping time. 
\begin{definition}\label{def:stopping time}
   We call a \(\mathbb{R}_+\cup \{+\infty\}\)-valued random variable \(\tau\) a stopping time for the filtration \(\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\) or \(\mathbb{F}\)-stopping time if, for every \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\), we have
\begin{equation*}
    \left\{\omega\in\Omega:\tau(\omega)\leq t\right\}\in\mathcal{F}_t.
\end{equation*}
We say that \(\tau\) is a finite stopping time if \(\tau:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}_+\), and we say its a bounded stopping time if \(\sup_{\omega\in\Omega}\tau(\omega)<\infty\).
\end{definition}
Furthermore, we define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stopped sigma-aglebra}
    \mathcal{F}_{\tau}\coloneqq \big\{A\in\mathcal{F}:A\cap\{\tau\leq t\}\in\mathcal{F}_t,\;\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+\big\}.
\end{equation}
Given a stochastic process \(X=(X_t)_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\), we might consider the stopped process 
\begin{equation*}
    X^{\tau}=(X^{\tau}_t)_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}
\end{equation*}
with \(X^{\tau}_t(\omega)\coloneqq X(\omega)^{\tau(\omega)}_t\). For the natural filtration of a process \(X\), an obvious question might be how \(\mathcal{F}_{\tau}\) in \eqref{eq:stopped sigma-aglebra} relates to the \(\sigma\)-algebra generated by the stopped process \(X^{\tau}\). In general, they are not the same, but under additional assumptions on \(\Omega\) or \(X\), they are. According to \parencite[Thm.~6]{Shiryaev2008OptimalStoppingRules}, the two \(\sigma\)-algebras coincide if \(\Omega\) is sufficiently rich in the sense that for every \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\) and \(\omega\in\Omega\) there exists \(\omega'\in\Omega\) such that \(X_{t\wedge s}(\omega)=X_{s}(\omega')\). Another case where both \(\sigma\)-algebras are closely related, or more precisely their completed versions, is when \(X\) is a Lévy-process. In this case, we have by \parencite[Thm.~13.45]{HeWangYan1992} that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stopped natural sigma algebra}
    \mathcal{F}_{\tau}=\sigma(\tau)\vee\sigma\Big(\bigvee_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\sigma(X^{\tau}_t)\vee\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\Big).
\end{equation}
We studied the completion of natural filtrations and the \(\sigma\)-algebras of stopped processes, but we have not touched on the second assumption of the usual hypotheses; right continuity.
For the latter, we refer to \parencite[Thm.~31,Ch.I]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}, stating that for Lévy-processes, the completed natural filtration of \(Y\) is automatically right-continuous, thus satisfies the usual hypotheses. Thus, we assume that \(Y\) is Lévy. The above implies that the corresponding completed natural filtration satisfies the usual hypotheses and behaves nicely under stopping in the sense that the stopped \(\sigma\)-algebra, based on the natural filtration of \(Y\), is of the form \eqref{eq:stopped natural sigma algebra}.



As motivated in the introduction of the current chapter, our goal is to develop an approximation theory for \(\mathcal{F_{\tau}}\)-measurable, bounded random variables, where \(\tau\) is a stopping time for the filtration \(\mathbb{F}\), and where \(\mathbb{F}\) is the completed filtration generated by a Lévy process \(Y\).
Thanks to Proposition \ref{prop:reduction to countable measurability}, we now have the machinery to do so. Throughout this chapter, we assume that the standing assumption \ref{def:standing assumptions} holds. The following corollary is essentially a special case of Proposition \ref{prop:reduction to countable measurability}. 
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:reduction to countable measurability with filtration}
    In the setup of Proposition \ref{prop:reduction to countable measurability} with \(Y\) a stochastic process and filtrations \(\mathbb{F}^0=(\mathcal{F}^0_t)_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\) and \(\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_t)_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\), where \(\mathcal{F}^0_t=\sigma(Y_t:0\leq s\leq t)\) and \(\mathcal{F}_t=\sigma(Y_t:0\leq s\leq t)\vee\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\) respectively. Then
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
        \item For fixed \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\cup\{\infty\}\), let \(X:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}\) be \(\mathcal{F}^0_t\)-measurable. Then, there exists a \textit{countable} set \(J\subseteq [0,t]\cap\mathbb{R}_+\) such that \(X\) is \(\mathcal{F}_J\)-measurable, where \(\mathcal{F}_J = \sigma(Y_t:t\in J)\). 
        \item Assume Y is a Lévy process. Let \(\tau\) be a \(\mathbb{F}\)-stopping time. Furthermore, let \(X:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}\) be \(\mathcal{F}_{\tau}\)-measurable. Then, there exists a countable set \(J\subseteq\mathbb{R}_+\) such that \(X\) is \(\mathcal{F}_J\)-measurable, where \(\mathcal{F}_J=\sigma(\tau)\vee\sigma(Y_t^{\tau}:t\in J)\vee \mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\).
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    The proof is an application of Proposition \ref{prop:reduction to countable measurability} with different choices of \(I\) and \((\mathcal{F}_i)_{i\in I}\). For (a) take \(I=\mathbb{R}_+\) and \(\mathcal{F}_i=\sigma(Y_i)\) for \(i\in I\). For (b), by \parencite[Thm.~13.45]{HeWangYan1992}, we have \(\mathcal{F}_{\tau}=\sigma(\tau)\vee\sigma(Y_t^{\tau}:t\in J)\vee \mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\) as \(Y\) is assumed to be Lévy. Take \(I=\mathbb{R}_+\cup\left\{a,b\right\}\) and \(\mathcal{F}_i=\sigma(Y_i^{\tau})\) for \(i\in \mathbb{R}_+\), \(\mathcal{F}_a=\sigma(\tau)\), and \(\mathcal{F}_b=\sigma(\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}})\). 
\end{proof}
Proposition \ref{prop:reduction to countable measurability with filtration} now motivates the following definition, which is the building block for our approximation theory of simple bounded predictable processes based on neural networks. 
\begin{definition}\label{def:algorithmic strategies}
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Assume that \(Y\) takes values in a topological vector space \(E\). We call an \(\sigma(Y)\)-measurable, real-valued random variable \(\vartheta\) \textit{neural} if there exists a neural network \(f\in\mathcal{NN}(E^*,\psi)\), such that 
        \begin{equation*}
            \vartheta = f(Y).
        \end{equation*}
        \item Assume \(Y\) is a stochastic process taking values in \(\mathbb{R}^d\) for \(d\in\mathbb{N}\). For \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\cup\{\infty\}\), we call an \(\mathcal{F}_t\)-measurable, real-valued random variable \(\vartheta\) \textit{algorithmically generated} if there exist deterministic times 
        \(t_1,\dots,t_n\) in \([0,t]\) for \(n\in\mathbb{N}\) and a neural network \(f\in\mathcal{NN}_\psi((\mathbb{R}^{d\times n})^*)\), such that
        \begin{equation*}
            \vartheta = f(Y_{t_1},\dots,Y_{t_n}).
        \end{equation*}
        We denote the class of these processes by \(\Theta_{\mathcal{NN}}(t,\psi)\). 
        \item
        Let \(Y\) be a Lévy process and \(\tau\) a finite \(\mathbb{F}\)-stopping time. We call a \(\mathcal{F}_{\tau}\)-measurable, real-valued random variable \(\vartheta\) \textit{algorithmically generated} if there exist deterministic times 
        \(t_1,\dots,t_n\) in \([0,t]\) for \(n\in\mathbb{N}\) and a neural network \(f\in\mathcal{NN}_\psi\big(\mathbb{R}^{1+d\times n}\big)\) such that
        \begin{equation*}
            \vartheta = f(\tau,Y^{\tau}_{t_1},\dots,Y^{\tau}_{t_n}).
        \end{equation*}
        We denote the class of these processes by \(\Theta_{\mathcal{NN}}(\tau,\psi)\). 
    \end{itemize}
\end{definition}
We now turn to the main approximation theoretic result upon which all the subsequent results of this chapter are based. We will show that the algorithmically generated random variables are dense in \(L^p(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\) for \(p\in[1,\infty)\).
For the proof of the following proposition, we need the martingale convergence theorem, which we restate here for the convenience of the reader. The proof for a more general version on Orlicz spaces can be found in \parencite[Thm.~5.12]{Arandjelovic2025}. We state the special case for the Orlicz space \(L^p(\mu)\) where \(\mu\) is a finite measure on a measure space \((\Omega,\mathcal{F})\) and \(p\in[1,\infty)\). This is a general remark for this chapter. 
\begin{remark}
All the results in this section involving \(L^p\)-spaces are proved in 
    \parencite{Arandjelovic2025} for the more general class of Orlicz spaces on locally convex spaces \(X\) satisfying the \(\Delta_2\)-property. As this thesis mainly focuses on deep-mean variance hedging and the latter, even in \parencite{Arandjelovic2025}, only requires the results for \(X=\mathbb{R}^n\), we restrict ourselves to the latter case. The exceptions to this are Proposition \ref{prop:reduction to countably generated sigma algebra is not fundamental, product topology} and \ref{prop:reduction to countably generated sigma algebra is not fundamental in the case of E = C}. For the Orlicz spaces, we take the special case of \(L^p\)-spaces for \(p\in[1,\infty)\). In particular, they belong to the class of Orlicz-spaces satisfying the property \(\Delta_2\). For the theory of Orlicz spaces, we refer to \parencite{Rao1991OrliczSpaces}.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Martingale Convergence Theorem]\label{lemma:Martingale Convergence Theorem}
    Let \((\mathcal{F}_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) be a filtration on \(\mathcal{F}\) and \(\mathcal{F}_{\infty}\coloneqq\bigvee_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{F}_n\). Then, for every \(f\in L^p(\mu)\), \(p\in[1,\infty)\), and some finite measure \(\mu\), we have
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{E}_{\mu}[f|\mathcal{F}_n]\to\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[f|\mathcal{F}_{\infty}],\quad n\to\infty
    \end{equation*}
 where convergence holds \(\mu\)-almost surely and in \(L^p(\mu)\).
\end{lemma}
The claim is generally incorrect if \(\mu\) is not a finite measure.
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:approximation of measurable maps by algorithmic strategies}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Let \(I\) be a non-empty set, and \((Y_i)_{i\in I}\) an indexed family of \(\mathcal{F}\)-measurable, \(\mathbb{R}^d\)-valued random variables. Let 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{F}_I^{\mathbb{P}}=\sigma(Y_i:i\in I)\vee\mathcal{N}^\mathbb{P}
    \end{equation*}
    and \(Z\in L^p(\mathbb{P})\) for \(p\in[1,\infty)\) be \(\mathcal{F}_I^{\mathbb{P}}\)-measurable. Then for every \(\epsilon>0\), there exists a \textbf{finite} set \(J=\left\{j_1,\dots,j_n\right\}\subseteq I\) for \(n\in\mathbb{N}\), and a neural network \(f\in\mathcal{NN}_\psi(\mathbb{R}^{dn})\), such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \lVert Z-f(Y_{j_1},\dots, Y_{j_n})\rVert_{p}<\epsilon.
    \end{equation*}
    \item Let  \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\cup\{\infty\}\),  \(Z\in L^p(\mathbb{P})\) for \(p\in[1,\infty)\) be \(\mathcal{F}_t\)-measurable. Then, for every \(\epsilon>0\), there exists \(\vartheta\in\Theta_{\mathcal{NN}}(t,\psi)\) such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \lVert \vartheta-Z\rVert_{p}<\epsilon.
    \end{equation*}
    \item Assume \(Y\) is a Lévy process and \(\mathbb{F}\) the completed filtration generated by \(Y\). Let \(\tau\) be a finite \(\mathbb{F}\)-stopping time, and \(Z\in L^p(\mathbb{P})\) for \(p\in[1,\infty)\), be \(\mathcal{F}_{\tau}\)-measurable. Then, for every \(\epsilon>0\), there exists \(\vartheta\in\Theta_{\mathcal{NN}}(\tau,\psi)\) such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \lVert \vartheta-Z\rVert_{p}<\epsilon.
    \end{equation*}
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}   
The main work is to prove part (1). Part (2) and (3) follow from the same arguments applied to particular instances of \(I\). 


The main idea for part (1) is to rewrite the \(\mathcal{F}_I^{\mathbb{P}}\)-measurable function \(Z\) as a conditional expectation and find a countable filtration \((\mathcal{F}_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) such that \(Z=\mathbb{E}[Z|\mathcal{F}_I^{\mathbb{P}}]\) is the \(L^p\)-limit of \(Z_n=\mathbb{E}[Z|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_n]\). We will use Proposition \ref{prop:reduction to countable measurability} to construct the filtration. Then we approximate the Doob-Dynkin function of \(Z_n\) by neural networks. Having laid out the proof strategy, we proceed with the details.


First, notice that for \(\mathcal{F}_I^{\mathbb{P}}\coloneqq\mathcal{F}_I\vee\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\), we have
\begin{align*}
    Z=\mathbb{E}\Big[Z\big|\mathcal{F}_I^{\mathbb{P}}\Big]=\mathbb{E}[Z|\mathcal{F}_I],\quad\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}. 
\end{align*}
Indeed, by definition, the conditional expectation is the \(\mathbb{P}\)-almost surely unique, integrable random variable \(\mathbb{E}[Z|\mathcal{F}_I^{\mathbb{P}}]\) such that \(\mathbb{E}[Z1_B]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[Z|\mathcal{F}_I^{\mathbb{P}}]1_B]\) for all \(B\in\mathcal{F}_I^{\mathbb{P}}\). By Lemma \ref{lemma:characterisation of completed sigma-algebra} we have \(B=F\cup N\) for \(F\in\mathcal{F}_I\) and \(N\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\), and we can choose without loss of generality \(F\cap N=\emptyset\). Hence \(1_{B}=1_N+1_F\). However, \(\mathbb{P}[N]=0\) Hence, \(\mathbb{E}[Z1_N]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[Z|\mathcal{F}_I^{\mathbb{P}}]1_N]\) for all \(N\in\mathcal{F}_I\) and \(\mathbb{E}[Z|\mathcal{F}_I^{\mathbb{P}}]\) is integrable by definition. Thus, \(\mathbb{E}[Z|\mathcal{F}_I^{\mathbb{P}}]=\mathbb{E}[Z|\mathcal{F}_I]\). For the scope of this proof, we denote \(Z_{\mathcal{F}_I}=\mathbb{E}[Z|\mathcal{F}_I]\) and \(Z_{\mathcal{F}_I^{\mathbb{P}}}=\mathbb{E}[Z|\mathcal{F}_I^{\mathbb{P}}]\).


Recall that \(\mathcal{F}_I=\sigma(Y_i:i\in I)\) and that \(\mathbb{E}[Z|\mathcal{F}_I]\) is \(\mathcal{F}_I\)-measurable by definition. 
By Proposition \ref{prop:reduction to countable measurability} we can find \(J= \left\{j_n:n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}\subseteq I\) such that \(\mathbb{E}[Z|\mathcal{F}_I]\) is \(\mathcal{F}_J=\sigma(Y_j:j\in J)\)-measurable. We define a filtration on \(\mathcal{F}_J\) by setting \(\mathcal{F}_n=\sigma(Y_{j_1},\dots,Y_{j_n})\) and \(\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\). We have \(\mathcal{F}_J=\bigvee_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{F}_n\). We set for \(n\in\mathbb{N}\):
\begin{equation*}
    M_n=\mathbb{E}[Z_{\mathcal{F}_I}|\mathcal{F}_n]
\end{equation*}
and denote the resulting \(\mathbb{F}\)-adapted process by \(M=(M_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\). By Lemma \ref{lemma:Martingale Convergence Theorem}, we have that 
\begin{equation*}
    M_n\to\mathbb{E}[Z_{\mathcal{F}_I}|\mathcal{F}_J]=Z_{\mathcal{F}_I}\quad n\to\infty
\end{equation*}
in \(L^p\).
In particular, because \(Z_{\mathcal{F}_I}=Z_{\mathcal{F}_I^{\mathbb{P}}}\) almost surely, we have \(\lim_{n\to\infty}M_n=Z_{\mathcal{F}_I^{\mathbb{P}}}\) in \(L^p\). Now, by the Doob-Dynkin representation lemma, there exists a \(\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^n)/\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})\)-measurable map \(f_n\) such that 
\begin{equation*}
    M_n = f_n(Y_{j_1},\dots,Y_{j_n}).
\end{equation*}
Because \(M_n\in L^p(\mathbb{P})\), we have that \(f_n\in L^p(\mathbb{R}^{d\times n},\mu_n)\), where \(\mu_n=\mathcal{L}(Y_{j_1},\dots,Y_{j_n})=\mathbb{P}\circ(Y_{j_1},\dots,Y_{j_n})^{-1}\). By Theorem \ref{thm:density of NN in L^p}, we can approximate \(f_n\) by a sequence \((f_n^m)_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq \mathcal{NN}_\psi({\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}})\) in \(L^p(\mu_n)\) (recall that we assume the standing assumption \ref{def:standing assumptions} in this chapter). 


Fix some \(\epsilon>0\). By the convergence of \(M_n\) to \(Z_{\mathcal{F}_I}\) in \(L^p(\mathbb{P})\) there exists \(n\in\mathbb{N}\) large enough such that \(\lVert M_n-Z_{\mathcal{F}_I}\rVert_{L^p(\mathbb{P})}<\epsilon/2\). By the convergence of \(f_n^m(Y_{j_1},\dots,Y_{j_n})\) to \(f_n(Y_{j_1},\dots,Y_{j_n})\) in \(L^p(\mathbb{P})\), or equivalently, the convergence of \(f_n^m\) to \(f_n\) in \(L^p(\mu_n)\), for every \(n\in\mathbb{N}\), there exists \(m\in\mathbb{N}\) large enough such that \(\lVert f_n^m-f_n\rVert_{L^p(\mu_n)}<\epsilon/2\). Hence, we have for \(n,m\) large enough, by the triangle inequality
\begin{align*}
    \lVert f_n^m(Y_{j_1},\dots,Y_{j_n})-Z\rVert_{L^p(\mathbb{P})}&= 
    \lVert f_n^m(Y_{j_1},\dots,Y_{j_n})-Z_{\mathcal{F}_I}\rVert_{L^p(\mathbb{P})}
    \\&\leq \lVert M_n-Z_{\mathcal{F}_I}\rVert_{L^p(\mathbb{P})}+\lVert f_n^m-f_n\rVert_{L^p(\mu_n)}<\epsilon\,,
\end{align*}
which finishes the proof of the first part. \\\\
For (2), the claim follows by the same argument as in (1) with \(I=[0,t]\cap\mathbb{R}_+\) for \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\cup\{\infty\}\) as in Proposition \ref{prop:reduction to countable measurability with filtration} part (a). For part (3), again by the same argument and Proposition \ref{prop:reduction to countable measurability with filtration} part (b) for the indexed family \(\{Y_s:s\in\mathbb{R}_+\}\cup\{\tau\}\). 
\end{proof}
The proof of Proposition \ref{prop:approximation of measurable maps by algorithmic strategies} reduces, for each given real-valued random variable \(Z\), measurability with respect to the \(\sigma\)-algebra generated by the possibly uncountable family \((Y_i)_{i\in I}\) to measurability with respect to a countably generated sub-\(\sigma\)-algebra. This reduction is convenient because it allows us to apply finite-dimensional universal approximation results, but it is not intrinsic to neural approximation. Alternatively, one may regard the entire family \(Y=(Y_i){i\in I}\) as a single random element of a suitable topological vector space \(E\) and apply Theorem \ref{thm:density of NN in L^p on topological vector space} directly to the law of \(Y\) on \(\sigma(E^*)\). We record two versions of this direct approach. Proposition \ref{prop:reduction to countably generated sigma algebra is not fundamental, product topology} uses the unrestricted trajectory space \(\mathbb R^{[0,\infty)}\) equipped with the product topology, whereas Proposition \ref{prop:reduction to countably generated sigma algebra is not fundamental in the case of E = C} uses the natural locally uniform topology on \(C[0,\infty)\) when \(Y\) has continuous paths.


Recall that given a space \(\Pi_{i\in I}E_i\), we define the coordinate maps as 
\begin{align*}
    \pi_i:\;&\Pi_{j\in I}E_j\to E_i
    \\&(f_j)_{j\in I}=f_i.
\end{align*}
Furthermore, considering the family of measure spaces \((E_i,\Sigma_i)_{i\in I}\) for \(\sigma\)-algebras \(\Sigma_i\), we define the product \(\sigma\)-algebra on \(\Pi_{i\in I}E_i\) as the smallest \(\sigma\)-algebra such that \((\pi_i)_{i\in I}\) is measurable and denote it by 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:product sigma algebra}
    \bigotimes_{i\in I}\Sigma_i.
\end{equation}
Recall that for a topological vector space \(E\), we might equip \(E\) with the \(\sigma\)-algebra \(\sigma(E^*)\), that is the smallest \(\sigma\)-algebra for which all the elements the topological dual of \(E\) are measurable. 
 The following Lemma found in \parencite[Lemma.1.9.]{Kallenberg2002FoundationsModernProbability} is needed for the subsequent proposition.
 \begin{lemma}\label{lemma:kallenberg}
For any measurable spaces $(\Omega,\mathcal{A})$, $(S_i,\mathcal{S}_i)$ and functions
$f_i:\Omega\to S_i$, $i\in I$, define
\[
f=(f_i):\Omega\to \prod_{i\in I} S_i.
\]
Then these conditions are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] $f$ is $\mathcal{A}/\bigotimes_{i\in I}\mathcal{S}_i$-measurable,
\item[(ii)] $f_i$ is $\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{S}_i$-measurable for every $i\in I$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:reduction to countably generated sigma algebra is not fundamental, product topology}
    Consider as in Proposition \ref{prop:approximation of measurable maps by algorithmic strategies} a collection of real-valued, Borel-measurable maps \((Y_{i})_{i\in I}\) with \(I=[0,\infty)\). Denote by \(Y\) the mapping to \(\mathbb{R}^I=\Pi_{i\in I}\mathbb{R}\) induced by the family \(Y=(Y_i)_{i\in I}\). Equip \(\mathbb{R}^I\) with the product topology (the coarsest topology such that the coordinate mappings are continuous). Let \(Y:(\Omega,\mathcal{F})\to (\mathbb{R}^I,\sigma((\mathbb{R}^I)^*))\) be measurable. Finally, denote \(\mathcal{F}_I=\sigma(Y_i:i\in I)\) and \(\mathcal{F}_I^{\mathbb{P}}=\mathcal{F}_I\vee\mathcal{N}^{\mathbb{P}}\). Let \(Z\) be \(\mathcal{F}_I^{\mathbb{P}}\)-measurable, real-valued, and in \(L^p(\mathbb{P})\) for some \(p\in[1,\infty)\). Assume that the standing assumptions \ref{def:standing assumptions} hold. Then, for every \(\epsilon>0\) there exists \(F\in\mathcal{NN}(\mathbb{R}^I,\psi)\) such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \lVert Z-F(Y)\rVert_{L^p(\mathbb{P})}<\epsilon.
    \end{equation*}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    For notational simplicity we denote \(E=\mathbb{R}^i\) and \(\Sigma\) the product \(\sigma\)-algebra on \(E\).
    Set \(Z=\mathbb{E}[Z|\mathcal{F}_I^{\mathbb{P}}]=\mathbb{E}[Z|\mathcal{F}_I]\) almost surely, and we denote the latter as \(Z_{\mathcal{F}_I}=\mathbb{E}[Z|\mathcal{F}_I]\). 
    As we equipped \(E\) with the product topology, the coordinate functions are continuous linear forms from \(E\) to \(\mathbb{R}\), hence \(\sigma(E)\)-measurable. But \(\Sigma\) is by definition the smallest such \(\sigma\)-algebra, hence \(\Sigma\subseteq\sigma(E^*)\). Hence, 
    \(Y\) being \(\mathcal{F}/\sigma(E^*)\)-measurable, it is in particular \(\mathcal{F}/\Sigma\)-measurable. Denote 
    \begin{equation*}
        \sigma_{\Sigma}(Y)\coloneqq Y^{-1}(\Sigma).
    \end{equation*}
 Similarly, let \(\mu=\mathcal{L}(Y)=\mathbb{P}\circ(Y)^{-1}\) be the law of \(Y\) on \((E,\sigma(E^*))\). We can define 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mu|_{\Sigma}=\mathcal{L}(Y)|_{\Sigma}=\mathbb{P}\circ(Y)^{-1}
    \end{equation*}
    that is the law of the \(\mathcal{F}/\Sigma\)-measurable \(Y\) on \((E,\Sigma)\).
    Applying Lemma \ref{lemma:kallenberg} with \(\mathcal{A}=\sigma(Y_i:i\in I)\) shows that \(\sigma(Y_i:i\in I)\subseteq\sigma_{\Sigma}(Y)\). Applying \ref{lemma:kallenberg} with \(\mathcal{A}=\sigma_{\Sigma}(Y)\) shows that \(\sigma_{\Sigma}(Y)\subseteq\sigma(Y_i:i\in I)\). Hence, \(\sigma_{\Sigma}(Y)=\sigma(Y_i:i\in I)\). Hence, \(Z_{\mathcal{F}_I}\) is \(\sigma_{\Sigma}(Y)\)-measurable, where \(Y\), so by Doob-Dynkin, there exists a \(\Sigma/\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})\)-measurable \(f\) such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        Z_{\mathcal{F}_I} = f(Y).
    \end{equation*}
    or in terms of \(Z\)
    \begin{equation*}
        Z = f(Y).
    \end{equation*}
    almost surely.  
 Because \(Z\in L^p(\mathbb{P})\), we have \(f\in L^p(E,\Sigma,\mu|_{\Sigma})\), where \(\mu|_{\Sigma}=\mathcal{L}(Y)|_{\Sigma}=\mathbb{P}\circ(Y)^{-1}\) the image measure of \(Y\) on \((E,\Sigma)\). We have previously argued that \(\Sigma\subseteq\sigma(E^*)\). In particular, any \(\Sigma/\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})\)-measurable function is also \(\sigma(E^*)/\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})\)-measurable. Hence,  \(L^p(E,\Sigma,\mu|_{\Sigma})\subseteq L^p(E,\sigma(E^*),\mu)\), where \(\mu=\mathcal{L}(Y)=\mathbb{P}\circ(Y)^{-1}\) is the image measure under \(Y\) on \((E,\sigma(E^*))\). In particular \(f\in L^p(E,\sigma(E^*),\mu)\). By
    Theorem \ref{thm:density of NN in L^p on topological vector space} applied to the topological vector space \(E\) we have 
    \begin{equation*}
        \overline{\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(E)}^{L^p}=L^p(E,\sigma(E^*),\mu).
    \end{equation*}
Thus, we can find an approximating sequence \((f_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) of \(f\) in \(L^p(E,\sigma(E^*),\mu)\). Hence, for every \(\epsilon>0\) there exists \(n\in\mathbb{N}\) large enough such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \lVert f(Y)-f_n(Y)\rVert_{L^p(\mathbb{P})}=\lVert f-f_n\rVert_{L^p(\mu)}<\epsilon.
    \end{equation*}
    Setting \(F=f_n\) concludes the proof.
\end{proof}
By imposing more regularity on the paths of \(Y\) and considering the appropriate topology (not the product topology), we get a similar approximation result.
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:reduction to countably generated sigma algebra is not fundamental in the case of E = C}
 Consider, as in Proposition \ref{prop:approximation of measurable maps by algorithmic strategies}, a collection of real-valued, Borel-measurable maps \((Y_{i})_{i\in I}\) with \(I=[0,\infty)\). Denote by \(Y\) the mapping to \(\mathbb{R}^I=\Pi_{i\in I}\mathbb{R}\) induced by the family \(Y=(Y_i)_{i\in I}\). Assume moreover that the mapping \(Y\) induced by the family \(Y=(Y_i)_{i\in I}\) has continuous paths, that is \(Y:\Omega\to C[0,\infty)\) and equip \(C[0,\infty)\) with the topology induced by the metric
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:locally uniform metric for continuous functions}
    d(f,g)=\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac{\max_{0\leq t\leq n}|f(t)-g(t)|\wedge 1}{2^n}.
\end{equation}
Denote \(\mathcal{F}_I=\sigma(Y_i:i\in I)\) and \(\mathcal{F}_I^{\mathbb{P}}=\mathcal{F}_I\vee\mathcal{N}^{\mathbb{P}}\).
 Let \(Z\) be \(\mathcal{F}_I^{\mathbb{P}}\)-measurable, real-valued, and in \(L^p(\mathbb{P})\) for some \(p\in[1,\infty)\).  Assume that the standing assumptions \ref{def:standing assumptions} hold. Then, for every \(\epsilon>0\) there exists \(F\in\mathcal{NN}(C[0,\infty),\psi)\) such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \lVert Z-F(Y)\rVert_{L^p(\mathbb{P})}<\epsilon.
    \end{equation*}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
for notational simplicity, we denote \(E=C[0,\infty)\).
    The proof is based on \parencite[Problem.~4.2,page 60]{KaratzasShreve1991Brownian}, which shows that \begin{equation}\label{eq:karatzras and shreve problem}
         \mathcal{B}(E)=\sigma(\pi_t:t\geq 0).
    \end{equation}
    This implies that 
    \begin{align*}
        \sigma(Y)&=Y^{-1}\big(\mathcal{B}(E)\big)
        \\& =Y^{-1}\big(\sigma(\pi_t:t\geq 0)\big)
        \\&=\sigma(\pi_t\circ Y:t\geq 0)=\sigma(Y_t:t\geq 0).
    \end{align*}
    Set \(Z=\mathbb{E}[Z|\mathcal{F}_I^{\mathbb{P}}]=\mathbb{E}[Z|\mathcal{F}_I]\) almost surely, and we denote the latter as \(Z_{\mathcal{F}_I}=\mathbb{E}[Z|\mathcal{F}_I]\). By definition, \(Z_{\mathcal{F}_I}\) is \(\mathcal{F}_I\)-measurable, but \(\mathcal{F}_I=\sigma(Y_i:i\in I)=\sigma(Y)\) follows from the above. By Doob-Dynkin, there exists a \(\mathcal{B}(E)/\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})\)-measurable \(f\) such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        Z_{\mathcal{F}_I} = f(Y).
    \end{equation*}
    or in terms of \(Z\)
    \begin{equation*}
        Z = f(Y).
    \end{equation*}
    almost surely. We have \(Z\in L^p(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\) by assumption, and thus \(f\in L^p(E,\mathcal{B}(E),\mu)\), where \(\mu\) is the law of \(Y\) on \(\mathcal{B}(E)\).
    Observe that in our setting
    \begin{equation*}
        \sigma\big(E^*\big)=\mathcal{B}(E).
    \end{equation*}
    Indeed, any continuous, linear map \(g\in E^*\) is, in particular, Borel-measurable; hence \(\sigma(E^*)\subseteq\mathcal{B}(E)\). Conversely, notice that \(\pi_t\) is continuous for the topology induced by the metric \(d\) (see the solution to \parencite[Problem.~4.2,page 60]{KaratzasShreve1991Brownian} given at page 119). As \(\pi_t\) are clearly linear, we have \(\pi_t\in E^*\). But  \(\sigma(\pi_t:t\geq 0)= \mathcal{B}(E)\). This shows that 
    \begin{equation*}
        f\in L^p\big(E,\mathcal{B}(E),\mu\big)=L^p\big(E,\sigma(E^*),\mu\big)
    \end{equation*}
    By Theorem \ref{thm:density of NN in L^p on topological vector space} applied to the topological vector space \((E,d)\) we have 
    \begin{equation*}
        \overline{\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(E)}^{L^p}=L^p\big(E,\sigma(E^*),\mu\big).
    \end{equation*}
    Thus, we can find an approximating sequence \((f_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) of \(f\) in \(L^p(E,\sigma(E^*),\mu)\). Hence, for every \(\epsilon>0\), there exists a \(n\in\mathbb{N}\) large enough such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \lVert f(Y)-f_n(Y)\rVert_{L^p(\mathbb{P})}=\lVert f-f_n\rVert_{L^p(\mu)}<\epsilon.
    \end{equation*}
    Setting \(F=f_n\) concludes the proof.
\end{proof}
A particular example in the setting of Proposition \ref{prop:reduction to countably generated sigma algebra is not fundamental in the case of E = C} 
is the classic Wiener process. Hence, in the case where the market filtration is generated by a Wiener process, the neural approximation can be done on the space of continuous functions directly. 








\section{From simple processes to stochastic integral}\label{sec:From simple processes to stochastic integral}
As discussed in Section \ref{sec:main ideas of alexandar}, using approximation theory for \(L^p\)-random variables that are measurable with respect to the market information process, we will approximate elementary predictable processes.
