This will enable us to build stochastic integration theory. The appropriate density properties of the approximated elementary predictable processes will then enable us to leverage the continuity properties of the integral mapping in order to approximate stochastic integrals. 
Furthermore, we show how the additional infinitesimal integrability assumption in \parencite[Thm.~5.32]{Arandjelovic2025} can be removed after an injective bounded transformation of the market information process and under the assumption of a continuous activation function. This, in turn, yields a corresponding refinement of the deep mean–variance hedging result in \parencite[Thm.~5.35]{Arandjelovic2025}. In this chapter, unless noted otherwise, assume that \(\psi\) satisfies the standing assumptions \ref{def:standing assumptions}.
For the convenience of the reader, we recall the definition of elementary predictable processes and simple predictable processes. 
\bigbreak
    We call \(\vartheta\) a \textit{simple predictable process} if there exists a finite, increasing sequence of finite \(\mathbb{F}\)-stopping times, \(0=\tau_0\leq\dots\leq\tau_{n+1}<\infty\), and a sequence of bounded random variables \((\varphi_i)_{i=0}^n\subseteq L^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}_{t_i},\mathbb{P})\) such that 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:simple representation}
        \vartheta = \varphi_01_{\left\{0\right\}}+\sum_{i=0}^n\varphi_i1_{(\tau_i,\tau_{i+1}]}.
    \end{equation}
In particular, we have that \(\varphi_i\) is \(\mathcal{F}_{\tau_i}\)-measurable. We denote the set of simple predictable processes by \(\mathcal{S}\) and we say \(\vartheta\in\mathcal{S}\) is associated with a sequence \((\tau_k)_{k=0}^{n+1}\). 
\bigbreak
If the sequence of stopping times is deterministic, we denote them by \(\tau_i=t_i\in\mathbb{R}\quad\forall i=0,\dots,n\) and call \(\vartheta\) an \textit{elementary predictable process} and denote the corresponding set by \(\mathcal{E}\). Analogously, we say \(\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}\) is associated with a sequence \((t_k)_{k=0}^{n+1}\).
For statements that are true for both \(\mathcal{E}\) and \(\mathcal{S}\) we shall formulate the result for \(\mathcal{S}\) only, as the case for \(\mathcal{E}\) results as a special case of the latter. In particular, \(\tau\) denotes a random time, which may be deterministic. We write \(t\) for deterministic times only. 
In accordance with Proposition \ref{prop:approximation of measurable maps by algorithmic strategies}, we equip the underlying, complete probability space with the completed filtration \(\mathbb{F}\) generated by a Lévy-process \(Y\). As observed in the discussion before Proposition \ref{prop:reduction to countable measurability with filtration}, the filtration with \(\mathcal{F}_t=\sigma(Y_s:s\leq t)\vee \mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\) satisfies the usual hypotheses. Hence, for this subsection, we assume a stochastic basis
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stochastic basis satisfying usual hypotheses}
    (\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
\end{equation}
which is complete as a measure space and with \(\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_t)_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\), where \(\mathcal{F}_t=\sigma(Y_s:s\leq t)\vee \mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\) for \(Y\) Lévy. The filtration is complete and right-continuous.


In particular, as we are in a finite measure space setting, notice that \(L^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}_{\tau_i})\subseteq L^{p}(\mathcal{F}_{\tau_i})\) for any \(p\in[1,\infty]\). By Proposition \ref{prop:approximation of measurable maps by algorithmic strategies}, we can find an approximating sequence of \((\varphi_i^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\Theta_{\mathcal{NN}}(\tau_i,\psi)\) that converges to \(\varphi_i\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}_{\tau_i})\) in \(L^p\). Indeed, take any null-sequence of positive real numbers \((\epsilon_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) and apply Proposition \ref{prop:approximation of measurable maps by algorithmic strategies} (3.) repeatedly. This motivates the definition of the following space
\begin{definition}
    Let \(\vartheta\) be a stochastic process with representation \eqref{eq:simple representation}, where \(\varphi_i\in\Theta_{\mathcal{NN}}(\tau_i,\psi)\) for \(i=0,\dots,n\). We denote this subspace of \(\mathcal{S}\) by \(\mathcal{S}(\psi)\) and call its elements \textit{simple algorithmic strategies}.
\end{definition}
\begin{definition}
    Let \(\vartheta\) be a stochastic process with representation \eqref{eq:simple representation}, where \(\varphi_i\in\Theta_{\mathcal{NN}}(t_i,\psi)\) for \(i=0,\dots,n\). We denote this subspace of \(\mathcal{E}\) by \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) and call its elements \textit{elementary algorithmic strategies}.
\end{definition}
The main motivation for the definition of these sets, and in a sense for all of Section \ref{sec:algorithmically generated random variables}, is that there exists a suitable topology in which \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) and \(\mathcal{S}(\psi)\) are dense in \(\mathcal{E}\) and \(\mathcal{S}\) respectively. By a suitable topology, we mean, firstly, that density actually holds and that it is compatible with the respective notion of stochastic integration; that is, stochastic integration is continuous with respect to the topology. The first two candidates that come to mind are \(\rm{ucp}\)-convergence and, in the square-integrable martingale setting, \(\mathcal{H}^2\)-convergence. Indeed, by Definition \ref{def:stochastic integral w.r.t. semimartingales} in the semimartingale setting and Theorem \ref{thm:Ito Isometry} in the square-integrable martingale setting, the respective stochastic integral maps \(\vartheta\mapsto \vartheta\bullet X\) are continuous in the respective topologies. We treat both settings separately as they involve convergence with respect to very different norms. We start with the \(\rm{ucp}\) setting. 
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:p-UCP density of S}
    For \(\vartheta\in\mathcal{S}\) and \(\epsilon>0\), there exists \(\vartheta^{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{S}(\psi)\) such that \(\lVert(\vartheta-\vartheta^{\epsilon})_{\infty}^*\rVert_p<\epsilon\) for \(p\in[1,\infty)\). In other words, \(S(\psi)\) is dense in \(\mathcal{S}\) for the \(p\)-\(\rm{ucp}\) topology for \(p\in[1,\infty)\).
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For \(\vartheta\in\mathcal{S}\). Consider the decomposition \eqref{eq:simple representation} we have \(\varphi_i\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}_{\tau_i})\subseteq L^p(\mathcal{F}_{\tau_i})\) for every \(p\in[1,\infty)\). Using Proposition \ref{prop:approximation of measurable maps by algorithmic strategies} (3.), there exists an approximating sequence \((\varphi_i_m)_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\Theta_{\mathcal{NN}}(\tau_i,\psi)\) in \(L^p(\mathcal{F}_{\tau_i},\mathbb{P})\) for every fixed index \(i=0,\dots,n\). Thus, we consider the obvious candidate sequence for our approximation, given by \((\vartheta^m)_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\), where we define the latter as follows
\begin{equation*}
    \vartheta^m = \varphi_0_m1_{\left\{0\right\}}+\sum_{i=0}^n\varphi_i_m1_{(\tau_i,\tau_{i+1}]}.
\end{equation*}
We have the obvious bound, uniformly in time
\begin{equation*}
   (\vartheta-\vartheta^m)_{\infty}^*(\omega)\leq \sum_{i=0}^n\big|\varphi_i(\omega)-\varphi_i_m(\omega)\big|\quad\forall\omega\in\Omega.
\end{equation*}
Hence, by the monotonicity of the \(L^p\)-norm and the triangle inequality
\begin{equation*}
    \lVert (\vartheta-\vartheta^m)_{\infty}^*\rVert_{p}\leq\sum_{i=0}^n\lVert \varphi_i-\varphi_i_m\rVert_p.
\end{equation*}
But by construction, the right-hand side converges to \(0\) as a finite sum of terms, each converging to \(0\). This shows density, as for every \(\epsilon>0\) we can find \(m\) big enough such that \(\lVert (\vartheta-\vartheta^m)_{\infty}^*\rVert_{p}<\epsilon\). In particular, this shows \(p\)-\(\rm{ucp}\)-density of \(\mathcal{S}(\psi)\) in \(\mathcal{S}\). Setting \(\vartheta^{\epsilon}=\vartheta^m\) finishes the proof.
\end{proof}
As remarked in Definition \ref{def:UCP convergence}, convergence in \(p\)-\(\rm{ucp}\) implies convergence in \(\rm{ucp}\). This relies on the fact that convergence in \(L^p\) for \(p\in[1,\infty)\) implies convergence in probability. The standard argument is based on the Chebyshev inequality. We show it explicitly. 
\begin{corollary}\label{cor:p-UCP density of S}
    \(S(\psi)\) is dense in \(\mathcal{S}\) for the \(\rm{ucp}\)-topology.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
   The claim thus follows from Chebyshev's inequality. Indeed,
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{P}\big[(\vartheta-\vartheta^m)_{\infty}^*>\epsilon\big]\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon^p}\int_{\Omega}\big((\vartheta-\vartheta^m)_{\infty}^*\big)^p\diff \mathbb{P}=\bigg(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\lVert (\vartheta-\vartheta^m)_{\infty}^*\rVert_p\bigg)^p
    \end{equation*}
By the continuity of the function \(x\mapsto x^{1/p}\), for any \(p\in[1,\infty)\) and by Proposition \ref{prop:p-UCP density of S} the right hand side converges to \(0\) as \(m\to\infty\) .  
\end{proof}

Notice that in Corollary \ref{cor:p-UCP density of S} we can substitute the stopping times \(\tau_i\) with deterministic times \(t_i\) everywhere, and the proof goes through unchanged. Thus, we get the immediate result for \(\mathcal{E}\) and \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) respectively.
\begin{corollary}\label{cor:UCP and p-UCP density of E}
    \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) is dense in \(\mathcal{E}\) for the \(p\)-\(\rm{ucp}\)-topology for \(p\in[1,\infty)\) and, in particular, for the \(\rm{ucp}\)-topology.
\end{corollary}

Following the results of Theorem \ref{thm:UCP continuity of general stochastic integral}, the simple processes are the basic building blocks of stochastic integration of adapted, càglàd processes with respect to semimartingales in the sense that the integral extends from simple integrands to càglàd integrands by continuity. Hence, Corollary \ref{cor:p-UCP density of S} implies that the integral of \(S(\psi)\)-integrands also extends to càglàd integrands by continuity. Indeed, this is the content of the following theorem.
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:Approx of stoch. Integrals}
    Let \(X\) be a classical semimartingale, and \(\vartheta\in \mathbb{L}\). Then, there exists a sequence \((\vartheta^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) in \(\mathcal{S}(\psi)\) that converges to \(\vartheta\) in \(\rm{ucp}\), such that
    \begin{equation*}
        \int \vartheta_t^n\diff X_t \to \int \vartheta_t\diff X_t,\quad n\to\infty,
    \end{equation*}
    where convergence holds in \(\rm{ucp}\).
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
 By Proposition \ref{prop:p-UCP density of S} \(\mathcal{S}(\psi)\) is dense in \(\mathcal{S}\) in the \(\rm{ucp}\)-topology. Furthermore, by Proposition \ref{prop:UCP density of simple process in cadlag processes} \(\mathcal{S}\) is dense in \(\mathbb{L}\) for the \(\rm{ucp}\)-topology. Hence, there exists a unique continuous extension 
    \begin{equation*}
        \tilde{I}_X:\overline{S(\psi)}^{\rm{ucp}}\to\mathbb{D}
    \end{equation*} 
    with \(\overline{S(\psi)}^{\rm{ucp}}=\mathbb{L}\). This extension is equal to the extension in Definition \ref{def:stochastic integral w.r.t. semimartingales} used to define the integral map
    \begin{equation*}
        \overline{I}_X:\mathbb{L}\to\mathbb{D}
    \end{equation*} 
    as the unique continuous extension of the map 
    \begin{equation*}
        I_X:\mathcal{S}\to \mathbb{D}.
    \end{equation*}
    defined as in \eqref{def:simple integration}.
    Indeed, we have
 \( \overline{I}_X|_{\mathcal{S}(\psi)}=\widetilde{I}_X|_{\mathcal{S}(\psi)}\); hence equality on \(\overline{\mathcal{S}(\psi)}^{\rm{ucp}}=\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{\rm{ucp}}\) follows by the uniqueness of the continuous extension of a bounded linear map with image in a complete metric space. Here we use that \((\mathbb{D},d_{\rm{ucp}})\) is a complete metric space.
\end{proof}
The goal is to formulate a deep mean-variance hedging result in the continuous time setup. We will first formulate a version proposed by \parencite{Arandjelovic2025} in the square-integrable martingale setup discussed in Section \ref{sec:Stochastic Integration for square-integrable Martingales}. In this context, the relevant continuity of the integral map is given by the Itô-isometry, which asserts that for some \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\), the linear operator
\begin{align*}
    I_M:& \vartheta\mapsto \vartheta\bullet M\\
    &L^2(M)\to \mathcal{H}_0^2
\end{align*}
is bounded and hence (uniformly) continuous. In order to establish such a mean-variance result, we still need a similar density result of elementary or simple algorithmic strategies in \(L^2(M)\). Note that this is a fundamentally different kind of density than the \(\rm{ucp}\) density discussed in Corollary \ref{cor:p-UCP density of S} and \ref{cor:UCP and p-UCP density of E}, as convergence in \(L^2(M)\) is with respect to the integral norm 
\begin{equation*}
    \lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}^2=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\infty} \vartheta_t^2\diff [M]_t\bigg].
\end{equation*}
Hence, we need a different argument for density. The following proposition shows that the respective density statement holds for the more general \(L^p(M)\)-spaces. We provide the proof in a very detailed manner to make the role of the \(\mathcal{H}^{p+\epsilon}_0\)-integrability condition on \(M\) explicit.
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:Lp(M) density of E(psi)}
    Let the stochastic basis be as in \eqref{eq:stochastic basis satisfying usual hypotheses}. Assume there exists \(\epsilon>0\) such that \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^{p+\epsilon}\). Then the set \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) is dense in \(L^p(M)\) with respect to the norm \(\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^p(M)}\) for \(p\in[1,\infty)\). Moreover, for every \(\vartheta\in L^p(M)\), there exists a sequence \((\vartheta^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) such that
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:elementary integrals converge}
        \lim_{n\to\infty}\int \vartheta^n\diff M=\int \vartheta\diff M,
    \end{equation}
    where convergence holds in \(\mathcal{H}^p\). 
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Let \(\vartheta\in L^p(M)\). Notice that we can assume without loss of generality that \(\vartheta\in L^p(M)\) is bounded. Indeed, if not, use \(\vartheta^{(n)}=-n\vee \vartheta\wedge n\) instead. If we are able to approximate \(\vartheta^{(n)}\) by elements of \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\), we can do so as well for \(\vartheta\). Indeed, by dominated convergence (DCT), we find \(\vartheta^{(n)}\to \vartheta\) in \(L^p(M)\). Hence, if there exists a sequence \((\xi^n_m)_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq \mathcal{E}(\psi)\) converging to \(\vartheta^{(n)}\) for every \(n\in\mathbb{N}\), we might choose for every fixed \(n\) a \(m(n)\) large enough so that \(\lVert \xi^n_{m(n)}-\vartheta^{(n)}\rVert_{L^p(M)}<\frac{1}{n}\). Hence, 
\begin{equation*}
    \lim_{n\to\infty}\big\lVert\xi^n_{m(n)}-\vartheta\big\rVert_{L^p(M)}\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\big\lVert\xi^n_{m(n)}-\vartheta^{(n)}\big\rVert_{L^p(M)}+\limsup_{n\to\infty}\big\lVert\vartheta^{(n)}-\vartheta\big\rVert_{L^p(M)}=0.
\end{equation*}
For the DCT argument, notice that in the case \(p=2\) we saw that \(L^2(M)\simeq L^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M)\), hence \(\vartheta^{(n)}\to\vartheta\) in \(L^2(M)\) directly by DCT. In the case \(p\neq 2\), we argue by two successive applications of DCT.
 Define \(I^n=\int_0^{\infty}(\vartheta^n_t-\vartheta_t)^2\diff[M]_t\) and \(I=\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta_t^2\diff[M]_t\). Moreover, notice
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:bound on vartheta}
     \Big(\vartheta_t^{(n)}(\omega)-\vartheta_t(\omega)\Big)^2\leq 4\vartheta_t(\omega)^2,\quad\forall(\omega,t)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+.
 \end{equation}
 From \(\mathbb{E}[I^{\frac{p}{2}}]<\infty\), we have \(I<\infty\) \(\mathbb{P}\)-almost surely. Hence, \(\vartheta^2(\omega)\) is \(\diff [M](\omega)\)-integrable for almost all \(\omega\). Hence, on a \(\mathbb{P}\)-measure 1 set \(\mathcal{N}^c\), we can apply DCT pathwise to obtain
 \begin{equation*}
     \lim_{n\to\infty}\int_0^{\infty}\Big(\vartheta^{(n)}_t(\omega)-\vartheta_t(\omega)\Big)^2\diff [M]_t(\omega)=0,\quad\forall\omega\in \mathcal{N}^c.
 \end{equation*}
 By the same bound as in \eqref{eq:bound on vartheta}, and because \([M]\) is non-decreasing with \([M]_0=0\), we have for every \(\omega\in\Omega\) that \(I^n(\omega)\leq 4 I(\omega)\) and, in particular, \(I^n(\omega)^{\frac{p}{2}}\leq 4^{\frac{p}{2}} I(\omega)^{\frac{p}{2}}\).  By assumption, \(I^{\frac{p}{2}}\) is \(\mathbb{P}\)-integrable. Hence, by DCT, we have 
 \begin{equation*}
  \lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(I^n\big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(\lim_{n\to\infty}I^n\big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\Big]=0.
 \end{equation*} 
 This finally shows that we might assume \(\vartheta\in L^p(M)\) to be bounded.



    Furthermore, recall from Proposition \ref{prop:density of E in Lp(M) via monotone class} that \(\mathcal{E}\) is dense in \(L^p(M)\). Thus, to show the density of \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) in \(L^p(M)\), it suffices to show that \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) is dense in \(\mathcal{E}\) as a subspace of \(L^p(M)\).

    
     For this, we will make use of the fact that \(M\in\mathcal{H}^{p+\epsilon}\). Thus, we have Doob's \(L^p\)-inequality and the BDG inequality at our disposal.
 
 
   Let \(\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}\) and  \(\epsilon>0\).
    Recall from Corollary \ref{cor:UCP and p-UCP density of E} that for every \(\epsilon>0\) and any \(q\in[1,\infty)\), there is a \(\xi\in\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) such that \(\lVert (\xi-\vartheta)^*_{\infty}\rVert_{q}<\epsilon^{1/p}\). By setting \(q=\frac{p(p+\epsilon)}{\epsilon}\) and using the trivial bound \((\xi_t(\omega)-\vartheta_t(\omega))\leq(\xi(\omega)-\vartheta(\omega))_{\infty}^*,\;\forall(\omega,t)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+\) we get
    \begin{equation*}
        \lVert \xi-\vartheta\lVert_{L^p(M)}^p=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}(\xi_t-\vartheta_t)^2\diff[M]\right)^{p/2}\bigg]\leq\mathbb{E}\big[\big((\xi-\vartheta)_{\infty}^*\big)^p[M]_{\infty}^{p/2}\big].
    \end{equation*}
    Denoting \(\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^p}=\lVert\cdot\rVert_{p}\) for readability, we further bound the previous expression by Hölder's inequality for the conjugate pair \(\tilde{p}=\frac{p+\epsilon}{\epsilon}\) and \(\tilde{q}=\frac{p+\epsilon}{p}\). We get
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{E}\big[\big((\xi-\vartheta)
        _{\infty}^*\big)^p[M]_{\infty}^{p/2}\big]\leq\big\lVert(\xi-\vartheta)_{\infty}^*)^p\big\rVert_{\tilde{p}}\big\lVert[M]_{\infty}^{p/2} \big\rVert_{\tilde{q}}.
    \end{equation*}
    We now first use the BDG inequality and then Doob's \(L^p\)-inequality to finally obtain
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:BDG bound}
        \big\lVert\big((\xi-\vartheta)_{\infty}^*\big)^p\big\rVert_{\tilde{p}}\big\lVert[M]_{\infty}^{p/2} \big\rVert_{\tilde{q}}\lesssim \big\lVert(\xi-\vartheta)_{\infty}^*\big\rVert_{q}^p\big\lVert M^*_{\infty}\big\rVert_{p+\epsilon}^p\leq\epsilon\big\lVert M\big\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{p+\epsilon}}^p,
    \end{equation}
    where we don't care about the exact BDG constants as they only depend on \(p\) and not on the process. Notice that in order to apply the BDG inequality, we need to use the equivalent definition of the measure \(\mathbb{P}_M=\mathbb{P}_{[M]}\) via the process \([M]\).
    As \(\mathcal{H}^{p+\epsilon}\) is finite by assumption, the right-hand side of \eqref{eq:BDG bound} can be made arbitrarily small, hence showing the first claim that \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) is dense in \(L^p(M)\) in the \(L^p(M)\)-norm topology.

    
      In order to show the last claim, that is \eqref{eq:elementary integrals converge}, we use the fact that for every \(\vartheta\in L^p(M)\) we have \(\vartheta\bullet M\in\mathcal{H}^p\) (see \parencite[Corollary.~12.3.6.]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications}). Furthermore, by the first part, for every \(\xi\in L^p(M)\) we can find a sequence \((\vartheta_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) that converges to \(\vartheta\) in \(L^p(M)\). Furthermore, we have \([\vartheta\bullet M]=\vartheta^2\bullet[M]\) (see \parencite[Corollary.~12.3.6.]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications}). Hence, by the BDG inequality, we get 
    \begin{align*}
        \left\Vert \int \vartheta\diff M-\int \vartheta^n\diff M\right\Vert_{\mathcal{H}^p}=&\mathbb{E}\big[\big((\vartheta-\vartheta^n)\bullet M)^*_{\infty}\big)^p\big]^{1/p}\\&\lesssim \mathbb{E}\big[[(\vartheta-\vartheta^n)\bullet M]_{\infty}^{p/2}\big]^{1/p}\\&=\mathbb{E}\big[\big((\vartheta-\vartheta^n)^2\bullet [M])_{\infty}\big)^{p/2}\big]^{1/p}
        =\lVert \vartheta-\vartheta^n\rVert_{L^p(M)},
    \end{align*}
    which concludes the proof. 
\end{proof}

One might find the additional \(\epsilon\) in the integrability condition of \(M\) in Proposition \ref{prop:Lp(M) density of E(psi)} unpleasant. First, notice that fundamentally it appears due to the application of Hölder's inequality. Indeed, in the proof of Proposition \ref{prop:Lp(M) density of E(psi)}, one wants to bound the \(L^p(M)\)-norm of \((\vartheta-\xi)\) for \(\vartheta\in L^p(M)\) and \(\xi\in\mathcal{E}(\psi)\). We obtain, repeating the first part of the proof, 
 \begin{equation*}
        \rVert \xi-\vartheta\lVert_{L^p(M)}^p=\mathbb{E}\Big[\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}(\xi_t-\vartheta_t)^2\diff[M]_t\right)^{p/2}\Big]\leq\mathbb{E}\big[(\xi-\vartheta_{\infty}^*)^p[M]_{\infty}^{p/2}\big].
\end{equation*}\;
Applying Hölder's inequality with conjugate exponents \(a,b\geq 1\), we get 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{E}\big[\big((\xi-\vartheta)_{\infty}^*\big)^p[M]_{\infty}^{p/2}\big]\leq \mathbb{E}\big[\big((\xi-\vartheta)_{\infty}^*\big)^{pa}\big]^{\frac{1}{a}}\mathbb{E}\big[[M]_{\infty}^{\frac{pb}{2}}\big]^{\frac{1}{b}}
\end{equation*}
Recall that \(\vartheta\) was chosen to be the \(\epsilon\)-close element of \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) with respect to the \(p\)-\(\rm{ucp}\) topology. We can find such a \(\vartheta\) for any \(p\in[1,\infty)\) according to Proposition \ref{prop:approximation of measurable maps by algorithmic strategies}. On the other hand, we want to have the least restrictive integrability assumption on \(M\). In other words, we want to take the smallest possible \(b\). The optimal choice \(b=1\) is not admissible as it entails \(a=\infty\), for which Proposition \ref{prop:approximation of measurable maps by algorithmic strategies} does not apply. Hence, any \(b>1\) will do the job, and due to BDG and Doob we have
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{E}\big[[M]_{\infty}^{pb/2}\big]\lesssim\mathbb{E}\big[(M_{\infty}^*)^{pb}\big]=\lVert M\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{pb}}^{pb}.
\end{equation*}
Requiring \(b>1\) is equivalent to requiring \(\tilde{\epsilon}>0\) such that \(b=1+\tilde{\epsilon}\) or \(pb=p+\epsilon\) with \(\epsilon=\tilde{\epsilon}p\). Thus, in this sense, the argument in Proposition \ref{prop:Lp(M) density of E(psi)} is optimal and can't be improved via a bound from Hölder's inequality. Taking a step back, one might ask why one would not conduct a similar monotone class argument directly on \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) to show its density in \(L^p(M)\), as is done for \(\mathcal{E}\). The fundamental objection to this approach is that \(\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^k)\) is not closed under pointwise multiplication, hence, neither is \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\). We show that under careful consideration of the properties \(Y\) needed for Proposition \ref{prop:Lp(M) density of E(psi)} to hold, one can eliminate the \(\epsilon\) by directly applying the monotone class on \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) for a well chosen \(\psi\) and then arguing for the general case by approximation.



The following idea is based on the observation made by \parencite{HornikStinchcombeWhite1989UniversalApproximators} and sketched in \cref{rem:NN don't form algebra}. We use that according to \parencite[Thm.~2]{Hornik1991} the activation function 
\begin{equation*}
    \psi=\cos:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},
\end{equation*}
is discriminatory as a Borel-measurable, non-constant, and bounded function in the sense \ref{def:discriminatory maps over reals}. Furthermore, recall the following trigonometric identity
\begin{align*}
    \cos(x)\cos(y)=\frac{1}{2}(\cos(x+y)+\cos(x-y)).
\end{align*}
In particular, \(\mathcal{NN}_{\cos}(\mathbb{R}^k)\) forms an algebra of functions; hence, \(\mathcal{E}(\cos)\) does so as well. The monotone class arguments of Proposition \ref{prop:density of E in L2(M) via monotone class} and Proposition \ref{prop:density of E in Lp(M) via monotone class} are totally analogous for \(\mathcal{E}(\cos)\) instead of \(\mathcal{E}\), provided we argue that 
\begin{equation*}
    \sigma\big(\mathcal{E}(\cos)\big)=\mathcal{P}.
\end{equation*}
We show more generally that  \(\sigma(\mathcal{E}(\psi))=\mathcal{P}\) for any continuous, discriminatory activation function \(\psi\).
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:E(psi) generated P}
 Let \(\psi\) be as in Definition \ref{def:standing assumptions} and \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\). Then 
    \begin{equation*}
        \overline{\sigma\big(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\big)}^{\mathbb{P}_M}=\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{P}_M},
    \end{equation*}
    where \(\overline{(\cdot)}^{\mathbb{P}_M}\) denotes the completion with respect to the measure \({\mathbb{P}_M}\).
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    We show that any process of the form 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:left continuous step function}
        \alpha_t = \varphi_01_{\left\{ 0\right\}}(t)+\sum_{i=0}^n \varphi_i1_{(t_i,t_{i+1}]}(t),\;t\in\mathbb{R}_+,
    \end{equation}
    for \(\varphi_i\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}_{t_i},\mathbb{P})\), 
    is the \(\mathbb{P}_M\)-almost sure limit of elements in \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\). It then follows that \(\alpha\) is measurable with respect to \(\overline{\sigma(\mathcal{E}(\psi))}\), the \(\mathbb{P}_M\)-completed \(\sigma\)-algebra generated by processes in \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\). As seen in the proof of Lemma \ref{lemma:elementary processes generated predictable sigma algebra}, every left-continuous process is approximated by processes of the form \eqref{eq:left continuous step function} (or pointwise limits of the latter). But the predictable \(\sigma\)-algebra is generated by left-continuous and adapted processes; hence, the claim.



     Because \(L^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}_{t_i},\mathbb{P})\subseteq L^p(\mathcal{F}_{t_i},\mathbb{P})\) for any \(p\in[1,\infty]\), as we are working on a probability space, we have by point (2.)  of Proposition \ref{prop:approximation of measurable maps by algorithmic strategies} that there exists a sequence \((\varphi_i^k)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\Theta_{\mathcal{NN}}(t_i,\psi)\) approximating \(\varphi_i\) in \(L^p\) for any \(p\in[1,\infty)\). 
    We might do this for every \(i=0,\dots, n\). Then 
    \begin{equation*}
        \alpha_t^k=\varphi_0^k1_{\left\{ 0\right\}}(t)+\sum_{i=0}^n \varphi_i^k1_{(t_i,t_{i+1}]}(t),
    \end{equation*}
    converges to \(\alpha_t\) as \(k\to\infty\) in \(L^p(\mathbb{P})\) for every \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\). Indeed, 
    \begin{equation*}
        \lVert \alpha_t-\alpha_t^k\rVert_{L^p}\leq\sum_{i=0}^n\lVert\varphi_i-\varphi_i^k\rVert_{L^p}\to0,\;k\to\infty
    \end{equation*}
    We want to have some \(\widetilde{\alpha}_t\) converging to \(\alpha_t\), \(\mathbb{P}\)-almost surely for every \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\). We construct \(\widetilde{\alpha}\) by extracting an almost surely converging subsequence from \((\varphi_i^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) for every \(i\) in such a way that \(\alpha_t\) also converges almost surely. We proceed as follows.
    We start with \(i=0\). We extract a subsequence such that \((\varphi_0^{n(k)})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\) converges to \(\varphi_0\) \(\mathbb{P}\)-almost surely. Along this subsequence \(n(k)\), we still have that \((\varphi_{1}^{n(k)})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\) converges in \(L^p\) to \(\varphi_1\), hence we can extract a subsubsequence such that \((\varphi_{1}^{n(k(s))})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}\) converges to \(\varphi_1\) almost surely. We can iterate this procedure up to \(i=n\) to get a subsequence, which we reindex, denoting the corresponding sequence \((\widetilde{\varphi_i}^k)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\) for all \(i=0,\dots,n\). Then we have 
   \begin{equation*}
        \widetilde{\alpha}_t^{k} = \widetilde{\varphi}^{k}_01_{\left\{ 0\right\}}(t)+\sum_{i=0}^n \widetilde{\varphi}_i^{k}1_{(t_i,t_{i+1}]}(t),
    \end{equation*}
    where \((\widetilde{\varphi}_i^{k})_{k}\subseteq\Theta_{\mathcal{NN}}(t_i,\psi)\) converge for almost all \(\omega\in\Omega\) to \(\varphi_i\), for all \(i=1,\dots,n\) and all \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\). Clearly such \(\widetilde{\alpha}^{k}\in\mathcal{E}(\psi),\) for every \(k\). Hence
    \begin{equation*}
        \widetilde{\alpha}^{k}\to\alpha
    \end{equation*}
    as \(k\to \infty\) 
    for all \((\omega,t)\in\Omega/\mathcal{N}\times\mathbb{R}_+=\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+/(\mathcal{N}\times \mathbb{R}_+)\), where \(\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{N}]=0\). Notice that  \(\mathcal{N}\times\mathbb{R}_+\) is a \(\mathbb{P}_M\)-null set. Indeed 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{P}_M(\mathcal{N}\times\mathbb{R}_+)=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\infty}1_\mathcal{N}1_{\mathbb{R}_+}(t)\diff [M]_t\bigg]=\mathbb{E}[1_\mathcal{N}[M]_{\infty}]=0
    \end{equation*}
    because \([M]_{\infty}\) is integrable by the assumption that \(M\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^2\). Hence \(\widetilde{\alpha}_t^k\to \alpha_t\), \(\mathbb{P}_M\)-almost surely. Thus we have shown that \(\alpha\) is measurable with respect to the completion of \(\sigma(\mathcal{E}(\psi))\) with respect to the measure \(\mathbb{P}_M\). We denote the latter by \(\overline{\sigma(\mathcal{E}(\psi))}^{\mathbb{P}_M}\). By the remarks at the beginning of this proof, this shows that 
     \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{P}\subseteq\overline{\sigma\big(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\big)}^{\mathbb{P}_M}
    \end{equation*}
    But \(\sigma(\mathcal{E}(\psi))\subseteq\mathcal{P}\) is clear; hence, the claim. 
\end{proof}
In light of our goal of showing the density of \(\sigma(\mathcal{E}(\cos))\) in \(L^2(M)\), the completion issue in Proposition \ref{prop:E(psi) generated P} seems problematic. Indeed, the monotone class argument in Proposition \ref{prop:density of E in L2(M) via monotone class} yields, in our present case, that the class 
\begin{equation*}
     \mathcal{H}=\left\{ \vartheta\in b\mathcal{P}:\vartheta\in L^2(M),\forall \epsilon>0, \exists \vartheta_{\epsilon}\in \mathcal{E}(\cos)\text{ with }\lVert \vartheta_{\epsilon}-\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}<\epsilon\right\}.
\end{equation*}
contains all bounded \(\sigma(\mathcal{E}(\cos))\)-measurable maps. The conclusion for density then relies on the fact that \(\mathcal{H}\) defines a monotone class and that \(\sigma(\mathcal{E}(\cos))=\mathcal{P}\).  Hence, Proposition \ref{prop:E(psi) generated P} doesn't allow us to conclude directly. Nevertheless, the completion issue can be dealt with by some standard considerations on null-sets.


Recall the well-known fact that for \(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{\mu}\)-measurable \(\widetilde{f}\)  there exists an \(\mathcal{F}\)-measurable \(f\) such that \(\mu\big(\big\{\omega\in\Omega:f(\omega)\neq \widetilde{f}(\omega)\big\}\big)=0\) (see \parencite[Thm.~1.55]{Salamon2020MeasureIntegration}). More precisely \(\overline{\mu}\big(\big\{\omega\in\Omega:f(\omega)\neq \widetilde{f}(\omega)\big\}\big)=0\), where \(\overline{\mu}\) is the unique extension of \(\mu\) to \(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{\mu}\), defined by \(\overline{\mu}(A\cup E)=\mu(A)\) for \(A\in\mathcal{F},E\in\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\). By Lemma \ref{lemma:characterisation of completed sigma-algebra}, this characterizes \(\overline{\mu}\) on all of \(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{\mu}\). We abuse notation and do not distinguish between \(\mu\) and its canonical completion \(\overline{\mu}\) notationally, as it is clear from the context which one is being considered.


By the monotone class argument, we get for every bounded \(\sigma(\mathcal{E}(\cos))\)-measurable \(f\), and for every \(\epsilon>0\), that there exists \(g_{\epsilon}\in \mathcal{E}(\cos)\) such that 
 \begin{equation*}
     \lVert f-g_{\epsilon}\rVert_{L^2(M)}<\epsilon.
 \end{equation*}
 We want to show that \(\mathcal{H}\) contains all bounded \(\overline{\sigma(\mathcal{E}(\cos))}^{\mathbb{P}_M}\)-measurable maps. Let \(\widetilde{f}\) be bounded and \(\overline{\sigma(\mathcal{E}(\cos))}^{\mathbb{P}_M}\)-measurable. There exists a bounded \(\sigma(\mathcal{E}(\cos))\)-measurable \(f\) that is \(\mathbb{P}_M\)-almost everywhere equal to \(\widetilde{f}\). Hence 
 \begin{equation*}
     \lVert \widetilde{f}-g_{\epsilon}\rVert_{L^2(M)}=\lVert f-g_{\epsilon}\rVert_{L^2(M)}<\epsilon.
 \end{equation*}
 Hence, we can approximate every \(\widetilde{f}\in L^2(\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{P}_M},\mathbb{P}_M)\) by elements in \(\mathcal{E}(\cos)\) and in particular every \(f\in L^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M)\) as well. We deduce the following result.
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:density of E(cos) in L2(M)}
Assume the conditions of Proposition \ref{prop:E(psi) generated P}. Then 
\begin{equation*}
    \overline{\mathcal{E}(\cos)}^{L^2(M)}=L^2(M).
\end{equation*}
\end{lemma}
\begin{remark}
    Note that we argued about the argument on the completion issue, which was based on the completion of the measure space \(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+,\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M\) for \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\), arguing about approximating elements in \(L^2(M)=L^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M)\) and the completed space \(L^2(M)=L^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M)\).
    The argument thus crucially uses that for \(p=2\), \(L^p(M)=L^2(\mathbb{P}_M)\), which holds in the case \(p=2\) and is not true for general \(p\in [1,\infty)\). As the case \(p=2\) is the relevant case for our use case, which is deep mean-variance hedging, this suffices.
\end{remark}
In order to show \(\overline{\mathcal{E}(\psi)}^{L^2(M)}=L^2(M)\) for any discriminatory activation function, it remains to show that
\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{E}(\cos)\subseteq \overline{\mathcal{E}(\psi)}^{L^2(M)}
\end{equation*}
for any activation function \(\psi\) and \(p\in[1,\infty)\) in the real-valued sense of Definition \ref{def:discriminatory maps over reals}. Indeed, if this holds, we get, together with Lemma \ref{lemma:density of E(cos) in L2(M)}, that
\begin{equation*}
    \overline{\mathcal{E}(\psi)}^{L^2(M)}=L^2(M)
\end{equation*}
for \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2\), without additional \(\epsilon\)-infinitesimal integrability conditions, \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^{p+\epsilon}\), as was the case in Proposition \ref{prop:Lp(M) density of E(psi)}.
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:density of E(psi) in L^2(M)}
    Assume \(Y_t\) takes values in a compact subset \(K^t\subseteq\mathbb{R}\) for every \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\). Then, under the assumptions of Proposition \ref{prop:E(psi) generated P}, we have for \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\)
    \begin{equation*}
        \overline{\mathcal{E}(\psi)}^{L^2(M)}=L^2(M)\,.
    \end{equation*}
    for any continuous \(\psi\) satisfying Definition \ref{def:discriminatory maps over reals}.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
As explained, it remains to show that 
    \begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{E}(\cos)\subseteq \overline{\mathcal{E}(\psi)}^{L^2(M)}
\end{equation*}
Let \(\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}(\cos)\):
\begin{equation*}
    \vartheta = \eta_01_{\left\{0\right\}}+\sum_{i=0}^n \eta_i(Y_{t_1}^i,\dots,Y_{t_{k(i)}}^i)1_{(t_i,t_{i+1}]}
\end{equation*}
with \(\eta_i\in\mathcal{NN}_{\cos}(\mathbb{R}^{k(i)})\). Note that by assumption, for every \(i=0,\dots,n\), \((Y_{t_1}^i,\dots,Y_{t_{k(i)}}^i)\) takes values in \(K^i\coloneqq K^{t_1}\times\dots\times K^{t_{k(i)}}\), with \(t_{k(i)}\leq t_i\) and \(Y_{t_i}(\Omega)\subseteq K^{t_i}\). As a product of compact sets, \(K^i\) is a compact subset of \(\mathbb{R}^{k(i)}\). By definition 
\begin{equation*}
    \eta_i=\sum_{j=0}^m\alpha_{ij}\cos(A_{ij}),
\end{equation*}
for coefficients \(\alpha_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}\) and
where \(A_{ij}:\mathbb{R}^{k(i)}\to\mathbb{R}\) is affine linear and \(i=0,\dots,n,j=0,\dots,m\).
By \parencite[Thm.~2]{Hornik1991}, the bounded, non-constant, continuous, real-valued activation function \(\cos\) is discriminatory in the sense \eqref{eq:discriminatory maps over reals}. Considering the \(\eta_i\) as functions over compact sets \(K^i\coloneqq K^{t_1}\times\dots\times K^{t_{k(i)}}\), we have by Theorem \ref{thm:density of NN in C(K)} applied to the continuous function \(\cos(A_{ij}):K^i\to\mathbb{R}\) that for every \(\epsilon>0\) and \((i,j)=\{0,\dots,n\}\times\{0,\dots,m\}\), there exists \(\varphi_{ij}^{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(K^i)\) such that 
\begin{equation*}
    \sup_{x\in K^i}\big|\cos(A_{ij}(x))-\vartheta_{ij}^{\epsilon}(x)\big|<\epsilon,
\end{equation*}
where \(\varphi_{ij}^{\epsilon}=\sum_{k=1}^l\beta_{ijk}\psi(B_{ijk})\) for some affine linear \(B_{ijk}\). Consider
\begin{equation*}
    \xi \coloneqq \widetilde{\eta}_0(Y_{t_1},\dots,Y_{t_{k(0)}})1_{\left\{ 0\right\}}+\sum_{i=0}^n\widetilde{\eta}_i(Y_{t_1},\dots,Y_{t_{k(i)}})1_{(t_i,t_{i+1}]}
\end{equation*}
with
\begin{equation*}
    \widetilde{\eta}_i(Y_{t_1},\dots,Y_{t_{k(i)}})\coloneqq\sum_{j=0}^m\alpha_{ij}\varphi_{ij}^{\epsilon(ij)}\in \mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(K^i),
\end{equation*}
where we set \(\epsilon(ij)\coloneqq\frac{\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}}{(n+1)(m+1)|(|\alpha_{ij}|+1)}\). Clearly \(\xi\in\mathcal{E}(\psi)\). Denoting \(x_i=(Y_{t_1}(\omega),\dots,Y_{t_{k(i)}}(\omega))\in K^i\). We have for every \(\omega\in\Omega\) that 
\begin{align*}
    (\vartheta-\xi)^*_{\infty}(\omega)=&\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}|\vartheta_t(\omega)-\xi_t(\omega)|
    \\&\leq \sum_{i=0}^{n}|\widetilde{\eta}_i(x_i)-\eta_i(x_i)|\leq \sum_{i=0}^{n}\sum_{j=0}^m\big|\alpha_{ij}\big|\big|\cos(A_{ij}(x_i))-\varphi_{ij}^{\epsilon(ij)}(x_i)\big|<\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}},
\end{align*}
where we used
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{i=0}^{n}\sum_{j=0}^m\big|\alpha_{ij}\big|\big|\cos(A_{ij}(x_i))-\varphi_{ij}^{\epsilon(ij)}(x_i)\big|
\leq \sum_{i=0}^{n}\sum_{j=0}^m\big|\alpha_{ij}\big|\sup_{x_i\in K^i}\big|\cos(A_{ij}(x_i))-\varphi_{ij}^{\epsilon(ij)}(x_i)\big|\leq
\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}.
\end{equation*}
This allows us to bound the \(L^2(M)\)-norm as follows
\begin{align*}
    \lVert\vartheta-\xi\rVert_{L^2(M)}^2=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}(\xi_t-\vartheta_t)^2\diff [M]_t\right)\bigg]\leq \mathbb{E}\big[\big((\xi-\vartheta)_{\infty}^*\big)^2[M]_{\infty}\big]\leq\epsilon\mathbb{E}\big[[M]_{\infty}\big].
\end{align*}
This holds for every \(\epsilon>0\) and as
\(\mathbb{E}[[M]_{\infty}]\lesssim\lVert M\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^2}^2<\infty\) due to the assumption that \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\), whence the claim.
\end{proof}
In particular, according to Proposition \ref{prop:sigmoidal implies discriminatory} we might take \(\psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\) to be a continuous, sigmoidal function. 


The compactness assumption on the image of \(Y_t\) is unpleasant and is violated in basic examples. Indeed, assume that our market information is given a price process, which is taken to be a Brownian motion. Then, by the law of iterated logarithm (see \parencite[Thm.~2.1]{Schweizer2025BrownianMotionStochasticCalculus}), it has almost surely unbounded paths. To eliminate the compactness assumption, we observe the following. We are only considering \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) at the moment. The relevant approximation property from the second part of Proposition \ref{prop:approximation of measurable maps by algorithmic strategies} does not require \(Y\) to be Lévy. So we might as well consider 
\begin{equation*}
    \widetilde{Y}_t=\tanh(Y_t),\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+.
\end{equation*} 
Then, clearly 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filtration invariant under bijective transformation}
    \mathcal{F}^0=\sigma(Y_t:t\in\mathbb{R}_+)=\sigma(\widetilde{Y}_t:t\in\mathbb{R}_+)=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^0,
\end{equation}
because \(\tanh:\mathbb{R}\to(-1,1)\) is injective, hence \(Y_t=\tanh^{-1}\circ\tanh\circ Y_t\). The same clearly holds for the respective \(\mathbb{P}\)-completions \(\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}\vee\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\) and \(\mathcal{F}^0=\overline{\mathcal{F}}^0\vee\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\). Denote by \(\widetilde{\Theta}_{\mathcal{NN}}(t,\psi)\) the class of random variables analogously defined as \(\Theta_{\mathcal{NN}}(t,\psi)\) but with \(\widetilde{Y}\) instead of \(Y\); that is, \(Z\in\widetilde{\Theta}_{\mathcal{NN}}(t,\psi)\) has the representation
\begin{equation*}
    Z =f(\widetilde{Y}_{t_1},\dots, \widetilde{Y}_{t_n})
\end{equation*}
for some \(f\in\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(\mathbb{R}^{d\times n})\). 
Denote by \(\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\psi)\) the set of processes of the form
\begin{equation*}
  \widetilde{\vartheta}=\widetilde{\eta}_01_{\left\{0\right\}}+\sum_{i=0}^n\widetilde{\eta}_i1_{(t_I,t_{i+1}]},
  \end{equation*}
 where \(\widetilde{\eta}_i\in\widetilde{\Theta}_{\mathcal{NN}}(t,\psi)\).  


 
 Observe that we can simply replace \({\Theta}_{\mathcal{NN}}(t,\psi)\) with \(\widetilde{\Theta}_{\mathcal{NN}}(t,\psi)\) in part (2.) of  Proposition \ref{prop:approximation of measurable maps by algorithmic strategies} to conclude that \(\widetilde{\Theta}_{\mathcal{NN}}(t,\psi)\) is dense in \(L^p({\mathcal{F}_{t_i}})\). Again, this is because the Lévy-assumption of \(Y\) is only relevant for properties of \(\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}\) that allow for stopping-times \(\tau\), which is part (3.) of Proposition \ref{prop:approximation of measurable maps by algorithmic strategies}. More precisely, one needs \(\mathcal{F}_{\tau}=\sigma(\tau)\vee\sigma(Y_t^{\tau}:t\in J)\vee\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\) in part (3.) of Proposition \ref{prop:approximation of measurable maps by algorithmic strategies}, which is true if \(Y\) is assumed to be Lévy. However, regarding the definition of \(\mathcal{E}\) and \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\), which involve deterministic times only, we don't need \(Y\) to be Lévy. Indeed, for part (1.) of Proposition \ref{prop:approximation of measurable maps by algorithmic strategies}, the arguments are unchanged for \(\widetilde{Y}\) instead of \(Y\). Indeed, the crucial Doob-Dynkin application for the process 
 \begin{equation*}
     \widetilde{M}_n=\mathbb{E}[Z_{\mathcal{F}_I}|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_n]
 \end{equation*}
 yields the existence of a measurable map \(\widetilde{f}_n\) such that
 \begin{equation*}
      \widetilde{M}_n=\widetilde{f}_n(\widetilde{Y}_{j_1},\dots,\widetilde{Y}_{j_n})\,.
 \end{equation*}
 Hence, the argument for part (1.) of Proposition \ref{prop:approximation of measurable maps by algorithmic strategies} is  completely analogous when replacing \(Y\) by \(\widetilde{Y}\).
 
 
 From a conceptual point of view, the trader/seller observes the market information at time \(t\) given by \(\mathcal{F}_t^0=\sigma(Y_s:s\leq t)=\sigma(\widetilde{Y}_s:s\leq t)=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_t^0\). Exchanging \(Y\) with \(\widetilde{Y}\) and vise versa should  be regarded as a change of coordinates for the seller/buyer, as long as the time of observation is deterministic. 



In particular, we have the density of \(\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\psi)\) in \(L^2(M)\). Crucially, considering \(\widetilde{Y}\) instead of \(Y\) has the following advantage. Observe that
\begin{equation*}
\widetilde{Y}_t=\tanh(Y_t)\in(-1,1)\subseteq[-1,1],\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+.
\end{equation*}
Hence, \(\widetilde{Y}\) takes values in a compact subset of \(\mathbb{R}\). Instead of requiring that the image of \(Y_t\) is included in a compact subset for every \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\), we might consider \(\widetilde{Y}\) instead.
Furthermore, notice that we need \(\psi\) to be continuous and discriminatory as a real map for the proof of Proposition \ref{prop:density of E(psi) in L^2(M)}. By Proposition \ref{prop:sigmoidal implies discriminatory} it is enough to take \(\psi\) to be continuous and sigmoidal. 
Furthermore, by \eqref{eq:filtration invariant under bijective transformation}, the completed filtration generated by \(\widetilde{Y}\) satisfies the usual hypotheses because it is equal to the completed filtration generated by the Lévy process \(Y\), which, in turn, satisfies the usual hypotheses (see discussion below \ref{eq:stopped natural sigma algebra}). In particular, we might still assume the same stochastic basis as in \eqref{eq:stochastic basis satisfying usual hypotheses}.
Hence, applying Proposition \ref{prop:density of E(psi) in L^2(M)} with \(\widetilde{Y}=\tanh(Y)\) and assuming \(\psi\) to be continuous and sigmoidal, we obtain the density of \(\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\psi)\) in \(L^2(M)\), leading to the following corollary of Proposition \ref{prop:Lp(M) density of E(psi)}.
\begin{corollary}\label{cor:Lp(M) density of tilde(E)(psi) for change of variables}
    Let \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^{2}\) and \(\psi\) be continuous, sigmoidal activation functions. Then the set \(\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\psi)\) is dense in \(L^2(M)\) with respect to the norm \(\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^2(M)}\). Moreover, for every \(\vartheta\in L^2(M)\), there exists a sequence \((\widetilde{\vartheta^n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\psi)\) such that
    \begin{equation*}
        \lim_{n\to\infty}\int \widetilde{\vartheta^n}\diff M=\int \vartheta\diff M,
    \end{equation*}
    where convergence holds in \(\mathcal{H}^2\). 
\end{corollary}
This version does not require the additional \(\epsilon\)-infinitesimal integrability of \(M\) as was the case in Proposition \ref{prop:Lp(M) density of E(psi)}.  


\chapter{Deep mean--variance hedging in continuous time}\label{ch:deep mean-square hedging in continuous time}


\section{Deep mean--variance hedging in the martingale case}
In this section, we state the deep mean-variance hedging results in continuous time. The respective result \parencite[Thm.~5.35]{Arandjelovic2025} is formulated in the square integrable martingale setting, that is, for a square integrable martingale price-process and strategies in \(L^2(M)\). More precisely, \parencite[Thm.~5.35]{Arandjelovic2025} requires \(M\in\mathcal{H}^{2+\epsilon}_0\) for some positive \(\epsilon\). Theorem \ref{thm:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging} ties together the square integrable martingale results: Proposition \ref{prop:square-integrable mean variance via KW} and Proposition \ref{prop:Lp(M) density of E(psi)}, which in turn are based on concepts like the stochastic integral in the square integrable martingale setting discussed in Section \ref{sec:Stochastic Integration for square-integrable Martingales}, shallow feed-forward neural networks discussed in Chapter \ref{ch:Neural Networks and Universal Approximation}, and the whole framework of deep-hedging developed in \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging} and discussed in \ref{ch:Deep Hedging in Discrete Time}, and in particular its generalized treatment in continuous time developed by \parencite{Arandjelovic2025} and presented in Chapter \ref{ch:Approximation by Algorithmic Strategies in Continuous Time}. 

We then discuss how to generalize theorem \ref{thm:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging} to the more general price processes in the class \(\mathcal{S}^2\) of semimartingales, which we refer to as the semimartingale setting. This involves results on mean-variance hedging as discussed in Section \ref{sec:Mean-variance Hedging with continuous price process} and, in particular, \parencite{DelbaenSchachermayer1998ExistenceAbsolutelyContinuousLMM}. Theorem \ref{thm:continuous time deep mean variance hedging for semimartingales} ties together all the concepts discussed in this thesis.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging}
Assume there exists \(\epsilon>0\) such that \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^{2+\epsilon}\) and let \(H\in L^2(\mathbb{P})\) be a real-valued \(\mathcal{F}_{\infty}\)-measurable random variable. Furthermore, let \(\psi\) be a bounded, measurable, and sigmoidal activation function, and assume \(\mathcal{F}_0\) to be trivial. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging}
    \min_{V_0\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in L^2(M)}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(V_0+\int_{0}^{\infty}\vartheta_t\diff M_t-H\bigg)^2\bigg]
    \,=\,\inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}(\psi)}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(V_0+\int_{0}^{\infty}\vartheta_t\diff M_t-H\bigg)^2\bigg],
\end{equation}
where the minimum on the left-hand side is unique in \(\mathbb{R}\times L^2(M)\). 
In particular, there are two statements: 
\\1) The existence and uniqueness of the minimum for the left-hand side of \eqref{eq:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging}
\\2) The equality in \eqref{eq:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging}. 
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem \ref{thm:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging}]
    The existence of the left-hand side of \eqref{eq:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging} and uniqueness of the minimizing pair \((V_0^*,\vartheta^*)\in \mathbb{R}\times L^2(M)\) follows from Proposition \ref{prop:square-integrable mean variance via KW}. It remains to show part (2). Observe that it is enough to show that for any \(\delta>0\) there exists a strategy \(\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) such that 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:delta property}
       \lVert V_0^*+(\vartheta^*\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}+\delta\geq \lVert V_0^*+(\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}.
    \end{equation}
Indeed, because \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\subseteq L^2(M)\), we have 
\begin{equation*}
    \min_{V_0\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in L^2(M)}\lVert V_0+(\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}\leq \inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}(\psi)}\lVert V_0+(\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}.
\end{equation*}
Conversely, if \eqref{eq:delta property} holds, we have
\begin{align*}
\lVert V_0^*+(\vartheta^*\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}+\delta
     &\geq \lVert V_0^*+(\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}
     \\&\geq\inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R}}\lVert V_0+(\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}
     \\&\geq \inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}(\psi)}\lVert V_0+(\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}.
\end{align*}
This shows that 
\begin{equation*}
    \min_{V_0\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in L^2(M)}\lVert V_0+(\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}\geq\inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}(\psi)}\lVert V_0+(\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}
\end{equation*}
and hence the desired equality.


    By Proposition \ref{prop:Lp(M) density of E(psi)}, for every \(\delta>0\) there exists \(\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) such that \(\lVert \vartheta
    ^*\bullet M-\vartheta\bullet M\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^2}\leq\delta\). Hence
    \begin{align*}
        \lVert V_0^*+(\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}&\leq\lVert V_0^*+(\vartheta^*\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}+\lVert (\vartheta^*\bullet M)_{\infty}-(\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}\rVert_{L^2}\\
        &\leq\lVert V_0^*+(\vartheta^*\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}+\lVert (\vartheta^*\bullet M)-(\vartheta\bullet M)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^2}
        \\&\leq\lVert V_0^*+(\vartheta^*\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}+\delta.
    \end{align*}
    Hence, there exists \(\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) such that \eqref{eq:delta property} holds and the theorem is proved.
\end{proof}
    As Theorem \ref{thm:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging} is formulated for \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\), according to the discussion leading up to Corollary \ref{cor:Lp(M) density of tilde(E)(psi) for change of variables}, we might assume \(\psi\) to be continuous and  substitute \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) with \(\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\psi)\) in order to replace the extra \(\epsilon\)-integrability assumption of \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^{2+\epsilon}\) with the assumption \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^{2}\).

\begin{remark}\label{rem:sufficient condition for extension}
      Notice that what made the proof of part (2.) work was essentially the continuity of the map \(\vartheta\mapsto(\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}\) as a continuous linear operator from \(L^2(M)\) to \(L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty},\mathbb{P})\). This is the essential idea that is going to lead us to extend Theorem \ref{thm:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging} to the semimartingale setting. 
\end{remark}
 


\section{Extension for semimartingales}\label{sec:extension of deep mean squared hedging to semimartingales}
Regarding Theorem \ref{thm:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging}, one is led to the natural question of whether one can generalize the theorem to a more general class of price processes. In this subsection, we put forward one way to do this. According to Remark \ref{rem:sufficient condition for extension} we study how to bound \(\lVert (\vartheta\bullet X)_{\infty}\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})}\) for \(X\in \mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{loc}}\) and \(\vartheta\) some admissible integrand. We are naturally led to consider the class of admissible integrands \(\Theta=L^2(M,A)\) corresponding to the class of integrable processes defined in \eqref{eq:X integrable processes for X in S^2}. In order to argue about the density of \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) in \(L^2(M,A)\) we study sufficient assumptions on \(X=X_0+M+A\) for which \(L^2(M,A)=L^2(M)\) and the density follows by Proposition \ref{prop:Lp(M) density of E(psi)}. The corresponding sufficient conditions have been worked out in \parencite{DelbaenMonatSchachermayerSchweizerStricker1997WeightedNormInequalities}, whose work we follow in this chapter. For what is to follow, we consider all stochastic processes to be real-valued processes, in particular, one-dimensional. 


Recall the standing assumption that \(X\in\mathcal{S}_{\rm{\rm{loc}}}^2\) has a unique canonical decomposition
\begin{equation}\label{eq:canonical decomposition}
    X=X_0+M+A,
\end{equation}
where \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{0,\rm{\rm{loc}}}\) is a locally square-integrable local martingale starting at \(0\) and \(A\), of finite variation, predictable, starting at \(0\) and with locally square-integrable total variation. 


In order to generalize Theorem \ref{thm:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging} for \(X\in\mathcal{S}_{\rm{loc}}^2\) (or rather \(X\in\mathcal{S}^2\) as we shall see), we need to answer a question analogous to claims (1) and (2) in Theorem \ref{thm:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging}. Part (1) necessitates the study of the existence and uniqueness of a solution to the mean-variance hedging problem in the semimartingale setup. 
 We treated it partly in Section \ref{sec:Mean-variance Hedging with continuous price process} and mainly refer to \parencite{Schweizer2001GuidedTourQuadraticHedging} for a good overview and \parencite{Schweizer1994ApproximatingRandomVariablesStochasticIntegrals} for exhaustive treatment. For part (2), we observed in Remark \ref{rem:sufficient condition for extension} that we need to study how to bound the \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\)-norm of \((\vartheta\bullet X)_{\infty}\). Thus, the following motivates further definitions of adapted admissible strategies for this subsection. Consider some \(\vartheta\) for which the integral \(\int_0^\infty\vartheta\diff X\) is well-defined. The following heuristics, bounding the \(L^2\)-norm of the integral from above, motivate the subsequent definitions 
\begin{align}\label{eq:bounding-L2-norm-stochastic-integral}
    \lVert (\vartheta\bullet X)_{\infty}\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})}=&\lVert (\vartheta\bullet A)_{\infty}+(\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})}
    \notag\\&\leq \lVert (\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})}+\lVert (\vartheta\bullet A)_{\infty}\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})}\notag\\&\leq\lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)} +\lVert (|\vartheta|\bullet |A|)_{\infty}\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})},
\end{align}
where we used the triangle inequality for the \(L^2\)-norm for the first inequality and the Itô-isometry for \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{0,\rm{loc}}\) (see Remark \ref{rem:local integrator global integrand yields global integral}), as well as the bound
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bound on FV integral}
    \bigg|\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta_t\diff A_t\bigg|\leq \int_0^{\infty}|\vartheta_t|\diff |A|_t,
\end{equation} 
for the second inequality (see \parencite[Prop.3.13.]{Folland1999RealAnalysis} for a justification of \eqref{eq:bound on FV integral}). We want to read the \eqref{eq:bounding-L2-norm-stochastic-integral} as a continuity statement for the linear operator \(I_{\infty}:\vartheta\to (\vartheta\bullet X)_{\infty}\) between normed spaces \( (\mathcal{D},\left\lvert\!\left\lvert\!\left\lvert \cdot\right\rvert\!\right\rvert\!\right\rvert)\to(L^2(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{F}_{\infty}),\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^2})\), that is,
\begin{equation*}
    \lVert (\vartheta\bullet X)_{\infty}\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})}\lesssim \left\lvert\!\left\lvert\!\left\lvert \vartheta\right\rvert\!\right\rvert\!\right\rvert
\end{equation*}
for some normed space  \((\mathcal{D},\left\lvert\!\left\lvert\!\left\lvert \cdot\right\rvert\!\right\rvert\!\right\rvert)\). By the above, the natural candidate for \(\mathcal{D}\) is the following space.
\begin{definition}\label{def:L2MA}
Let \(X\in\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{loc}}\) with canonical decomposition as in \eqref{eq:canonical decomposition}. In particular, \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{0,\rm{loc}}\) and \(|A|\) is locally in \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\). We define the following space
\begin{align*}
     \mathcal{L}^2(M,A)\coloneqq\left\{\vartheta \text{ predictable}:\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\infty} \vartheta_t^2\diff \langle M\rangle_t\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}+\mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(\int_0^{\infty} |\vartheta_t|\diff |A|_t\right)^2\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}\eqqcolon\lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}<\infty\right\}.
\end{align*}
    Then, as usual, we define
    \begin{equation*}
        L^2(M,A)=\mathcal{L}^2(M,A)/\sim,
    \end{equation*}
    where \(X\sim Y\) if and only if \(\lVert X-Y\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}=0\). 
    Further, we introduce the following set
 \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{L}^2(A)=\left\{\vartheta \text{ predictable }:\bigg\lVert\int_{0}^{\infty}|\vartheta_t|\diff |A|_t\bigg\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})}\eqqcolon\lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(A)}<\infty\right\}
   \end{equation*}
    and the corresponding quotient space \(L^2(A)\).
\end{definition}
With this notation, we have
\begin{equation*}
    L^2(M,A)=L^2(A)\cap L^2(M)
\end{equation*}
and \begin{equation}\label{eq:norm-L2MA}
    \lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}=\lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(A)}+\lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}.
\end{equation}
As usual, we abuse notation when discussing elements in \(L^2(M,A)\) as processes, although we implicitly pick representatives of the corresponding equivalence class.
 Furthermore, by  Definition \ref{def:X integrable processes for X in S^2_loc}, processes in \(L^2(M,A)\) are integrable with respect to \(X\in\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{\rm{loc}}}\) by definition with \(\int \vartheta\diff X\in\mathcal{S}^2\). Also, by \eqref{eq:bounding-L2-norm-stochastic-integral}, we have
\(\Theta=L^2(M,A)\subseteq \Theta^{(2)}\). These remarks justify the heuristics in \eqref{eq:bounding-L2-norm-stochastic-integral} for \(\vartheta\in L^2(M,A)\).
The following is a useful lemma.
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:norm 0 implies process indistinguishable from 0}
    Let \(\vartheta\in L^2(M,A)\) and \(\lVert\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}\) be as in \eqref{eq:norm-L2MA}. Then \(\lVert\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}=0\) if and only if the process \(\vartheta\bullet X\) is indistinguishable from the \(0\)-process.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    There are two directions to show. Assume \(\lVert\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}=0\). Then \(\lVert\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}=0\) and \(\lVert\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(A)}=0\). For the martingale part, we have
    \begin{equation*}
\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^\infty\vartheta_t^2\diff [M]_t \bigg]=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg[\int_0^{\cdot}\vartheta\diff M\bigg]_\infty \bigg]=0.
    \end{equation*}
 Since the optional quadratic variation process is a non-decreasing process, starting at zero, it is non-negative; hence, the above implies \begin{equation*}
     \bigg[\int_0^{\cdot}\vartheta\diff M\bigg]_t=0\;\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+.
 \end{equation*}
 By the Remark \ref{rem:local integrator global integrand yields global integral}, \(\int\vartheta\diff M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\) for \(\vartheta\in L^2(M)\) and \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{0,\rm{loc}}\). Hence, by BDG, this implies that \(\int \vartheta\diff M=0\) \(\mathbb{P}\)-almost surely. For the finite variation part, simply notice that
 from \(\lVert\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(A)}=0\) we get \(\int_0^\infty|\vartheta_t|\ \diff |A|_t=0\) almost surely.
 Hence, by \eqref{eq:bound on FV integral} we get \(\int \vartheta\diff A=0\) almost surely. 

Conversely, assume that \(\vartheta\bullet X = \vartheta\bullet M+\vartheta\bullet A=0\) almost surely. Hence, \(\vartheta\bullet M=-\vartheta\bullet A\). In particular, \(\vartheta\bullet M\) is a predictable martingale of finite variation starting at zero; hence, it is equal to the zero process (see \parencite[Lemma 10.3.9.(ii)]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications}). This, in turn, implies that \(\vartheta\bullet A\) is equal to the zero process as well. 
By 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:variation of FV integral}
    |\int_0^\cdot\vartheta\diff A|_{\infty}=\int_0^\infty|\vartheta_t|\diff |A|_t,
\end{equation}
where the left-hand side denotes the total variation process of the process \(\int \vartheta\diff A\), we have that \(\vartheta\bullet A = 0\) implies that \(\lVert\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(A)}=\lVert (|\vartheta|\bullet|A|)_{\infty}\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})}=0\). See \parencite[Thm.~2.11]{Gushchin2015MathematicalBasisFinance} for a proof of \eqref{eq:variation of FV integral}. By the Itô-isometry, we have \(0=\lVert(\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})}=\lVert\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}\). Hence, \(\lVert\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}=\lVert\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}+\lVert\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(A)}=0\).
\end{proof}
The following shows that \((L^2(M,A),\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^2(M,A)})\) is a Banach space.
\begin{proposition}
    Let \(L^2(M,A)=L^2(M)\cap L^2(A)\) be defined as in Definition \ref{def:L2MA} and equipped with the norm \(\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}\) as defined in \eqref{eq:norm-L2MA}. Then \((L^2(M,A),\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^2(M,A)})\) is a complete metric space, that is, every Cauchy sequence converges.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    The following proof follows the same proof strategy used to show the completeness of \(L^p(\mu)\) space \parencite[Thm.~4.5]{LeGall2022MeasureTheoryProbabilityStochasticProcesses}. We consider a fast-decreasing Cauchy sequence and use it to construct an almost-sure limit, which we take as a  candidate for our limit process in \(L^2(M,A)\). One then needs to show that the pointwise limit is also a \(L^2(M,A)\)-limit to conclude the proof. The main difficulty in the case \(L^2(M,A)\) compared to the classic \(L^p(\mu)\)-case arises from the fact that \(L^2(A)\) is not a pure \(L^2(\mu)\) space for some measure \(\mu\) on the space of predictable processes, but a mix of \(L^2\) and \(L^1\). 



    Let \((\vartheta^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq L^2(M,A)\) be a Cauchy sequence in \((L^2(M,A),\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^2(M,A)})\). 
    Without loss of generality, assume 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:fastly converging cauchy}
        \lVert \vartheta^{n+1}-\vartheta^{n}\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}\leq\frac{1}{2^n}.
    \end{equation}
    If this is not the case, consider a subsequence \((\vartheta^{n(k)})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\) that satisfies the above. Indeed, showing that a subsequence of a Cauchy sequence converges in norm implies that the original Cauchy sequence converges to that same limit.

    
    Furthermore, observe that the definition of \eqref{eq:norm-L2MA} implies that \((\vartheta^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) is a Cauchy sequence in \(L^2(M)\) and \(L^2(A)\) separately, and \eqref{eq:fastly converging cauchy} holds in both spaces separately as well. 
    Consider the formal expression
    \begin{equation*}
        g=|\vartheta^1|+\sum_{n=1}^\infty|\vartheta^{n+1}-\vartheta^{n}|,
    \end{equation*}
    where the series may or may not be finite at this point. For the following, we denote the partial sums as follows
    \begin{equation*}
        g_N=|\vartheta^1|+\sum_{n=1}^N|\vartheta^{n+1}-\vartheta^{n}|.
    \end{equation*}
    We show that \(g\in L^2(M,A)\). We do this by arguing that \(g\in L^2(M)\) and \(g\in L^2(A)\). Clearly, \(g\) is predictable as a pointwise limit of predictable processes \(g_N\), which are in turn predictable as a finite sum of predictable processes \(\vartheta^i\) with \(i=1,\dots,N+1\).
    
    
    Recall that \(L^2(M)\simeq L^2(\mathbb{P}_M)\) for the measure \(\mathbb{P}_M=\mathbb{P}_{\langle M\rangle}=\mathbb{P}_{[M]}\) on the predictable \(\sigma\)-algebra, where we readily choose \(\mathbb{P}_M=\mathbb{P}_{\langle M\rangle}\) defined as
    \begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{P}_M:B\mapsto\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\infty}1_B^2\diff\langle M\rangle\bigg],\;B\in\mathcal{P}.
    \end{equation*}
    Hence, by the monotone convergence theorem and the triangle inequality
    \begin{align*}
        \lVert g\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P}_M)}&=\lVert \lim_{N\to\infty}g_N\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P}_M))}
        \\&=\lim_{N\to\infty}\lVert g_N\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P}_M)}\leq \lVert
        \vartheta^1\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P}_M)}+\sum_{n=1}^\infty\lVert \vartheta^{n+1}-\vartheta^n\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P}_M)}<\infty,
    \end{align*}
    where we used in the last step that the series is bounded from above by the convergent series \(\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac{1}{2^n}<\infty\). For the \(L^2(A)\)-part, we need to argue by successive applications of monotone convergence.
    \begin{align*}
    \mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(\int_0^{\infty}\lim_{N\to\infty}g_N\diff|A|\right)^2\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}&=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\lim_{N\to\infty}\left(\int_0^{\infty} g_N\diff|A|\right)^2\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}
    \\&=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(\int_0^{\infty} g_N\diff|A|\right)^2\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}
    \\&\leq \lVert\vartheta^1\rVert_{L^2(A)}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\lVert \vartheta^{n+1}-\vartheta^{n}\rVert_{L^2(A)}<\infty,
    \end{align*}
    where the first equality is due to the monotone convergence for every \(\omega\) of the increasing, non-negative sequence \(g_N(\omega)\), and the second equality is again due to the monotone convergence for the increasing, non-negative sequence \(\int_0^{\infty}g_N\diff |A|\). The last inequality follows from the triangle inequality for \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\). This shows that
    \begin{equation*}
        g\in L^2(M,A).
    \end{equation*}
    Next, we want to argue that \(g<\infty\) pointwise on a large enough set (we will see what we mean by "large enough"). We just showed that \(g\in L^2(M)\). Hence, 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:g is P_M finite}
        g<\infty,\;\mathbb{P}_M\text{-a.s.}
    \end{equation}
    Equivalently, for
     \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{K}=\left\{(\omega,t):g(\omega,t)=\infty\right\}
    \end{equation*}
    we have by \eqref{eq:g is P_M finite} that
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{P}_M(\mathcal{K})=0.
    \end{equation*}
    Indeed, as we can write
    \begin{equation*}
         \mathcal{K}=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left\{g\geq n\right\}
     \end{equation*}
     \(\mathcal{K}\) is a predictable set.

    
    Concretely, outside of the \(\mathbb{P}_M\) null-set \(\mathcal{K}\) we have 
    \begin{equation*}
        g(\omega,t)=|\vartheta^1(\omega,t)|+\sum_{n=1}^\infty|\vartheta^{n+1}(\omega,t)-\vartheta^n(\omega,t)|<\infty,\;(\omega,t)\in \mathcal{K}^{c}.
    \end{equation*}
    For the \(L^2(A)\) part, the matter is more delicate. 
    We want to show that 
     \begin{equation*}
         \mathbb{P}_{|A|}(\mathcal{K}) = \mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\infty}1_\mathcal{K}\diff |A|\bigg]=0,
     \end{equation*}
     where \(\mathbb{P}_{|A|}:B\mapsto \mathbb{E}[\int_0^{\infty}1_B\diff |A|]\) defines a measure on \((\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+,\mathcal{P})\) (contrary to the map \(B\mapsto \mathbb{E}[\left(\int_0^{\infty}1_B\diff |A|\right)^2]\)). First, notice that \(\mathcal{K}\) is a predictable set, as it can be written as a countable intersection of predictable sets
     \begin{equation*}
         \mathcal{K}=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left\{g\geq n\right\}.
     \end{equation*}
We have shown that \(g\in L^2(A)\) hence
     \begin{equation*}
          \mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}g\diff |A|\right)^2\bigg]<\infty\implies \int_{0}^{\infty}g(\omega,t)\diff |A|_t(\omega)<\infty
     \end{equation*}
     for almost all \(\omega\in \Omega\).
  Equivalently
  \begin{equation*}
      \int_0^\infty 1_{\mathcal{K}}(\omega,t) \diff |A|_t(\omega)=0
  \end{equation*}
  for almost all \(\omega\in\Omega\). Hence, 
  \begin{equation*}
     \mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^\infty 1_{\mathcal{K}}(\omega,t) \diff |A|_t(\omega)\bigg]=0. 
  \end{equation*}
   That means
     \begin{equation*}
         g<\infty,\; \mathbb{P}_{|A|}\text{-a.s.}
     \end{equation*}
     Hence, we can define the following series
 \begin{equation*}
     h(\omega,t) = \vartheta^1(\omega,t)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\vartheta^{n+1}(\omega,t)-\vartheta^n(\omega,t)\right)\;(\omega,t)\in\mathcal{K}^c
 \end{equation*}
and set \(h=0\) on \( \mathcal{K}\). 
Observe that the partial sums \(h^N=\vartheta^1+\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}\big(\vartheta^{n+1}-\vartheta^n\big)=\vartheta^N\). Hence, on \(\mathcal{K}^c\) we have 
\begin{equation*}
    h(\omega,t)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\vartheta^n(\omega,t).
\end{equation*}
Furthermore, as the predictable set \(\mathcal{K}\) is both a \(\mathbb{P}_M\)-null set and a \(\mathbb{P}_{|A|}\)-null set, we have that 
\begin{equation*}
    h=\lim_{n\to\infty}\vartheta^n
\end{equation*}
pointwise, both \(\mathbb{P}_{|A|}\)-almost surely and \(\mathbb{P}_M\)-almost surely. Furthermore, by \parencite[Lemma.~1.15]{LeGall2022MeasureTheoryProbabilityStochasticProcesses}, \(h\) is measurable. We want to show that \(h\in L^2(M,A)\). Again, we show that it is in \(L^2(M)\), and that it is in \(L^2(A)\) as well. But both clearly follow from 
\begin{equation*}
    |h|\leq g
\end{equation*}
and the monotonicity of the norms on \(L^2(M)\) and \(L^2(A)\), which in turn follows from the monotonicity of \(x\mapsto x^p\) and the Lebesgue integral. 


It remains to show that 
\begin{equation*}
    \vartheta^n\overset{L^2(M,A)}{\to}h.
\end{equation*}
Equivalently, we show that \(\lVert\vartheta^n-h\rVert_{L^2(M)}\to 0\) and \(\lVert\vartheta^n-h\rVert_{L^2(A)}\to 0\). Observe that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:DCT bound for L^2(M,A) completeness}
    |h-\vartheta^n|^2\leq 4g^2,\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}
\end{equation}
pointwise. Indeed, on \(\mathcal{K}^c\), this holds by the triangle inequality and the fact that
\begin{align*}
    |\vartheta^n|=\bigg|\vartheta^1+\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}(\vartheta^{k+1}-\vartheta^k)\bigg|\leq |\vartheta^1|+\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}|\vartheta^{k+1}-\vartheta^k|\leq g
\end{align*}
and on \(\mathcal{K}\) we defined \(h=0\); hence, the same bound holds.
We have already shown that \(g\in L^2(M)\) and \(L^2(A)\). Hence, by dominated convergence, we get 
\begin{equation*}
    \lVert h-\vartheta^n\rVert_{L^2(M)}\to 0.
\end{equation*}
Similarly, as \(\int_0^{\infty}g(\omega,t)\diff |A|_t(\omega)<\infty\) \(\mathbb{P}\)-almost surely, we have for almost all \(\omega\), by dominated convergence with domination by \eqref{eq:DCT bound for L^2(M,A) completeness} that
\begin{equation*}
    \int_0^{\infty}|h(\omega,t)-\vartheta^n(\omega,t)|\diff |A|_t(\omega)\to 0.
\end{equation*}
Then, due to \(\int_0^{\infty}|h(\omega,t)-\vartheta^n(\omega,t)|\diff |A|_t(\omega)\lesssim\int_0^{\infty}|g(\omega,t)|\diff |A|_t( \omega)\), we apply the dominated convergence theorem again to get
\begin{equation*}
     \lVert h-\vartheta^n\rVert_{L^2(A)}\to 0.
\end{equation*}
which concludes the proof.
\end{proof}

 By construction, or more precisely by \eqref{eq:bounding-L2-norm-stochastic-integral}, we have for processes \(\vartheta\in L^2(M,A)\) that the map
 \begin{align*}\label{eq:final integral map}
    I_{\infty}:\;&\vartheta\mapsto (\vartheta\bullet X)_{\infty},
     \\&L^2(M,A)\to L^2(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{F}_{\infty})
 \end{align*} is a continuous, linear map.

 Hence, an approximating sequence of \(\vartheta\in L(M,A)\) yields an approximating sequence of the integral \((\vartheta\bullet X)_{\infty}\) in \(L^2\). We have seen in Proposition \ref{prop:density of E in Lp(M) via monotone class} that we can approximate \(\vartheta\in L^2(M)\) by elements in \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\). We thus need to argue under what assumptions we can approximate \(\vartheta\in L^2(M,A)\) by elements of \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\). Our approach is based on the observations that both \(L^2(M)\) and  \(L^2(M,A)\) are defined through their norms on the space of predictable processes. Hence, if we find conditions on the semimartingale \(X\) such that both norms are equivalent, then the spaces \(L^2(M)\) and \(L^2(M,A)\) contain the same (equivalence classes of) processes and thus, \(L^2(M)=L^2(M,A)\) as normed spaces. In particular, if \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) is dense in \(L^2(M)\), it is clearly dense in \(L^2(M,A)\) as well. Thus, we get a deep mean variance hedging result for an appropriate class of semimartingales \(X\). 

 
This motivates further investigations of conditions for which the norms \(\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^2(M)}\) and \(\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}\) are equivalent. First, notice that for all integrable processes \(\vartheta\) we clearly have
\begin{equation*}
    \lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}\leq\lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}.
\end{equation*}
Hence, we want to understand under which conditions there exists \(C<\infty\) such that 
\begin{equation*}
    \lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}\leq C \lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)},\quad \forall \vartheta \in L^2(M)\cap L^2(A)\,.
\end{equation*}
or, equivalently, if there exists \(C<\infty\) such that
\begin{equation}\label{condition_D_2(P)}
    \lVert\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(A)}\leq C\lVert\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)},\quad \forall \vartheta\in L^2(M)\cap L^2(A).
\end{equation}
For notational consistency with \parencite{DelbaenMonatSchachermayerSchweizerStricker1997WeightedNormInequalities}, we write 
\begin{equation*}
    X\in D_2(\mathbb{P})
\end{equation*}
if there exists \(C<\infty\) such that the semimartingale \(X\in\mathcal{S}_{\rm{loc}}^2\) satisfies \eqref{condition_D_2(P)}. 
Although \eqref{condition_D_2(P)} is initially formulated on
\(L^2(M)\cap L^2(A)\), the inequality extends to every
\(\vartheta\in L^2(M)\). Indeed, let
\((\tau_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) be an increasing localizing sequence for
\(A\) such that \(\tau_n \nearrow\infty
    \quad \mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}\) and
\begin{equation*}
    \sup_{0\leq t<\infty}|A|^{\tau_n}_t\in L^2(\mathbb{P}),\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.
\end{equation*}
In particular, notice that this convention implies
\begin{equation*}
    |A|_{\tau_n}=|A|_{\infty}^{\tau_n}\coloneqq\lim_{t\to\infty}|A|^{\tau_n}_t\leq\sup_{0\leq t<\infty}|A|_t^{\tau_n}\in L^2(\mathbb{P}),
\end{equation*}
where the limit exists as \(|A|\) is an increasing process.
Define
\begin{equation*}
    \vartheta^{(n)}
    \coloneqq
    \vartheta
    \mathbf{1}_{\{|\vartheta|\leq n\}}
    \mathbf{1}_{\rrbracket 0,\tau_n\rrbracket}.
\end{equation*}
Then \(\vartheta^{(n)}\in L^2(M)\), since
\(\lVert\vartheta^{(n)}\rVert_{L^2(M)}
    \leq
    \lVert\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}\).

Moreover,
\begin{equation*}
    \int_0^\infty
    \big|\vartheta^{(n)}_t\big|\diff |A|_t
    =
    \int_0^{\tau_n}
    |\vartheta_t|
    \mathbf{1}_{\{|\vartheta_t|\leq n\}}
    \diff |A|_t\leq
    n|A|_{\tau_n}.
\end{equation*}
Hence \(\vartheta^{(n)}\in L^2(A)\), and therefore
\(\vartheta^{(n)}\in L^2(M)\cap L^2(A)\). Consequently,
\eqref{condition_D_2(P)} yields
\begin{equation*}
    \|\vartheta^{(n)}\|_{L^2(A)}
    \leq
    C\|\vartheta^{(n)}\|_{L^2(M)}.
\end{equation*}

Since \((\tau_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) is increasing and converges to
\(\infty\) \(\mathbb{P}\)-almost surely, we have, outside a \(\mathbb{P}\)-null set,
\begin{equation*}
    \big|\vartheta^{(n)}_t\big|
    \nearrow
    |\vartheta_t|
    \qquad
    \text{for every }t> 0\text{ as }n\to\infty.
\end{equation*}
and 
\begin{equation*}
    \int_0^\infty|\vartheta^{(n)}_t|\diff |A|_t
    \nearrow
    \int_0^\infty|\vartheta_t|\diff |A|_t
    \quad\text{ as }n\to\infty,\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.},
\end{equation*}
because \(|A|_t\) is a non-negative increasing process.
The monotone convergence theorem therefore gives
\begin{equation*}
    \|\vartheta^{(n)}\|_{L^2(M)}^2
    \nearrow
    \|\vartheta\|_{L^2(M)}^2
\end{equation*}
and
\begin{equation*}
    \|\vartheta^{(n)}\|_{L^2(A)}^2
    \nearrow
    \|\vartheta\|_{L^2(A)}^2.
\end{equation*}
 Passing to the limit in
\eqref{condition_D_2(P)}, we obtain
\begin{align*}
    \|\vartheta\|_{L^2(A)}^2
    &=
    \lim_{n\to\infty}
    \|\vartheta^{(n)}\|_{L^2(A)}^2
    \notag\\
    &\leq
    C^2
    \lim_{n\to\infty}
    \|\vartheta^{(n)}\|_{L^2(M)}^2
    \notag\\& =
    C^2\|\vartheta\|_{L^2(M)}^2
    <
    \infty.
\end{align*}
Thus \(\vartheta\in L^2(A)\), and
\eqref{condition_D_2(P)} holds for every
\(\vartheta\in L^2(M)\). In particular,
\begin{equation*}
    L^2(M)\subseteq L^2(A),
\end{equation*}
and consequently
\begin{equation*}
    L^2(M,A)=L^2(M)
\end{equation*}
as vector spaces. Moreover, their respective norms are equivalent.



First, we give sufficient conditions for \cref{condition_D_2(P)} by using simple bounds. After this, we provide necessary conditions. A natural condition to relate the \(L^2(A)\) norm with the \(L^2(M)\)-norm is given by the following assumption.
\begin{equation}\label{eq:absolute continuity}
    \diff A_t\ll \diff\langle M\rangle_t\quad\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.},\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+,
\end{equation}
with predictable density \(\lambda\).
That is 
\begin{equation*}
    \exists \lambda=(\lambda_t)_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+} \text{ predictable}:\; \diff A_t=\lambda_t \diff \langle M\rangle_t\quad\;\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.},\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+.
\end{equation*}
Equation \eqref{eq:absolute continuity} is to be understood as the absolute continuity of the pathwise Lebesgue-Stieltjes measures.
Lastly, note that we assume absolute continuity with respect to the process \(\langle M\rangle\), and not \([M]\). Indeed, as remarked in Definition \ref{def:L^p(M)}, this does not matter with regard to the definition of the \(L^2(M)\)-norm. However, by \parencite[Thm.~7]{AnselStricker1992LoisMartingale} it is only for the predictable process \(\langle M\rangle\) that we know the density \(\lambda\) can be taken to be predictable.
If \eqref{eq:absolute continuity} holds, we have, in particular, (using \(\langle M\rangle\) instead of \([M]\))

\begin{align}\label{eq:bounding-L2MA-norm-necessary-assumption}
\lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}
 &=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\infty} \vartheta_t^2\diff \langle M\rangle_t\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}
 +\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(\int_0^{\infty} |\vartheta_t||\lambda_t|\diff\langle M\rangle_t\bigg)^2\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\leq\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\infty} \vartheta_t^2\diff \langle M\rangle_t\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}
 +\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta_t^2\diff \langle M\rangle_t\cdot\int_0^{\infty}\lambda_t^2\diff \langle M\rangle_t\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}\notag \\
&\leq\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\infty} \vartheta_t^2\diff \langle M\rangle_t\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}
 +\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta_t^2\diff \langle M\rangle_t\bigg)^{p}\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2p}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(\int_0^{\infty}\lambda_t^2\diff \langle M\rangle_t\bigg)^{q}\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2q}}\notag 
,
\end{align}
where we used the pathwise Cauchy-Schwarz inequality in the second step and Hölder's inequality for conjugate exponents \(p,q\geq 1\) in the third step. In the case \(p=1,q=\infty\) the above reads
\begin{equation*}
    \lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}\leq\lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}\left(1+\bigg\lVert\int_0^{\infty}\lambda_t^2\diff \langle M\rangle_t\bigg\rVert_{\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right).
\end{equation*}
This motivates the definition of the mean-variance tradeoff (MVT)  
\begin{equation*}
    K_t\coloneqq\int_0^{t}\lambda_s^2\diff \langle M\rangle_s=\int_0^{t}|\lambda_s|\diff |A|_s.
\end{equation*}
Thus, imposing
\begin{equation*}
