    \lVert K_{\infty}\rVert_{\infty}<\infty,
\end{equation*}
Equation \eqref{eq:bounding-L2MA-norm-necessary-assumption} shows that if there exists a predictable density \(\lambda\) such that \(\diff A_t=\lambda_t\diff\langle M\rangle_t\) holds almost surely for all \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\), and if the \(MVT\) is essentially bounded, we have
\begin{equation*}
    \lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(A)}\leq C\lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}
\end{equation*} 
for \(C=\lVert K_{\infty}\rVert_{\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}}<\infty\). 
We thus find that condition \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\) holds for the bounded mean-variance tradeoff, where the existence of MVT necessitates the existence of the predictable density \(\lambda\).  The following condition is common in the literature
\begin{definition}\label{def:condition SC_ttro}
    Assume \(X\) is a semimartingale in \(\mathcal{S}_{\rm{\rm{loc}}}^2\) with canonical decomposition \(X=X_0+M+A\). Then we say that \(X\) satisfies the structure condition \((SC)\) if
    \begin{itemize}
        \item  \(\diff A_t\ll \diff\langle M\rangle_t,\quad\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.},\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+\),
        \item \(K_t=\int_0^t \lambda_s^2\diff \langle M\rangle_s<\infty,\quad\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.},\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+.\) 
    \end{itemize}
\end{definition}
Note that assuming a bounded MVT, in particular, implies the \(SC\) condition. Indeed, \(\langle M\rangle\) is a non-decreasing process and \(\lambda^2\geq 0\). Hence, \(K_t=(\lambda^2\bullet \langle M\rangle)_t\) is non-decreasing. Hence, its limit at \(\infty\) is well-defined (possibly infinite), and if it is bounded at infinity, it is bounded for all previous times.



One can further provide a nice conceptual motivation for the existence of the predictable density appearing in the structure condition. Indeed, according to \parencite[Thm.~3.5.]{DelbaenSchachermayer1998ExistenceAbsolutelyContinuousLMM}, for locally bounded \(X\) satisfying a no-arbitrage condition (see \parencite[Def.~1.2]{DelbaenSchachermayer1998ExistenceAbsolutelyContinuousLMM}), there exists a predictable density \(\lambda\). In other words, we can motivate the absolute continuity of the predictable finite variation part \(A\) of \(X\) with respect to \(\langle M\rangle\) via a no arbitrage condition and thus relate it to an economic assumption rather than an ad hoc mathematical assumption. 
The sufficient conditions for \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\) allow extending the density result in Proposition \ref{prop:Lp(M) density of E(psi)} to the space \(L^2(M,A)\). Indeed, the condition \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\) implies that \(L^2(M,A)=L^2(M)\); thus, the density of \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) in \(L^2(M)\) implies the density of \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) in \(L^2(M,A)\). If we want to formulate an extension of Theorem \ref{thm:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging} to price processes \(X\in L^2(M,A)\), we need to argue about the existence of the mean-variance solution as well, not just how it can be approximated. That is, we need to further investigate the closedness of
\begin{equation*}
    G_{\infty}\big(L^2(M,A)\big)=\left\{\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta_t\diff X_t:\vartheta\in L^2(M,A)\right\}\subseteq L^2(\mathbb{P}).
\end{equation*}
For this, let's take a step back to our general considerations. For the following, we use results obtained in \parencite{DelbaenSchachermayer1998ExistenceAbsolutelyContinuousLMM}.


Recall that in order to develop an \(L^2\)-approximation theory of stochastic integrals, or more precisely their terminal values, we were naturally led to consider the map 
\begin{align*}
I_{\infty}:&(L^2(M,A),\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^2(M,A)})\to(G_{\infty}\big(L^2(M,A)\big),\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})}),
\\&
    \vartheta\mapsto (\vartheta\bullet X)_{\infty}.
\end{align*}
By construction of \(L^2(M,A)\) the latter is a continuous and surjective map,
where \(G_{\infty}\big(L^2(M,A)\big)\subseteq L^2(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{F}_{\infty})\).
What is more, if there exists an ELMM for \(X\) with a square-integrable density, \(I_{\infty}\) is even bijective.
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:integral map is bijective}
    Assume \(X\) is a semimartingale in \(\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{loc}}\) and assume that there exists an equivalent local martingale measure \(\mathbb{Q}\) with square-integrable density \(\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\). Then the map \(I_{\infty}:L^2(M,A)\to G_{\infty}\big(L^2(M,A)\big)\) is bijective.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    We denote \(L^2(M,A)=\Theta\). It remains to show that \(I_{\infty}\) is injective. It suffices to show that 
    \begin{equation*}
        G_{\infty}(\vartheta)=0\implies (\vartheta\bullet X)_{t}=0,\quad\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.},\;\forall \vartheta\in\Theta,\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+.
    \end{equation*}
 Indeed, by Lemma \ref{lemma:norm 0 implies process indistinguishable from 0}, the latter implies that \(\lVert\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}=0\) and thus, \(\vartheta=0\) in \(L^2(M,A)\)
 showing that \(I_{\infty}\) is injective as a linear operator. Define the density process
    \begin{equation*}
        Z_t\coloneqq \mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}|\mathcal{F}_t\bigg],\quad 0\leq t\leq \infty\;.
    \end{equation*}
    The process \(Z\) defines a uniformly integrable \(\mathbb{P}\)-martingale.
    Because \(\mathbb{Q}\approx \mathbb{P}\), \(\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}>0\) we have \(Z_t>0\) for all \(t\in [0,\infty]\). Moreover, due to the conditional Jensen's inequality and the tower property of conditional expectation, we have 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\bigg|\mathcal{F}_t\bigg]\right)^2\bigg]\leq\mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\right)^2\bigg|\mathcal{F}_t\bigg]=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\right)^2\bigg]<\infty,
    \end{equation*}
    as \(\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\) is square integrable by assumption.
    Hence \(Z_t\in L^2(\mathbb{P})\) for all \(t\in[0,\infty]\). Moreover, as \(\mathbb{P}\approx\mathbb{Q}\), \(G(\vartheta)\) being a local \(\mathbb{Q}\)-martingale implies, by Bayes' formula, that \(G(\vartheta)Z\) is a local \(\mathbb{P}\)-martingale. Indeed, for \(s\leq t\) and a localizing sequence \((\tau_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\), for which  \(G(\vartheta)\) is a \(\mathbb{Q}\)-martingale, we have, by Bayes' formula, for the stopping times \(\tau_n\wedge s\leq \tau_n\wedge t\)
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[G(\vartheta)^{\tau_n}_{t}Z^{\tau_n}_{t}|\mathcal{F}_s]=Z^{\tau_n}_{s}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[G(\vartheta)^{\tau_n}_{t}|\mathcal{F}_s]=G(\vartheta)^{\tau_n}_{s}Z^{\tau_n}_{s}.
    \end{equation*}
    and \(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[Z_{t\wedge\tau_n}|G(\vartheta)_{t\wedge\tau_n}|]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[|G(\vartheta)_{t\wedge\tau_n}|]<\infty\).

    
          By Doob's martingale inequality, the process \(Z^*=(Z^*_t)_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}=\big(\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}|Z_s|\big)_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\) is square-integrable, because \(Z\) is square-integrable, as we have just shown. It is easily checked that the same is true for \(G(\vartheta)=\int\vartheta\diff X=\int\vartheta\diff M+\int\vartheta\diff A\) by the definition of \(L^2(M,A)\). Indeed, recall that for \(M\in\mathcal{H}_{0,\rm{loc}}^2\) we have for \(\vartheta\in L^2(M)\) \(G(\vartheta)\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\), hence \((\vartheta\bullet M)^*\) is square-integrable by Doob's martingale inequality. For the finite variation part, observe that \(\sup_{t<\infty}\int_0^t|\vartheta|_s\diff |A|_s=\int_0^\infty|\vartheta|_s\diff |A|_s\) as the process \(\int_0^t|\vartheta|_s\diff |A|_s\) is increasing. The claim then follows directly from the assumption that \(\vartheta\in L^2(A)\). Hence, \((G(\vartheta)Z)^*\) is \(\mathbb{P}\)-integrable by Cauchy-Schwarz. Thus, by Lemma \ref{lemma:local martingale is a true martingale}, \(G(\vartheta)Z\) is a true (uniformly integrable) \(\mathbb{P}\)-martingale with \((G(\vartheta)Z)_{\infty}=0\) because \(G_{\infty}(\vartheta)=0\) by assumption. Thus, \((G(\vartheta)Z)_t=0\) holds for all \(t\), \(\mathbb{P}\)-almost surely. But \(Z\) is almost surely strictly positive. Hence, \(G(\vartheta)=\vartheta\bullet X\) vanishes almost surely.
\end{proof}
This shows that \(I_{\infty}\) is a continuous bijective map from the Banach space \(L^2(M,A)\) to \(G_{\infty}\big(L^2(M,A)\big)\subseteq L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty},\mathbb{P})\). 
Furthermore, notice that the former proof shows the following auxiliary lemma. For this, recall the notation \(\mathbb{P}_s^2(X)\) and \(\mathbb{P}_e^2(X)\) introduced in \ref{def:signed martingale measure} and before Lemma \ref{lemma:ELMM and P_e^2(X)}.
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:P_s(Theta) in P_e(X)}
Let \(\mathbb{Q}\in \mathbb{P}_e^2(X)\). Then \(\mathbb{Q}
\in\mathbb{P}_s^2(X)\).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    By Lemma \ref{lemma:ELMM and P_e^2(X)} we can identify \(\mathbb{P}_e^2(X)\) with the space of ELMM with square integrable density. To show the claim, it remains to argue that \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[G_{\infty}(\vartheta)]=0,\quad\forall \vartheta\in L^2(M,A).
    \end{equation*}
    We have shown in the proof of Lemma \ref{lemma:integral map is bijective} that \(ZG(\vartheta)\) is a uniformly integrable \(\mathbb{P}\)-martingale. Hence 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[G_{\infty}(\vartheta)]=E_{\mathbb{P}}[Z_{\infty}G_{\infty}(\vartheta)]=E_{\mathbb{P}}[Z_{0}G_{0}(\vartheta)]=0.
    \end{equation*}
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    Recall from Lemma \ref{lemma:ELMM and P_e^2(X)} that the condition that there exists an ELMM \(\mathbb{Q}\) for \(X\) with a square integrable density is equivalent to assuming the existence of a measure \(\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}_e^2(X)\).
\end{remark}
\begin{remark}
    Recall our discussion about the uniqueness of the mean-variance solution, if it exists, as a pair \((V_0^*,g^*)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{G}\) in Section \ref{sec:Mean-variance Hedging with continuous price process}. We found that the decomposition is unique if \(1\notin\mathcal{G}\). However, in the case of \(\mathcal{G}=G_{\infty}(\Theta)\), even if \(g^*=\int_0^\infty\vartheta^*_t\diff X_t\) is unique in \(\mathcal{G}=G_{\infty}(\Theta)\), the mean-strategy \(\vartheta^*\) need not be unique. Nevertheless, it is unique if the map \(I_{\infty}:\vartheta\mapsto\int_0^\infty\vartheta_t\diff X_t\) is injective. Hence, Lemma \ref{lemma:integral map is bijective} yields exactly the necessary condition for this to be true. 
\end{remark}
For our purposes, the key observation is the following:
      \(I_{\infty}\) is a linear homeomorphism onto its image if and only if \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\) is a Banach space. Equivalently, \(I_{\infty}\) is a linear homeomorphism onto its image if and only if \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\) is a closed subspace of \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\).
Under the assumption that there exists an ELMM with a square-integrable density, the precise statement in our setting is given and proved in the following proposition.
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:closedness of G_T in L^2 functional analytic iff condition}
Assume there exists an ELMM for \(X\in\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{loc}}\) with a square-integrable density. Then
 \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\subseteq 
L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty},\mathbb{P})\) is closed if and only if there exists \(C>0\) such that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:homeo condition}
    \lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}\leq C\lVert G_{\infty}(\theta)\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})},\quad\forall\vartheta\in L^2(M,A).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    There are two directions to show. Assume  \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\subseteq 
L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty},\mathbb{P})\) is closed. Then, by Lemma \ref{lemma:integral map is bijective}, under the assumption that there exists an ELMM with square-integrable density, \(I_{\infty}\) is a continuous, linear, bijective mapping onto its image. By the inverse operator theorem (see \parencite[Cor.3.2,Ch.4]{SteinShakarchi2005RealAnalysis}), it is even a homeomorphism for the Banach spaces \(L^2(M,A)\) and \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\). In particular, its inverse is a continuous linear map, whence \eqref{eq:homeo condition} holds. Conversely, assume \eqref{eq:homeo condition} holds. Again, by Lemma \ref{lemma:integral map is bijective}, \(I_{\infty}\) is a bijective, linear map onto its image. Hence, its inverse exists and is a linear map from \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\) onto \(L^2(M,A)\). Equation \eqref{eq:homeo condition} implies that the inverse is itself bounded, hence continuous. But then \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\) is the image of the Banach space \(L^2(M,A)\) under the linear homeomorphism \(I_{\infty}\). Hence, \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\) is a closed subspace of \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\). Indeed, let \(((\vartheta_n\bullet X)_{\infty})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) be a sequence in \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\subseteq L^2(\mathbb{P})\) that converges in \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\). In particular, it is a Cauchy sequence. Moreover
   \begin{align*}
       \lVert \vartheta_n-\vartheta_m\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}&=\lVert I_{\infty}^{-1}\big((\vartheta_n\bullet X)_{\infty}\big)-I_{\infty}^{-1}\big((\vartheta_m\bullet X)_{\infty}\big)\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}\\&\leq C\lVert (\vartheta_n\bullet X)_{\infty}-(\vartheta_m\bullet X)_{\infty}\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})},
   \end{align*}
   for any \(n,m\in\mathbb{N}\).
   Thus, with \(\vartheta^n=I_{\infty}^{-1}(\vartheta_n\bullet X)_{\infty})\) the above implies that  \((\vartheta_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) is a Cauchy sequence in \(L^2(M,A)\) which is complete. Hence, there exists \(\vartheta^*\in L^2(M,A)\) such that 
   \begin{equation*}
       \lim_{n\to\infty}\vartheta_n=\vartheta^*
   \end{equation*}
   in \(L^2(M,A)\).
 By the continuity of \(I_{\infty}\), it follows that 
   \begin{equation*}
       \lim_{n\to\infty}I_{\infty}(\vartheta
       _n)=I_{\infty}(\vartheta^*)
   \end{equation*}
   We have thus shown that every sequence in \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\) that converges in \(L^2(\mathbb{P}\) has its limit in \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\). For normed spaces, this characterizes the closedness of a subspace; hence, \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\subseteq L^2(\mathbb{P})\) is closed. 
\end{proof}
This yields a functional analytic, sufficient and necessary condition for the closedness of \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\) and hence the existence of the mean-variance hedging solution. Surprisingly, the following theorem shows a close connection between the closedness of \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\) and the condition \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\). 
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:G_T closed implies D_2(P)}
    Let \(X\) be a semimartingale in \(\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{loc}}\) such that there exists an ELMM with square-integrable density and such that \(A\) is continuous.  If \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\) is closed in \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\), then the inequality \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\) is holds. 
\end{theorem}
The proof will use the following auxiliary result

\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:predictable epsilon}
Suppose that $A$ is a continuous process of finite variation . Let $\vartheta \in \Theta$ and $\eta>0$. Then there exists a predictable process $\varepsilon$ with values in $\{-1,+1\}$ such that
\begin{equation*}
    \quad \left|\int_0^t \varepsilon_s\,\vartheta_s\,dA_s\right|\le \eta,\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+.
\end{equation*}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Without loss of generality, we can assume that $\theta\cdot A$ is increasing. For the argument as to why this can be assumed without loss of generality, see Appendix \ref{Appendix:w.l.o.g.}.


We define a sequence $(\tau_n)_{n\ge 0}$ of stopping times by setting
\[
\tau_0=0
\quad\text{and}\quad
\tau_{n+1}=\inf\left\{t\ge \tau_n \ \middle|\  \int_0^{\infty} \mathbf{1}_{(\tau_n,t]}(s)\,\vartheta_s\,\diff A_s \ge \eta\right\}.
\]
Finally, we set $\varepsilon=1$ on $(\tau_{2n},\tau_{2n+1}]$ and $\varepsilon=-1$ elsewhere. Furthermore, \(\epsilon\) is predictable as a sum of indicators of predictable sets and their union. Because \(\vartheta\bullet A\) is increasing and continuous (due to the continuity of \(A\)), we get 
\begin{equation*}
    0\leq (\vartheta\epsilon\bullet A)_t\leq \eta.
\end{equation*}
This completes the proof.
\end{proof}
We can now provide the proof of Theorem \ref{thm:G_T closed implies D_2(P)}.
\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem \ref{thm:G_T closed implies D_2(P)}]
    Let \(\vartheta\in L^2(M,A)\) and \(\epsilon\) the predictable process of Lemma \ref{lemma:predictable epsilon}. Then 
    \begin{align*}
        \lVert G_{\infty}(\epsilon\vartheta)\rVert_{L^2}&\leq \lVert (\epsilon\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}\rVert_{L^2}+\lVert (\epsilon\vartheta\bullet A)_{\infty}\rVert_{L^2}
        \\
        &\leq \lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}+\eta,
    \end{align*}
    by the same reasoning as in \eqref{eq:bounding-L2-norm-stochastic-integral} and Lemma \ref{lemma:predictable epsilon}. As we assume the existence of an ELMM \(\mathbb{Q}\) with \(\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\in L^2(\mathbb{P})\) we know by Lemma \ref{lemma:integral map is bijective} that \(I_{\infty}\) is bijective. By Proposition \ref{prop:closedness of G_T in L^2 functional analytic iff condition}, under the assumption that \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\subseteq L^2(\mathbb{P})\) is closed, we then have
    \begin{align*}
        \lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}+\lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(A)}&=\lVert \epsilon\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}+\lVert \epsilon\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(A)}\\&
        \leq C\lVert{G_{\infty}(\epsilon\vartheta)}\rVert_{L^2}
        \\&\leq C(\lVert\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}+\eta).
    \end{align*}
    This holds for any \(\eta>0\); hence, \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\) is satisfied.
\end{proof}
Note that, in particular, a sufficient condition for \(A\) to be continuous is to consider \(X\in\mathcal{S}_{\rm{loc}}^2\) continuous (see the Corollary after Theorem 35 of \parencite{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}). 



Recall that in light of the mean-variance hedging problem, the closedness of \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\subseteq L^2(\mathbb{P})\) implies the existence of a solution to the mean-variance hedging problem, as the latter reduces to a \(L^2\)-projection problem of the liability \(H\in L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty})\) onto the closed subspace \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)+\mathbb{R}\). On the other hand, as previously discussed, condition \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\) implies that \(\Theta=L^2(M,A)=L^2(M)\). From Proposition \ref{prop:density of E(psi) in L^2(M)}, we have the density of \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) in \(L^2(M)\), which allows for an extension of the deep mean variance hedging result, Theorem \ref{thm:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging}. For the latter, we need both existence and approximation. Theorem \ref{thm:G_T closed implies D_2(P)} shows that studying the closedness of \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) solves both the existence of a mean-variance optimal strategy and approximation by algorithmic strategies at once, if we assume the price process \(X\) to be continuous, or at least its finite variation part to be so. Conversely, one might ask under which assumptions the reversed implication is true, that is, under what additional conditions \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\) implies the closedness of \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\). In general, \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\) is neither sufficient nor necessary for the closedness of \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\), as shown by the counter-examples in 3.9-3.12. in \parencite{DelbaenSchachermayer1998ExistenceAbsolutelyContinuousLMM}. Nevertheless, it is interesting that, in the case where there exists an ELMM with a square integrable density and such that the finite variation part of \(X\) is continuous, we have that \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\) holds if and only if \(G(\Theta)\coloneqq \{\vartheta\bullet X:\vartheta
\in\Theta\}\) is closed in the space of adapted square-integrable processes under the norm   
\begin{equation*}
    \lVert\vartheta\rVert_{\mathcal{R}^2(\mathbb{P})}=\lVert\vartheta_\infty^*\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})}.
\end{equation*}
See \parencite[Thm.~3.13.]{DelbaenMonatSchachermayerSchweizerStricker1997WeightedNormInequalities} for details.
We will not further investigate this route as we independently require the closedness of \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) in \(L^2\), which is a sufficient condition for \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\) as just seen. We have already stated Theorem \ref{thm:closedness of G_T(Theta)} which completely characterizes the closedness of \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) in \(L^2\) in the case that \(X\) is a continuous semimartingale in \(\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{loc}}\). 
We recall it here for convenience.
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:recalling closedness of G_T(Theta)}
Let $X\in\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{loc}}$ be continuous. Then the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \(\mathbb{P}_e^2(X)\neq\emptyset\) and
 $G_{\infty}(\Theta)$ is closed in $L^2(\mathbb{P})$.
\item There exists some \(\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}_e^2(X)\) satisfying the inequality
      $\mathcal{R}_2(\mathbb{P})$.
\item The variance-optimal martingale measure \(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}\) is in \(\mathbb{\mathbb{P}}_e^2(X)\) and satisfies the \(\mathcal{R}^2(\mathbb{P})\)-inequality.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
Thus, to have an existence claim about the mean-variance hedging solution, we shall require \(X\) to be continuous. We can finally state an extension of 
Theorem \ref{thm:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging} to price processes in \(L^2(M,A)\). Recall that  \(\mathbb{\mathbb{P}}_e^2(X)\) denotes the set of equivalent local martingale measures \(\mathbb{Q}\approx \mathbb{P}\) such that \(\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\in L^2(\mathbb{P})\).


\begin{proposition}\label{prop:continuous time deep mean variance hedging for semimartingales under compactness}
    Let the stochastic basis be as in \eqref{eq:stochastic basis satisfying usual hypotheses}.
    Assume the \(X=X_0+M+A\) is a continuous semimartingale in \(\mathcal{S}^2\) such that there exists \(\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}_e^2(X)\) satisfying the \(\mathcal{R}_2(\mathbb{P})\)-inequality. Moreover, let \(\psi\) be a bounded, measurable, and sigmoidal activation function. Furthermore, assume that there exists \(\epsilon>0\) for which \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^{2+\epsilon}\). Let \(H\in  L^2(\mathbb{P})\) be a real-valued \(\mathcal{F}_{\infty}\)-measurable random variable, and assume \(\mathcal{F}_0\) to be trivial. Then we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:min equals inf semimartingale mean variance hedging with epsilon integrability}
    \min_{V_0\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in L^2(M,A)}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(V_0+\int_{0}^{\infty}\vartheta_t\diff X_t-H\bigg)^2\bigg]
    \,=\,\inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in \mathcal{E}(\psi)}
    \mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(V_0+\int_{0}^{\infty}\vartheta_t\diff X_t-H\bigg)^2\bigg],
\end{equation}
where the minimum on the left-hand side is unique in \(\mathbb{R}\times L^2(M,A).\)
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
   By Theorem \ref{thm:recalling closedness of G_T(Theta)} part (2.) the assumption that there exists \(\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}_e^2(X)\) satisfying the \(\mathcal{R}_2(\mathbb{P})\)-inequality implies that \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\) is closed in \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\). In particular, according to Lemma \ref{lemma:A closed if G closed}, we have that \(\mathbb{R}+G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\) is closed as well. Hence, the minimum in \eqref{eq:min equals inf semimartingale mean variance hedging with epsilon integrability} exists. 
  Furthermore, the assumption that \(X\in \mathcal{S}^2\) is continuous implies that \(A\) is continuous and, as \(\mathbb{P}_e^2(X)\neq\emptyset\) and \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\subseteq L^2(\mathbb{P})\) are closed by the above, we have by Theorem \ref{thm:G_T closed implies D_2(P)} that \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\) holds, and hence \(L^2(M,A) = L^2(M)\). In particular, by Proposition \ref{prop:Lp(M) density of E(psi)}, with \(M\in\mathcal{H}^{2+\epsilon}\), we have that \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) is dense in \(L^2(M)=L^2(M,A)\). Hence, the equality in \eqref{eq:min equals inf semimartingale mean variance hedging with epsilon integrability} follows with the same argument as in the proof of Theorem \ref{thm:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging}.

   Regarding uniqueness, first notice that the uniqueness of the mean-variance of a mean-variance portfolio \((V_0^*,\vartheta^*)\) as an element \(V_0^*+\int_0^\infty\vartheta_t^*\diff X_t\) in \(\mathbb{R}+G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\) is given by the uniqueness of the Hilbert-projection theorem of \(H\) onto the \(L^2\)-closed subspace \(\mathbb{R}+G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\). Furthermore, by Lemma \ref{lemma:P_s(Theta) in P_e(X)}, we have that \(\mathbb{P}_e^2(X)\neq \emptyset\) implies, in particular, that \(\mathbb{P}_s^2(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\neq \emptyset\). Hence, by Lemma \ref{lemma:no approximate profits}, \(1\notin G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\). Thus, the discussion prior to Definition \ref{def:no approximate profits} implies that the mean-variance portfolio is also unique as an element  \((V_0^*,\int_0^\infty\vartheta^*\diff X)\in\mathbb{R}\times G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\). Finally, again by Lemma \ref{lemma:integral map is bijective}, under the current assumption of the theorem, \(I_{\infty}\) is injective, whence the uniqueness of the mean-variance portfolio \((V_0^*,\vartheta^*)\in\mathbb{R}\times L^2(M,A)\) follows.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:relaxed assumptions}
    We could also relax the assumptions on \(X\) to be in \(\mathcal{S}^2_{loc}\) with the additional requirement that the local martingale part \(M\) of \(X\) is actually a true martingale in \(\mathcal{H}_0^{2+\epsilon}\) for some \(\epsilon>0\). The difference is that for \(\mathcal{S}_{\rm{loc}}^2\), one requires the finite variation process \(A\) to be locally square-integrable only. Notice that the requirement that \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\) and not just locally in \(\mathcal{H}^2_0\) is crucial in our development, as if \(M\) is only in  \(\mathcal{H}^2_{0,\rm{loc}}\), \(\mathcal{E}\), and \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) need not be subsets of \(L^2(M)\).
\end{remark}
Notice that Proposition \ref{prop:continuous time deep mean variance hedging for semimartingales under compactness} indeed generalizes Theorem \ref{thm:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging}. Indeed, if \(X\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\), the finite variation process \(A=0\) is trivially continuous, and as \(X\) is itself a martingale, \(\mathbb{P}\in \mathbb{P}_e^2(X)\) with density \(\frac{\diff \mathbb{P}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}=1\in L^2(\mathbb{P})\) and \(Z_t=\mathbb{E}[1|\mathcal{F}_t]=1\) trivially satisfies the \(\mathcal{R}_2(\mathbb{P})\)-inequality. Furthermore, for \(A=0\) we clearly have \(L^2(A)=\{\text{predictable processes}\}\) hence \(L^2(M,A)=L^2(M)\). 
 Furthermore, observe that Proposition \ref{prop:continuous time deep mean variance hedging for semimartingales under compactness} only involves \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\). Hence, 
according to the discussion before Corollary \ref{cor:Lp(M) density of tilde(E)(psi) for change of variables}, considering a continuous, sigmoidal activation function \(\psi\) and the market information process \(\widetilde{Y}=\tanh(Y)\) instead of \(Y\), effectively replacing \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) with \(\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\psi)\), we might let go of the additional \(\epsilon\)-integrability condition on \(M\). We get the following version. 


\begin{theorem}\label{thm:continuous time deep mean variance hedging for semimartingales}
Let the stochastic basis be as in \eqref{eq:stochastic basis satisfying usual hypotheses}.
    Assume the \(X=X_0+M+A\) is a continuous semimartingale in \(\mathcal{S}^2\) such that there exists \(\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}_e^2(X)\) satisfying the \(\mathcal{R}_2(\mathbb{P})\)-inequality. Moreover, take \(\psi\) to be a continuous, sigmoidal activation function. Let \(H\in L^2(\mathbb{P})\) be a real-valued \(\mathcal{F}_{\infty}\)-measurable random variable and assume \(\mathcal{F}_0\) to be trivial. Then we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:martingale setup, continuous deep variance hedging}
    \min_{V_0\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in L^2(M,A)}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(V_0+\int_{0}^{\infty}\vartheta_t\diff X_t-H\bigg)^2\bigg]
    \,=\,\inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in \widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\psi)}
    \mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(V_0+\int_{0}^{\infty}\vartheta_t\diff X_t-H\bigg)^2\bigg],
\end{equation}
where the minimum on the left-hand side is unique in \(\mathbb{R}\times L^2(M,A).\)
\end{theorem}
The proof is the same as for Proposition \ref{prop:continuous time deep mean variance hedging for semimartingales under compactness}, using the density of \(\widetilde{\mathcal{E}(\psi)}\) in \(L^2(M)\) by Corollary \ref{cor:Lp(M) density of tilde(E)(psi) for change of variables}.
Remark \ref{rem:relaxed assumptions} applies here as well. It is enough that the local martingale part \(M\) of \(X\in\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{loc}}\) is a true martingale in \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\).





\chapter{Conclusion}
\section{Summary of contributions}
This thesis studied the theoretical approximation aspects of neural networks in continuous-time mathematical finance, with a particular focus on mean–variance hedging. The starting point was the classical hedging problem of approximating a contingent claim \(H\) by the terminal values of self-financing portfolios. In particular, we studied in depth the mean-variance hedging optimization problem 
\begin{equation*}
    \inf_{(V_0,\vartheta)\in\mathbb{R}\times\Theta}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(H-V_0-\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta\diff X\right)^2\bigg],
\end{equation*}
where we studied different choices for \(X\) and \(\Theta\). More precisely, we studied the mean-variance hedging problem in the square-integrable setting: \(X\in \mathcal{H}^2_0\) and \(\Theta=L^2(M)\); and the semimartingale setting: \(X\in\mathcal{S}^2\) and \(\Theta=L^2(M,A)\). In the square-integrable martingale setting, the problem reduces to a Hilbert-projection problem, and the solution is explicitly characterized by the Kunita–Watanabe decomposition of \(H\) with respect to \(X\). In particular, the closedness of 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{A}(\Theta)=\mathbb{R}+G_{\infty}(\Theta),
\end{equation*}
was a direct consequence of the properties of the respective notion of stochastic integration.
For the semimartingale case, the closedness of \(\mathcal{A}(\Theta)\) in \(L^2\) was a consequence of assuming the existence of an equivalent measure \(\mathbb{Q}\) under which the semimartingale \(X\) becomes a local martingale, such that the density process \(Z=(Z_t)_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\) with
\begin{equation*}
    Z_t=\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\Big|\mathcal{F}_t\Big],\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+,
\end{equation*}
satisfies the reverse Hölder-inequality under \(\mathbb{P}\) for \(p=2\). In all cases, we discussed the existence and uniqueness of the mean-variance solution which is intimitely related to the Hilbert projection theorem and the wether the \(L^2\)-closure of \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) containes the constant random variable \(1\).




We especially focused on how to approximate the optimal mean-variance solution by leveraging the density properties of neural networks in \(L^p\) spaces. In the finite sample space and finite time-grid setup, we discussed in depth the theoretical approach proposed in \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging}. For the general case, we followed \parencite{Arandjelovic2025}, understood why the approach is particularly convenient, and how it can be used to formulate and prove a deep-mean-variance hedging result in the continuous time, square-integrable martingale setting. In particular, we showed how to improve on the integrability conditions of \parencite[Thm.~5.32]{Arandjelovic2025} by a change of coordinates for the market information process. 



Finally, we showed how to generalize this continuous time deep mean variance hedging result to the semimartingale case, in particular by leveraging the density arguments \parencite[Prop.~5.28.]{Arandjelovic2025} to the space
\begin{equation*}
    \Theta=L^2(M)\cap L^2(A).
\end{equation*}

Overall, the thesis shows that, if done properly, the approach developed in \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging} is extremely powerful, particularly in the treatment of the mean-variance hedging problem. Neural networks are used as dense classes of deterministic functions from which predictable trading strategies are generated. The key mathematical task is to identify the correct function spaces and continuity properties such that the approximating predictable strategies lead to an approximation of the respective value processes.

The results obtained in this thesis are purely theoretical. Numerical implementation, training procedures, convergence rates, and empirical performance were not considered. These aspects would form natural directions for future work. Another possible direction is to study more explicitly how restrictive the assumptions in the semimartingale extension are in concrete financial models. Finally, it would be interesting to investigate whether similar approximation ideas lead to time-continuous extensions of deep convex-risk measure hedging or to time-continuous models that include additional market frictions.

\section{Consistency of completed \(\sigma\)-algebra}\label{Appendix:Consistency of completed sigma-algebra}
We show the following statement by double inclusion
\begin{equation}\label{eq:appendix consistency of completion}
    \sigma\Big(\sigma(\bigcup_{t\geq 0}\mathcal{F}_t)\cup\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\Big)=\sigma\Big(\bigcup_{t\geq 0}\sigma(\mathcal{F}_t\cup\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}})\Big),
\end{equation}
\begin{proof}
Denote by \(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{'}\) the left-hand side of \eqref{eq:appendix consistency of completion} and by \(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{''}\) its right-hand side. We show \(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{'}\subseteq \overline{\mathcal{F}}^{''}\). For this, observe that \(\bigcup_{t\geq 0}\mathcal{F}_t\subseteq \overline{\mathcal{F}}^{''}\), and hence \(\sigma(\bigcup_{t\geq 0}\mathcal{F}_t)\subseteq \overline{\mathcal{F}}^{''}\) is also so. Similarly, \(\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{''} \). Hence \(\sigma(\bigcup_{t\geq 0}\mathcal{F}_t)\cup \mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\subseteq \overline{\mathcal{F}}^{''}\), and thus \(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{'}\subseteq \overline{\mathcal{F}}^{''}\) as well. Conversely, we proceed by similar reasoning. \(\mathcal{F}_t\cup\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\subseteq \overline{\mathcal{F}}^{'}\), hence so is its generated \(\sigma\)-algebra. But if \(\sigma(\mathcal{F}_t\cup\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}})\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{'}\) for every \(t\geq 0\), then \(\bigcup_{t\geq 0}\sigma(\mathcal{F}_t\cup\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}})\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{'}\). Indeed, if \(A\in \bigcup_{t\geq 0}\sigma(\mathcal{F}_t)\) there exists \(t\geq 0\) such that \(A\in \sigma(\mathcal{F}_t)\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{'}\). Then again, this implies that the generated \(\sigma\)-algebra is also included, \(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{''}\subseteq \overline{\mathcal{F}}^{'}\), which concludes the proof. 
\end{proof}

\section{Direct proof of Finite Deep mean variance Hedging}\label{Appendix:Direct proof of Finite Deep mean variance Hedging}
    Recall that 
    \begin{align*}
        &\Theta=\left\{\vartheta=(\vartheta_k)_{k=1}^n:\vartheta_k \text{ is }\mathcal{F}_{k-1}\text{-measurable}\right\}\\
        &\Theta_M=\left\{\vartheta=(\vartheta_k)_{k=1}^n:\vartheta_k=F_k(Y_0,\dots Y_{k-1}),F_k\in\Psi_M\right\}\\
        &\Theta_{\infty}=\bigcup_{M\in\mathbb{N}}\Theta_M.
    \end{align*}
    As for every \(\vartheta\in\Theta_{\infty}\) there exists an \(M\in\mathbb{N}\) such that \(\vartheta\in\Theta_M\) and hence \(\vartheta_k=F_k(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\) are \(\mathcal{F}_{k}\)-measurable we have 
    \begin{equation*}
        \Theta_{\infty}\subseteq\Theta
    \end{equation*}
    We want to show that \(\overline{\Theta_{\infty}}^{\tau}=\Theta\) for topology \(\tau\).
   First, observe that 
    \begin{equation*}
    \Theta=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}L^2(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{k-1},\mathbb{P}).
    \end{equation*}
    Indeed, every \(\vartheta_k\in\Theta\) is trivially square integrable because \(\Omega\) is finite
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{E}\big[|\vartheta_k|^2\big]=\sum_{i=1}^N|\vartheta_k(\omega_i)|^2\mathbb{P}[\{\omega_i\}]<\infty.
    \end{equation*}
    Because \(\vartheta\) is an \(\mathbb{F}\)-predictable process, \(\vartheta_k\) is \(\mathcal{F}_{k-1}\)-measurable. Conversely, every \(\vartheta\in\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}L^2(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{k-1},\mathbb{P})\) is, by definition, of the form \(\vartheta=(\vartheta_1,\dots,\vartheta_k)\) with \(\vartheta_k\in L^2(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{k-1},\mathbb{P})\). 

    Furthermore, by the Doob-representation lemma, we have for \(\vartheta\in\Theta\) that there exists \(f:\mathbb{R}^{rk}\to\mathbb{R}\),  (Borel-)measurable such that
    \begin{equation*}
        \vartheta_k=f(Y_0,\dots Y_{k-1}).
    \end{equation*}
    Because \(\Omega\) is finite, we have that \(f_k\in L^2(\mathbb{R}^{rk},\mu)\), where \(\mu=\mathcal{L}(Y_0,\dots Y_{k-1})\).
    Let
    \begin{equation*}
        \Xi_k=\left\{X: L^0(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}):X=F(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\text{ where } F_k\in\Psi\right\}
    \end{equation*}
    where 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:density of psi in L^2}
        \overline{\Psi}^{L^2}=L^2(\mu).
    \end{equation}
    for any finite measure \(\mu\).
    Recalling that \(\Psi=\Psi_{\infty}=\bigcup_{M\geq 1}\Psi_M\)
    \begin{equation*}
        \Theta_{\infty}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}\Xi_k.
    \end{equation*}
    By the density of \(\Psi\) in \(L^2\), we have 
    \begin{equation*}
    \overline{\Xi_k}^{L^2}=L^2(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{k-1},\mathbb{P}).
    \end{equation*}
    Indeed, clearly we have that \(\Xi_k\subseteq L^2(\mathcal{F}_{k-1},\mathbb{P})\) for all \(k=0,\dots,n\) because \(\Psi\subseteq L^2\). The density follows by Doob's representation lemma. Let \(Z\in L^2(\mathcal{F}_{k-1},\mathbb{P})\). There exists \(g\in L^2(\mu)\), where \(\mu=\mathcal{L}(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\). By \eqref{eq:density of psi in L^2} there exists \((f_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\Psi\) converging to \(g\) in \(L^2(\mu)\). Equivalently
    \begin{equation*}
        \lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[(f_n-g)^2]=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[(f_n(Y_0,\dots,Y_k)-Z)^2]=0.
    \end{equation*}
    where \(f_n(Y_0,\dots,Y_k)\in\Xi_k\).

    
We define the following norm on \(\Theta\):
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sum norm}
    \lVert(\vartheta_1,\dots,\vartheta_{n})\rVert_{(+)}\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{n}\lVert \vartheta_k\rVert_{L^2}
\end{equation}
Furthermore, we have 
\begin{lemma}
    Let \(\lVert\cdot\rVert_{(+)}\) be defined as in \eqref{eq:sum norm}. Then 
    \begin{equation*}
        \overline{\Theta_{\infty}}^{\lVert\cdot\rVert_{(+)}}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}\overline{\Xi_k}^{L^2}
    \end{equation*}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    We check both inclusions. Let \(\vartheta\in\overline{\Theta_{\infty}}^{\lVert\cdot\rVert_{(+)}}\). Hence, there exists \((\vartheta^l)_{l\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\Theta_{\infty}\) such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \lim_{l\to\infty}\lVert \vartheta^l-\vartheta\rVert_{(+)}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{k=1}^n\lVert\vartheta^l_k-\vartheta_k\rVert_{L^2}=0.
    \end{equation*}
    In particular, 
    \begin{equation*}
        \lim_{l\to\infty}\lVert\vartheta^l_k-\vartheta_k\rVert_{L^2}=0,\quad\forall k=1,\dots,n
    \end{equation*}
    Hence, \(\vartheta_k\in\overline{\Xi_k}^{L^2}\) for all \(k=1,\dots,n\), thus, \(\vartheta\in\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}\overline{\Xi_k}^{L^2}\). Conversely, let \(\vartheta\in\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}\overline{\Xi_k}^{L^2}\). Hence, for every \(k=1,\dots,n\), there exists \((\vartheta^l_k)_{l\in\mathbb{N}}\), with \(\vartheta^l_k\in\Xi_k\), such that 
    \begin{equation*}
    \lim_{l\to\infty}\lVert \vartheta^l_k-\vartheta_k\rVert_{L^2}=0.
    \end{equation*}
    In particular, \(\vartheta^l=(\vartheta^l_k)_{k=1}^n\in\Theta_{\infty}\) for every \(l\in\mathbb{N}\).
 Hence,
    \begin{equation*}
        \lim_{l\to\infty}\sum_{k=1}^n\lVert\vartheta^l_k-\vartheta_k\rVert_{L^2}=\lim_{l\to\infty}\lVert\vartheta^l-\vartheta\rVert_{(+)}=0
    \end{equation*}
    showing that \(\vartheta\in\overline{\Theta_{\infty}}^{\lVert\cdot\rVert_{(+)}}\).
\end{proof}
Thus, we have
\begin{equation*}
    \overline{\Theta_{\infty}}^{\lVert\cdot\rVert_{(+)}}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}\overline{\Xi_k}^{L^2}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}L^2(\mathcal{F}_{k-1},\mathbb{P})=\Theta.
\end{equation*}

If we show that the map 
\begin{align*}
    J:&\Theta_{\infty}\to\mathbb{R}_+
    \\&\vartheta\mapsto\mathbb{E}\Big[(V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X)_{T}-H)^2\Big],
\end{align*}
is continuous with respect to the \(\lVert\cdot\rVert_{(+)}\)-induced topology, then  
Proposition \ref{prop:finite deep mean variance hedging} follows from Lemma \ref{lemma:minimization of closure} and the observation that in the finite case 
\begin{equation*}
    \big|(\vartheta\bullet X)_T\big|\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n}|\vartheta_k||X_{k}-X_{k-1}|\leq2C\sum_{k=1}^n|\vartheta_k|,
\end{equation*}
where \(C\coloneqq\max_{0\leq k\leq n}\max_{1\leq i\leq N}|X_k(\omega_i)|<\infty\) as every \(X_k\) is bounded as a random variable on the finite sample space \(\Omega\). Hence, for \(\vartheta,\eta\in\Theta_{\infty}\), due to the inverse triangle inequality and the linearity of the stochastic integral,
\begin{align*}
    |J(\vartheta)^{\frac{1}{2}}-J(\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}}|&=\Big|\lVert V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H\rVert_{L^2}-\lVert V_0+(\eta\bullet X)_T-H\rVert_{L^2}\Big|
    \\&\leq\lVert ((\vartheta-\eta)\bullet X)_T\rVert_{L^2}\leq 2C\sum_{k=1}^n\lVert\vartheta_k-\eta_k\rVert_{L^2}=2C\lVert\vartheta-\eta\rVert_{(+)}.
\end{align*}
Hence, \(J^{\frac{1}{2}}\) is continuous, and 
thus, the map \(J\) is continuous, as it is the composition of the continuous functions \(J^{\frac{1}{2}}\) and \(x\mapsto x^2\). 

\section{Borel \(\sigma\)-algebra of separable metric space is countably generated}\label{Appendix:Borel sigma-algebra of separable metric space is countably generated}
Let \((S,d)\) be a metric space and \(D\subseteq S\) a countable set that is dense in \(S\) for the topology induced by the metric \(d\), which is defined by the basis of open balls \(B(x,r)=\{y\in S:d(x,y)<r\}\) for \(x\in X\) and \(r>0\).
 It suffices to show that the set 
 \begin{equation*}
     \mathcal{C}=\{B(x,\epsilon):x\in D,\epsilon\in\mathbb{Q}_+\}
 \end{equation*}
is a basis for the topology induced by the metric in \(S\). Indeed, as \(\mathcal{C}\) is a countable collection of sets, if we show that any open set \(U\) in \(S\) can be written as a union of sets in \(\mathcal{C}\), then this union is, in particular, countable, and hence every open set is in \(\sigma(\mathcal{C})\). As the open sets generate the Borel \(\sigma\)-algebra on \(S\), \(\sigma(\mathcal{C})\) does as well.

Let \(U\) be an open set in \((S,d)\). As the open balls form a basis for the metric topology, we have that for every \(u\in U\), there exists \(\epsilon_u>0\) such that \(B(u,\epsilon_u)\subseteq U\). Hence
\begin{equation*}
    U=\bigcup_{u\in U}B(u,\epsilon_u).
\end{equation*}
As \(D\) is dense in \(X\), for every \(u\in U\) and for every \(\delta>0\) there exists \(v\in D\) such that \(d(u,v)<\delta\). For every \(u\), choose \(v_u\in D\) and \(\delta_u>0\) such that \(\delta_u<\epsilon_u/2\). By the density of \(\mathbb{Q}_+\) in \(\mathbb{R}_+\), there exists \(r_u\in\mathbb{Q}_+\) such that \(\delta_u<r_u<\epsilon_u/2\). Hence, \(u\in B(v_u,r_u)\subseteq B(u,\epsilon_u)\) by the triangle inequality. We have 
\begin{equation*}
   U=\bigcup_{u\in U}B(u,\epsilon_u)= \bigcup_{u\in U}B(v_u,r_u).
\end{equation*}
Notice that, even though the union in \(\bigcup_{u\in U}B(v_u,r_u)\) is taken over the uncountable set \(U\), it is a union of sets in the countable collection \(\mathcal{C}\). Hence, there can be at most countably many distinct balls over which the union is taken, proving the claim.


\section{Without loss of generality argument }\label{Appendix:w.l.o.g.}
We provide the details on why, in Lemma \ref{lemma:predictable epsilon}, we might \(\vartheta\bullet A\) be increasing without loss of generality. Hence, in what follows, assume Lemma \ref{lemma:predictable epsilon} to be true for increasing processes \(\vartheta\bullet A\).


Since \(A\) is a predictable process of finite variation, its variation process \(|A|\) is predictable (see \parencite[Prop.~3.3]{JacodShiryaev2003LimitTheoremsStochasticProcesses}). Pathwise, the signed Lebesgue-Stieltjes measure \(\diff A(\omega)\) is absolutely continuous with respect to its total-variation measure \(\diff |A|(\omega)\). Hence, by Proposition \parencite[Prop.~3.13]{JacodShiryaev2003LimitTheoremsStochasticProcesses}, there exists a predictable process \(\alpha\) such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:predictable absolute continuity of |A| and A}
    \mathrm{d}A_t = \alpha_t\,\mathrm{d}|A|_t,\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+.
\end{equation}
By the polar decomposition of signed measures, the Radon--Nikod\'ym derivative of a real signed measure with respect to its total variation takes values in \(\{-1,1\}\) almost everywhere (see \parencite[Prop.~3.13]{Folland1999RealAnalysis}). Consequently, for all \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\) we have
\begin{equation*}
    |\alpha_t|=1.
\end{equation*}
Hence, we might apply the reasoning in Lemma \ref{lemma:predictable epsilon} to the increasing finite-variation process
\begin{equation*}
    B_t\coloneqq\bigg|\int_0^\cdot \vartheta_s\diff A_s\bigg|_t .
\end{equation*}
Indeed, due to 
\begin{equation*}
    \bigg|\int \vartheta\diff A\bigg|=\int |\vartheta|\diff |A|,
\end{equation*}
which holds up to indistinguishability 
(see \parencite[Thm.~2.11]{Gushchin2015MathematicalBasisFinance}),
\(B\) is indeed of finite variation, as \(A\) is of finite variation and \(\vartheta\in L^2(A)\). It is clear that \(|\vartheta|\in L^2(A)\) if \(\vartheta\in L^2(A)\). Furthermore, it is continuous if \(A\) is continuous.
Hence, by Lemma \ref{lemma:predictable epsilon} for the increasing process \(B\), there exists a predictable \(\widetilde{\epsilon}\) with \(|\widetilde{\epsilon}_t|=1\) for all \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\) such that 
\begin{equation*}
    \bigg|\int_0^t|\vartheta_s|\widetilde{\epsilon}_s\diff |A|_s\bigg|\leq\eta,\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+.
\end{equation*}
But 
\begin{equation*}
    \int_0^t|\vartheta_s|\widetilde{\epsilon}_s\diff |A|_s=\int_0^t|\vartheta_s|\widetilde{\epsilon}_s\alpha_s^2\diff |A|_s=\int_0^t|\vartheta_s|\widetilde{\epsilon}_s\alpha_s\diff A_s=\int_0^t\vartheta_s\rm{sgn}(\vartheta)_s\widetilde{\epsilon}_s\alpha_s\diff A_s,
\end{equation*}
where \(\rm{sgn}(\vartheta)\coloneqq1_{\vartheta\geq 0}-1_{\vartheta<0}\), which is predictable as a sum of indicator functions of predictable sets (because \(\vartheta\) is predictable by assumption). 
Hence, defining
\begin{equation*}
    \epsilon_s = \rm{sgn}(\vartheta)_s\widetilde{\epsilon}_s\alpha_s
\end{equation*}
we have that \(\epsilon\) is predictable as a pointwise product of predictable processes, and clearly \(|\epsilon_t|=1\) for all \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\). Hence 
\begin{equation*}
\bigg|\int_0^t\vartheta_s\epsilon_s\diff A_s\bigg|\leq\eta,\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+.
\end{equation*}
